A Humble Servant and Fellow Witness
:::


:::abstract
The Quran corrects the prophets it names and does not soften the corrections, and four of the five figures it says were given a book carry heavy records. The fifth, ʿĪsā AS, carries none. Beside him stands Yaḥyā AS, of whom the negation is stated outright rather than merely omitted. This commentary takes that asymmetry as its starting point, reads the one passage in which the corpus interrogates ʿĪsā AS directly and finds it returns clean, and holds that the silence is a guard rather than an elevation: it is what makes the servanthood declared from the cradle a thing chosen rather than a thing conceded.

From that position the earliest Gospel is read entire. Not eighteen selections, and not a harmony: every passage of Mark from the first verse to the last, in order, with nothing skipped and nothing smoothed. Each passage is quoted, read against the corpus, and then tested as a structure, and where the words of the two texts diverge on the surface, the structural reading is allowed to finish the sentence the words began. The reading finds one pattern running the length of the book, authority received rather than held, and it finds that pattern to be the same one the corpus attaches to every miracle it records of this prophet: by My permission, four times in a single verse.

What emerges is a Gospel that agrees with the Quran far more deeply than either community has been taught to expect, and that diverges from it in a small number of places that can be named, counted, and in most cases traced to the same operation the corpus performs on itself. Eight such corrections are recorded. The threads that bind the book, a verb of strength, a spitting, four Aramaic words, six interruptions, nine silencings, are counted as they arrive, with one preposition standing apart at the close. And the ending that stops at fear is set beside the verse that lifts fear from the friends of Allah ﷻ, so that the last word of the one text meets the first mercy of the other.
:::

# THE FINDING IN BRIEF

For fourteen centuries the Quran and the Gospel have been read as opposite accounts of the same figure. This commentary reports that on the evidence of the two texts themselves, they are not.

**The Quran corrects its prophets and does not soften the corrections.** Adam forgot. Noah asked for what he had no right to know. Abraham confessed a fault in his own prayer. Moses killed a man and asked for a sight he was refused. Jonah ran. David was forgiven a named thing. Solomon was corrected by a bird. Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, was corrected by revelation seven times, itemised in Part I, and twice told to seek forgiveness for his own offence.

**Jesus is the only bearer of a scripture in the whole corpus with a clean file.** Four of the five who were given a book carry heavy records. He carries nothing: not a rebuke, not a lapse, not a line. And of Yaḥyā AS, who stands beside him in the same surah, the negation is stated outright: he was not a tyrant, not disobedient, in the very word the corpus uses of Adam's slip.

**The silence is not an elevation. It is a guard.** Had a fault been recorded, the servanthood would have been proved by deficiency: a servant because he could not be more. Instead the corpus has him declare himself a servant from the cradle, before anything else is said about him, and states one verse later that the Messiah does not disdain to be a servant. Disdain presupposes a height. The silence is what makes the servanthood chosen rather than conceded.

**What the corpus addresses in the received picture is not a set of claims but three grammatical constructions, and to each it supplies the correct form.** The equation that makes the Messiah convertible with Allah ﷻ is refused, and the same construction is written affirmatively elsewhere with the office correctly filled. The number is barred, in the very verse that supplies three designations for one referent. And begetting is denied by asking how there could be offspring with no consort, with the mechanism given in the next clause, *kun fa-yakūn*. Nothing is taken and left empty. And the earliest Gospel, written before the settlement that raised those constructions, uses none of them once.

**Where it denies begetting it names what replaces it, in the same breath.** Twice, at Q 2:117 and Q 19:35, the claim of offspring is refused and the mechanism is given in the next clause: kun fa-yakūn, Be, and it is. Command, not procreation. The corpus also meets the inference from a birth without a father with a harder case: Adam had neither father nor mother, and nobody has ever concluded divine sonship from that. Fatherlessness is not the ground. The mechanism is.

**And the earliest Gospel drops the begetting clause with its own hand.** At the baptism the voice from heaven speaks in the words of the second Psalm, you are my son, and the Psalm's next clause, today I have begotten you, is the clause Mark does not carry. The quotation stops one phrase short of generation, at the beginning of the book, in the mouth of the voice itself. What the corpus fences six centuries later, the first evangelist had already declined to write.

**What the corpus keeps is nearly everything else.** The conception without a father, stated four separate times in four surahs. The title Messiah, eleven times, never withdrawn even in the clauses that condemn what was said about it. Word from Him. Spirit from Him. Strengthened by the Holy Spirit, three times. Speech from the cradle. A book given. Clay formed and breathed to life. The blind and the leper healed. The dead raised. The taking and the raising. Peace over his birth, his death, and his rising. A place among the five prophets of the solemn covenant. Compassion and mercy placed in the hearts of his followers.

**The grammar the corpus condemns, it also demonstrates in its own voice, and that locates the condemnation precisely.** The corpus rejects the sentence that fills the office of protector and life-giver with a creature. Then at Q 42:9 it writes the very construction it rejected and fills it correctly: fa-Allāhu huwa al-Walī, wa-huwa yuḥyī al-mawtā, so Allah ﷻ, He is the Protector, and He gives life to the dead. Five times across the corpus the same form recurs with the same subject. The condemnation was never of the sentence-shape. It was of the name placed in it. The office is real, the throne is real, and the corpus's whole quarrel is with the occupant the settlement seated there.

**The prayer of this Gospel and the prayer of this corpus run on a single voice, and the corpus proves it with a missing word.** Through Sūrat al-Baqarah the questions come to the Prophet ﷺ, they ask you about the new moons, about fighting, about wine, and every answer is routed through him: say, qul. One question breaks the pattern. When My servants ask you about Me, the answer comes with the messenger removed from the sentence: I am near, I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me. No qul. The one subject on which the intermediary is deleted from the grammar itself is Him. Mark's prophet withdraws alone to pray, tells the paralytic's household that the forgiving runs above, and dies with a address on his lips. The two texts keep the same line open and keep it unbrokered.

**Even the salt is the same covenant.** Mark closes the hardest chapter of discipleship with have salt in yourselves, and the instruction has puzzled readers because salt is the one thing in the ancient kitchen a man cannot grow. That is the point, and the Torah had already made it law: the covenant of salt at Numbers 18:19 is a stipend, the priests eat what they did not produce, and the court of Persia says we eat the palace's salt to mean we are on the king's provision. A covenant sealed in salt is a declaration of dependence built into the material itself. The corpus carries the same economy without the token: every joining of hearts is His purchase, not yours, and what you were given to keep, you were given.

**The blood goes the same way as the salt.** At the supper Mark's prophet calls the cup the blood of the covenant, in the words of Sinai, where the blood was thrown upon the people and never drunk. The corpus, correcting its own community's sacrifice, states the principle that closes the arc: neither their flesh nor their blood reaches Allah ﷻ, but the reverence from you reaches Him. Blood applied, then salt added, then the word alone: the covenant token is progressively interiorised across the three scriptures until nothing ascends but taqwā. The Gospel's cup stands at the middle of that arc, and the corpus stands at its end.

**Where the two texts seem to part on the resurrection of the married, they are two halves of one sentence.** Mark says that in the rising they neither marry nor are given in marriage: the human transaction is denied. The corpus never once puts the wedding verb in creaturely hands in the Garden; it says We paired them, zawwajnāhum, with He as the only subject the verb ever takes there. The one text removes the contract from the creatures, the other assigns the pairing to the Lord, and together they say a single thing: the transacting ends, the gift remains.

**A tower of readings collapses when one Hebrew word is put back.** The riddle of Psalm 110, David calling his own descendant my lord, powered centuries of argument for a more-than-human son. In Hebrew the verse is two different words, YHWH said to adoni, the second an ordinary honorific said to kings and to Joseph the slave-steward. Greek rendered both as kyrios, and the riddle was born in translation. The corpus keeps the two tiers the Hebrew kept, rabb reserved, sayyid human, and names the collapse of names as the error itself.

**And the corpus has a word for the space the woman with the flask occupied.** She anointed him unbidden, the objectors priced her act, and he called it a good work and bound it to the gospel wherever it goes. The corpus names that category: things Allah ﷻ has left alone, ʿafā Allāhu ʿanhā, an unlegislated space that the legalists rush to fill and the Lord had deliberately left open. The objectors legislate; the master receives; the corpus supplies the doctrine of the open ground under both.

**The Gospel ends at fear, and the corpus's first word over the friends is that fear is lifted.** The oldest manuscripts of Mark stop mid-sentence: they said nothing to anyone, for they were afraid. The corpus's proclamation over the awliyāʾ is lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa-lā hum yaḥzanūn, no fear upon them, nor shall they grieve. The one text breaks off where the other begins, and the reader who holds both watches the sentence finish across the gap of six centuries.

**Nothing in the argument requires taking anyone's word for anything.** Every claim rests on a construction anyone can check with a concordance, a lexicon, and a standard grammar. Where a claim rests instead on the structural method this commentary carries, that is said in the open, and the method is set out in the appendix in plain terms.

---

# PROLOGUE · THE READING DEVICE

This commentary reads the Gospel of Mark from a single fixed position: the position of a reader who holds the Quranic account of ʿĪsā AS entire, and holds nothing else.

Not a neutral reader, because there is no such reader. Not a Christian reader testing the Quran against the creeds, and not a polemicist testing the Gospel against a verdict already reached. The device is narrower and more honest than either: take the corpus's own picture of this prophet, the whole of it, the affirmations with the denials, and walk through the earliest Gospel asking of each passage one question. Does this stand, from here?

Three disciplines govern the walk, and they are stated now so they need not be repeated.

**The first discipline is that the text is quoted before it is read.** Every passage of Mark appears in this commentary, in order, from the first verse to the last. Nothing is skipped because it is inconvenient, and nothing is rearranged because it would read better. Where the Gospel is difficult for this reading, the difficulty is exhibited. Where it is difficult for the received reading, that is exhibited too. A commentary that selects is an argument wearing a commentary's clothes.

**The second discipline is that grammar outranks tradition.** Both texts arrive wrapped in centuries of interpretation, and both wrappings are set aside in the same motion. The corpus is read by its own usage: what a root does across its whole attested field, not what a school later said it must mean. The Gospel is read by its own Greek: what the evangelist wrote, not what the settlement four centuries later decided he must have meant. When this commentary says the two texts agree or part, it means the sentences do, checkable in a lexicon, not the communities.

**The third discipline is the one that makes this a structural commentary and not only a comparative one.** Every claim a text makes has three registers: what stands, what moves, and how the two bind. A reading closes when the three hold together and enclose something, the way three edges enclose a face; it breaks when they collapse into each other; it stays open when a register is missing and nothing in either text supplies it. This commentary lets that test finish what the surface words begin. Where Mark and the corpus diverge in wording, the structural reading is sovereign: if the two sentences occupy the same geometry, the divergence is dissolved and the dissolution is shown; if they do not, the divergence is named and kept. And where neither the words nor the structure can close a question, the commentary says in one sentence what is missing and from which side it would have to come, and leaves the door standing open. That sentence is not a failure of the reading. It is the reading, working, at the edge of what a reading can do.

A word about voice. The commentary speaks once. The work behind it involved excavation and testing, drafts and corrections, and none of that history appears here, because a finished reading owes the reader its findings and not its diary. What is framework-internal is said to be framework-internal. What is checkable is left checkable. And what was learned in the making, that the text kept opening past what the makers expected, is acknowledged in a single sentence here at the threshold: this reading was taught, in the making of it, more than it brought, and the One who taught it is generous beyond what is asked from below.

---

# CONTENTS

**Part I · The Reader.** Who is holding the book: the fault-record of the prophets, the two files that carry nothing, and what the silence is for.

**Part II · The Distilled Quranic Christology.** The distilled Quranic account of ʿĪsā AS: what is affirmed, what is addressed and in exactly what grammar, what replaces generation, and why the condemned construction turns out to be the corpus's own affirmative form with the wrong name removed.

**Part III · Why Mark.** The case for the earliest Gospel as the right text for this reading: priority, roughness, Aramaic under the Greek, and the absence of the settlement's vocabulary.

**Part IV · The Gospel Entire.** The whole of Mark in ten movements, every passage in order: the voice and the wilderness, Galilee, the Twelve and the seed, the crossings, the bread, the way, the city, the discourse, the cup, and the tomb.

**Part V · The Threads.** What runs the length of the book, counted as it arrives: a verb of strength, a spitting, four Aramaic words, six interruptions, three divine passives, twelve roughnesses, nine silencings, and the ledger of images that crossed between the scriptures whole, in part, or not at all. A closing section stands apart on the one preposition that carries the whole comparison.

**Part VI · The Closing Architecture.** The counterfeit law, the eight corrections, the sustenance the Twelve could not read, and the door the reading leaves open.

**Appendix A · The Structural Commentary.** The method in plain terms, for the reader who wants to see the instrument and not only its work.

**Appendix B · Readings Attempted.** Every structural reading in the book, with the term that binds its three registers.

**Appendix C · Method Disclosure and Author's Position.** What was used, how it was applied, how the sources were weighted, what is not claimed, and the two places this reading is uncomfortable for its own reception.

**Appendix D · Glossary of Arabic and Greek Terms.** Every non-English term that carries argument, alphabetically, so that any occurrence can be resolved without turning back.

**Appendix E · Index of Principal Quranic Citations.** Grouped by the function each verse performs, so a verse can be found by the argument it belongs to.

---

# HOW TO READ THIS COMMENTARY

**The shape of a movement.** Part IV runs in ten movements. Each opens with a short box, *What to Watch*, naming the four things in that stretch a reader should keep an eye on. Then the Gospel is read passage by passage, in order, and every passage has the same three parts.

**The three parts of a passage.** First the Gospel itself, set as a block quotation and given in the earliest recoverable form. Then *Commentary based on Quran*, which is not a footnote to the Gospel but a reading of it from the Quranic register, and which corrects where the two part. Then, where one is given, *Structural Commentary*, which asks a different question and is set out at Appendix A. Ten of those close a movement entire and are marked as such; five belong to a single passage, because in each of those cases one scene holds the whole architecture rather than one move within it.

**The two registers.** The running commentary reads Mark from the Quranic position: what the corpus affirms, where the two texts meet, and where they part. The Structural Commentary asks only whether the passage holds together, and on what. A passage can hold together and be false, and the two questions are kept apart throughout.

**The mark ⟀.** It closes a Structural Commentary when the passage's three registers stand apart and compose. Raised, ⟀ GOL↑, it marks the two places where a single utterance carries the whole architecture. Where a reading does not close, no mark is used and the reason is stated in words. Appendix B lists every reading and the term that binds it.

**The boxes.** Boxes are set apart from the running text and are distinguished by their titles. *What to Watch* opens a movement. A numbered box, such as *Box 1 | The Two Devices*, holds a standing idea that governs more than the passage it sits in. *The Fence* marks the limit of what a section establishes. A *Part Summary* closes a part, and an *at a Glance* box gathers a list the book has been counting. The Structural Commentary is not a box: it is a heading, and it is one of the three parts of a passage described above.

**Quotation and citation.** Gospel passages are set as block quotations and given in the earliest recoverable form. Quranic references are surah:verse. The Gospel is cited as Mk in running prose and as Mark in headings, boxes, and tables; any other book is named in full. Arabic and Greek appear wherever the word itself is the evidence, transliterated with long vowels marked (ā, ī, ū), ʿ for *ʿayn* and ʾ for *hamza*, and emphatic consonants dotted beneath. No knowledge of either script is assumed anywhere.

**Honorifics** are components of the names they attach to and travel through every line: Allah ﷻ, the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, and the prophets AS.

---
# PART I · THE READER

Four things the Quranic register carries into the Gospel before a line of it is read.

:::box Part I Summary

**The corpus corrects its prophets and does not soften it.** Thirteen named figures carry a recorded fault, including four of the five who were given a book.

**Two carry nothing, on two different grounds.** ʿĪsā AS is the only one of the five scripture-bearers with a clean file, the other four carrying heavy records. And of Yaḥyā AS the negation is stated outright rather than merely omitted.

**The silence is a guard, not an elevation.** A recorded fault would have proved servanthood by deficiency. The clean file is what makes it chosen, which Q 4:172 states one verse after the three designations.

**The two clean files are two ends of one relation.** Confirmation requires two parties. Merging them deletes the relation rather than perfecting it.

**Fidelity is guaranteed; comprehension never is.** Q 75:19 places the explication after the recitation and assigns it upstream.
:::

## I.1 · The Fault-Record, and the Two It Does Not Touch

Take the corpus at its own word and the inventory is stark. The table gives it at a glance; the paragraphs beneath give each case in the corpus's own words.

Table*: The prophetic fault-record at a glance

| Prophet | What the corpus records | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Adam AS | Forgot the covenant; found without resolve; disobeyed and erred | 20:115, 20:121 |
| Nūḥ AS | Asked for what he had no knowledge of; warned against ignorance | 11:46 |
| Ibrāhīm AS | Confesses a fault in his own prayer; the star, moon, and sun sequence | 26:82, 6:76-78 |
| Lūṭ AS | Offers his daughters; wishes for tribal strength rather than the Ground | 11:78, 11:80 |
| Yaʿqūb AS | Grief past its bounds until his eyes whiten; a precaution he concedes useless | 12:84-86, 12:67 |
| Yūsuf AS | An inclination restrained from outside; reliance on the cupbearer | 12:24, 12:42 |
| Hārūn AS | Charged with disobeying his brother's command | 20:93 |
| Ayyūb AS | An oath requiring accommodation to discharge; called ever-returning | 38:44 |
| Mūsā AS | A killing he calls Satan's work; a refused request and explicit repentance; his brother seized | 28:15, 7:143, 7:150 |
| Dāwūd AS | Realised he was tested; sought forgiveness and was forgiven a named thing | 38:24 |
| Sulaymān AS | Distracted past the hour; corrected by a bird on knowledge and haste | 38:31, 27:22 |
| Yūnus AS | Absconded like a runaway slave; thought he would not be constrained | 37:140, 21:87 |
| Muhammad ﷺ | Seven corrections: frowned and turned away (Q 80:1); pardoned for a permission granted before verification (Q 9:43); concealed within himself and feared people (Q 33:37); the ransoms taken at Badr (Q 8:67); a near-inclination averted from outside (Q 17:74); forbade himself a lawful thing (Q 66:1); and twice commanded to seek forgiveness for his own offence (Q 40:55, Q 47:19) | seven loci, listed at left |
| **Yaḥyā AS** | **Nothing. The negation stated outright: not a tyrant, not disobedient** | **19:14** |
| **ʿĪsā AS** | **Nothing at all** | |

**Adam AS.** *Wa-laqad ʿahidnā ilā Ādama min qablu fa-nasiya wa-lam najid lahu ʿazmā* (Q 20:115). We took a covenant from him before, but he forgot, and We found in him no resolve. Two named deficits, and then *ʿaṣā Ādamu rabbahu fa-ghawā* (Q 20:121), disobeyed and erred. His own confession, *rabbanā ẓalamnā anfusanā* (Q 7:23), is self-attributed wronging, and the words of return were given to him rather than found by him (Q 2:37).

**Nūḥ AS.** He interceded for his son and was answered *innahu laysa min ahlik, innahu ʿamalun ghayru ṣāliḥ, fa-lā tasʾalni mā laysa laka bihi ʿilm, innī aʿiẓuka an takūna min al-jāhilīn* (Q 11:46). Do not ask Me what you have no knowledge of; I admonish you lest you be among the ignorant. That word is spoken to a prophet in the middle of a flood.

**Ibrāhīm AS.** In his own prayer of praise: *wa-lladhī aṭmaʿu an yaghfira lī khaṭīʾatī yawm ad-dīn* (Q 26:82), and who I hope will forgive me my fault on the Day of Judgment. Unprompted, in a passage of glorification, a prophet asserts a *khaṭīʾa* attached to himself. Beside it the star-moon-sun sequence at Q 6:76-78, three times *hādhā rabbī* and three times withdrawn, and the prayer for a father later disowned at Q 9:114.

**Lūṭ AS.** At Q 11:78 he offers his daughters to the crowd, a proposal the text neither endorses nor has him retract. At Q 11:80, *law anna lī bikum quwwatan aw āwī ilā ruknin shadīd*, if only I had power against you or could take refuge in a strong support, a wish for tribal muscle rather than for the Ground he serves.

**Yaʿqūb AS.** Grief past its bounds until his eyes whiten (Q 12:84) and his own sons tell him he will destroy himself (Q 12:85). A precaution ordered at Q 12:67 and conceded useless in the same breath. Then his own correction, *innamā ashkū baththī wa-ḥuznī ilā Allāh* (Q 12:86).

**Yūsuf AS.** *Wa-laqad hammat bihi wa-hamma bihā lawlā an raʾā burhāna rabbih* (Q 12:24), the inclination placed on him with an external restraint interposed. And Q 12:42, the request to the cupbearer followed by *fa-ansāhu sh-shayṭānu dhikra rabbihi fa-labitha fī s-sijni biḍʿa sinīn*, several years added to the sentence.

**Hārūn AS.** *A-fa-ʿaṣayta amrī* (Q 20:93), did you disobey my command, and his defence that he feared being blamed for splitting the people.

**Ayyūb AS.** An oath sworn that required a divine accommodation to discharge without perjury, *khudh bi-yadika ḍighthan fa-ḍrib bihi wa-lā taḥnath* (Q 38:44), with *awwāb*, ever-returning, as his epithet.

**Mūsā AS.** The killing, self-typed as *hādhā min ʿamal ash-shayṭān* and *rabbī innī ẓalamtu nafsī* (Q 28:15-16) and later called *dhanb* in his own mouth (Q 26:14). The request at Sinai, *rabbi arinī anẓur ilayk*, refused with *lan tarānī*, and his waking words *subḥānaka tubtu ilayk* (Q 7:143), an explicit repentance for having asked. The tablets thrown and his brother seized by the head (Q 7:150), then *rabbi ghfir lī wa-li-akhī* (Q 7:151), forgive me and my brother, himself first. And three broken undertakings with Khiḍr AS, with *lā tuʾākhidhnī bimā nasītu* (Q 18:73) as his own admission.

**Dāwūd AS.** *Wa-ẓanna Dāwūdu annamā fatannāhu fa-staghfara rabbahu wa-kharra rākiʿan wa-anāb* (Q 38:24), followed by *fa-ghafarnā lahu dhālik*, so We forgave him that. The pronoun has a referent.

**Sulaymān AS.** The horses and the missed hour (Q 38:31-33). The body cast on the throne and the return, *thumma anāb* (Q 38:34). And the hoopoe returning with *aḥaṭtu bimā lam tuḥiṭ bihi*, I have encompassed what you have not, correcting the prophet-king on knowledge and on haste in one line (Q 27:20-22).

**Yūnus AS.** *Abaqa ilā al-fulk al-mashḥūn* (Q 37:140), absconded, the verb for a runaway slave. *Fa-ẓanna an lan naqdira ʿalayh* (Q 21:87), he thought We would not constrain him. His confession *innī kuntu min aẓ-ẓālimīn*. And the Prophet ﷺ told directly, *wa-lā takun ka-ṣāḥib al-ḥūt* (Q 68:48), do not be like the man of the fish.

**Muhammad ﷺ.** *ʿAbasa wa-tawallā* (Q 80:1), he frowned and turned away. *ʿAfā Allāhu ʿanka lima adhinta lahum* (Q 9:43). *Wa-tukhfī fī nafsika mā Allāhu mubdīhi wa-takhshā an-nāsa wa-Allāhu aḥaqqu an takhshāh* (Q 33:37). *Lima tuḥarrimu mā aḥalla Allāhu lak* (Q 66:1). The Badr ransoms (Q 8:67-68). The near-inclination averted only by external firming (Q 17:73-75). *Mā taqaddama min dhanbika wa-mā taʾakhkhar* (Q 48:2), *wa-waḍaʿnā ʿanka wizrak* (Q 94:2), and twice the imperative *wa-staghfir li-dhanbik* (Q 40:55, Q 47:19).

Against all of that, silence on two.

The comparison has to be drawn in the right class or it proves nothing. Of the twenty-five prophets the corpus names, most appear only in a list, or in a short cycle with an adversary and a deliverance, and carry no recorded fault for the plain reason that there is no narrative in which one could be recorded. Nothing follows from their silence. Idrīs AS occupies two verses. Dhū al-Kifl AS and al-Yasaʿ AS are names in an enumeration.

So the class that carries information is the figures the corpus treats at length, and within that class the sharpest and least arguable line is those given a book. There are five.

Table*: The scripture-bearers

| Prophet | The book | Fault recorded |
|---|---|---|
| Ibrāhīm AS | the ṣuḥuf, Q 87:19 | Yes. A fault confessed in his own prayer, Q 26:82 |
| Mūsā AS | the Tawrāt and the Furqān, Q 2:53 | Yes. The heaviest record in the corpus |
| Dāwūd AS | the Zabūr, Q 4:163 | Yes. A named thing forgiven, Q 38:24 |
| Muhammad ﷺ | the Qurʾān | Yes. Seven corrections, itemised above |
| **ʿĪsā AS** | **the Injīl, Q 5:46** | **None** |

Four of the five carry heavy records. The fifth carries nothing, and that is the observation. It does not rest on counting minor figures, it does not turn on where a borderline case falls, and it cannot be dissolved by pointing at a prophet the corpus mentions in passing.

Beside him stands one further figure, distinctive on a different ground.

**Yaḥyā AS** receives the negation stated outright: *wa-barran bi-wālidayhi wa-lam yakun jabbāran ʿaṣiyyā* (Q 19:14), dutiful to his parents, and he was not a tyrant, not disobedient. That last word is the root used of Adam AS at Q 20:121 and of Hārūn AS at Q 20:93. It is applied to Yaḥyā AS only in order to be denied.

**ʿĪsā AS** receives nothing at all. Not a correction, not a rebuke, not a lapse. He is the only scripture-bearer in the corpus with a clean file.

And the pairing with Yaḥyā AS is lexical rather than incidental. Q 19:7 marks his name as unprecedented, *lam najʿal lahu min qablu samiyyā*, We had given that name to no one before, and the corpus then plays on it: his blessing at Q 19:15 closes on *yawma yubʿathu ḥayyā*, the day he is raised alive, the Arabic form of the name and the word for living sharing their consonants. The paronomasia is the corpus's own and does not depend on any claim about the name's ultimate derivation, which is from the Hebrew Yôḥānān. ʿĪsā AS is *rūḥun minhu*, a spirit from Him. The two figures the fault-record does not touch are the one whose name the corpus rhymes with living and the one who is spirit from Him, and both stand at the same register.

The distribution is also asymmetric by design. If the lapses were a literary convention for humanising the prophetic type they would fall evenly across the figures the corpus treats at length. They do not: they fall on four of the five scripture-bearers and on most of the extended narrative cycles, and they are absent from exactly the one place where a reader of either tradition would most expect to find one. Fault is recorded where a mechanism is being taught and withheld where it is not.

## I.1a · The One Candidate, and Why It Returns Clean

A negative claim about a corpus is only as good as the search behind it, and there is exactly one passage that a reader will raise against it. It should be raised here rather than left.

Q 5:116 is the only place in the corpus where the Source addresses ʿĪsā AS directly and puts a question to him. The setting is the reckoning, and the question is an accusation in form: *a-anta qulta li-l-nāsi ittakhidhūnī wa-ummiya ilāhayni min dūni Allāh*, did you say to the people, take me and my mother as two gods besides Allah ﷻ. If a rebuke of ʿĪsā AS exists anywhere in the corpus, it is here.

Read the exchange whole and it returns the opposite of a rebuke.

The question is put and the answer is immediate and threefold. *Subḥānak*, glory be to You, a refusal of the premise before anything else. *Mā yakūnu lī an aqūla mā laysa lī bi-ḥaqq*, it is not for me to say what is not mine by right, a refusal to occupy a formulation rather than a denial of a deed he might have done. Then the epistemic boundary, *taʿlamu mā fī nafsī wa-lā aʿlamu mā fī nafsik*, You know what is in my self and I do not know what is in Yours, which places the verification with the questioner rather than with the answerer. And then the affirmative content at Q 5:117: *mā qultu lahum illā mā amartanī bihi an uʿbudū Allāha rabbī wa-rabbakum*, I said to them only what You commanded me, worship Allah ﷻ, my Lord and your Lord.

The passage is therefore an interrogation that returns clean, and an interrogation that returns clean is evidence for a file rather than against it. The corpus has the form available for the other outcome and uses it freely elsewhere: *fa-lā tasʾalni mā laysa laka bihi ʿilm* to Nūḥ AS, *lan tarānī* to Mūsā AS, *ʿafā Allāhu ʿanka* and *ʿabasa wa-tawallā* to the Prophet ﷺ. Here the question is put and no fault is returned.

Two related passages are worth naming with it and are the same shape. At Q 5:110 the corpus recounts every favour and every work and attaches its permission-clause four times, and adds no correction. And at Q 43:59 the corpus says of him only *in huwa illā ʿabdun anʿamnā ʿalayhi wa-jaʿalnāhu mathalan li-Banī Isrāʾīl*, he was only a servant whom We favoured and made an example. The strongest available reduction in the corpus's vocabulary is *only a servant*, and it carries no fault with it.

So the ʿĪsā AS row of the table above is empty because the one entry a reader would expect to find there was examined and returned nothing.

## I.2 · What the Silence Is For

The obvious reading of the silence is elevation. It is the wrong reading, and the corpus supplies the right one in the next verse of the very passage that raises the question.

Track the relation between exception and servanthood across the corpus and it runs inverse: the higher the ontological anomaly, the heavier the servanthood declaration. ʿĪsā AS is the only figure who speaks from the cradle, and the first word out of his mouth in the entire corpus is *ʿabd*. *Innī ʿabdu Allāh, ātāniya al-kitāba wa-jaʿalanī nabiyyā* (Q 19:30). Servant first, book second, prophet third, at the moment of maximum miraculous exception.

Now see what the silence buys. Had the corpus recorded a fault for ʿĪsā AS, that fault would have become the proof of mere humanity, and the servanthood would have been established by deficiency. A servant because he could not be more. Instead the corpus establishes servanthood by declaration from a figure it never faults, which is the harder and stronger claim, and it states it one verse after the three designations:

> **The Messiah will never disdain to be a servant of Allah ﷻ, nor will the angels brought near.**
>
> *Lan yastankifa al-Masīḥu an yakūna ʿabdan lillāh wa-lā al-malāʾikatu al-muqarrabūn.* · Q 4:172

*Yastankif* is the verb of disdain, of holding oneself above. It presupposes a height from which disdain would be intelligible. The verse does not say he is too small to be more. It says he is not too proud to be less. And the angels are placed beside him in the same clause, which sets the height at which the disdain would have been conceivable.

That is the voluntariness guard, and it is the office the fault-silence performs.

:::box 1 The Two Devices
The corpus runs two complementary devices on the prophetic record and they must not be confused.

**The clean file** on ʿĪsā AS and Yaḥyā AS is the voluntariness guard. Servanthood declared by a figure never faulted is chosen; servanthood forced by deficiency is not. Q 4:172 and Q 19:30 state it.

**The marked file** on the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is the anti-counterfeit device. To impersonate a figure whose record contains *ʿabasa wa-tawallā* addressed to himself in the third person, an impostor would have to write his own rebukes. No claimant volunteers a fault-file. The corrections are tamper-evidence, and the corpus adds two further locks: the office is sealed by declaration at Q 33:40, and at Q 3:144 an anti-cult clause is revealed at the exact moment a rumour of his death ran through a battlefield.

Different mechanisms, different purposes, both running at once.
:::

## I.3 · The Two-Place Office

Because the two clean files stand together, the natural next thought is that they are one figure. The corpus closes that route with a single verse and opens a better one.

> **Allah ﷻ gives you glad tidings of Yaḥyā, confirming a word from Allah ﷻ.**
>
> *Anna Allāha yubashshiruka bi-Yaḥyā, muṣaddiqan bi-kalimatin min Allāh.* · Q 3:39

Confirmation is two-place. A confirmer attests something, and the something is not himself. If the word is ʿĪsā AS, and the natural reading six verses later at Q 3:45 makes it so, then Yaḥyā AS is attester and ʿĪsā AS is attested, and no one attests his own arrival. The relation requires two terms, and collapsing them deletes it rather than perfecting it.

Q 6:85 enumerates them as members of a set, *wa-Zakariyyā wa-Yaḥyā wa-ʿĪsā wa-Ilyās, kullun min aṣ-ṣāliḥīn*, and enumeration presupposes distinctness. Q 19 runs them as adjacent cycles with incompatible origins: a barren wife for one (Q 19:2-15) and no man at all for the other (Q 19:16-33). The peace-formula is spoken *over* Yaḥyā AS at Q 19:15 and spoken *by* ʿĪsā AS about himself at Q 19:33, the same words in two grammatical positions.

What the pairing is, then, is one two-place office with two seats. And that reading explains the silence at both ends where the merger cannot: an attestation is only as clean as both of its poles. A confirmer with a recorded fault has his attestation impeached; a confirmed figure with a recorded fault has an unimpeached attestation pointing at damaged goods. The joint cleanness is a structural requirement of the office, not a coincidence of two biographies.

The corpus has the same pattern elsewhere, named. Mūsā AS asks *wa-jʿal lī wazīran min ahlī, Hārūna akhī, ushdud bihi azrī, wa-ashrikhu fī amrī* (Q 20:29-32). One command, two persons, the sharing requested and granted, and the two never once collapsed. One office, two nodes, persons kept distinct.

This matters immediately, because Mark stages the collapse and declines it. Herod says John the baptiser has been raised, and adds the beheading himself so no reader misses that he knows better. The crowd cycles John, Elijah, or one of the prophets. Mark reports three confident identifications and ratifies none, and the reader who read chapter one already holds the correct structure.

## I.4 · Transmission and Comprehension

The reader carries one more law, and it governs what he is permitted to conclude from anything he finds.

The corpus guarantees the fidelity of its transmission in its most severe available language and never once guarantees the exhaustive comprehension of its transmitter.

Fidelity: *wa-mā yanṭiqu ʿan al-hawā, in huwa illā waḥyun yūḥā* (Q 53:3-4), he does not speak from desire. *Qul mā yakūnu lī an ubaddilahu min tilqāʾi nafsī* (Q 10:15), it is not for me to alter it of my own accord, with the self barred by name. And *wa-law taqawwala ʿalaynā baʿḍa al-aqāwīl, la-akhadhnā minhu bil-yamīn, thumma la-qaṭaʿnā minhu al-watīn* (Q 69:44-46), a threat of a severed aorta that never fired.

Comprehension: the opposite treatment. *Qul lā aqūlu lakum ʿindī khazāʾinu Allāh wa-lā aʿlamu al-ghayb* (Q 6:50). *Law kuntu aʿlamu al-ghayba la-staktharu min al-khayr* (Q 7:188). *Mā adrī mā yufʿalu bī wa-lā bikum* (Q 46:9). Each prefixed with the imperative *say*, so these are commanded declarations rather than concessions extracted. And a standing open petition, *wa-qul rabbi zidnī ʿilmā* (Q 20:114), the only thing in the corpus its bearer is told to ask increase of.

Then the verse that separates the two axes explicitly and assigns both to the Source:

> **Do not move your tongue with it to hasten it. Upon Us is its gathering and its recitation. So when We have recited it, follow its recitation. Then, upon Us is its explication.**
>
> *Lā tuḥarrik bihi lisānaka li-taʿjala bih. Inna ʿalaynā jamʿahu wa-qurʾānah. Fa-idhā qaraʾnāhu fa-ttabiʿ qurʾānah. Thumma inna ʿalaynā bayānah.* · Q 75:16-19

Gathering is His. Recitation is His. The instruction to the transmitter is *follow*, a fidelity verb and not a comprehension verb. And then *thumma*, afterward, in sequence: the explication is also His, placed after the recitation and stated as an ongoing undertaking rather than a completed state.

The in-corpus precedent is the Khiḍr AS cycle at Q 18:65-82. A scripture-bearing messenger of the first rank is shown a knowledge from a source he does not hold and is told he could not have patience with what he did not encompass. A full narrative cycle spent on the proposition that completeness of office does not entail completeness of comprehension.

The consequence for this commentary is exact and it is what makes the reading device honest. A verbatim standard preserves structure independently of any transmitter's or reader's grasp. A token deposited unaltered carries whatever it carries whether or not anyone downstream unfolds it. So the structures recovered below are present in the two texts as tokens, and nothing in the recovery requires or asserts that any transmitter unfolded them.

---

# PART II · THE DISTILLED QURANIC CHRISTOLOGY

This is the equipment the reader brings. It is set out as an inventory rather than as an argument, because the first thing an inventory does is dissolve the assumption on which both receptions rest, that the two accounts stand at opposite ends of a spectrum.

:::box Part II Summary

**Affirmed: sixteen items,** listed in full at II.3.

**Addressed: three grammatical forms only.** A convertible identity marked by the separative pronoun, an ordinal inside a count, and offspring in the generative sense.

**And each is answered rather than merely refused.** The corpus writes the equational form affirmatively elsewhere with the office correctly filled; it keeps threeness as structure in the very verse that bars the number; and it supplies *kun fa-yakūn*, command rather than procreation, in the clause adjacent to the denial at Q 2:117 and Q 19:35. Nothing is taken and left empty.

**The Word has a prior residence and seven predicates** the corpus gives to nothing created, and is then named with a man's name in a clause where the grammar breaks to follow the person.

**The crucifixion verse denies their agency and their knowledge,** not a death; the taking is assigned to the Source in three places, one of them in his own mouth.
:::

## II.1 · What the Corpus Affirms

Gathered without selection, from every surah in which the material occurs.

**The conception.** *Fa-arsalnā ilayhā rūḥanā fa-tamaththala lahā basharan sawiyyā* (Q 19:17), We sent to her Our Spirit and it presented itself to her as a well-formed man. Her protest, *annā yakūnu lī ghulāmun wa-lam yamsasnī basharun wa-lam aku baghiyyā* (Q 19:20), no man has touched me and I have not been unchaste, and the answer, *huwa ʿalayya hayyin wa-li-najʿalahu āyatan li-l-nāsi wa-raḥmatan minnā wa-kāna amran maqḍiyyā* (Q 19:21), a sign for mankind and a mercy from Us, and a matter decreed. The parallel at Q 3:47 with the same protest and the same answer, *kadhāliki Allāhu yakhluqu mā yashāʾ*. And twice more in the corpus's own summary voice: *wa-allatī aḥṣanat farjahā fa-nafakhnā fīhā min rūḥinā wa-jaʿalnāhā wa-bnahā āyatan li-l-ʿālamīn* (Q 21:91), and *wa-Maryama bnata ʿImrāna allatī aḥṣanat farjahā fa-nafakhnā fīhi min rūḥinā wa-ṣaddaqat bi-kalimāti rabbihā wa-kutubihi wa-kānat min al-qānitīn* (Q 66:12).

The virgin conception is therefore stated four separate times, in four surahs, in the corpus's own voice, with the mother's own denial of contact preserved in two of them.

**The mother.** Chosen and purified: *inna Allāha iṣṭafāki wa-ṭahharaki wa-ṣṭafāki ʿalā nisāʾi al-ʿālamīn* (Q 3:42). Provisioned without reckoning at Q 3:37. Named a *ṣiddīqa*, a woman of truth, at Q 5:75. The only woman named in the whole corpus, and a surah carries her name.

**The titles.** *Al-Masīḥ*, the Messiah, eleven times: Q 3:45, Q 4:157, Q 4:171, Q 4:172, Q 5:17 twice, Q 5:72 twice, Q 5:75, Q 9:30, and Q 9:31. Four of the eleven stand inside formulas the corpus is condemning, which sharpens the observation rather than weakening it: the corpus rejects the predication and never withdraws the title, so the very clause that rejects *Allah ﷻ is the Messiah* calls him the Messiah while rejecting it. *Kalima*, a word from Him: Q 3:39, Q 3:45, Q 4:171. *Rūḥ minhu*, a spirit from Him: Q 4:171. *Wajīhan fī al-dunyā wa-l-ākhirati wa-mina al-muqarrabīn* (Q 3:45), eminent in this world and the next and of those brought near. *Mubārakan aynamā kuntu* (Q 19:31), blessed wherever I am. *Ghulāman zakiyyā* (Q 19:19), a pure boy, spoken by the angel before the conception. *Āyatan li-l-nāsi wa-raḥmatan minnā* (Q 19:21). And *innahu la-ʿilmun li-l-sāʿati* (Q 43:61), he is knowledge of the Hour.

**The strengthening.** *Wa-ayyadnāhu bi-rūḥi al-qudus*, We strengthened him with the Holy Spirit, three times: Q 2:87, Q 2:253, Q 5:110. The verb is from the root for strength in the hand and means bracing, not replacing.

**The speech.** *Wa-yukallimu al-nāsa fī al-mahdi wa-kahlan* (Q 3:46, and again at Q 5:110), he will speak to people in the cradle and in maturity. And the cradle speech itself at Q 19:30-33, in the first person, opening on *ʿabd*.

**The teaching given.** *Wa-yuʿallimuhu al-kitāba wa-l-ḥikmata wa-l-Tawrāta wa-l-Injīl* (Q 3:48, and Q 5:110). And the Injīl itself described: *wa-ātaynāhu al-Injīla fīhi hudan wa-nūrun wa-muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayhi min al-Tawrāti wa-hudan wa-mawʿiẓatan li-l-muttaqīn* (Q 5:46), guidance and light and admonition.

**The works.** *Annī akhluqu lakum min al-ṭīni ka-hayʾati al-ṭayri fa-anfukhu fīhi fa-yakūnu ṭayran bi-idhni Allāh, wa-ubriʾu al-akmaha wa-l-abraṣa wa-uḥyī al-mawtā bi-idhni Allāh, wa-unabbiʾukum bimā taʾkulūna wa-mā taddakhirūna fī buyūtikum* (Q 3:49). Formation from clay with breath, the blind and the leper healed, the dead raised, and knowledge of what is eaten and stored in houses. Repeated in the second person at Q 5:110 with the permission-clause attached four separate times. And *bi-idhnī*, by My permission, is attached to every one of them without exception.

**The mission.** *Wa-muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayya min al-Tawrāti wa-li-uḥilla lakum baʿḍa alladhī ḥurrima ʿalaykum* (Q 3:50), confirming the Torah and making lawful some of what had been forbidden. Sent *ilā Banī Isrāʾīl* (Q 3:49), a bounded mission. And announcing what comes after him at Q 61:6.

**The disciples.** *Man anṣārī ilā Allāh, qāla al-ḥawāriyyūna naḥnu anṣāru Allāh* (Q 3:52, and Q 61:14). Called helpers of Allah ﷻ in the corpus's own approving voice.

**The table.** *Rabbanā anzil ʿalaynā māʾidatan min al-samāʾi takūnu lanā ʿīdan li-awwalinā wa-ākhirinā wa-āyatan mink* (Q 5:114), and the answer *innī munazziluhā ʿalaykum* (Q 5:115).

**The taking and the raising.** *Innī mutawaffīka wa-rāfiʿuka ilayya wa-muṭahhiruka mina alladhīna kafarū* (Q 3:55). *Bal rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayhi wa-kāna Allāhu ʿazīzan ḥakīmā* (Q 4:158).

**The peace.** *Wa-l-salāmu ʿalayya yawma wulidtu wa-yawma amūtu wa-yawma ubʿathu ḥayyā* (Q 19:33), the threefold peace spoken by him about himself.

**The covenant rank.** *Wa-idh akhadhnā mina al-nabiyyīna mīthāqahum wa-minka wa-min Nūḥin wa-Ibrāhīma wa-Mūsā wa-ʿĪsā bni Maryam wa-akhadhnā minhum mīthāqan ghalīẓā* (Q 33:7). Named among the five from whom the solemn covenant was taken. And at Q 42:13 the same religion is enjoined on Nūḥ, Ibrāhīm, Mūsā, and ʿĪsā AS in one verse. And revelation given to him at Q 4:163 in the same list as the rest.

**The community.** *Wa-jaʿalnā fī qulūbi alladhīna ittabaʿūhu raʾfatan wa-raḥmah* (Q 57:27), We placed compassion and mercy in the hearts of those who followed him. And *la-tajidanna aqrabahum mawaddatan li-lladhīna āmanū alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā, dhālika bi-anna minhum qissīsīna wa-ruhbānan wa-annahum lā yastakbirūn* (Q 5:82), the nearest in affection, and the reason given is the absence of arrogance among their priests and monks.

That is the affirmed inventory. It is not a residue. It is nearly the whole apparatus.

## II.2 · What the Corpus Addresses, and in What Grammatical Form

What the corpus speaks to is three constructions, and each is identifiable by its grammar rather than by its vocabulary. This is the observation on which the whole comparison turns, and it is checkable by anyone who can read an Arabic clause.

**The convertible identity.**

> **They have disbelieved who said: Allah ﷻ is the Messiah, son of Maryam.**
>
> *La-qad kafara alladhīna qālū inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥu bnu Maryam.* · Q 5:17, and again at Q 5:72

Definite subject, *Allāha*. Separative pronoun, *huwa*. Definite predicate, *al-Masīḥ*. In Arabic this is the strongest available identity construction: it asserts convertibility with no remainder on either side, A is B and B is A. The pronoun exists precisely to mark an equational sentence.

And the precision of the target can be measured from inside the corpus, because the corpus writes this very construction in its own voice and fills it correctly. *Fa-Allāhu huwa al-Walī*, so Allah ﷻ, He is the Protector, *wa-huwa yuḥyī al-mawtā*, and He gives life to the dead (Q 42:9). Definite subject, separative pronoun, definite predicate: the identical equational form the denial condemns, now with the life-giving clause attached in the next breath. The form recurs with the same subject across the corpus, *huwa al-ḥaqq* and *huwa yuḥyī al-mawtā* at Q 22:6, again at Q 22:62 and Q 31:30, *inna Allāha huwa al-Razzāq* at Q 51:58, and again at Q 57:24. Six occurrences in all. So the condemnation at Q 5:17 was never of the sentence-shape. The corpus reserves that shape and uses it freely of Him. What is condemned is the occupant of it: the office of protector and of giver of life to the dead is real, the equational seat is real, and the disbelief is seating a creature there. That is worth carrying through the whole addressed-file, because it means the corpus is not banning a grammar. It is guarding a throne.

**The numeral.**

> **They have disbelieved who said: Allah ﷻ is the third of three.**
>
> *La-qad kafara alladhīna qālū inna Allāha thālithu thalāthah.* · Q 5:73
>
> **And do not say three. Stop; it is better for you.**
>
> *Wa-lā taqūlū thalāthah, intahū khayran lakum.* · Q 4:171

An ordinal inside a count, and a prohibition on a numeral.

**The generation.**

> **The Jews said ʿUzayr is the son of Allah ﷻ, and the Christians said the Messiah is the son of Allah ﷻ.**
>
> *Wa-qālati al-Yahūdu ʿUzayrun ibnu Allāh, wa-qālati al-Naṣārā al-Masīḥu ibnu Allāh.* · Q 9:30
>
> **It was not for Allah ﷻ to take to Himself any offspring. Glory be to Him.**
>
> *Mā kāna lillāhi an yattakhidha min waladin, subḥānah.* · Q 19:35
>
> **How could He have offspring when He has no consort?**
>
> *Annā yakūnu lahu waladun wa-lam takun lahu ṣāḥibah.* · Q 6:101

The last of these is decisive for what the denial targets. It asks how He could have a *walad* when He has no consort, and *walad* is the generative-biological term. The mechanism denied is procreation. And in two of these places the corpus supplies the replacement in the same breath, at Q 2:117 and again at Q 19:35, which is set out at II.5a. And Q 112:3, *lam yalid wa-lam yūlad*, denies begetting in both directions, with Q 112:2 supplying the reason first: *aṣ-Ṣamad*, the solid without hollow, since a thing that begets must have an interior from which something issues.

And Q 43:81 is worth having whole because it shows the register of the refusal: *qul in kāna li-l-Raḥmāni waladun fa-anā awwalu al-ʿābidīn*. Say: if the Merciful had a son, I would be the first of the worshippers. The refusal is not of honour paid; it is of the mechanism claimed.

**And Q 5:116, which names its own target.** *A-anta qulta li-l-nāsi ittakhidhūnī wa-ummiya ilāhayni min dūni Allāh.* Did you say to the people, take me and my mother as two gods besides Allah ﷻ. The triad named is Allah ﷻ, ʿĪsā AS, and Maryam AS. That corresponds to no conciliar formula of any Christian communion, then or since. Whatever its historical referent, the target the corpus names is a Marian triad and a numbered polytheism.

So the whole addressed-file, gathered, condemns exactly three forms: a definite convertible identity, an enumeration, and biological generation. No fourth appears anywhere in the corpus.

## II.3 · What Is Actually Addressed

Set the two inventories side by side and the result is not the one either polemic expects. But they are not two lists of the same kind, and presenting them as one grid has misled readers in both directions.

The first is content: sixteen items the corpus affirms about ʿĪsā AS, gathered without selection. The second is not content at all. It is three grammatical constructions, each addressed for a stated reason, and each answered with a correct filling supplied elsewhere in the corpus and, in two of the three, in the adjacent clause. What the received picture leaves behind after the corpus has spoken to it is a residue only if one assumes something was taken and nothing put back. Nothing here is taken and left empty.

A term should be fixed before the lists, because the whole comparison turns on it. Throughout this commentary *the received picture* means the fourth-and-fifth-century conciliar settlement and the piety it authorised: eternal generation from the Father's substance, begotten not made; consubstantial identity stated as an equation; a threeness of persons stated as a count; and the devotional grammar built on those clauses. It does not mean the New Testament text itself. The relation between that text and the settlement raised on it is exactly what Part IV measures, passage by passage, and the measurement should not be prejudged by the vocabulary.

Table: The affirmed inventory

| What the corpus affirms of ʿĪsā AS |
|---|
| Conception without a father, stated four times |
| The title Messiah, eleven times, never withdrawn |
| A word from Him |
| A spirit from Him |
| Strengthened by the Holy Spirit, three times |
| Speech in the cradle and in maturity |
| A book given, with guidance and light |
| Clay formed and breathed to life, by permission |
| The blind and the leper healed, by permission |
| The dead raised, by permission |
| Purity declared by the angel before conception |
| The taking and the raising to Him |
| Peace over his birth, his death, and his rising |
| A place among the five of the solemn covenant |
| Compassion and mercy placed in his followers' hearts |
| Knowledge of the Hour |

Table*: The three constructions addressed, with their grounds and their fillings

| The construction addressed | The ground the corpus gives | What stands in its place |
|---|---|---|
| **The convertible equation.** Definite subject, separative pronoun, definite predicate: *inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥu bnu Maryam* (Q 5:17, Q 5:72) | Destructibility, stated in the same verse: *fa-man yamliku mina Allāhi shayʾan in arāda an yuhlika al-Masīḥa bna Maryam*, who could avail against Allah ﷻ if He willed to destroy the Messiah. With *kānā yaʾkulāni al-ṭaʿām* (Q 5:75) and *al-Ḥayyi alladhī lā yamūt* (Q 25:58) | The identical construction, affirmed, with the office correctly filled: *fa-Allāhu huwa al-Walī wa-huwa yuḥyī al-mawtā* (Q 42:9). The same form five further times, at Q 22:6, Q 22:62, Q 31:30, Q 51:58, and Q 57:24 |
| **The ordinal inside a count:** *thālithu thalāthah* (Q 5:73), and the prohibition *wa-lā taqūlū thalāthah* (Q 4:171) | Membership in a counted set. The nearest parallel construction in the corpus is *wa-Manāta al-thālithata al-ukhrā* (Q 53:20), a named triad of the pre-Islamic Arab cult, where the ordinal constitutes a set and its members. What is barred is enumeration used to make Him one of a number | The identical construction, affirmed, with the relation correctly stated: *mā yakūnu min najwā thalāthatin illā huwa rābiʿuhum wa-lā khamsatin illā huwa sādisuhum*, there is no counsel of three but He is the fourth of them, nor of five but He is the sixth (Q 58:7). And threeness as structure kept in the very verse that bars the number, three designations for one referent at Q 4:171, with Sūrat al-Ṣāffāt swearing by three ranks and concluding *inna ilāhakum la-wāḥid* (Q 37:1-4) |
| **Offspring in the generative sense:** *walad* (Q 9:30, Q 19:35, Q 112:3) | No consort: *annā yakūnu lahu waladun wa-lam takun lahu ṣāḥibah* (Q 6:101). With the reason given before the consequence at Q 112:2, *aṣ-Ṣamad*, the solid without hollow, since a thing that begets must have an interior from which something issues | *Kun fa-yakūn*, supplied in the next clause at Q 2:117 and again at Q 19:35, with the inference closed at Q 3:59 by the harder case of Adam AS. Set out at II.5a |

Read that way, the three rows are one operation performed three times. The corpus does not remove a claim and leave a hole. It identifies a construction, states why that filling fails, and writes the correct form.

And the second row is not an exception to the first. Q 58:7 writes the ordinal inside a count of Him, affirmatively, in the corpus's own voice: *He is the fourth of them* where three confer, *the sixth* where five do. So ordinal-in-count is no more forbidden as a grammar than the equational form is, and Q 9:40 says *thāniya thnayni*, the second of two, of the Prophet ﷺ in the cave. What Q 5:73 speaks to is narrower and the distinction is exact: at Q 58:7 He is the fourth of three *by presence*, not by membership, since the counsel is of three and He is not counted into it. At Q 5:73 the number is used to constitute a set of which He is made a member. Presence, not membership: that is the whole of the second row, and the corpus supplies the verse that draws the line.

And the first row carries a further observation that refines II.2 rather than qualifying it. The construction addressed at Q 5:17 is not faulted as a construction. The corpus writes it affirmatively six times over, with the separative pronoun and the definite predicate intact, and twice with the life-giving clause following directly. So what is refused is the predicate placed in the frame and never the frame itself. A recipient-title cannot occupy the position, and the morphology says so before any theology does: *masīḥ* is the *faʿīl* form in the passive sense, the one acted upon, like *qatīl* and *jarīḥ*, while *walī* is not passive. Q 42:9 puts the whole of it in one verse: the taking of protectors besides Him refused, the frame filled correctly, and then *wa-huwa yuḥyī al-mawtā*.

The third row is real and stays, and one thing that does not follow from it should be stated here. The corpus states the denial of generation and names its targets. It does not follow that the Gospel read in Part IV holds the position denied, and it does not: Mark uses no begetting-verb of him in relation to God anywhere, and at the baptism he quotes the second Psalm and declines the clause of generation that stands in the psalm's next breath.

:::box The Fence
What is established in this section is a fact about grammatical form and a fact about vocabulary distribution, and both are checkable with a concordance.

What is not established, and what nothing here licenses, is an inference from either to a doctrine the corpus refuses in the same breath. Q 5:73 closes on *wa-mā min ilāhin illā ilāhun wāḥid*, and Q 37:4 is the conclusion drawn from the three ranks and not a qualification of it.

The honest form of the observation is about method rather than about content: the corpus addresses a filling and demonstrates the correct filling in the same form elsewhere, so a reader who attends to construction rather than to vocabulary sees both moves. That is how it teaches. It seals nothing about what is taught.
:::

The affirmed inventory is substantially closer to the received picture than either reception has been willing to say, and what the corpus addresses is narrow and precisely aimed. Classical Christian theology also denies begetting in time and by division, and it also denies that the Word is a second god counted beside the first. What the corpus does that the later councils did not is refuse the *number* as a permissible way of stating the relation at all, and it refuses it in the same verse in which it supplies three names for one referent.

## II.4 · The Word: Residence, Predicates, and the Naming

The reader's doctrine of the Word is stated in his own book and owes nothing to any later theology.

**The prior residence.** *Wa-innahu fī ummi al-kitābi ladaynā la-ʿaliyyun ḥakīm* (Q 43:4), in the Mother of the Book, with Us, exalted and wise. *Wa-ʿindahu ummu al-kitāb* (Q 13:39), with Him. *Bal huwa qurʾānun majīd, fī lawḥin maḥfūẓ* (Q 85:21-22). *Innahu la-qurʾānun karīm, fī kitābin maknūn* (Q 56:77-78). Four locative expressions and one structure: the recitation has a residence that is not its earthly descent, and the residence is with the Source. The descent-language presupposes it from the other direction, *innā anzalnāhu fī laylati al-qadr* (Q 97:1) and *nazala bihi al-rūḥu al-amīnu ʿalā qalbik* (Q 26:193-194).

**The priority.** Surah ar-Raḥmān opens on four verses in strict order. *Ar-Raḥmān. ʿAllama al-Qurʾān. Khalaqa al-insān. ʿAllamahu al-bayān.* The Merciful. He taught the Recitation. He created the human. He taught him the discrimination. The teaching stands at verse two and the creating at verse three: the gathering is taught before the receiver exists to receive it. And the name at the head is the mercy-name and not the creation-name, so the assembling of what is known is placed under mercy.

**The instrumentality.** *Innamā amrunā li-shayʾin idhā aradnāhu an naqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn* (Q 16:40, and Q 36:82). Everything that comes to be, comes to be by an utterance. The Word is not one created item among others; it is the operator under which created items arrive. And Q 2:117, Q 3:47, and Q 19:35 all carry the same clause.

**The predicates.** The corpus assigns to its own Word a set it assigns to nothing created. *ʿAliyyun ḥakīm* (Q 43:4), exalted and wise, both of them divine names. *Qurʾānun majīd* (Q 85:21) and *qurʾānun karīm* (Q 56:77), glorious and noble, both of them divine names. Inexhaustible under a hypothetical of unbounded material supply: *qul law kāna al-baḥru midādan li-kalimāti rabbī la-nafida al-baḥru qabla an tanfada kalimātu rabbī wa-law jiʾnā bi-mithlihi madadā* (Q 18:109), and with seven seas added at Q 31:27 the outcome is unchanged. Complete and unalterable: *wa-tammat kalimatu rabbika ṣidqan wa-ʿadlan, lā mubaddila li-kalimātih* (Q 6:115), and *lā tabdīla li-kalimāti Allāh* (Q 10:64).

Seven predicates across six verses, four of them divine names, all of them carried by a term the same verses locate with Him. Inexhaustibility in particular is predicated of no created thing anywhere in the corpus.

**The naming.** The corpus does not stop at a doctrine. It attaches the Word to a person, and the Arabic performs the attachment in its own grammar:

> **Maryam, Allah ﷻ gives you glad tidings of a word from Him. His name is the Messiah, ʿĪsā son of Maryam, eminent in this world and the next.**
>
> *Yā Maryamu inna Allāha yubashshiruki bi-kalimatin minhu, ismuhu al-Masīḥu ʿĪsā bnu Maryam, wajīhan fī al-dunyā wa-l-ākhirah.* · Q 3:45

The word for *word* is feminine in Arabic. The pronoun in *ismuhu*, his name, is masculine, and *wajīhan*, eminent, is masculine accusative. The agreement breaks at exactly the point where the announcement of a word becomes the naming of a man. The grammarians noticed it; al-Zamakhsharī explains the break by agreement with the thing named rather than with the naming word. That explanation is correct and it is the point. The verse announces a word and names it as a man in one clause, and where the announcement turns into a personal reference the grammar changes gender to follow.

Then Q 4:171 adds the third designation, and Q 3:39 attaches the term a second time by making Yaḥyā AS its confirmer.

## II.5 · Three Registers, and the Barred Count

Q 4:171 supplies three tokens for one referent in a single breath and then, in the same verse, bars the arithmetic.

> **The Messiah, ʿĪsā son of Maryam, is only the messenger of Allah ﷻ, and His word which He cast to Maryam, and a spirit from Him. So believe in Allah ﷻ and His messengers, and do not say three.**
>
> *Innamā al-Masīḥu ʿĪsā bnu Maryama rasūlu Allāhi wa-kalimatuhu alqāhā ilā Maryama wa-rūḥun minhu. Fa-āminū billāhi wa-rusulihi wa-lā taqūlū thalāthah.* · Q 4:171

The verse does not delete the structure it just supplied. Three orthogonal registers of one object are not three objects, and the count is the collapse. Read as contradiction, one half must be gutted. Read as one move, the grammar is exact.

**The origin register.** *Rūḥun minhu*, a spirit from Him. The preposition is origin-marking and not partitive, the same construction used of Adam AS at Q 15:29 and Q 38:72 and of the human generally at Q 32:9. This is the register the corpus describes only apophatically: *quli al-rūḥu min amri rabbī wa-mā ūtītum mina al-ʿilmi illā qalīlā* (Q 17:85).

**The articulate register.** *Kalimatuhu alqāhā ilā Maryam*, His word which He cast to Maryam AS. The verb is to cast down from a height: a form laid into a receiving substrate. This is where the Word of Q 43:4 stands, with Him, exalted and wise, in the Mother of the Book.

**The delivered register.** *Rasūlu Allāh*, the sent one, placed firmly inside creaturely conditions. *Mā al-Masīḥu bnu Maryama illā rasūlun qad khalat min qablihi al-rusul, wa-ummuhu ṣiddīqatun, kānā yaʾkulāni al-ṭaʿām* (Q 5:75), they both used to eat food. And *in huwa illā ʿabdun anʿamnā ʿalayhi wa-jaʿalnāhu mathalan li-Banī Isrāʾīl* (Q 43:59).

One referent, three registers, three prepositions doing the joining, and no count.

And a further verse governs the whole: *inna mathala ʿĪsā ʿinda Allāhi ka-mathali Ādam, khalaqahu min turābin thumma qāla lahu kun fa-yakūn* (Q 3:59). This is standardly read as the demotion that settles the matter, and it does something narrower and something broader. Narrower, because the verse is explicitly a likeness and the likeness it draws is on the mode of origination alone: it says nothing about the attributes of the Word, nothing about the residence, nothing about the divine names carried by the Book. Broader, because a comparison runs in both directions. If ʿĪsā AS is like Adam AS in the mode of the utterance, then Adam AS was constituted by the Word too, and Q 15:29 adds the second token to Adam AS as well. Both designations of Q 4:171 have Adamic parallels. The effect of Q 3:59 read carefully is not to lower ʿĪsā AS to dust but to raise the whole anthropology to Word-and-Spirit.

Where, then, is the differentia? The text puts it in the casting: *alqāhā ilā Maryam*, direct and unmediated, with no father in the line, which Q 3:47 and Q 19:20 both make explicit. The distinction is in the mode of arrival.

One clause has to be exact here or the seven predicates of II.4 leak. The casting is an act, and an act is not a transfer of the thing predicated. What bears exaltation, wisdom, glory, nobility, inexhaustibility, completion, and unalterability is the Word in its residence, *fī ummi al-kitābi ladaynā*. The corpus predicates none of the seven of any vessel, and it predicates none of them of Adam AS either, though Adam AS carries both designations. So the reading is that the casting proceeds from what carries the predicates, and not that the predicates travel with the cast. Every fence in II.6 holds on that reading and fails on any other.

## II.5a · Kun fa-yakūn: What the Corpus Supplies in Place of Generation

The denials of II.2 raise a question the corpus answers and this commentary, in earlier form, walked past. If the mechanism denied is generation, what is the mechanism affirmed?

The corpus names it eight times, in one fixed phrase, and the distribution of those eight is the argument.

Table: Every occurrence of *kun fa-yakūn*

| Locus | Context |
|---|---|
| Q 2:117 | Immediately after the claim that Allah ﷻ took offspring |
| Q 3:47 | The conception announced to Maryam AS |
| Q 3:59 | The likeness of ʿĪsā AS to Adam AS |
| Q 6:73 | The Day He says *kun* |
| Q 16:40 | After a denial that the dead are raised |
| Q 19:35 | Immediately after the denial that Allah ﷻ takes offspring |
| Q 36:82 | After the question who gives life to decayed bones |
| Q 40:68 | He who gives life and causes death |

Two at the offspring-denial, two at ʿĪsā AS, and four at raising and at life-and-death. Not one of the eight sits in a passage about ordinary manufacture. A phrase that could serve as a general formula for divine making is instead concentrated on exactly the questions this commentary is reading.

**The denial and the mechanism stand in one breath, twice.** This is the observation that most directly serves Part II, and the two verses should be read whole.

> **And they said, Allah ﷻ has taken offspring. Glory be to Him. Rather, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, all devoutly obedient to Him. Originator of the heavens and the earth. When He decrees a matter He only says to it: Be, and it is.**
>
> *Wa-qālū ittakhadha Allāhu waladā, subḥānah, bal lahu mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi kullun lahu qānitūn. Badīʿu al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi wa-idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn.* · Q 2:116-117

> **It was not for Allah ﷻ to take to Himself any offspring. Glory be to Him. When He decrees a matter He only says to it: Be, and it is.**
>
> *Mā kāna lillāhi an yattakhidha min waladin, subḥānah, idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn.* · Q 19:35

The same three moves in the same order, in two separate surahs: the offspring claim, the glorification that refuses it, and then the mechanism that replaces it. The corpus does not deny generation and leave a gap. It denies generation and names what stands in its place in the next clause.

Which sharpens II.3 considerably. Where the corpus addresses generation it supplies the alternative by name, in the adjacent clause. The relation between the Source and what proceeds from it is command and not procreation, and the two verses that state the denial most sharply are the two that say so.

**The likeness at Q 3:59 is a kun-likeness, and it answers the sonship inference directly.** The verse does not merely compare two originations. It gives the mechanism: *khalaqahu min turābin thumma qāla lahu kun fa-yakūn*. Dust, then *kun*.

And the term of comparison is exact. The inference the verse meets is that a birth without a father implies a divine father. Its answer is a case that is more anomalous still and from which nobody has ever drawn that conclusion. Adam AS had no father *and* no mother. ʿĪsā AS had a mother and no father. If absence of paternal generation grounded divine sonship, the stronger claim would be owed to Adam AS, and no one has ever made it. So fatherlessness cannot be the ground. The mechanism is, and the mechanism is command.

That is why the two threads of Part II converge here. The corpus denies *walad* on precisely the consort-grounds, *annā yakūnu lahu waladun wa-lam takun lahu ṣāḥibah* (Q 6:101), how could He have offspring with no consort. Generation requires two, and where there is no second there is no generation to speak of, so the human anomaly cannot supply a divine paternity that the corpus has already ruled out on its own side. What the anomaly does mark is a mode of arrival, which is what Q 3:47 says in answer to Maryam AS's own protest, *annā yakūnu lī waladun wa-lam yamsasnī bashar*: *kadhāliki Allāhu yakhluqu mā yashāʾ, idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn*. Thus He creates what He wills; when He decrees a matter He says to it, Be. And Q 19:21 answers the same protest with *huwa ʿalayya hayyin*, it is easy for Me.

No father, and no difficulty either. Which is why the reading of II.5 holds and runs in both directions: if what is said of the one is *kun*, what is said of the other is *kun*, and Q 15:29 and Q 38:72 add the second designation to Adam AS as well. The effect of Q 3:59 is to raise the anthropology rather than to lower ʿĪsā AS.

**And the particle is immediate.** The phrase is *fa-yakūn*, not *thumma yakūn*. The *fa-* marks consequence without interval, where *thumma* would mark sequence with one. Command and result are not separated by a process. That is worth carrying because the same instantaneity is read at Mk 1:41 in Part IV, where the conditional is cashed in two words and the restoration carries Mark's own *euthys*.

**One further tie the corpus makes and this commentary uses at II.8.** The Spirit is said to be *min amri rabbī*, from the *amr* of my Lord (Q 17:85), and the *amr* is said to be *kun*: *innamā amruhu idhā arāda shayʾan an yaqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn* (Q 36:82). Two verses, one term between them. Whatever else the corpus withholds about the Spirit, and Q 17:85 withholds a great deal in the same breath, it places it on the command-side rather than on the made-side, and the command is the utterance.

**Where this stops.** The clustering is a distributional fact and the two denial-plus-mechanism verses are a grammatical one, and both are checkable by anyone with a concordance. What neither establishes is anything about rank. Q 3:59 makes the mechanism common to ʿĪsā AS and Adam AS, and Q 15:29 and Q 32:9 make the second designation common to the human being as such, so a mechanism shared by every created thing distinguishes nothing within the class of created things. The fence of II.6 holds unchanged: the shared layer establishes layer-structure and no rank, and the differentia at Q 4:171 remains where the text puts it, in *alqāhā*, the mode of the casting.

## II.6 · The Layer Split, and the Grammar of the Crucifixion Verse

The corpus splits the human figure by its own word-discipline, and the split is what governs the passion narrative.

What perishes is named with one word: *kullu man ʿalayhā fān* (Q 55:26) and *kullu shayʾin hālikun illā wajhah* (Q 28:88). What remains is named with another: *wa-yabqā wajhu rabbika dhū al-jalāli wa-l-ikrām* (Q 55:27). The face is never once placed under the death-law and the self is never once placed in the remainder.

And the death-law's quantifier is bounded by a separate declaration in negation-grammar. *Kullu nafsin dhāʾiqatu al-mawt* (Q 3:185, Q 21:35, Q 29:57) ranges over created selves, because the Uncreated is separately declared deathless: *wa-tawakkal ʿalā al-Ḥayy alladhī lā yamūt* (Q 25:58), with *al-Ḥayy al-Qayyūm, lā taʾkhudhuhu sinatun wa-lā nawm* (Q 2:255). The exception is stated on its own warrant, in its own verse, before any inference arrives.

One inference is worth refusing explicitly, because a reader who has followed the corpus's own attribution of a *nafs* to the divine, at Q 3:28, Q 5:116, Q 6:54, and Q 20:41, will construct it. The syllogism runs: the death-clause carries no *illā*, the divine *nafs* is attested, therefore the divine *nafs* is under the clause. It does not close. An unrestricted quantifier in one verse is not the only way a corpus can mark a boundary, and Q 25:58 marks this one directly rather than by exception: the Living who does not die, in negation-grammar, in its own verse. And the word-discipline runs the other way as well, since what remains at Q 55:27 and Q 28:88 is named *wajh* and never *nafs*, and what perishes is never named *wajh*. The corpus does not owe an exception clause in one place for something it declares outright in another.

Now the crucifixion verse, read as it is written.

> **And for their saying, we killed the Messiah, ʿĪsā son of Maryam, the messenger of Allah ﷻ. They did not kill him and they did not crucify him, but it was made to resemble so for them. Those who differ about it are in doubt of it; they have no knowledge of it, only the following of conjecture. They did not kill him with certainty.**
>
> *Wa-qawlihim innā qatalnā al-Masīḥa ʿĪsā bna Maryama rasūla Allāhi, wa-mā qatalūhu wa-mā ṣalabūhu wa-lākin shubbiha lahum. Wa-inna alladhīna khtalafū fīhi la-fī shakkin minhu, mā lahum bihi min ʿilmin illā ittibāʿa al-ẓann, wa-mā qatalūhu yaqīnā.* · Q 4:157

The subject throughout is *they*. The verse denies their accomplishment and their knowledge, and it states its own ground immediately: they have no knowledge of it, only the following of conjecture. It is a verse about a claim to know, made by people the verse says were operating on supposition.

Then the agent changes and the taking is affirmed. *Innī mutawaffīka wa-rāfiʿuka ilayya* (Q 3:55), the agent Allah ﷻ. *Fa-lammā tawaffaytanī kunta anta al-raqība ʿalayhim* (Q 5:117), in his own mouth. And *yawma amūtu* (Q 19:33), also his own. The corpus never denies a death-event. It denies their agency in it and their certainty about it, and assigns the taking to the Source.

*Shubbiha lahum* carries the rest. The root is the root of *mutashābihāt* at Q 3:7, resemblance. A resemblance-object was presented to their sight, which requires no substitute body. It requires that their instrument reached the manifest layer while the event ran at a layer their sight did not reach. And the corpus states that stratification of the manifest and the hidden directly: *huwa al-awwalu wa-l-ākhiru wa-l-ẓāhiru wa-l-bāṭinu wa-huwa bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīm* (Q 57:3).

One fence closes this and it is the corpus's own. The surviving layer is universal, not an exemption. *Min rūḥī* is said of Adam AS and of the human generally, so a layer untouched by the death of the perishing one establishes layer-structure and nothing about rank. Even the raising is stated in the universal return-vocabulary: *rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh*, the same direction-token as *innā lillāhi wa-innā ilayhi rājiʿūn* (Q 2:156) and *wa-ilayhi yurjaʿu al-amru kulluh* (Q 11:123).

## II.7 · What the Corpus Supplies That the Gospel Lacks

The reader notices immediately that Mark begins in the wrong place, and that his own book begins where Mark does not.

Mark has no infancy narrative. No annunciation, no conception, no birth, no genealogy, no childhood. He opens in the wilderness and by verse nine the man is in the water.

The Quran supplies exactly the missing front end, in detail. The annunciation to Zakariyyā AS and the annunciation to Maryam AS run as adjacent panels in Surah Maryam and again in Surah Āl ʿImrān. The conception with the mother's own protest preserved. The withdrawal to a distant place, the palm trunk, the stream, the shaking of the dates, the vow of silence, the return to her people carrying the child, their accusation, her gesture toward the infant, and the cradle speech. Q 19:16-33 is a continuous narrative of everything Mark omits.

So the two texts are complementary at the front end rather than contradictory. And the reader, holding Q 19, reads Mark 1 as a text that begins its account at the Jordan and says nothing about what came before, which is exactly what Mark's own silence is.

The corpus also supplies four other things Mark does not carry: the Injīl as a book given rather than composed (Q 3:48, Q 5:46), the table from heaven (Q 5:112-115), the raising (Q 3:55, Q 4:158), and the sign of the Hour (Q 43:61).

And there is one detail where Mark and the Quran agree against the other three Gospels. Mark alone calls him *ho huios tēs Marias*, the son of Mary, with no father named, at Mk 6:3. The reading is not unanimous: a minority stream, P45 among the witnesses, has the son of the carpenter and of Mary, which is the harmonising direction and is what the critical editions therefore decline. The variant is noted here on the same grounds the longer ending is noted at V.1. Matthew changes it to the carpenter's son. The Quran names him invariably and in every single occurrence *ʿĪsā bnu Maryam*, son of Mary. On the naming convention, the earliest Gospel and the Quran use the same form, and the two later Gospels do not.

## II.8 · The Word Before the Word

One more thing the reader holds: the knowledge that the doctrine of the divine Word is not the property of either community.

Memphite Ptah conceives in the heart and commands with the tongue by the early Old Kingdom, with the personified powers of utterance and perception beside him.
