Sumerian and Akkadian cosmology carry the creative command. Vedic speech is personified and speaks in the first person at Rig Veda 10.125, and the Upanishads state that speech is the absolute. The Avestan sacred formula is a spoken thing with cosmological force. Genesis 1 runs ten creative speech-acts and Psalm 33:6 compresses the whole to word and breath. The Aramaic Targums render the operative divine presence as the Memra, which creates, speaks, walks, covenants, judges, and saves, and which is functionally identified with the Name while remaining grammatically distinct. Heraclitus makes the *logos* cosmological. Wisdom is personified as preexistent and instrumental in three separate books. Philo synthesises the whole into a first-begotten, an image of God, and that through whom all things came to be, decades before any Christian text exists.

Two of those nodes matter for this reading and the rest are context.

The Memra matters because it is Aramaic and not Greek, Jewish and not Christian, liturgical and contemporary, and because it carries the with-and-as structure exactly: distinct in grammar and identified in function. Whatever John 1:1 states, it was not a Christian invention when it was stated, and it was available in a Semitic language in synagogue liturgy.

And beneath the Memra runs a root chain. The Aramaic term is a noun from the root for saying. Genesis 1's ten instances of *and God said* are the same Semitic root. And the Arabic reflex of that root is *amr*, command, which the Quran deploys at precisely the creative-utterance position: *innamā amrunā li-shayʾin idhā aradnāhu an naqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn* (Q 16:40). One triliteral root, three corpora, one syntactic position.

And the corpus closes the chain on itself. Q 17:85 ties the Spirit-term to the same root by preposition, the Spirit is *min amri rabbī*, from the *amr* of my Lord; and Q 36:82 says what the *amr* is, *innamā amruhu idhā arāda shayʾan an yaqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn*. Spirit from command, command as utterance. The root that carries the Word in Genesis and in the Targums is the root the corpus uses for its own creative command, and it is also the root by which it locates the Spirit. That is an internal link rather than a comparative one, and it is set out with the rest of the *kun* material at II.5a.

This is recorded as convergence and never as descent. Independent strata arriving at one architecture is evidence about the architecture; it is not a transmission claim, and none is made.

## II.9 · Bayān: The Faculty, and Why It Is a Fourth Thing

The held distinction that runs through all of the above is sterile until something is done with it, and the corpus says what.

Return to the four verses that open Surah ar-Raḥmān. Source, inscription, substrate, faculty. Three of those are coplanar in the sense that matters: a source, a form, and a receiver do not by themselves constitute a reading. Something must perform the distinguishing, and the fourth verse names it.

*Bayān* is from the root of the preposition *bayna*, between. It is the faculty of between-making: of holding two things apart and registering the distinction as a distinction. And the Word is named by the same operation. *Kitāb mubīn*, the distinguishing Book, at Q 12:1, Q 26:2, Q 27:1, Q 28:2, Q 43:2, Q 44:2. And *al-Furqān*, from the root meaning to split apart, which is the name given to the whole revelation at Q 25:1 and to a prophet's equipment at Q 2:53 and to a decisive day at Q 8:41.

So what passes from the articulate register to the created vessel at Q 55:4 is not content. It is the operation itself. The bifurcation between the Ground and the Word actuates downstream as the receiver's own capacity to perform the very holding-apart that constitutes it. Not a merger and not a collapse, but instantiation: the structure reproduces itself in the receiver as the receiver's own faculty.

The corpus keeps the distinction lexically, which is the check. Vocalisation is predicated of birds at Q 27:16, *ʿullimnā manṭiqa al-ṭayr*, and of skins and of all things at Q 41:21, *anṭaqanā Allāhu alladhī anṭaqa kulla shayʾ*. Everything speaks. Only the human distinguishes.

And the gift is universal: *al-insān*, the human as such, not one human and not a class. Which is also the standing offer at Q 8:29: *in tattaqū llāha yajʿal lakum furqānan*, if you guard yourselves He will make for you a criterion, addressed in the plural to the ordinary and conditional on nothing but the guarding.

:::box 2 The Four-Verse Architecture
The corpus states its whole apparatus in four verses, and this commentary is one performance of the operation the third verse commands.

**The Word given.** Q 55:2, before the receiver exists.

**The faculty given.** Q 55:4, the between-making operation, to the human as such.

**The test issued.** Q 4:82, *afalā yatadabbarūna al-Qurʾān, wa-law kāna min ʿindi ghayri Allāhi la-wajadū fīhi ikhtilāfan kathīrā*. Trace it through: had it been from other than Allah ﷻ they would have found much discrepancy. The corpus supplies its own defeater and names the register, internal coherence, and the verb means to follow a thing to its terminus.

**The completion deferred.** Q 75:19, the explication retained upstream and placed after the recitation, so that the test is real and its answer is not pre-supplied.

Deposit, instrument, challenge, deferral.
:::

## II.10 · Aḥad and Wāḥid: Two Words for One

A last register-fact, because it governs both the cosmic passages of Mark and the whole question of counting.

Surah aṣ-Ṣāffāt opens on an oath sworn by ranking itself, *wa-l-ṣāffāti ṣaffā* (Q 37:1), and lands at *inna ilāhakum la-wāḥid* (Q 37:4). It runs the lineage polemic and closes it at Q 37:158 with the sharpest available word, *wa-jaʿalū baynahu wa-bayna al-jinnati nasabā*, they made between Him and the jinn a kinship. And then the ranked ones speak for themselves: *wa-mā minnā illā lahu maqāmun maʿlūm, wa-innā la-naḥnu al-ṣāffūn, wa-innā la-naḥnu al-musabbiḥūn* (Q 37:164-166). There is none of us but has a known station.

One ordered line, every position holding a determinate known station, and kinship a relation between stations while the Source holds none.

Surah al-Ikhlāṣ states the negative of the same geometry, and it denies exactly three rank-relations: no successor, no predecessor, no peer, the last of these using a word for rank-equality. All three are positions in an ordered line.

And the two surahs use two different words for one. Ṣāffāt says *wāḥid*, one-of-a-count, spoken from inside the ranked line where counting is the native operation. Ikhlāṣ says *aḥad*, one-without-a-second, the word that refuses the count, and it is the word used in negative constructions for not-a-single-one. Two registers, two words, and the choice tracks the register exactly.

That is the same move as the prohibition on the numeral at Q 4:171. The corpus is not hostile to threeness as a structure; it supplies three names in one breath. It is hostile to the operation of counting applied where counting does not belong, and it marks the boundary with its own vocabulary for one.

---

# PART III · WHY MARK

Before the commentary itself, the plain report on the text the reader has opened.

Mark is the earliest of the four, roughly 66 to 70, and it opens with nothing. No genealogy, no infancy, no prologue. By verse nine the man is in the water. Whatever the other three do to set a frame before anything happens, Mark simply starts.

The prose is paratactic to the point of bluntness, *and* upon *and*, with *immediately* roughly forty times. Much of it is in the historic present, past events narrated in present tense about a hundred and fifty times, which nearly every translation flattens into the past. In Greek it reads like someone telling you what happened while it is happening.

The Greek is rough, and the roughness is demonstrably original because the two Gospels that copy him tidy it. Three columns side by side and two of them are smoothing the third.

And what he leaves in is the receipt. At Nazareth he *could* do no mighty work there (Mk 6:5), which Matthew softens to *did not*. He is the carpenter, the son of Mary, with no father named (Mk 6:3). His own family come to seize him because they think he is out of his mind (Mk 3:21). The disciples are thick for sixteen chapters and never stop being thick. Peter is called Satan. Nobody in the book comes off well, including the narrator's own side, and there is no visible effort to manage that.

Then it ends at Mk 16:8 in the oldest witnesses. The women flee the tomb and say nothing to anyone, for they were afraid. The book ends on a conjunction.

That roughness is the same property the Quranic fault-record has, one register over. A composed hagiography does not include *he frowned and turned away* addressed to its own founder in the third person, and a composed gospel does not include a family who think the subject is mad and an ending that stops mid-breath. Both texts authenticate by what they declined to remove, and the reader posited here recognises the property immediately, because his own book taught it to him.

One further fact makes Mark the right text for this reading. Mark has no doctrine of the Word. None. No *logos*, no preexistence, no with-God clause. The entire apparatus of the Johannine prologue is absent from the earliest Gospel, which is exactly what the lineage of II.8 predicts if that prologue is a later editorial synthesis drawing on an inheritance already available in Alexandria and in the synagogue. The stratification shows up as an absence in the oldest layer.

Which is why the primitive layer's own register is the one closest to the Quranic reading. *Why do you call me good? No one is good but God alone* (Mk 10:18), a predicate deflected rather than accepted, which is the corpus's own *lā aqūlu lakum ʿindī khazāʾinu Allāh* (Q 6:50). *Not even the Son* (Mk 13:32), which is *lā yujallīhā li-waqtihā illā huw* (Q 7:187). Repeated commands to say nothing. A text with no interest in establishing what four centuries of councils would establish from it.

Mark reads clean because there is almost no overlay to strip.

One distinction guards this choice from a familiar objection. The critique of Hellenisation, that the message of a Galilean teacher was recast in the categories of Greek metaphysics, lands squarely on the settlement and on the doctrinal vocabulary that grew toward it, the essence-language, the person-count, the equational creed. It does not land on Mark. His Greek is a carrier and nothing more: paratactic, Semitic under the surface, and four times it stops translating altogether and keeps the Aramaic in the prophet's mouth, *talitha koum*, *ephphatha*, *Abba*, *Elōi Elōi lema sabachthani*, each glossed for the Greek reader as a foreign thing preserved. A text being Hellenised does not keep the source language at the moments of address and translate it in an aside. Mark's Greek is wrapping paper around an Aramaic voice; the settlement's Greek is replacement contents. The critique that condemns the second is the very reason this commentary trusts the first. And the four preserved addresses will be counted again in Part V, because they turn out to fall on a pattern.

:::box Part III Summary

**Mark is earliest and least edited.** Roughly 66 to 70, no genealogy, no infancy, no prologue.

**The roughness is demonstrably original.** The two Gospels that copy him tidy it, and you can watch the tidying in parallel columns.

**What he left in is the receipt.** A family who thought him mad, a *could not* where a later hand wrote *did not*, disciples who never understand, and an ending that stops on a conjunction.

**He has no doctrine of the Word at all.** The Johannine prologue's whole apparatus is absent from the earliest Gospel.

**Which puts his own register closest to the Quranic one:** predicates deflected, the hour reserved, and repeated commands to say nothing.
:::

---

# PART IV · THE GOSPEL ENTIRE

What follows is the whole of Mark, in ten movements, every passage in its place. The discipline is the one announced in the prologue: the Gospel is quoted, then read from the fixed position, then tested as a structure. Corpus verses are given in translation with the Arabic where the Arabic itself is the evidence. Threads that run the length of the book, a preposition, a verb, a gesture, are counted aloud as they arrive, and the counts are gathered in Part V. Where the reading closes, it closes quietly. Where it cannot, the missing piece is named in one sentence and the walk continues.

One practical note. Mark's text is quoted from the earliest recoverable form, and where the manuscripts divide at a point that matters for this reading, the division itself is reported, because the copyists' hands are part of the evidence.

## The Voice and the Wilderness · Mark 1:1-13

Thirteen verses, and the whole book in miniature: a title, a forerunner, a baptism, a voice, and a wilderness. Mark has no genealogy and no infancy because his beginning is not a birth. It is an announcement arriving on a man already grown, which is the corpus's own shape for every prophet it sends.

:::box What to Watch
**The title line** carries *euangelion* and *Christos*, the announcement and the anointed, which are the corpus's *bushrā* and *al-Masīḥ*.

**The forerunner** performs the two-place office of Q 3:39: an attester pointing away from himself, measuring the distance by a sandal-thong.

**The voice at the water** quotes the second Psalm and stops one clause before *today I have begotten you*.

**The wilderness** places the appointed trial where Q 22:52 places it, as a stage of the office rather than a hazard to it.
:::
### The Title Line · Mark 1:1

> The beginning of the gospel of Jesus Christ.

#### Commentary based on Quran

*Archē tou euangeliou Iēsou Christou.* Four words in Greek, and two of them are load-bearing.

*Euangelion*, good news, is not a genre here; the book-name came later. It is the content: a glad announcement. The corpus's word is *bushrā*, and its verb *yubashshiru* is the very verb of the annunciations, Allah ﷻ *gives you glad tidings* of Yaḥyā at Q 3:39, *gives you glad tidings* of a word from Him at Q 3:45. The two texts open the same account with the same speech-act: not a biography beginning, but good news announced.

And *Christou* is a title, not a surname: the anointed, *al-Masīḥ*, which the corpus confers eleven times and never once withdraws. So the first line of the earliest Gospel says, in its own Greek, exactly what the corpus's annunciation says in its Arabic: here begins the glad announcement concerning the Messiah.

A textual matter belongs here because it sits in the very first verse. Many manuscripts continue *huiou theou*, son of God, and the earliest hand of Codex Sinaiticus does not. The words were supplied by a corrector. Whether the phrase fell out by a copyist's slip or was added by a copyist's piety cannot be settled from the outside, and this commentary does not need to settle it. What can be said is narrow and checkable: the one Gospel whose opening line carries the phrase insecurely is the earliest one, and the direction of every later scribal instinct we can watch in this book runs toward adding reverence, not subtracting it. The title line that is certain is the one this reading needs: the beginning of the good news of the Messiah.

### The Forerunner · Mark 1:2-8

> As it is written in Isaiah the prophet: Behold, I send my messenger before your face, who will prepare your way; the voice of one crying in the wilderness, Prepare the way of the Lord, make his paths straight. John appeared, baptizing in the wilderness and proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins. And there went out to him all the country of Judea and all Jerusalem, and they were baptized by him in the river Jordan, confessing their sins. Now John was clothed with camel's hair and wore a leather belt around his waist and ate locusts and wild honey. And he preached, saying: After me comes he who is mightier than I, the strap of whose sandals I am not worthy to stoop down and untie. I have baptized you with water, but he will baptize you with the Holy Spirit.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Mark opens with a citation and gets the label rough: the composite is Malachi and Isaiah welded together, and he files the whole weld under Isaiah. Matthew and Luke, copying him, quietly keep only the Isaiah half so the label becomes true. The seam is original and the smoothing is watchable, the first exhibit of the pattern Part III promised: this text preserves its own roughness, and the roughness is the receipt.

But the content of the weld is the structure the reader already holds from Q 3:39. A messenger sent *before the face*, preparing the way of one who comes after: that is confirmation as a two-place office, an attester and an attested, *muṣaddiqan bi-kalimatin min Allāh*, confirming a word from Allah ﷻ. Mark's first scene after the title is the corpus's Yaḥyā AS doing the corpus's Yaḥyā-work: pointing away from himself at the one he exists to announce. And the pointing is total. *After me comes he who is mightier than I*: *erchetai ho ischyroteros mou opisō mou*. Mark the word *ischyroteros*, mightier, the comparative of strength, because this book is going to conduct an argument in that root, and this is its first occurrence. The count begins here: strength, one.

Then the sandal. *The strap of whose sandals I am not worthy to stoop down and untie.* Every tradition that received this figure kept this image; it is one of the very few sayings that crossed into every stream whole. And the corpus's tradition kept it in the mouth of its own Prophet ﷺ in the transmitted literature concerning the two of them, the honouring of the one who comes by the one who announces. The image survives because it is doing exact work: untying the sandal-thong is the task of the lowest servant of the house, and the announcer declares himself below it. Confirmation is not rivalry. The attester's greatness is measured by how low he places himself before the attested, and Q 3:39 had already given the announcer his own honours in the same breath as his office, *sayyidan wa-ḥaṣūran wa-nabiyyan*, a master, restrained, a prophet, so the lowness is not lack. It is the office performed.

And the last line of the forerunner's speech hands the whole scene upward. *I have baptized you with water, but he will baptize you with the Holy Spirit.* The corpus says of ʿĪsā AS, three times, that he was strengthened *bi-rūḥi al-qudus*, with the Holy Spirit, Q 2:87, Q 2:253, Q 5:110. The forerunner's sentence and the corpus's sentence draw the same line in the same place: water is what a man can administer; the Spirit is administered from above. John's own grammar keeps him on the near side of that line, and the corpus keeps the Spirit as the strengthening *given to* the Messiah, not a possession held. Both texts route the higher baptism through the same upward door.

### The Baptism and the Voice · Mark 1:9-11

> In those days Jesus came from Nazareth of Galilee and was baptized by John in the Jordan. And immediately, coming up out of the water, he saw the heavens being torn open and the Spirit descending on him like a dove. And a voice came from heaven: You are my beloved Son; with you I am well pleased.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Three verses, and each carries something the later tradition would have written differently if it could have.

First, the fact itself: he is baptized by John, in a baptism Mark has just called a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins. Matthew, copying, inserts an objection from John and a justification from Jesus before allowing it; Mark has no embarrassment to manage and manages none. From the fixed position this is not a difficulty at all. The corpus's clean file (Part I) is a file of *recorded fault*, and there is none here either: Mark narrates no confession from him, only the entering of the water everyone else entered. What the scene does is place the prophet inside the practice of the community he is sent to, under the hand of the attester, and the corpus's picture of the *ʿabd* who does not disdain (Q 4:172) stands closer to this scene than the later picture that needed the objection inserted.

Second, the tearing. Mark says the heavens were *torn open*, *schizomenous*, a violent word; Matthew and Luke soften it to *opened*, a door-word. Mark chose the tearing and will use its root exactly once more, at the far end of the book, when the veil of the sanctuary is *torn*, *eschisthē*, from top to bottom (Mk 15:38). Two tearings, one at the water and one at the death, both from the upper side downward, frame the entire Gospel: the barrier is breached from above at the beginning and at the end, and never by the man from below. That is the direction this whole commentary will keep finding, and Mark built it into his structure before his story had properly begun. The corpus states the same direction as law: *lā tufattaḥu lahum abwābu al-samāʾ* (Q 7:40) is its word over those for whom the doors do not open, and the opening of what is above is His act, from His side, in every verse that mentions it.

Third, the voice, and here the earliest Gospel performs the most precise refusal in its opening chapter. *You are my beloved Son; with you I am well pleased.* The first clause is the second Psalm: *You are my son*, Psalm 2:7, the enthronement formula of the Davidic king, recited over a man at his installation. Every hearer who knew the Psalter knew the next clause: *today I have begotten you*. Mark's voice does not say it. The quotation stops at sonship-as-office and declines sonship-as-generation, at the one moment in the book where heaven itself is speaking and could have said anything. The Western text of Luke's parallel famously completes the Psalm, *today I have begotten you*, which shows both that the continuation was available and that a scribal hand willing to write it existed. Mark's hand did not write it. The fence of Q 19:35, *it was not for Allah ﷻ to take to Himself any offspring*, and the mechanism of Q 3:47, *kun fa-yakūn*, arrive six centuries later at exactly the line where the earliest Gospel had already stopped its quotation. The construction the corpus addresses, Mark never carried.

And the clause it does carry is the one the corpus can read without adjustment. *Agapētos*, beloved, the Septuagint's word for Isaac in Genesis 22, the one and cherished. *With you I am well pleased*, *eudokēsa*, the verb of favour. The corpus's account of the same figure runs on the same two notes: *wajīhan fī al-dunyā wa-l-ākhirah*, eminent in this world and the next, *wa-min al-muqarrabīn*, and of those brought near (Q 3:45). Favour conferred, nearness granted, an office announced from above onto a servant below. Set the two opening declarations side by side. Mark: the first words spoken *over* him in this Gospel are heaven's, naming him beloved and favoured. The corpus: the first words spoken *by* him in that book are his own, from the cradle, *innī ʿabdu Allāh*, I am the servant of Allah ﷻ (Q 19:30). The voice from above says *chosen*; the voice from below says *servant*; and Part I has already shown that these are the two halves of the one guarded position. Neither text lets the other half go missing. Mark's heaven confers; the corpus's cradle receives; the relation is identical, read from its two ends.

### The Wilderness · Mark 1:12-13

> And immediately the Spirit drove him out into the wilderness. And he was in the wilderness forty days, being tempted by Satan. And he was with the wild beasts, and the angels were ministering to him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Two verses where Matthew and Luke each spend a scene, and Mark's compression is itself the reading.

*The Spirit drove him out*: *ekballei*, the same verb Mark will use for the casting out of unclean spirits a few verses later. The prophet does not stride into the wilderness; he is propelled, by the Spirit, under an authority that moves him before he has performed a single work. The corpus's grammar for its prophets is the same grammar: sent, commanded, carried, *wa-mā natanazzalu illā bi-amri rabbik* (Q 19:64), and none descends except by the command of your Lord. Agency in both books runs downhill from above, and Mark's verb choice puts even the Messiah's first movement inside it.

*Forty days, tempted by Satan.* Forty is Mūsā AS on the mountain, *arbaʿīna laylatan*, forty nights (Q 7:142), the proving-number of the earlier scripture carried forward. And the tempter is the corpus's *Shayṭān* under his own name, the enemy declared *ʿaduwwun mubīn*, an open adversary, in the corpus's first story about the first man. Mark gives the temptation no content, no dialogue, no three questions; that is Matthew's and Luke's expansion. In the earliest layer there is only the fact: the announced one is placed under trial by the adversary, at the outset, before any public work. The corpus states that placement as a universal law of the office: *And We did not send before you any messenger or prophet except that when he recited, Satan cast into his recitation* (Q 22:52), with the next clause giving the outcome, *fa-yansakhu Allāhu mā yulqī al-shayṭān*, then Allah ﷻ abolishes what Satan casts. Trial by the adversary is not a hazard the mission runs into. It is a stage the mission is appointed to pass through, and the abolishing is His.

*And he was with the wild beasts, and the angels were ministering to him.* Mark alone has the beasts, and the line has puzzled readers into allegory. Read plainly it is a location-claim and a protection-claim: the man is placed where creation is unsubdued, and he is unharmed, and the service he receives there is angelic, *diēkonoun autō*, they were serving him. The corpus's wilderness has the same furniture arranged the same way: the adversary given his respite, *matāʿun ilā ḥīn*, an enjoyment for a time (Q 2:36), the servant protected, and the angels as ministers of the command, *bal ʿibādun mukramūn*, honoured servants who precede Him not in speech and act by His command (Q 21:26-27). Note who serves whom, because Mark will pick the verb up again: here the angels serve him; at Mk 10:45 he will say the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve. The ministry he receives in the wilderness he will spend the book handing on downward, and the corpus's Messiah, who *does not disdain to be a servant*, is the same figure drawn from the other side.

Thirteen verses in, and the architecture of the whole is already standing: announcement, attestation, water, the torn heaven, the guarded sonship-without-begetting, the driven servant, the appointed trial, the ministering angels. Nothing in it required adjustment from the fixed position, and one thing in it, the dropped clause of the Psalm, turned out to be the corpus's fence built six centuries early.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** One office with two seats. A forerunner who measures himself by a sandal strap, and one who comes after him, ranked in the forerunner's own mouth before the narrative has moved.

**What moves.** The descent. The heavens are torn, the Spirit comes down onto a located point, and the same Spirit immediately drives the one it landed on into the wilderness. A real traversal with a named landing-site and a named cost.

**How they bind.** The ordering. *Opisō mou*, after me, is what makes the first figure an attester rather than a rival and the second a term rather than a return, and it is the *taṣdīq* relation of Q 3:39 stated from the other side.

The three stand apart and compose. Collapse the two seats and Herod's sentence in chapter six becomes a datum instead of a confusion. Collapse the descent and the voice has no addressee. Collapse the ordering and nothing distinguishes an attestation from two competing claims. ⟀ GOL on the opening frame.

## Galilee · Mark 1:14-3:6

The public work opens, and within two chapters every major instrument of the book is on the table: the proclamation, the calling, the authority, the silencing, the healing in the passive voice, the forgiveness dispute, the table-fellowship scandal, the bridegroom's taking, the appointed day, and the first plot. Mark builds fast. The reading walks each piece in order.

:::box What to Watch
**The first public sentence** is corpus-grammar in all four clauses: appointed term filled, arrival announced, turn, believe.

**The healings run in the passive.** *Be cleansed* and *the hand was restored*: the agent of the cure is left above the sentence.

**The scribes object in the Quran's own words,** *who can forgive sins but God alone*, and the scene never leaves their doctrine.

**The bridegroom is *taken away*,** in the passive, six centuries before *rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh* names the hand.
:::
### The Proclamation · Mark 1:14-15

> Now after John was arrested, Jesus came into Galilee, proclaiming the gospel of God, and saying: The time is fulfilled, and the kingdom of God is at hand; repent and believe in the gospel.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The forerunner is handed over, *paradothēnai*, the verb that will later carry the Messiah himself, and only then does the public work begin. The office of confirmation passes at the attester's arrest: two seats, one relay, exactly the structure Q 3:39 set.

The first public sentence has four clauses and every one of them is corpus-grammar. *The time is fulfilled*: *peplērōtai ho kairos*, a perfect passive, the appointed term has been filled, and the corpus's word for that structure is *ajal musammā*, a term appointed, which nothing hastens and nothing delays, *fa-idhā jāʾa ajaluhum lā yastaʾkhirūna sāʿatan wa-lā yastaqdimūn* (Q 7:34). *The kingdom of God is at hand*: *ēngiken*, has drawn near, and the corpus opens one of its surahs on the same announcement in the same aspect, *atā amru Allāhi fa-lā tastaʿjilūh*, the command of Allah ﷻ has come, so do not seek to hasten it (Q 16:1), arrival announced, haste forbidden. *Repent*: *metanoeite*, turn, the corpus's *tūbū ilā Allāhi jamīʿan* (Q 24:31). *And believe in the good news*: *āminū* and *bushrā* in one clause. A Muslim reader can recite Mk 1:15 without adjusting a single term. It is the sentence every messenger in the corpus is sent to say.

### The Four Called · Mark 1:16-20

> Passing alongside the Sea of Galilee, he saw Simon and Andrew the brother of Simon casting a net in the sea, for they were fishermen. And Jesus said to them: Follow me, and I will make you become fishers of men. And immediately they left their nets and followed him. And going on a little farther, he saw James the son of Zebedee and John his brother, who were in their boat mending the nets. And immediately he called them, and they left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired servants and followed him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The call is two words and the response is one motion. Mark's *euthys*, immediately, does its first real work here: no deliberation is narrated, no terms are negotiated. The corpus commands exactly this shape of response and gives its reason: *istajībū lillāhi wa-li-l-rasūli idhā daʿākum limā yuḥyīkum*, respond to Allah ﷻ and to the messenger when he calls you to what gives you life (Q 8:24). The call is to life; delay is the only loss.

And the promise is worded with a care worth keeping. *I will make you become fishers of men*: *poiēsō hymas genesthai*. Not *you will be*, but *I will make you become*, the transformation is granted, not achieved, and the granting is the caller's. That is the corpus's *rabbāniyyūn* structure at Q 3:79: the true teacher says *be devoted servants of the Lord*, *kūnū rabbāniyyīn*, by what you have been taught, the becoming commanded from above and enabled by what was given. Four working men leave nets, boat, father, and hire, and the leaving is the first enacted commentary on a sentence the rich man will fail at nine chapters later. Mark plants both ends of that comparison himself; the reading only has to notice that he did.

### A Sabbath in Capernaum · Mark 1:21-28

> And they went into Capernaum, and immediately on the Sabbath he entered the synagogue and was teaching. And they were astonished at his teaching, for he taught them as one who had authority, and not as the scribes. And immediately there was in their synagogue a man with an unclean spirit. And he cried out: What have you to do with us, Jesus of Nazareth? Have you come to destroy us? I know who you are, the Holy One of God. But Jesus rebuked him, saying: Be silent, and come out of him. And the unclean spirit, convulsing him and crying out with a loud voice, came out of him. And they were all amazed, so that they questioned among themselves, saying: What is this? A new teaching with authority. He commands even the unclean spirits, and they obey him. And at once his fame spread everywhere throughout all the surrounding region of Galilee.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Two things happen in this synagogue and the second interprets the first.

The first is the teaching *as one who had authority, and not as the scribes*: *hōs exousian echōn*. The scribes taught by chain of citation, authority borrowed sideways from precedent. He teaches as one *holding* authority, and the word is *exousia*, which throughout this Gospel means conferred warrant, authority held because given. The crowd hears the difference before they can name it. The corpus names it: *wa-mā yanṭiqu ʿan al-hawā, in huwa illā waḥyun yūḥā*, he does not speak from desire; it is only a revelation revealed (Q 53:3-4). Speech from the upstream source needs no sideways chain.

The second is the spirit, and here Mark records something stranger and more precise than an exorcism. The unclean spirit *knows*. *I know who you are, the Holy One of God.* The identification is true, early, and supernatural, and the response is not gratitude for the testimony. It is a muzzle: *phimōthēti*, be silenced, the word for gagging an animal. Verse 34 will generalise it: *he would not permit the demons to speak, because they knew him*. Knowledge from that register is real and its testimony is refused.

The corpus has the doctrine for exactly this, stated about its own revelation: *wa-mā tanazzalat bihi al-shayāṭīn, wa-mā yanbaghī lahum wa-mā yastaṭīʿūn, innahum ʿani al-samʿi la-maʿzūlūn*, the satans did not bring it down; it is not fitting for them, nor are they able; they are barred from the hearing (Q 26:210-212). The spirit-register is excluded from the transmission channel as a matter of standing law, not because its information is false but because its testimony is unauthorised. Mark's Messiah enforces in a synagogue the same exclusion the corpus states as cosmology, and the silencing thread that will run the whole first half of this Gospel begins here, on a witness whose statement was *correct*. Hold that fact. It is the key to every silencing that follows: what is being managed is not truth but authorisation, and the count of the silencings starts now: one.

### The Evening at the Door · Mark 1:29-34

> And immediately he left the synagogue and entered the house of Simon and Andrew, with James and John. Now Simon's mother-in-law lay ill with a fever, and immediately they told him about her. And he came and took her by the hand and lifted her up, and the fever left her, and she began to serve them. That evening at sundown they brought to him all who were sick or oppressed by demons. And the whole city was gathered together at the door. And he healed many who were sick with various diseases, and cast out many demons. And he would not permit the demons to speak, because they knew him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The first healing in the book is domestic, unnamed in request, and wordless in method: a hand taken, a lifting, *ēgeiren autēn*, the raising verb that will close the Gospel at the tomb. And her first act on being raised is *diēkonei autois*, she began to serve them. Mark has now used the serving verb twice in one chapter, of the angels in the wilderness and of a woman in a kitchen, and both times of ministry offered to him. The book is quietly stockpiling the verb for the day its subject inverts it: *the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve* (Mk 10:45). Every early instance of receiving service is capital the later sentence will spend.

Then the door, the whole city, the many healed. And the closing clause repeats the law of the synagogue in general form: *he would not permit the demons to speak, because they knew him*. Silencing, two, and now explicitly grounded: *because* they knew. True knowledge, barred testimony, Q 26:212's *maʿzūlūn* enforced at a doorway in Capernaum.

### Before Dawn, Alone · Mark 1:35-39

> And rising very early in the morning, while it was still dark, he departed and went out to a desolate place, and there he prayed. And Simon and those who were with him hunted for him, and they found him and said to him: Everyone is looking for you. And he said to them: Let us go on to the next towns, that I may preach there also, for that is why I came out. And he went throughout all Galilee, preaching in their synagogues and casting out demons.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Three verses that later theology never managed to make comfortable, and this reading does not need to.

He rises in the dark, *ennycha lian*, deep in the night, leaves the house where the whole city had gathered, and goes where no one is, *erēmon topon*, to pray. Alone. The scene has no audience inside the story; Mark asserts it happened where nobody could watch. A man to whom the whole city is queuing withdraws from the queue to stand as a petitioner. The corpus prescribes this exact watch to its own Prophet ﷺ: *qumi al-layla illā qalīlā*, rise in the night, except a little (Q 73:2), and *wa-mina al-layli fa-tahajjad bihi nāfilatan laka*, and in part of the night keep vigil with it, as an addition for you (Q 17:79). The night office is the prophetic office's private half, and Mark's Messiah keeps it before his first full day of work is over. What the scene establishes is direction: the one they are all seeking is himself seeking, and his seeking runs upward, unwitnessed, unbrokered. The single voice that Part II found in the grammar of Q 2:186 is here as narrative: no intermediary appears in the desolate place, because between the servant and the Lord there is nothing to appear.

Simon *hunted him down*, *katediōxen*, a pursuit verb with an edge on it, and the report is the report of every manager of a successful man: everyone is looking for you, the crowd is an asset, come back. The answer redirects the whole enterprise in one clause: *let us go elsewhere, that I may preach there also, for that is why I came out*. *Exēlthon*, I came out: the sent one states his sending, and the purpose is proclamation, not concentration of a following. The corpus states the same office-definition as a bare rule: *wa-mā ʿalā al-rasūli illā al-balāgh*, nothing is upon the messenger except the delivery (Q 24:54). The crowd at Capernaum's door is not the mission. The delivery is, and the delivery moves.

### The Leper · Mark 1:40-45

> And a leper came to him, imploring him, and kneeling said to him: If you will, you can make me clean. Moved with compassion, he stretched out his hand and touched him and said to him: I will; be clean. And immediately the leprosy left him, and he was made clean. And Jesus sternly charged him and sent him away at once, and said to him: See that you say nothing to anyone, but go, show yourself to the priest and offer for your cleansing what Moses commanded, for a proof to them. But he went out and began to talk freely about it, and to spread the news, so that Jesus could no longer openly enter a town, but was out in desolate places, and people were coming to him from every quarter.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The leper's own sentence carries a theology: *ean thelēs dynasai me katharisai*, if you are willing, you are able to make me clean. He does not question the ability. He locates it inside a will, and waits on the will. That is precisely where the corpus locates every prophetic ability: real, and conditional on a permission that sits above the prophet. The corpus's ʿĪsā AS says it with this very disease in the list: *wa-ubriʾu al-akmaha wa-l-abraṣa bi-idhni Allāh*, and I heal the blind and the leper, by the permission of Allah ﷻ (Q 3:49). The *abraṣ* of the corpus and the *lepros* of Mark are the same sufferer, and the two texts hang the same cure on the same hinge. The leper in Mark, kneeling, has intuited the permission-structure from below; the corpus states it from above; the scene and the verse interlock like a socket and its plug.

Then the answer, and Mark's grammar performs the doctrine. *Thelō*, I am willing. *Katharisthēti*: be cleansed. A passive imperative. Not *I cleanse you*, active, first person, but a command that the cleansing happen, with the agent of the cleansing unnamed, which in the speech of that world is the reverent way of naming the Unnamed. Divine passive, one, and the count that Part V will gather begins here. The compassion is his, *splanchnistheis*, moved in the guts; the touch is his; the willingness is his; and the verb of the cure is written so that the cure itself arrives from the unstated Agent above the sentence. Mark's Greek and Q 3:49's Arabic are two drawings of one circuit.

#### Structural Commentary · The Leper, Mark 1:40-45

**What stands.** A sufferer, a disease, and a healer who holds no inventory of cures.

**What moves.** A willing stated, a touch given, and a cleansing that arrives.

**How they bind.** The passive imperative. *Be cleansed* names the event and leaves the Agent above the sentence, so the willing belongs to the servant and the cleansing to the One unnamed.

The three stand apart and compose, which is why the reading closes. Collapse the third into the first, and the healer becomes the source of the cure; the text declines that collapse in its own grammar, and Q 3:49 hangs the same circuit on *bi-idhni Allāh*, four times in one verse. ⟀ GOL on the leper, the instantaneous lock.

Two more details close the passage. The cleansed man is sent to the priest with the offering *Moses commanded*: the Torah is upheld, not bypassed, *muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayya mina al-Tawrāt*, confirming what came before me of the Torah, as the corpus's ʿĪsā AS says of himself (Q 3:50). And the man is charged to silence, *say nothing to anyone*, silencing three, this time on a beneficiary rather than a spirit, and he disobeys, and the cost is immediate and structural: the healer *can no longer openly enter a town*. Unauthorised proclamation, even grateful, even true, distorts the mission's geometry; the crowds displace the delivery. Mark has now shown the silencing rule enforced on a demon and broken by a healed man, and shown the price of the breach, and the reader begins to understand that the secrecy in this book is not mystification. It is traffic control on testimony, until the appointed hour when the ban will be lifted by the only voice with the authority to lift it.

### The Paralytic, and the Scribes' Sentence · Mark 2:1-12

> And when he returned to Capernaum after some days, it was reported that he was at home. And many were gathered together, so that there was no more room, not even at the door, and he was preaching the word to them. And they came, bringing to him a paralytic carried by four men. And when they could not get near him because of the crowd, they removed the roof above him, and when they had made an opening, they let down the bed on which the paralytic lay. And when Jesus saw their faith, he said to the paralytic: Child, your sins are forgiven. Now some of the scribes were sitting there, questioning in their hearts: Why does this man speak like that? He is blaspheming. Who can forgive sins but God alone? And immediately Jesus, perceiving in his spirit that they thus questioned within themselves, said to them: Why do you question these things in your hearts? Which is easier, to say to the paralytic, Your sins are forgiven, or to say, Rise, take up your bed and walk? But that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins, he said to the paralytic: I say to you, rise, pick up your bed, and go home. And he rose and immediately picked up his bed and went out before them all, so that they were all amazed and glorified God, saying: We never saw anything like this.

#### Commentary based on Quran

This is the first collision of the book, and the astonishing thing, from the fixed position, is what the collision is made of.

Begin with the scribes' objection, because it is a sentence this reader already knows by heart. *Who can forgive sins but God alone?* *Tis dynatai aphienai hamartias ei mē heis ho theos.* Now hear the corpus: *wa-man yaghfiru al-dhunūba illā Allāh*, and who forgives sins except Allah ﷻ (Q 3:135). The same sentence. The same rhetorical question, the same exclusive *illā*, the same doctrine, and in the corpus it is spoken in praise of the God-fearing who seek forgiveness from the only place forgiveness lives. The opponents of Mark's Messiah state the Quran's own clause, word for word across the two languages. So the question the scene puts is not whether the doctrine is true. Both books hold it true. The question is whether anything in the scene breaks it.

Walk the scene's grammar and nothing does. What is said to the paralytic is *aphientai sou hai hamartiai*: your sins *are forgiven*, a passive, present, with the forgiving agent unstated, the same reverent passive as the leper's *katharisthēti* one column earlier. He does not say *I forgive you*. No Gospel has him say it, here or anywhere. The announcement declares a forgiveness whose Agent sits above the sentence, exactly as the cleansing did. And when the objection is engaged, the claim made is not identity but warrant: *the Son of Man has authority*, *exousian echei*, *on earth* to forgive sins. *Exousia* is this Gospel's word for conferred authority, the teaching *as one having exousia* of the synagogue scene, and *on earth* names the jurisdiction of a delegate, not the throne of the Principal. An announcement in the divine passive, grounded in a delegated warrant, with the visible healing offered as the *proof* of the invisible grant, *that you may know*: this is, clause for clause, the corpus's permission-structure at Q 3:49, where every mighty work of ʿĪsā AS is listed with *bi-idhni Allāh* hung on it like a seal, and the works are *āyatan lakum*, a sign for you, the visible attesting the granted.

Even the perception is in the inventory. Mark says he perceived *in his spirit* what they reasoned *in their hearts*, and the corpus's ʿĪsā AS announces, in the same Q 3:49 list, by the same permission: *wa-unabbiʾukum bimā taʾkulūna wa-mā taddakhirūna fī buyūtikum*, and I inform you of what you eat and what you store in your houses, knowledge of the hidden granted as a sign. The heart-reading in Capernaum is not an attribute leaking through a disguise. It is an item on the granted list, and the corpus wrote the list.

And Mark himself files the verdict, in the crowd's mouth, in the last line: *they were all amazed and glorified God*. *Edoxasan ton theon.* The scene that began with a charge of blasphemy ends with the room's worship going upward, past the healer, to the Agent of the passives, and the narrator records the arrow without comment. The corpus's rule for every servant, *wa-mā bikum min niʿmatin fa-mina Allāh*, whatever favour is with you is from Allah ﷻ (Q 16:53), is the crowd's instinct and the evangelist's punctuation. The first blasphemy charge in the Gospel dissolves under the two texts read together: the scribes were right about the doctrine and wrong about the scene, because the scene never left the doctrine.

One title entered quietly and should be marked on its way in. *The Son of Man*: its first appearance in this Gospel, and its second will come at the end of the next dispute (Mk 2:28). Not in a vision, not in glory, but in a forgiveness quarrel and a Sabbath quarrel, both of them arguments about *warrant*. The book introduces its subject's chosen self-designation in the two scenes where what is at issue is delegated authority, and the designation itself, in the Aramaic under the Greek, is *bar enasha*, the son of the human, the human one. The corpus's ʿĪsā-file and its Prophet-file both anchor in the same self-word: *innamā anā basharun mithlukum*, I am only a human like you (Q 18:110). The title will gather weight through the book, and the reading will meet it fully on the road to Jerusalem. Here it is enough to record where it chose to be born: among the humans, in a dispute about what a human may carry.

### Levi, and the Physician · Mark 2:13-17

> He went out again beside the sea, and all the crowd was coming to him, and he was teaching them. And as he passed by, he saw Levi the son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax booth, and he said to him: Follow me. And he rose and followed him. And as he reclined at table in his house, many tax collectors and sinners were reclining with Jesus and his disciples, for there were many who followed him. And the scribes of the Pharisees, when they saw that he was eating with sinners and tax collectors, said to his disciples: Why does he eat with tax collectors and sinners? And when Jesus heard it, he said to them: Those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are sick. I came not to call the righteous, but sinners.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The call crosses a boundary the first four calls did not. Fishermen were poor and ritually ordinary; a tax farmer for the tetrarchy was a collaborator, presumed dishonest, socially unclean. The call is the same two words, and the response is the same single motion, and Mark makes the point by making no point: the call does not check credentials on its way in.

The objection comes at table, where that world kept its boundaries, and the answer is a proverb and a mission-statement. *Those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are sick.* The corpus carries the same medical figure for the same office: the revelation is *shifāʾun limā fī al-ṣudūr*, a healing for what is in the breasts (Q 10:57), and *We send down of the Qurʾān that which is healing and mercy* (Q 17:82). A physician's presence among the sick is not contamination; it is deployment. And the mission-clause, *I came not to call the righteous, but sinners*, is the corpus's own address written as a sending: *qul yā ʿibādiya alladhīna asrafū ʿalā anfusihim lā taqnaṭū min raḥmati Allāh*, say, O My servants who have transgressed against their own souls, do not despair of the mercy of Allah ﷻ (Q 39:53). The corpus aims its tenderest verse at the very class the Pharisees' scribes would not recline with, and Mark's Messiah eats his way through the same doctrine. Two texts, one triage protocol: the call goes where the sickness is.

### The Bridegroom, and the Taking · Mark 2:18-22

> Now John's disciples and the Pharisees were fasting. And people came and said to him: Why do John's disciples and the disciples of the Pharisees fast, but your disciples do not fast? And Jesus said to them: Can the wedding guests fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they cannot fast. The days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them, and then they will fast in that day. No one sews a piece of unshrunk cloth on an old garment. If he does, the patch tears away from it, the new from the old, and a worse tear is made. And no one puts new wine into old wineskins. If he does, the wine will burst the skins, and the wine is destroyed, and so are the skins. But new wine is for fresh wineskins.

#### Commentary based on Quran

A question about fasting, and inside the answer, half-hidden, the first prediction in this Gospel of the end of the story.

*The days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them.* The verb is *aparthē*, from *apairō*, to be taken away, and it is passive: the bridegroom does not leave, is not lost, does not die in this sentence. He *is taken*, by an Agent the grammar declines to name, and the reader of this commentary has met that grammar twice already in two chapters. Now set the corpus beside it: *innī mutawaffīka wa-rāfiʿuka ilayya*, I am taking you and raising you to Myself (Q 3:55), and *bal rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh*, rather, Allah ﷻ raised him to Himself (Q 4:158). The corpus's whole account of this prophet's departure is an active *taking* with Allah ﷻ as the subject; Mark's earliest prediction of the departure is a passive *being taken* with the subject unstated; and a passive with an unstated divine agent is the same sentence written from below. Long before this Gospel narrates an arrest, it has told the reader, in a wedding metaphor, that the ending of this story is a removal performed from above. The two texts will part later over what the removal passed through. They do not part over whose hand performs it.

And then the fasting-clause completes, and it is a prophecy with a checkable afterlife: *and then they will fast in that day*. While the bridegroom is present, feasting; when he is taken, the fast returns. The corpus, six centuries downstream of the taking, writes to the community it gathers: *kutiba ʿalaykumu al-ṣiyām kamā kutiba ʿalā alladhīna min qablikum*, fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you (Q 2:183). A community on the far side of the bridegroom's taking, fasting as those before them fasted, is what Mk 2:20 says the future holds. The verse and its fulfilment are in different scriptures, and they interlock.

The two little parables that close the passage state why the new cannot be administered inside the old containers, and Mark's vocabulary winks at his own book: the patch pulls away and *a worse tear is made*, *cheiron schisma ginetai*, the tearing-root of the baptism and the veil, here in miniature. New wine, fresh skins: a new deposit requires a new vessel, or both are destroyed. The corpus knows the principle as the succession of revelations, each *muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayh wa-muhayminan ʿalayh*, confirming what preceded it and standing guard over it (Q 5:48): continuity of the wine, renewal of the skin, and the guarding of the one by the other.

### The Grainfields, and the Appointed Day · Mark 2:23-28

> One Sabbath he was going through the grainfields, and as they made their way, his disciples began to pluck heads of grain. And the Pharisees were saying to him: Look, why are they doing what is not lawful on the Sabbath? And he said to them: Have you never read what David did, when he was in need and was hungry, he and those who were with him, how he entered the house of God, in the time of Abiathar the high priest, and ate the bread of the Presence, which it is not lawful for any but the priests to eat, and also gave it to those who were with him? And he said to them: The Sabbath was made for man, not man for the Sabbath. So the Son of Man is lord even of the Sabbath.

#### Commentary based on Quran

First, the famous rough spot, because this commentary promised to exhibit the text's difficulties wherever they fall. The priest of the showbread episode in 1 Samuel 21 is Ahimelech; Abiathar is his son. Mark writes *in the time of Abiathar the high priest*. Matthew and Luke, copying the argument, both drop the name, the copyists' hands smoothing the earliest text for the third time in three chapters. The reading takes the seam as it took the Isaiah label at Mk 1:2: as the signature of a text preserved rougher than its copies, which is the property the whole of Part III was built on. A forger polishes; a witness transmits.

The argument itself is scripture against scripture-as-fence: David, in need, ate what only priests may eat, and the text that records it records no condemnation. Need, in the precedent, outranked the fence. Then the principle, and it is worth hearing with corpus ears: *the Sabbath was made for man, not man for the Sabbath*. *To sabbaton dia ton anthrōpon egeneto*: the Sabbath *came to be*, was made, appointed, for the human. The corpus says of the same institution, with the same verb-idea: *innamā juʿila al-sabtu ʿalā alladhīna ikhtalafū fīh*, the Sabbath was appointed only upon those who differed concerning it (Q 16:124). *Egeneto* and *juʿila*: both texts class the Sabbath as a made thing, an ordinance instituted for a purpose within history, not an uncreated absolute. And a made thing serves the purpose of its Maker for the ones it was made for. The corpus states the Maker's purpose in its own legislation, in, of all places, its fasting-chapter: *yurīdu Allāhu bikumu al-yusra wa-lā yurīdu bikumu al-ʿusr*, Allah ﷻ intends for you ease and does not intend for you hardship (Q 2:185), and again, *wa-mā jaʿala ʿalaykum fī al-dīni min ḥaraj*, He has placed upon you in the religion no strangling difficulty (Q 22:78). The Sabbath-principle of Mk 2:27 is the ease-principle of Q 2:185, applied to the seventh day.

Then the closing sentence, *the Son of Man is lord even of the Sabbath*, and the fixed position reads it inside the structure the passage itself built. The Sabbath is a made ordinance; ordinances rank below the purpose they serve; and the one sent to declare the purpose carries warrant over the ordinance's application, which is exactly the office the corpus assigns its Messiah toward this very law-code: *wa-li-uḥilla lakum baʿḍa alladhī ḥurrima ʿalaykum*, and to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you (Q 3:50). A messenger with delegated authority to loosen appointed restrictions, *by permission*, is both books' picture. The sentence claims jurisdiction over an ordinance, under the Ordainer; it does not claim the Ordainer's seat, and the whole argument that carries it, David's precedent, the made-for-man principle, would be unnecessary scaffolding for anyone who held that seat. You do not argue from precedent for what you own outright. The argument's very existence is the sentence's own commentary.

### The Withered Hand, and the First Plot · Mark 3:1-6

> Again he entered the synagogue, and a man was there with a withered hand. And they watched him, to see whether he would heal him on the Sabbath, so that they might accuse him. And he said to the man with the withered hand: Come here. And he said to them: Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or to do harm, to save life or to kill? But they were silent. And he looked around at them with anger, grieved at their hardness of heart, and said to the man: Stretch out your hand. He stretched it out, and his hand was restored. And the Pharisees went out and immediately held counsel with the Herodians against him, how to destroy him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The Sabbath question returns sharpened to a fork: *is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or to do harm, to save life or to kill?* The verb is *exestin*, is it permitted, the vocabulary of warrant again, and the fork is built so that silence itself answers it, because refusing to permit the good is choosing the harm. *But they were silent*: *hoi de esiōpōn*. Mark has spent two chapters silencing spirits and beneficiaries whose testimony, though true, was unauthorised. Here the watchers, whose office it was to speak the law, silence themselves rather than answer it. The two silences face each other across the book's first act: the silenced who knew, and the self-silenced who would not say. The corpus's diagnosis for the second silence is the one Mark reaches for in the same breath, *hardness of heart*: *thumma qasat qulūbukum min baʿdi dhālika fa-hiya ka-l-ḥijārati aw ashaddu qaswah*, then your hearts hardened after that and became like stones, or harder (Q 2:74), spoken to the same community's ancestors about the same organ.

The healing itself is two imperatives and a passive. *Come here. Stretch out your hand.* And then: *apekatestathē hē cheir autou*, his hand *was restored*. No touch this time, no spittle, no formula, not even a healing-word addressed to the limb: a command to the man, and a restoration whose agent the grammar leaves unstated. Divine passive, two. The circuit of Q 3:49 runs again with even the wires hidden: the prophet supplies the occasion and the command; the restoring arrives from above the sentence.

And then the movement closes on its darkest line. *The Pharisees went out and immediately held counsel with the Herodians against him, how to destroy him.* Chapter three, verse six, and the plot to kill is already convened, between two parties who agreed on nothing else. Mark's *symboulion*, counsel, is the corpus's *makr*: *wa-makarū wa-makara Allāh, wa-Allāhu khayru al-mākirīn*, and they plotted, and Allah ﷻ plotted, and Allah ﷻ is the best of plotters (Q 3:54), a verse the corpus writes about this very story, in the chapter that carries this very prophet's name. The plotters have entered the narrative. The counter-plot, both books agree, was already in place before theirs convened, and the rest of this Gospel is the two plots converging on a hill outside the city, where the corpus will say the plotters' version of events was never what happened, and Mark, read closely, will turn out to have left more room for that sentence than fourteen centuries of readers assumed.

This movement stands, then, with nothing forced. The proclamation was corpus-grammar entire. The callings ran on *istajībū*. The authority was *exousia*, conferred. The silencings enforced Q 26:212's bar on the spirit-register and began their count at three. The healings ran in the divine passive, two of them now, on the circuit Q 3:49 wired. The scribes objected in the Quran's own sentence and the scene never left the sentence's doctrine. The bridegroom's taking was announced in the passive six centuries before *rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh* named the hand. The Sabbath was a made thing in both books, serving the ease both books legislate. And the plot convened on schedule, opposite a counter-plot older than itself.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** A conferred authority. The crowd notices it in the first synagogue and Mark never explains where it came from, which is itself the report: it is remarked upon as unlike the scribes' and never derived from the man.

**What moves.** The healings, each of them an expenditure with a cost and an instant, and each of them written with the acting verb in the passive: *be cleansed*, *the hand was restored*, *your sins are forgiven*.

**How they bind.** The permission, and the movement's own grammar is what carries it. Across five healings the acting verb goes passive at exactly the moment of the cure, so the authority remarked upon in the synagogue is never once written as the healer's possession. What Q 3:49 states as a clause, *bi-idhni Allāh*, four times in one verse, this movement builds into the morphology of its verbs and never explains.

Collapse the authority into the person and the healer is a technician with an inventory. Collapse the healings and the authority is a claim with no discharge. Collapse the permission and every passive in the movement is decoration, which is exactly what a reader has to assume in order to miss the pattern. ⟀ GOL on the permission circuit.

## The Twelve and the Seed · Mark 3:7-4:34

The middle of the Galilean act: a withdrawal, an appointment, the gravest charge in the book answered, a family redefined, and then a chapter of seed. Mark sets his hardest doctrine, the veiling, inside his gentlest imagery, the sowing, and the arrangement is itself a teaching.

:::box What to Watch
**The Twelve** are appointed on the precedent the corpus itself records: twelve chieftains at the covenant of the Children of Israel, Q 5:12.

**The one unforgivable sin** and the one unforgivable *shirk* are the same act read from two sides: misattribution at the root of the ledger.

**Kinship is redrawn by deed,** which is Q 11:46 spoken gently: *he is not of your family*.

**Four seed-parables** relocate growth, yield, timing, and scale away from the human middle and upward.
:::
### The Crowds and the Sea · Mark 3:7-12

> Jesus withdrew with his disciples to the sea, and a great crowd followed, from Galilee and Judea and Jerusalem and Idumea and from beyond the Jordan and from around Tyre and Sidon. When the great crowd heard all that he was doing, they came to him. And he told his disciples to have a boat ready for him because of the crowd, lest they crush him, for he had healed many, so that all who had diseases pressed around him to touch him. And whenever the unclean spirits saw him, they fell down before him and cried out: You are the Son of God. And he strictly ordered them not to make him known.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The catchment widens to the whole map, north, south, east, and across the water, and the pressure becomes physical: a boat kept ready so the crowd does not crush him. The healed press to touch; the spirits fall and shout a title. And the closing line runs the standing law again: *he strictly ordered them not to make him known*. Silencing, four. The title the spirits shout is the very one the later church would build on, and Mark shows it being refused from the mouths that offered it earliest and loudest. The bar of Q 26:212 holds precisely where holding it costs the most: testimony from the barred register is not promoted to doctrine merely because it flatters. If anything, the scene teaches the harder half of the rule, that the spirit-register's flattery is the more dangerous half of its speech, and the corpus says as much of the whisperer whose speech is *ghurūran*, delusion by adornment (Q 4:120). What the spirits know is managed; what they proclaim is muzzled; and the reader is being trained, four scenes in, to ask of every title in this book not *is it exalted* but *who authorised it*.

### The Twelve Appointed · Mark 3:13-19

> And he went up on the mountain and called to him those whom he desired, and they came to him. And he appointed twelve, whom he also named apostles, so that they might be with him, and he might send them out to preach and have authority to cast out demons. He appointed the twelve: Simon, to whom he gave the name Peter; James the son of Zebedee and John the brother of James, to whom he gave the name Boanerges, that is, Sons of Thunder; Andrew, and Philip, and Bartholomew, and Matthew, and Thomas, and James the son of Alphaeus, and Thaddaeus, and Simon the Zealot, and Judas Iscariot, who betrayed him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Up the mountain, the appointment-height of both scriptures, and the election is stated twice over before a single name is given: *he called to him those whom he desired*, *hous ēthelen autos*, whom he himself willed, and *he appointed twelve*, *epoiēsen dōdeka*, literally he *made* twelve. Election by the caller's will, and a making rather than a recruiting: this is the corpus's own vocabulary of prophetic company. *Inna Allāha ṣṭafā*, Allah ﷻ *chose* Adam and Nūḥ and the house of Ibrāhīm and the house of ʿImrān above the worlds (Q 3:33): *iṣṭifāʾ*, election from above, never application from below.

And the number is not free-floating. The corpus itself records the precedent, in the covenant-scene of the earlier community: *wa-baʿathnā minhumu ithnay ʿashara naqībā*, and We raised up from among them twelve chieftains (Q 5:12), one for each tribe, at the taking of the covenant of the Children of Israel. Twelve appointed overseers at the covenant of Mūsā AS; twelve appointed companions at the renewal under ʿĪsā AS; the number is the covenant-community's own architecture, carried forward, and the corpus is the book that states the earlier twelve in so many words.

The purpose-clause is two-stage and the order matters: *that they might be with him*, first, *and that he might send them out*, second. Companionship, then commission; formation, then mission. The corpus's word for this cohort is *al-ḥawāriyyūn*, and it records their enlistment in the same two-stage grammar, the call and the response: *man anṣārī ilā Allāh, qāla al-ḥawāriyyūna naḥnu anṣāru Allāh*, who are my helpers toward Allah ﷻ? The disciples said: we are the helpers of Allah ﷻ (Q 3:52, and again at Q 61:14). Helpers *toward* Allah ﷻ, not toward a cause of their own: the preposition assigns the mission's direction in both books, upward through the sender.

Two shadows sit inside the list, and Mark plants both without comment. Simon is renamed, and the renamer's authority to rename is simply exercised, the way the corpus's Lord renames and retitles at will. And the list ends: *Judas Iscariot, who betrayed him*. The betrayal is announced in the appointment-scene itself, twelve chapters early, the wound built into the roster from the first hour. The corpus states the general law of which this is the instance: *wa-kadhālika jaʿalnā li-kulli nabiyyin ʿaduwwan*, and thus We appointed for every prophet an enemy (Q 6:112, and Q 25:31). The enemy is *appointed*, part of the architecture of the office, not an accident that befalls it, and Mark's roster, with its last name pre-marked, is that verse in list form.

### The House, the Strong Man, and the Unforgivable · Mark 3:20-30

> Then he went home, and the crowd gathered again, so that they could not even eat. And when his family heard it, they went out to seize him, for they were saying: He is out of his mind. And the scribes who came down from Jerusalem were saying: He is possessed by Beelzebul, and by the prince of demons he casts out the demons. And he called them to him and said to them in parables: How can Satan cast out Satan? If a kingdom is divided against itself, that kingdom cannot stand. And if a house is divided against itself, that house will not be able to stand. And if Satan has risen up against himself and is divided, he cannot stand, but is coming to an end. But no one can enter a strong man's house and plunder his goods, unless he first binds the strong man. Then indeed he may plunder his house. Truly, I say to you, all sins will be forgiven the children of man, and whatever blasphemies they utter, but whoever blasphemes against the Holy Spirit never has forgiveness, but is guilty of an eternal sin. For they were saying: He has an unclean spirit.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Here Mark begins the first of his interruptions, the device this commentary will count as it recurs: a story opened, a second story inserted whole, the first story resumed, so that each reads the other. The family set out to seize him at verse 21; they will arrive at verse 31; and between departure and arrival Mark seats the gravest charge and the gravest warning in the book. Intercalation, one. The frame and the filling are one argument: those who should know him best call him mad, and those whose office is discernment call him possessed, and the two misreadings are left standing side by side so the reader can weigh which one the warning falls on.

The charge from Jerusalem is not that the works are fake. No one in this Gospel ever disputes that the works happen. The charge concerns the *source*: by the prince of demons he casts out the demons. And the first answer is pure structure: a power warring on itself is a power ending. *How can Satan cast out Satan?* The corpus argues the unity of the Source by the same instrument, from order to singleness: *law kāna fīhimā ālihatun illā Allāhu la-fasadatā*, had there been in the heavens and the earth gods other than Allah ﷻ, both would have gone to ruin (Q 21:22). A divided sovereignty cannot produce a standing order; the standing of the order is the proof of the undivided hand. Mark's Messiah applies the theorem downward to the adversary's kingdom, and the corpus applies it upward to the throne, and it is one theorem.

Then the strong man. *No one can enter a strong man's house and plunder his goods, unless he first binds the strong man.* The word is *ischyros*, and the count that began with the forerunner's *ischyroteros* now reads: strength, two. The forerunner announced one mightier; here the mightier one describes his own work as the binding of the strong. The exorcisms are not negotiations, they are the evidence that the binder has already entered the house. The corpus's cosmology of the same event is the barring of the listeners: the satans *are struck from every side, repelled* (Q 37:8-9), pursued by the piercing lamp when they steal a hearing (Q 15:17-18, Q 72:8-9). Both books describe the same military fact from two theatres: the spirit-realm's freedom of movement has been curtailed by a stronger hand, and the plundering of its house, the freeing of the possessed, is proceeding.

Then the sentence that has frightened readers for twenty centuries, and the fixed position is the one place from which its logic is plain. *All sins will be forgiven the children of man, and whatever blasphemies they utter, but whoever blasphemes against the Holy Spirit never has forgiveness.* One sin unforgivable, everything else open to pardon: that is the exact architecture of the corpus's own mercy-clause, *inna Allāha lā yaghfiru an yushraka bihi wa-yaghfiru mā dūna dhālika li-man yashāʾ*, Allah ﷻ does not forgive that partners be set beside Him, and He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills (Q 4:48). One exception, total amnesty available beneath it. And the two exceptions are the same act read from two sides. Mark names the act by its direction here: the scribes watched the Spirit of Allah ﷻ work and filed the work under the unclean, attribution of the Holy to the demonic. Shirk is the same misfiling reversed: attribution of what is His alone to another. Both are root-level misattribution, the corruption of the ledger itself, and both books mark that one corruption as the sin the system cannot process, because it is not a sin *within* the ledger but a falsification *of* the ledger. Mark even supplies the referent-line lest anyone read the warning as mystery: *for they were saying, he has an unclean spirit*. The eternal sin is not a mysterious trapdoor. It is calling the Spirit unclean while looking straight at His work, and its Quranic twin is calling a creature the Creator while looking straight at creation.

### The Family at the Door · Mark 3:31-35

> And his mother and his brothers came, and standing outside they sent to him and called him. And a crowd was sitting around him, and they said to him: Your mother and your brothers are outside, seeking you. And he answered them: Who are my mother and my brothers? And looking about at those who sat around him, he said: Here are my mother and my brothers. For whoever does the will of God, he is my brother and sister and mother.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The interrupted story resumes: the family that set out to seize him arrives and stands *outside*, *exō*, the word Mark will use in the next chapter for those to whom the mystery is not given. The seating is the sermon: blood at the door, listeners in the circle.

And the answer redefines kinship by a criterion, not an insult. *Whoever does the will of God, he is my brother and sister and mother.* The corpus performed this exact redefinition first, in its severest scene, on a prophet's own son at the height of the flood: *innahu laysa min ahlik, innahu ʿamalun ghayru ṣāliḥ*, he is not of your family; he is unrighteous conduct (Q 11:46). The Arabic is more radical than any translation: the son is not merely *doing* unrighteous deeds, he *is* an unrighteous deed; conduct has replaced substance in the definition of *ahl*, family. Membership in a prophet's house is constituted by deed, and forfeited by deed, and blood neither confers nor protects it. The corpus runs the doctrine in both directions to close it: the wives of Nūḥ AS and of Lūṭ AS, inside two prophetic households and lost, and the wife of Firʿawn, inside the enemy's own house and saved, with Maryam AS set beside her (Q 66:10-12). Mark's sentence at the door of the crowded house is Q 11:46 spoken gently: the will of God draws the family line, and it runs through the room, not the bloodline.

And note what the sentence does *not* do, because the restraint is the second half of the teaching. It does not disown; it enlarges. The mother standing outside this scene, the corpus honours above the women of the worlds (Q 3:42), and this Gospel will place her at the cross when the circle has fled. The criterion that admits the crowd does not expel her; it tells her, and everyone, on what ground all standing rests. Nothing in either book lets nearness to the prophet substitute for obedience to the One who sent him, and the corpus says that to its own community about its own Prophet ﷺ in the plainest terms: on the Day the ties will be as if cut, *fa-lā ansāba baynahum yawmaʾidhin*, no kinship among them that day (Q 23:101). The intercalation closes with its two halves interpreting each other: the charge from Jerusalem showed discernment corrupted at the root, and the scene at the door shows kinship reordered at the root, and both corrections cut in the same direction, toward the will of God as the only load-bearing tie.

### The Sower · Mark 4:1-9

> Again he began to teach beside the sea. And a very large crowd gathered about him, so that he got into a boat and sat in it on the sea, and the whole crowd was beside the sea on the land. And he was teaching them many things in parables, and in his teaching he said to them: Listen. Behold, a sower went out to sow. And as he sowed, some seed fell along the path, and the birds came and devoured it. Other seed fell on rocky ground, where it did not have much soil, and immediately it sprang up, since it had no depth of soil. And when the sun rose, it was scorched, and since it had no root, it withered away. Other seed fell among thorns, and the thorns grew up and choked it, and it yielded no grain. And other seeds fell into good soil and produced grain, growing up and increasing and yielding thirtyfold and sixtyfold and a hundredfold. And he said: Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The teacher sits in the boat, the congregation stands on the shore, and the first word of the first parable is the first word of the corpus's own creed-sentence: *Listen*, *akouete*, the shore-side *shemaʿ*. Then one sower, one seed, four grounds, and the whole variance of outcome located in the receiving soil. Nothing is wrong with the sower's arm and nothing is wrong with the seed; the parable is a doctrine of reception. The corpus carries the same doctrine in the same image: *wa-l-baladu al-ṭayyibu yakhruju nabātuhu bi-idhni rabbih, wa-lladhī khabutha lā yakhruju illā nakidā*, and the good land, its vegetation comes forth by the permission of its Lord, and that which is bad brings forth nothing but scantily (Q 7:58), with the verse's own closing note that this is how the signs are turned about *li-qawmin yashkurūn*, for a people who give thanks. Even the yield-ladder has its corpus twin: the good soil's thirty, sixty, hundredfold is the grain of Q 2:261 that grows seven ears with a hundred grains in each ear, *wa-Allāhu yuḍāʿifu li-man yashāʾ*, and Allah ﷻ multiplies for whom He wills. Same seed, same soils, same arithmetic of grace: the increase is never the ground's achievement, and always the Giver's multiplication.

### Why Parables · Mark 4:10-12

> And when he was alone, those around him with the twelve asked him about the parables.
