And he said to them: To you has been given the mystery of the kingdom of God, but for those outside everything is in parables, so that they may indeed see but not perceive, and may indeed hear but not understand, lest they should turn and be forgiven.

#### Commentary based on Quran

This is the hardest saying in the chapter and among the hardest in the Gospel, and the commentary will not soften what Mark wrote. The purpose-clause is real: *hina*, so that, seeing without perceiving, hearing without understanding. The words are Isaiah's commission (Isaiah 6:9-10), and Mark quotes them as the standing explanation of the parable-form itself.

The fixed position does not need to blunt this, because the corpus carries the same doctrine in the same grammar and supplies the piece that makes it just. *Wa-jaʿalnā ʿalā qulūbihim akinnatan an yafqahūhu wa-fī ādhānihim waqrā*, and We have placed over their hearts coverings, lest they understand it, and in their ears a heaviness (Q 17:46): the veiling is His act, stated with the same purpose-syntax, *an yafqahūhu*, lest they understand. And the justice of it is in the sequence the corpus states at its front door: *inna alladhīna kafarū sawāʾun ʿalayhim... khatama Allāhu ʿalā qulūbihim*, those who have refused, it is the same to them whether you warn them or not, *then* Allah ﷻ has set a seal upon their hearts (Q 2:6-7). The refusal precedes the seal; the seal ratifies a choice already made and persisted in. The veiling is judicial, not arbitrary.

And now look at where Mark placed this teaching, because the placement is the theodicy. The parable-veiling is announced immediately after the Beelzebul scene, in which the watchers of Israel looked at the Spirit's work and pronounced it unclean. The capital misattribution comes first; the parables follow. Mark's sequence is Q 2:6-7's sequence enacted in narrative: first the settled refusal, publicly filed, then the form of teaching that veils exactly those who have veiled themselves. *Those outside*, *tois exō*, is not a class created by the parables; it is the class the previous chapter watched constitute itself, standing at the door of its own accord. To the circle, the mystery *has been given*, *dedotai*, one more divine passive in the reverent convention, gift-grammar; to the outside, story-form, which preserves the truth in a sealed container they may yet choose to open, for the parable that veils also keeps, and whoever turns finds the meaning intact where it was left. Both books hold the door's handle on the inside of the hearer.

### The Sower Explained · Mark 4:13-20

> And he said to them: Do you not understand this parable? How then will you understand all the parables? The sower sows the word. And these are the ones along the path, where the word is sown: when they hear, Satan immediately comes and takes away the word that is sown in them. And these are the ones sown on rocky ground: the ones who, when they hear the word, immediately receive it with joy. And they have no root in themselves, but endure for a while; then, when tribulation or persecution arises on account of the word, immediately they fall away. And others are the ones sown among thorns. They are those who hear the word, but the cares of the age and the deceitfulness of riches and the desires for other things enter in and choke the word, and it proves unfruitful. But those that were sown on the good soil are the ones who hear the word and accept it and bear fruit, thirtyfold and sixtyfold and a hundredfold.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The decoding names the seed as *the word* and the four grounds as four careers of hearing, and the third ground is where the two texts touch item for item. What chokes the word: *the cares of the age, the deceitfulness of riches, and the desires for other things*. Now the corpus's inventory of the same choking growth: *know that the life of this world is play and diversion and adornment and boasting among you and rivalry in wealth and children*, and the verse continues, in the same breath, into a seed-parable of its own: *like rain whose vegetation delights the tillers; then it dries and you see it turn yellow; then it becomes chaff* (Q 57:20). The same chapter of the corpus that lists the thorns grows the withering crop beside them. Mark's thorn-list and Q 57:20's world-list are one inventory in two languages, and both books plant it inside the same agricultural warning, green growth without endurance, delight without yield. The deceit of riches, *hē apatē tou ploutou*, is the corpus's *matāʿu al-ghurūr*, the enjoyment of delusion, which is Q 57:20's own closing phrase. The parable and its Quranic twin end on the same word: deceit.

### The Lamp That Comes · Mark 4:21-25

> And he said to them: Does the lamp come to be put under a basket, or under a bed, and not on a stand? For nothing is hidden except to be made manifest; nor is anything secret except to come to light. If anyone has ears to hear, let him hear. And he said to them: Pay attention to what you hear. With the measure you use, it will be measured to you, and still more will be added to you. For to the one who has, more will be given, and from the one who has not, even what he has will be taken away.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The Greek of the first question is stranger than any translation shows, and the strangeness is the doctrine. *Mēti erchetai ho lychnos*: does the lamp *come*? Lamps do not come; lamps are brought. Mark writes the lamp as a subject in motion, an arriving thing, a *sent* thing, and the grammarians have noticed the oddity for centuries. The fixed position reads it without strain, because the corpus has a verse that assigns every term of this image its rank. *Allāhu nūru al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ*, Allah ﷻ is the Light of the heavens and the earth; *mathalu nūrihi ka-mishkātin fīhā miṣbāḥ*, the likeness of His light is as a niche in which is a lamp (Q 24:35). The Light, unqualified, is His and is Him; the lamp, *miṣbāḥ*, belongs to the *likeness* of the light, the bearer-image within the similitude, glass and niche and blessed oil around it. A lamp is not the light's source. It is where the light is set so that it reaches the house. And a lamp that *comes* is a lamp on assignment: kindled elsewhere, arriving here, placed on the stand for the sake of the room. Mark's odd verb and the corpus's careful simile are the same discipline: the prophet is luminous the way a lamp is luminous, derivatively, positionally, sent into the room and set up high, and the Light itself stays where Q 24:35 put it, above every likeness. The hiddenness-clause then gives the lamp its schedule: *nothing is hidden except to be made manifest*. The concealments this Gospel has been counting, the silencings, the private explanations, are not the story's destination; they are its staging. The veil is temporary by design, and the corpus's word for the Day is simply *yawma tublā al-sarāʾir*, the day the secrets are tested and laid bare (Q 86:9).

Then the measure. *With the measure you use, it will be measured to you, and still more will be added.* The reciprocity is law and the surplus is grace, and the corpus writes both clauses in its own arithmetic: *man jāʾa bi-l-ḥasanati fa-lahu ʿashru amthālihā*, whoever brings a good deed shall have ten like it (Q 6:160), and the ladder runs on up through seven hundred, *wa-Allāhu yuḍāʿifu li-man yashāʾ* (Q 2:261). Measure for measure is justice; the *added more* is the Giver exceeding the scale He Himself set, and both books refuse to let the arithmetic of return close at par. The severe closing line, that the one who has not loses even what he has, is the rocky ground restated as economics: a gift unrooted is a gift in departure, and the corpus's *fa-lammā zāghū azāgha Allāhu qulūbahum*, when they deviated, Allah ﷻ made their hearts deviate (Q 61:5), is the same compounding, downward.

### The Seed That Grows of Itself · Mark 4:26-29

> And he said: The kingdom of God is as if a man should scatter seed on the ground. He sleeps and rises night and day, and the seed sprouts and grows; he knows not how. The earth produces by itself, first the blade, then the ear, then the full grain in the ear. But when the grain is ripe, at once he puts in the sickle, because the harvest has come.

#### Commentary based on Quran

This parable is Mark's alone; neither of the Gospels that copied him kept it, and it is the most Quranic paragraph in the book. A man scatters seed and then does the two least impressive things a man can do, he sleeps and he rises, and the growth proceeds *he knows not how*, *hōs ouk oiden autos*, and the earth produces *automatē*, of itself, with no further agency from the farmer at all. The corpus asks the question to which this parable is the answer, and asks it as a challenge: *a-fa-raʾaytum mā taḥruthūn, a-antum tazraʿūnahu am naḥnu al-zāriʿūn*, have you considered what you till? Is it you who make it grow, or are We the Grower? (Q 56:63-64). Mark's farmer, asleep while the blade rises, is the corpus's answer drawn as a cartoon: not you. The *automatē* is not a claim that the earth is autonomous; it is the narrative removal of the human middle so that the real Agency stands alone behind the process, the same divine passive this Gospel has been writing in its healings, now written across a field.

And the corpus binds this exact image to the resurrection, which is where the death-register of this commentary first opens. *Iʿlamū anna Allāha yuḥyī al-arḍa baʿda mawtihā*, know that Allah ﷻ gives life to the earth after its death (Q 57:17), the seed-cycle commanded as a thing to *know*, because it is the annual public rehearsal of the raising: *wa-aḥyaynā bihi baldatan maytan, kadhālika al-khurūj*, and We give life thereby to a dead land; thus is the coming-forth (Q 50:11). The sleeping farmer and the self-raising grain are both books' primer on the power the Sadducees will deny in chapter twelve, and Mark has quietly stocked the answer eight chapters before the question. Then the sickle, *because the harvest has come*: Joel's sickle (Joel 3:13), the appointed cutting at the appointed ripeness, the corpus's *ajal* closing the parable the way it closed the proclamation at Mk 1:15.

### The Mustard Grain · Mark 4:30-32

> And he said: With what can we compare the kingdom of God, or what parable shall we use for it? It is like a grain of mustard seed, which, when sown on the ground, is the smallest of all the seeds on earth, yet when it is sown it grows up and becomes larger than all the garden plants and puts out large branches, so that the birds of the air can make nests in its shade.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The corpus holds the same seed in its hand. *Yā bunayya innahā in taku mithqāla ḥabbatin min khardalin fa-takun fī ṣakhratin aw fī al-samāwāti aw fī al-arḍi yaʾti bihā Allāh*, O my son, if it be but the weight of a grain of mustard, and it be in a rock or in the heavens or in the earth, Allah ﷻ will bring it forth (Q 31:16), and the same mustard-weight calibrates the scales of the Day at Q 21:47, where no soul is wronged by so much as it. The two books reach for the identical seed to make adjoining points: Mark, that no beginning is too small for the kingdom's full growth; the corpus, that no deed is too small for the King's full accounting; and beneath both, the shared premise that smallness is invisible only to creatures. The great shrub with the nesting birds is the old promise-image of the kingdom-tree sheltering the nations, Ezekiel's cedar sprig grown until *birds of every sort* nest in its shade (Ezekiel 17:22-23), and the corpus draws the same botany of the good word: *kalimatan ṭayyibatan ka-shajaratin ṭayyibah*, a good word is like a good tree, its root firm and its branches in the sky, giving its fruit in every season by the permission of its Lord (Q 14:24-25). Small sowing, vast shelter, permission all the way up the trunk.

### As They Were Able to Hear · Mark 4:33-34

> With many such parables he spoke the word to them, as they were able to hear it. He did not speak to them without a parable, but privately to his own disciples he explained everything.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The chapter closes on the teaching-method's mercy-clause: *kathōs ēdynanto akouein*, as they were able to hear. The dosage is calibrated to capacity, which is the corpus's stated law of every burden: *lā yukallifu Allāhu nafsan illā wusʿahā*, Allah ﷻ does not task a soul beyond its capacity (Q 2:286). And the private explaining, *epelyen*, resolves the chapter's tension honestly: the veiling of Mk 4:12 was never the whole policy; alongside it runs the unveiling, to those in the circle, of *everything*. Concealment for the self-sealed, explication for the given: the corpus's pair is the sealing of Q 2:7 alongside the promise of Q 75:19, *thumma inna ʿalaynā bayānah*, then upon Us is its explication. Both books run the two channels at once, and both put the explication on the Teacher's side of the ledger, not the pupil's.

The movement closes with the field sown. The Twelve stand appointed on the corpus's own precedent of twelve. The one unforgivable sin has been identified in both books as the same root-misattribution. The family line has been redrawn through deed, as Q 11:46 drew it. And four seed-parables have relocated every agency that matters, growth, yield, timing, scale, away from the human middle and upward, which is the single direction this Gospel has pointed since the heavens tore. The next movement takes the boat out onto the water, where the question of the book is finally asked aloud: who then is this?

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** One deposit. A single seed in four soils, a single word given to hearers who differ, with the differentiator never once located in what was sown.

**What moves.** The growth, *automatē*, of itself, proceeding through the farmer's sleep and his rising and known by him at no stage, first the blade, then the ear, then the full grain.

**How they bind.** The receiving condition. The soil determines nothing about the seed and everything about the yield, and its inversion is the one unforgivable act: a discriminator that calls the clean thing unclean has broken the very faculty by which it would return.

Collapse the seed into the soil and the word varies by audience, which the parable forecloses by using one seed. Collapse the growth into the sower and the kingdom is a work rather than a gift, which Q 56:63-64 refuses by question. Collapse the receiving condition and the four outcomes have no explanation and the Beelzebul charge has no mechanism. ⟀ GOL on the reception structure.

## The Crossings · Mark 4:35-6:6

Four scenes across and around the lake: a storm, a legion, a woman inside a raising, and a hometown that cannot receive. The movement is built on boundaries, sea and shore, clean and unclean, death and sleep, honour and offence, and at every boundary the same question surfaces in a new mouth: who, then, is this? Mark asks it and declines to answer it in his own voice; the two texts together answer it more precisely than either community's later shorthand.

:::box What to Watch
**A man sleeps in the stern** of a boat owned by the One of whom Q 2:255 says neither slumber nor sleep seizes Him.

**The sea is muzzled** with the exorcism's own verb, under the same conferred authority.

**Power goes out of him** and he turns to find where, and credits the healing to the woman's trust.

**At Nazareth he *could not*,** which Matthew converts to *did not*, and which is the permission-structure showing its underside.
:::
### The Storm Muzzled · Mark 4:35-41

> On that day, when evening had come, he said to them: Let us go across to the other side. And leaving the crowd, they took him with them in the boat, just as he was. And other boats were with him. And a great windstorm arose, and the waves were breaking into the boat, so that the boat was already filling. But he was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. And they woke him and said to him: Teacher, do you not care that we are perishing? And he awoke and rebuked the wind and said to the sea: Peace. Be muzzled. And the wind ceased, and there was a great calm. He said to them: Why are you so afraid? Have you still no faith? And they were filled with great fear and said to one another: Who then is this, that even the wind and the sea obey him?

#### Commentary based on Quran

Two details anchor the whole scene, and they pull in opposite directions on purpose.

The first is the sleep. Mark alone furnishes the cushion, *proskephalaion*, the steersman's pillow in the stern, and on it the exhausted teacher sleeps through a storm that is filling the boat. The corpus has a verse whose entire work is to mark the line this detail draws: *Allāhu lā ilāha illā huwa al-Ḥayyu al-Qayyūm, lā taʾkhudhuhu sinatun wa-lā nawm*, Allah ﷻ, there is no god but He, the Living, the Self-Subsisting; neither slumber seizes Him nor sleep (Q 2:255). Sleep is the creature's signature; the Sustainer of the boat and the sea does not do it. A sleeping man in the stern is, by the Throne Verse's own criterion, on the near side of the line, and Mark paints the sleep in before he paints the power, so that the reader must hold both.

The second is the muzzle. He rebukes the wind and says to the sea, *siōpa, pephimōso*, be silent, be muzzled, and *pephimōso* is the identical verb he used on the unclean spirit in the Capernaum synagogue (Mk 1:25). The sea is addressed with the exorcism's vocabulary, under the same *exousia*, in the same direction: delegated command exercised over an unruly subject. And the corpus states whose subjection the sea stands under as standing law: *Allāhu alladhī sakhkhara lakumu al-baḥra li-tajriya al-fulku fīhi bi-amrih*, Allah ﷻ is the one who subjected the sea for you, that the ships may run upon it by His command (Q 45:12). The sea is *musakhkhar*, already subjected, held on His leash; a word of command spoken with His warrant pulls the leash, and the great calm follows. Psalm 107 had sung the same circuit generations earlier, they cried to the LORD and *He* stilled the storm to a whisper, and the disciples in the boat, who knew that psalm, ask the question the psalm forces: *who then is this, that even the wind and the sea obey him?* Mark leaves their question standing, and the two texts read together answer it without strain: this is the servant through whom the Owner of the sea is exercising the Owner's calm, the sleeping conduit of a power that does not sleep. The corpus even supplies the scene's missing sermon, spoken to sailors: *and when harm touches you at sea, those you call upon besides Him vanish; and when He delivers you to the land, you turn away* (Q 17:67). The disciples' great fear after the calm is the correct fear, and it is fear of the One the calm disclosed.

And the rebuke that matters most is the one aimed at the crew: *why are you so afraid? Have you still no faith?* The storm was never the examination; the sleep was. Whether they could trust the sent one to be carried by the Sender, boat and all, was the question, and the corpus sets the same examination as a general syllabus: *wa-la-nabluwannakum bi-shayʾin mina al-khawfi*, and We will surely test you with something of fear (Q 2:155). The storm passes; the syllabus stands.

### The Legion and the Herd · Mark 5:1-20

> They came to the other side of the sea, to the country of the Gerasenes. And when Jesus had stepped out of the boat, immediately there met him out of the tombs a man with an unclean spirit. He lived among the tombs. And no one could bind him anymore, not even with a chain, for he had often been bound with shackles and chains, but he wrenched the chains apart, and he broke the shackles in pieces. No one had the strength to subdue him. Night and day among the tombs and on the mountains he was always crying out and cutting himself with stones. And when he saw Jesus from afar, he ran and fell down before him. And crying out with a loud voice, he said: What have you to do with me, Jesus, Son of the Most High God? I adjure you by God, do not torment me. For he was saying to him: Come out of the man, you unclean spirit. And Jesus asked him: What is your name? He replied: My name is Legion, for we are many.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The far shore is Gentile ground, and the first figure on it is the book's extreme case: a man the community could not hold. Mark's sentence for it carries the thread: *oudeis ischyen auton damasai*, no one had the strength to subdue him. Strength, three. The teaching of Mk 3:27 is now enacted on a beach: here is the strong man's house in a single body, chains snapped, shackles ground to pieces, and the binder of the strong steps off the boat and the unbindable falls at his feet before a word is spoken. The corpus keeps the image of the fettered rebellious in the same vocabulary, *muqarranīna fī al-aṣfād*, bound together in shackles (Q 14:49, Q 38:38): what human chains could not do to this man, the arriving authority does without chains at all.

And the spirit's own speech is a small seminar in rank. *I adjure you by God*: *horkizō se ton theon*. The demon swears the healer by God, invoking the higher Name against the one tormenting him, and the grammar of the oath concedes the whole structure this commentary has been tracing: even the unclean know where the top is, and it is not the man in front of them, or the oath would be nonsense. The corpus's satans have the same forced theology, *fa-bi-ʿizzatika*, then by Your might, Iblīs swears by the Lord's own honour in the act of rebelling (Q 38:82). Rebellion in both books is conducted in the vocabulary of the throne it rebels against, because there is no other vocabulary.

> And he begged him earnestly not to send them out of the country. Now a great herd of pigs was feeding there on the hillside, and they begged him: Send us to the pigs; let us enter them. So he gave them permission. And the unclean spirits came out and entered the pigs; and the herd, numbering about two thousand, rushed down the steep bank into the sea and drowned in the sea. The herdsmen fled and told it in the city and in the country. And people came to see what it was that had happened. And they came to Jesus and saw the demon-possessed man sitting there, clothed and in his right mind, the man who had had the legion, and they were afraid. And those who had seen it described to them what had happened to the demon-possessed man and to the pigs. And they began to beg Jesus to depart from their region. And as he was getting into the boat, the man who had been possessed with demons begged him that he might be with him. And he did not permit him but said to him: Go home to your friends and tell them how much the Lord has done for you, and how he has had mercy on you. And he went away and began to proclaim in the Decapolis how much Jesus had done for him, and everyone marveled.

Even the spirits' exit runs on the book's one grammar: they *beg*, and *he gave them permission*, *epetrepsen autois*. Permission, the corpus's *idhn*, governs the traffic of the unseen in both directions, and the drowned herd is the region's ledger made visible: two thousand pigs against one man restored, and the region does its arithmetic and asks the restorer to leave. The healed man asks the opposite, *to be with him*, the very purpose-clause of the Twelve's appointment, and is refused it, and the refusal is a commission: *go home to your friends and tell them how much the Lord has done for you*.

Mark the wording, because the silencing policy has just inverted, and the inversion proves the policy's logic. In Galilee, where a wrong messianic politics stood ready to ignite, testimony was muzzled. Here in the Decapolis, Gentile ground with no such tinder, the healed man is *ordered* to proclaim, and the content he is given is attribution upward: what *the Lord*, *ho kyrios*, has done for you, how *He* has had mercy. The commission's grammar sends the glory past the healer to the Source, exactly as the crowd's doxology did at Mk 2:12. And then Mark, honest to a fault, records what the man actually proclaimed: *how much Jesus had done for him*. The instruction said the Lord; the proclamation said Jesus; and the narrator preserves the slippage without smoothing it, the first field-report in the book of how attribution drifts one link down the chain in transmission. Six centuries later the corpus would build its sternest fences at exactly the joint where this one sentence shows the timber already creeping. The first commissioned preacher of this Gospel is a Gentile ex-demoniac, and his first sermon already needed the fence.

### The Woman Inside the Raising · Mark 5:21-43

> And when Jesus had crossed again in the boat to the other side, a great crowd gathered about him, and he was beside the sea. Then came one of the rulers of the synagogue, Jairus by name, and seeing him, he fell at his feet and implored him earnestly: My little daughter is at the point of death. Come and lay your hands on her, so that she may be made well and live. And he went with him. And a great crowd followed him and thronged about him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The book's second interruption opens, and it is the most deliberate. Intercalation, two. A father's errand is suspended for twelve verses while a woman's twelve-year affliction is healed, and Mark stitches the two halves with a needle he lets the reader see: the woman has bled *twelve years*, the girl is *twelve years old*. One number, two lives, and it is the span of the girl's whole existence set against the span of the woman's whole suffering. The insertion is not a delay. It is a lens.

> And there was a woman who had had a discharge of blood for twelve years, and who had suffered much under many physicians, and had spent all that she had, and was no better but rather grew worse. She had heard the reports about Jesus and came up behind him in the crowd and touched his garment. For she said: If I touch even his garments, I will be made well. And immediately the flow of blood dried up, and she felt in her body that she was healed of her disease.

Twelve years unclean under Leviticus, cut off from the sanctuary and from touch, her money gone to physicians who made her worse. She approaches from behind, in a crowd, silently, and her whole theology is one conditional: *if I touch even his garments, I will be made well*. The verb is passive again, *sōthēsomai*, I shall be saved, agent unstated. She does not say he will heal her. She says healing will come, and she reaches for the nearest edge of the channel it comes through.

> And Jesus, perceiving in himself that power had gone out from him, immediately turned about in the crowd and said: Who touched my garments? And his disciples said to him: You see the crowd pressing around you, and yet you say, Who touched me? And he looked around to see who had done it. But the woman, knowing what had happened to her, came in fear and trembling and fell down before him and told him the whole truth. And he said to her: Daughter, your faith has made you well; go in peace, and be healed of your disease.

Mark writes this exchange with a precision the later tradition found awkward and this reading finds decisive. *Perceiving in himself that power had gone out from him*: *epignous tēn ex autou dynamin exelthousan*. Power went *out* of him, and he perceives the outflow, and then he asks a question, twice, and looks around to find the answer. The disciples' reply is nearly mocking, and the narrator lets it stand. Whatever is happening in this scene, it is not a possessor of unlimited knowledge staging a teaching moment. It is a conduit registering a withdrawal and turning to find where it went. The corpus's grammar for its own prophets fits this scene without a seam: *lā aqūlu lakum ʿindī khazāʾinu Allāh wa-lā aʿlamu al-ghayb*, I do not say to you that with me are the treasuries of Allah ﷻ, nor do I know the unseen (Q 6:50), and everything given is given *bi-idhn*, by permission, on the occasion. The power is real, it flows, and it is not inventory the channel holds and audits.

And the verdict he pronounces removes himself from the transaction entirely: *your faith has made you well*, *hē pistis sou sesōken se*. Not *I have healed you*. The healing is credited to her trust, and her trust reached upward through the garment's hem. She had grasped, in a crowd, the one available edge of the rope, and the corpus's name for that grasp is *al-ʿurwa al-wuthqā*, the most trustworthy handhold that does not break, given to whoever entrusts himself to Allah ﷻ (Q 2:256, Q 31:22). She is called *daughter*, spoken to publicly, and sent *in peace*: the untouchable is restored to touch, to speech, and to the congregation, in one sentence.

> While he was still speaking, there came from the ruler's house some who said: Your daughter is dead. Why trouble the Teacher any further? But overhearing what they said, Jesus said to the ruler of the synagogue: Do not fear, only believe. And he allowed no one to follow him except Peter and James and John the brother of James. They came to the house of the ruler, and Jesus saw a commotion, people weeping and wailing loudly. And when he had entered, he said to them: Why are you making a commotion and weeping? The child is not dead but sleeping. And they laughed at him. But he put them all outside and took the child's father and mother and those who were with him and went in where the child was. Taking her by the hand he said to her: Talitha koum, which means: Little girl, I say to you, arise. And immediately the girl got up and began walking; for she was twelve years of age. And they were overcome with amazement. And he strictly charged them that no one should know this, and told them to give her something to eat.

The interruption has cost the father his daughter, and the sentence he receives is the sentence the crowd received in the boat: *do not fear, only believe*. The same examination, run on a father instead of a crew.

Then the clause the mourners laughed at: *the child is not dead but sleeping*, *ou apethanen alla katheudei*. The mockery is Mark's guarantee that the girl was, by every observer's judgment, dead; and the correction is not a medical claim but a re-typing of the state, and the corpus supplies the same re-typing as a general doctrine of the two conditions: *Allāhu yatawaffā al-anfusa ḥīna mawtihā wa-llatī lam tamut fī manāmihā, fa-yumsiku allatī qaḍā ʿalayhā al-mawta wa-yursilu al-ukhrā ilā ajalin musammā*, Allah ﷻ takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that have not died, in their sleep; then He withholds those upon whom He has decreed death and releases the others until an appointed term (Q 39:42). Sleep and death are one administration with two settings, and the holding and the releasing are His verbs. In that verse's light the sentence over the girl is exact rather than euphemistic: this soul is on the released setting, until a term appointed elsewhere. The corpus's sleepers of the cave lie under the same word for centuries and are called awakened, *baʿathnāhum*, when the term expires (Q 18:12).

*Talitha koum.* The Aramaic is kept and glossed: retention, one, and the pattern this commentary flagged in Part III begins its count here. The evangelist writes for Greek readers and still preserves the two words the child actually heard, at the moment of address, and translates them in an aside. And the verb of the raising is *egeire*, arise, whose passive *ēgerthē* will be spoken by the young man at the tomb in the last chapter. Mark rehearses his final sentence on a twelve-year-old in a back room, and then, with the household in uproar, has the raiser order silence, five, and add the most ordinary instruction in the Gospel: *give her something to eat*. The sign is sealed and the child is fed. Both books end their miracles the same way, in the register of daily provision: the corpus's Maryam AS, at the height of her own impossible sign, is told to shake the palm and eat and drink and cool her eye (Q 19:25-26).

### Nazareth, and What Could Not Be Done · Mark 6:1-6

> He went away from there and came to his hometown, and his disciples followed him. And on the Sabbath he began to teach in the synagogue, and many who heard him were astonished, saying: Where did this man get these things? What is the wisdom given to him? How are such mighty works done by his hands? Is not this the carpenter, the son of Mary and brother of James and Joses and Judas and Simon? And are not his sisters here with us? And they took offense at him. And Jesus said to them: A prophet is not without honor, except in his hometown and among his relatives and in his own household. And he could do no mighty work there, except that he laid his hands on a few sick people and healed them. And he marveled because of their unbelief. And he went about among the villages teaching.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Three things in six verses, and every one of them is a sentence a later hand would have written differently.

The townspeople's own question contains the doctrine they are rejecting. *What is the wisdom given to him?* *Hē sophia hē dotheisa toutō*: given, passive, by the unnamed Giver. Even the scoffers phrase it correctly. And *how are such mighty works done by his hands*, again passive, the works *done* through the hands rather than generated by them. Mark puts the whole permission-structure of Q 3:49 into the mouths of the men refusing it, which is precisely how the corpus records the refusals of every town: the mechanism is understood well enough to be resented.

*Is not this the carpenter, the son of Mary?* A man in that culture is named for his father; this one is named for his mother, in his own village, by neighbours who have known him since childhood. Mark does not explain it, and the two Gospels that copy him both alter it, one to *the carpenter's son*, the other to *Joseph's son*. The earliest text names the mother, and the corpus names him the same way, always and without exception: *ʿĪsā ibn Maryam*, in every one of its references, the matronymic carried as his standing title. Two scriptures, one naming convention, and between them a village's raised eyebrow preserved without comment.

Then the line that has embarrassed commentators for eighteen centuries. *And he could do no mighty work there*: *ouk edynato ekei oudemian dynamin poiēsai*. Could not. Matthew, copying, writes *did not do many mighty works there*, converting an inability into a decision, and the smoothing is watchable in parallel columns. Mark's *could not* is the harder reading and therefore the original, and from the fixed position it is not an embarrassment at all but the permission-structure showing its underside. If every work is *bi-idhni Allāh*, by the permission of Allah ﷻ, granted per occasion, then where the occasion is refused the permission does not issue, and the channel stands dry. The corpus states the same conditionality of every messenger in the same grammar: *wa-mā kāna li-rasūlin an yaʾtiya bi-āyatin illā bi-idhni Allāh*, it was not for any messenger to bring a sign except by the permission of Allah ﷻ (Q 40:78, and Q 13:38). A prophet who *could not* is a prophet whose ability was never his own inventory, and Mark's sentence is the cleanest single proof in the Gospel that this is the kind of power the book is describing.

And the last clause completes it: *he marveled because of their unbelief*, *ethaumasen*. He is surprised. Surprise requires not having known, and the corpus's own Prophet ﷺ is commanded to say *mā adrī mā yufʿalu bī wa-lā bikum*, I do not know what will be done with me or with you (Q 46:9). The Gospel and the corpus keep the same creaturely interior in their prophets, and neither treats it as a deficiency to be managed.

The movement closes on that dry channel. Across the water the strong man was bound without chains and the freed man's first sermon already drifted one link down the chain of attribution. In the boat the sleeping conduit stilled a sea that never sleeps. In the crowd a woman's grasp drew power the conduit felt leaving him and credited to her faith. In the back room a death was re-typed as sleep in the corpus's own administration of souls, and the Aramaic of the raising was kept. And in his own village the permission did not issue, and the earliest evangelist wrote that down.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** A creation already under command. The sea is *musakhkhar* before anyone speaks to it, so its obedience is a fact about the sea and not only about the speaker.

**What moves.** The word spoken into it, and the power that leaves him and that he turns around to trace, and the exit of the legion into a herd. Real expenditures, one of them measured by the one paying it.

**How they bind.** The permission, and the movement supplies its own control. The spirits ask and *he gave them permission*; the sea is stilled under the *bi-amrih* of Q 45:12; and at Nazareth, where the anchor on the receiving side is cut, the permission does not issue and Mark writes *ouk edynato*, he could not.

Collapse the subjection and every stilling is a seizure of nature. Collapse the outgoing power and the healings are declarations rather than costs. Collapse the permission and the hometown scene is inexplicable, because a possessed power does not fail where it is least welcome. ⟀ GOL on the subjection-and-permission structure, with Nazareth as its negative exhibit.

## The Bread · Mark 6:7-8:26

The longest movement of the Galilean half, and its subject is provision: who sends, who supplies, who eats, and who cannot see that he has already been fed. Mark builds it around two feedings and hangs between them a beheading, a walk on water, a quarrel about washing, a Gentile mother's argument, and a deaf man's tongue loosed. It ends with a blind man healed in two stages, which is the movement's own self-portrait.

:::box What to Watch
**The mission kit** strips stored provision and leaves walking gear: Q 65:3's *He is sufficient for him*, enacted.

**Herod's oath** is the cage the corpus legislates a door out of, at the price of ten meals (Q 5:89).

**Both feedings** open with the eyes going up, and the corpus preserves the prayer that was said when they went up (Q 5:114).

**The sign is refused in the passive,** because the signs are *ʿinda Allāh*, with Him.
:::
### The Twelve Sent · Mark 6:7-13

> And he called the twelve and began to send them out two by two, and gave them authority over the unclean spirits. He charged them to take nothing for their journey except a staff, no bread, no bag, no money in their belts, but to wear sandals and not put on two tunics. And he said to them: Whenever you enter a house, stay there until you depart from there. And if any place will not receive you and they will not listen to you, when you leave, shake off the dust that is on your feet as a testimony against them. So they went out and proclaimed that people should repent. And they cast out many demons and anointed with oil many who were sick and healed them.

#### Commentary based on Quran

*Two by two*, *dyo dyo*: the pairing is the corpus's own prescription for standing up in truth, *an taqūmū lillāhi mathnā wa-furādā*, that you stand for Allah ﷻ in twos and singly (Q 34:46), and the second half of Mark's sentence explains why the first half is possible at all: *he gave them authority*, *edidou autois exousian*. Delegated warrant, handed down one further level, and the whole chain now visible in a single verse, the Sender above, the sent one, the sent ones' sent ones, each carrying only what was given.

The kit is a study in dependence. No bread, no bag, no coin in the belt, one tunic, and, in Mark alone, a staff and sandals permitted. The two Gospels that copy him forbid both, which tells us the permission was original and felt too lax to keep. What the list strips is stored provision; what it leaves is walking gear. The corpus writes the same posture as a promise rather than a rule: *wa-man yattaqi Allāha yajʿal lahu makhrajan wa-yarzuqhu min ḥaythu lā yaḥtasib, wa-man yatawakkal ʿalā Allāhi fa-huwa ḥasbuh*, whoever is mindful of Allah ﷻ, He makes for him a way out and provides for him from where he does not reckon; and whoever entrusts himself to Allah ﷻ, He is sufficient for him (Q 65:2-3). Travel without stores is the enacted form of *ḥasbuh*, He is enough. And the sandals are the one item of this kit that crossed every tradition intact, the same footwear the forerunner declared himself unworthy to untie in chapter one: transit, full, one.

The dust shaken from the feet is a legal gesture, not a curse: it terminates responsibility and leaves the record with the refusers. The corpus gives the messengers the identical closing formula, *fa-in tawallaw fa-innamā ʿalayka al-balāgh*, if they turn away, upon you is only the delivery (Q 3:20, Q 16:82). Delivery made, receipt refused, ledger closed, and the messenger walks on with nothing added to his pack and nothing owed.

### The Death of the Forerunner · Mark 6:14-29

> King Herod heard of it, for Jesus' name had become known. Some said: John the Baptist has been raised from the dead. That is why these miraculous powers are at work in him. But others said: He is Elijah. And others said: He is a prophet, like one of the prophets of old. But when Herod heard of it, he said: John, whom I beheaded, has been raised.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The Twelve go out at verse thirteen and return at verse thirty, and between the sending and the return Mark inserts the killing of the attester. Intercalation, three, and the deepest in the book: the mission of the messengers is framed by the fate of a messenger, so that no reader can hear *they went out and proclaimed* without also hearing what proclamation costs.

The chapter opens on three confident identifications, and Mark ratifies none of them. John raised, Elijah returned, a prophet like the old ones. This commentary noted in Part I that the corpus closes the merger of the two clean files with a single verse, *muṣaddiqan bi-kalimatin min Allāh* (Q 3:39), because a confirmer and the confirmed cannot be one person. Here the collapse is staged by a guilty king, who adds the beheading himself so no reader misses that he knows better, and it is left standing as rumour. The reader who carries the two-place office already holds the correction the narrative declines to supply.

> For it was Herod who had sent and seized John and bound him in prison for the sake of Herodias, his brother Philip's wife, because he had married her. For John had been saying to Herod: It is not lawful for you to have your brother's wife. And Herodias had a grudge against him and wanted to put him to death. But she could not, for Herod feared John, knowing that he was a righteous and holy man, and he kept him safe. When he heard him, he was greatly perplexed, and yet he heard him gladly.

The portrait of the king is the finest piece of characterisation in Mark, and it is a portrait of a man ruled by what other people think of him. He fears the prophet, protects him, listens to him gladly, and is perplexed by him, and none of it survives contact with a dinner party. What John said was a plain application of the law, *it is not lawful*, and the corpus's Yaḥyā AS file has exactly that steel in it: *wa-ātaynāhu al-ḥukma ṣabiyyā*, We gave him judgment while a boy (Q 19:12), and *lam yakun jabbāran ʿaṣiyyā*, he was not a tyrant, not disobedient (Q 19:14). A man who is not a tyrant tells a tyrant the law, and the tyrant, in this scene, half wants to hear it.

> But an opportunity came when Herod on his birthday gave a banquet for his nobles and military commanders and the leading men of Galilee. For when Herodias's daughter came in and danced, she pleased Herod and his guests. And the king said to the girl: Ask me for whatever you wish, and I will give it to you. And he vowed to her: Whatever you ask me, I will give you, up to half of my kingdom. And she went out and said to her mother: For what should I ask? And she said: The head of John the Baptist. And she came in immediately with haste to the king and asked, saying: I want you to give me at once the head of John the Baptist on a platter. And the king was exceedingly sorry, but because of his oaths and his guests he did not want to break his word to her. And immediately the king sent an executioner with orders to bring John's head. He went and beheaded him in the prison and brought his head on a platter and gave it to the girl, and the girl gave it to her mother. When his disciples heard of it, they came and took his body and laid it in a tomb.

*Up to half of my kingdom* is Ahasuerus's offer to Esther, word for word from the Greek of that book, the banquet, the pleased king, the open-ended oath, the request routed through a queen. In Esther the machinery saves a people; here the same machinery kills a prophet. The image crossed into this scene and was inverted: transit, partial, and the ledger records it as a borrowed frame with a reversed outcome, which is itself a comment on how much depends on who is standing behind the girl.

Then the hinge of the whole passage: *because of his oaths and his guests*. Two chains, and Mark names them in that order. The king is exceedingly sorry, *perilypos*, and kills anyway, because he has sworn in front of men whose opinion he cannot bear to lose. And here the corpus does something worth stating plainly: it legislates the exit Herod could not find. *Lā yuʾākhidhukumu Allāhu bi-l-laghwi fī aymānikum wa-lākin yuʾākhidhukum bimā ʿaqqadtumu al-aymān, fa-kaffāratuhu iṭʿāmu ʿasharati masākīn*, Allah ﷻ does not take you to task for what is thoughtless in your oaths, but He takes you to task for the oaths you have deliberately sworn; its expiation is feeding ten needy persons (Q 5:89). An oath sworn to a wrong outcome is not a cage; it is a debt with a published price, ten meals or a fast, and the verse ends *dhālika kaffāratu aymānikum idhā ḥalaftum*, that is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn. The corpus's own Prophet ﷺ was corrected in exactly this territory, told *lima tuḥarrimu mā aḥalla Allāhu lak*, why do you forbid yourself what Allah ﷻ has made lawful, and given the same release, *qad faraḍa Allāhu lakum taḥillata aymānikum* (Q 66:1-2). Herod had no such door, or believed he had none, and a man who thinks his word to a room is stronger than a life will always find the room heavier. The corpus's diagnosis is the shorter one: *wa-takhshā al-nāsa wa-Allāhu aḥaqqu an takhshāh*, and you feared people, when Allah ﷻ is worthier of being feared (Q 33:37). Herod feared the guests.

And Mark closes with the disciples coming for the body and laying it in a tomb, a sentence he will need again, in almost the same words, when a councillor asks for another body and lays it in another tomb. The forerunner goes ahead of his Messiah in death as he did in preaching, and the corpus's word over him covers both: *wa-salāmun ʿalayhi yawma wulida wa-yawma yamūtu wa-yawma yubʿathu ḥayyā*, peace be upon him the day he was born, the day he dies, and the day he is raised alive (Q 19:15). The peace was already spoken over this ending six centuries before the platter, and the same threefold peace is spoken by ʿĪsā AS over himself eighteen verses later in the same surah.

### The Five Thousand · Mark 6:30-44

> The apostles returned to Jesus and told him all that they had done and taught. And he said to them: Come away by yourselves to a desolate place and rest a while. For many were coming and going, and they had no leisure even to eat. And they went away in the boat to a desolate place by themselves. Now many saw them going and recognized them, and they ran there on foot from all the towns and got there ahead of them. When he went ashore he saw a great crowd, and he had compassion on them, because they were like sheep without a shepherd. And he began to teach them many things.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The intercalation closes: the messengers come back from their mission into the shadow of the murdered messenger, and the first thing said to them is *come away and rest*. The care is practical, they have not had time to eat, and the crowd defeats it by running around the lake on foot.

*Like sheep without a shepherd* is Moses's own phrase, asking that Israel not be left leaderless (Numbers 27:17), and the shepherd-image is one this Gospel will pick up again at the arrest, when the sheep scatter. The image reaches the corpus only in part: the corpus keeps the pastoral care as *raʾfa* and *raḥma* placed in the hearts of his followers (Q 57:27) and keeps the flock-language for the Day, but it does not build the shepherd-title as a Christological office. Transit, partial, and the ledger notes the reason: an office-title is exactly the kind of thing the corpus's fences are careful about, while the care itself passes through untouched. And note what the compassion produces here: *he began to teach them many things*. Hungry crowd, remote place, and the first ration issued is instruction. The corpus ranks the two provisions in the same order, teaching before bread, in the four verses this commentary set at the head of Sūrat al-Raḥmān: *ʿallama al-Qurʾān* stands at verse two and *khalaqa al-insān* at verse three.

> And when it grew late, his disciples came to him and said: This is a desolate place, and the hour is now late. Send them away to go into the surrounding countryside and villages and buy themselves something to eat. But he answered them: You give them something to eat. And they said to him: Shall we go and buy two hundred denarii worth of bread and give it to them to eat? And he said to them: How many loaves do you have? Go and see. And when they had found out, they said: Five, and two fish. Then he commanded them all to sit down in groups on the green grass. So they sat down in groups, by hundreds and by fifties. And taking the five loaves and the two fish, he looked up to heaven and said a blessing and broke the loaves and gave them to the disciples to set before the people. And he divided the two fish among them all. And they all ate and were satisfied. And they took up twelve baskets full of broken pieces and of the fish. And those who ate the loaves were five thousand men.

*You give them something to eat*: *dote autois hymeis phagein*, the pronoun emphatic. The disciples' answer is a budget objection, and the counter-question is an inventory question, *how many loaves do you have? Go and see*. The provision begins with a count of what is already in hand, which is the corpus's own method with Mūsā AS at the burning bush, *wa-mā tilka bi-yamīnika yā Mūsā*, and what is that in your right hand, O Mūsā (Q 20:17), a staff, and the staff is what the miracle is made of. Neither book conjures from nothing in these scenes; both start from the little already given.

Then the gesture on which the whole passage turns. *He looked up to heaven and said a blessing*, *anablepsas eis ton ouranon eulogēsen*. The eyes go up before the bread is broken. In a scene later readers would treat as the supreme display of the miracle-worker's own power, the miracle-worker is looking upward and blessing the One above, and Mark records the direction rather than the words. This is the pattern the Finding in Brief named as running the length of the book, and here it stands in its clearest single frame: authority received, acknowledged upward, then distributed downward through the hands of servants, for it is the disciples who set the bread before the people.

And the corpus has the scene, told from the other side. *Qāla ʿĪsā ibnu Maryama: Allāhumma rabbanā anzil ʿalaynā māʾidatan mina al-samāʾi takūnu lanā ʿīdan li-awwalinā wa-ākhirinā wa-āyatan minka, wa-rzuqnā wa-anta khayru al-rāziqīn*, ʿĪsā son of Maryam said: O Allah ﷻ, our Lord, send down to us a table from heaven, to be for us a festival for the first of us and the last of us, and a sign from You, and provide for us, for You are the best of providers (Q 5:114). The corpus's account of the miraculous meal is a *prayer*, in his own mouth, addressed upward, asking the Provider to send and asking Him to provide, and the answer comes from Allah ﷻ: *innī munazziluhā ʿalaykum*, I will send it down to you (Q 5:115). Set the two accounts side by side and they are one event described from its two ends. Mark, from below, sees the man look up and bless; the corpus, from above, records what was said when he looked and Who answered. The Gospel's *anablepsas* and the corpus's *Allāhumma rabbanā* are the same upward turn. And the twelve baskets of surplus are the corpus's *rizq* in its usual excess, the same arithmetic as the multiplied grain: what is asked of the Provider returns more than was set down.

The corpus attaches a warning to its table, that whoever disbelieves after it will be punished as none other (Q 5:115), and the honest note is that Mark attaches no such clause; what he attaches instead is the disciples' failure to understand the loaves, twice, which is the same theme in a lower key. The bread was given, and the ones who carried the baskets did not read what they were carrying.

### The Walking on the Sea · Mark 6:45-52

> Immediately he made his disciples get into the boat and go before him to the other side, to Bethsaida, while he dismissed the crowd. And after he had taken leave of them, he went up on the mountain to pray. And when evening came, the boat was out on the sea, and he was alone on the land. And he saw that they were making headway painfully, for the wind was against them. And about the fourth watch of the night he came to them, walking on the sea. He meant to pass by them, but when they saw him walking on the sea they thought it was a ghost, and cried out, for they all saw him and were terrified. But immediately he spoke to them and said: Take heart; it is I. Do not be afraid. And he got into the boat with them, and the wind ceased. And they were utterly astounded, for they did not understand about the loaves, but their hearts were hardened.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Between the feeding and the water Mark puts a mountain and a prayer, alone, at night, again. The pattern of Mk 1:35 recurs at the height of public success: the greater the crowd, the more deliberate the withdrawal upward. Whatever crossed the water at the fourth watch had spent the evening on a hillside praying.

Then the strangest clause in the chapter: *he meant to pass by them*, *ēthelen parelthein autous*. Readers have found it inexplicable, and the Greek Old Testament supplies its home. When Mūsā AS asks to see, the Lord sets him in the cleft and says *I will cover you with my hand until I have passed by* (Exodus 33:22); when Elijah stands at the cave, *behold, the LORD passed by* (1 Kings 19:11). *Passing by* is the technical form of a self-disclosure to a prophet on a mountain. Mark uses the form, and then declines it: they cry out, he speaks, and *he got into the boat with them*. The passing-by is aborted for a boarding. Whatever the scene was arranged to display, its ending is a man climbing into a boat with frightened fishermen and the wind dropping. The corpus's own account of a disclosure requested is the refusal at Sinai, *lan tarānī*, you shall not see Me (Q 7:143), and what is granted instead is manifestation to the mountain and the prophet's fall. Both books stage the approach and both withhold the seeing, and Mark's withholding takes the form of a servant getting in and sitting down.

*Take heart; it is I. Do not be afraid.* The Greek is *egō eimi*, which is both the ordinary way to say *it is me* in the dark and the words of the burning bush in the Septuagint. Readers have long divided over which is heard here. The commentary states both and lets the scene decide: the clause sits between *take heart* and *do not be afraid*, which is the standard formula of reassurance to a terrified party, and the terror it answers is the fear of a *phantasma*, a ghost. The sentence's work in its own context is identification, not proclamation, and the corpus's angels say the equivalent in every annunciation, *lā takhaf*, do not fear, *innā bushshirnāka*, we bring you good news (Q 15:53, Q 51:28). The higher reading is available to those who bring it; the sentence itself does no more than name the walker and calm the boat, and this reading declines to make a creed out of a reassurance.

And Mark's closing diagnosis is aimed at the disciples, not the sea: *they did not understand about the loaves, but their hearts were hardened*, *pepōrōmenē*. The failure is not that they doubted a man could walk on water; it is that they had just carried twelve baskets of surplus and had not read them. The corpus's word for the same organ in the same condition is *qasat qulūbukum* (Q 2:74), and its word for the failure to read given provision is the one it uses of every ungrateful witness: the signs were shown, the eyes passed over them. The men in this boat have seen the sign and eaten it, and Mark says the hardening is theirs, inside the circle, not the crowd's.

### Gennesaret · Mark 6:53-56

> When they had crossed over, they came to land at Gennesaret and moored to the shore. And when they got out of the boat, the people immediately recognized him and ran about the whole region and began to bring the sick people on their beds to wherever they heard he was. And wherever he came, in villages, cities, or countryside, they laid the sick in the marketplaces and implored him that they might touch even the fringe of his garment. And as many as touched it were made well.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Four verses of summary, and one word in them is worth stopping for. The sick ask to touch *the fringe of his garment*, *tou kraspedou tou himatiou autou*, and *kraspedon* is the Greek Old Testament's word for the tassel the Torah commands on the corners of a garment, the *ṣīṣīt* of Numbers 15:38, worn as a reminder to keep the commandments. The earliest Gospel dresses its subject in the tassels of the law, in passing, without comment, in a sentence about crowds. He is an observant man in observant clothing, and the corpus says of him exactly that: *muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayya mina al-Tawrāt*, confirming what was before me of the Torah (Q 3:50). The detail is small and it is load-bearing, because the next passage is a quarrel about the law, and Mark has just shown, in his own quiet way, which side of that quarrel his subject dresses on.

The rest of the summary is the woman of chapter five multiplied: the same reaching for the hem, the same *esōzonto*, they were made well, in the passive with the Agent unnamed. What one woman found by grasping, a region now practises openly.

### Corban, and the Commandment Set Aside · Mark 7:1-13

> Now when the Pharisees gathered to him, with some of the scribes who had come from Jerusalem, they saw that some of his disciples ate with hands that were defiled, that is, unwashed. For the Pharisees and all the Jews do not eat unless they wash their hands, holding to the tradition of the elders, and when they come from the marketplace, they do not eat unless they wash. And there are many other traditions that they observe. And the Pharisees and the scribes asked him: Why do your disciples not walk according to the tradition of the elders, but eat with defiled hands? And he said to them: Well did Isaiah prophesy of you hypocrites, as it is written: This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me; in vain do they worship me, teaching as doctrines the commandments of men. You leave the commandment of God and hold to the tradition of men.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The charge is precise and it is not a charge against the law. The disciples are accused of breaking *the tradition of the elders*, *tēn paradosin tōn presbyterōn*, and Mark, writing for readers outside the community, explains the custom in an aside because they would not know it. The answer separates two things that had been welded together: *the commandment of God*, *tēn entolēn tou theou*, and *the tradition of men*, *tēn paradosin tōn anthrōpōn*. Isaiah's line supplies the diagnosis, *teaching as doctrines the commandments of men*, and the whole of the corpus's critique of religious inheritance stands in that phrase.

The corpus makes the same cut and names the mechanism by which the human accretion acquires divine weight. *Ittakhadhū aḥbārahum wa-ruhbānahum arbāban min dūni Allāh*, they took their scholars and their monks as lords besides Allah ﷻ (Q 9:31), and the classical explanation, inside that tradition, is that no one worshipped a rabbi or a monk; the lordship consisted in accepting their permissions and prohibitions as binding where Allah ﷻ had not bound. That is Mk 7:8 stated as a doctrine of *shirk* in legislation, and it is the sharper of the two formulations because it names what the elevation of tradition actually is.

And the corpus goes further, to the specific device: *mā jaʿala Allāhu min baḥīratin wa-lā sāʾibatin wa-lā waṣīlatin wa-lā ḥām, wa-lākinna alladhīna kafarū yaftarūna ʿalā Allāhi al-kadhib*, Allah ﷻ has not instituted the slit-eared camel, nor the freed camel, nor the twin-bearing ewe, nor the stallion released; but those who refuse invent falsehood about Allah ﷻ (Q 5:103). Four named categories of animal set aside by human vow and thereby withdrawn from ordinary use, invented dedications treated as sacred obligations. The corpus's list and Mark's next paragraph describe the identical device.

> He said to them: You have a fine way of rejecting the commandment of God in order to establish your tradition! For Moses said: Honor your father and your mother; and: Whoever reviles father or mother must surely die. But you say: If a man tells his father or his mother, Whatever you would have gained from me is Corban (that is, given to God), then you no longer permit him to do anything for his father or mother, thus making void the word of God by your tradition that you have handed down. And many such things you do.

*Corban*, and Mark glosses the Aramaic for his Greek readers: *qorbān*, an offering, from the root of drawing near. The corpus uses the same word twice, at Q 3:183 and Q 5:27, in both places for a genuine offering brought to Allah ﷻ, and the second is the offering of Adam's two sons, where one is accepted and one is not, with the reason given: *innamā yataqabbalu Allāhu mina al-muttaqīn*, Allah ﷻ accepts only from those who guard themselves. So the word itself is clean in both scriptures. What Mark exposes is its use as an instrument: a man declares his assets dedicated, the dedication outranks the duty, and the parents go without. A vow has been used to cancel a commandment.

*Making void the word of God by your tradition*: *akyrountes ton logon tou theou*. And the corpus's parallel is Q 5:103's *yaftarūna ʿalā Allāhi al-kadhib*, inventing a falsehood about Allah ﷻ, which is what any human dedication becomes the moment it is treated as binding at the divine level. Both texts locate the corruption in the same place, not in the desire to give to God, but in the ranking that lets a self-imposed vow overrule an imposed duty.

And the duty in question is the one the corpus places immediately after the command to worship none but Him: *wa-qaḍā rabbuka allā taʿbudū illā iyyāhu wa-bi-l-wālidayni iḥsānā*, and your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him, and to parents good conduct (Q 17:23), with the verse continuing into *lā taqul lahumā uff*, do not say to them a word of contempt. Mark's Messiah defends the fifth commandment against a pious device, and the corpus wires the same commandment directly into the first. On this passage the two texts are not merely compatible; they are running one argument, and the corpus supplies both the name of the error and the case law.

### What Defiles · Mark 7:14-23

> And he called the people to him again and said to them: Hear me, all of you, and understand: There is nothing outside a person that by going into him can defile him, but the things that come out of a person are what defile him. And when he had entered the house and left the people, his disciples asked him about the parable. And he said to them: Then are you also without understanding? Do you not see that whatever goes into a person from outside cannot defile him, since it enters not his heart but his stomach, and is expelled? And he said: What comes out of a person is what defiles him. For from within, out of the heart of man, come evil thoughts, sexual immorality, theft, murder, adultery, coveting, wickedness, deceit, sensuality, envy, slander, pride, foolishness. All these evil things come from within, and they defile a person.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The spoken teaching is a relocation of defilement from the surface to the interior, and every clause of it is at home in the corpus. The heart is the organ of accountability, *wa-lākin yuʾākhidhukum bimā kasabat qulūbukum*, He takes you to task for what your hearts have earned (Q 2:225); it is the heart that is sealed, hardened, softened, or made sound, and the only thing a man brings on the Day that counts is *qalbin salīm*, a sound heart (Q 26:89). The vice-list itself could be lifted into the corpus without a seam: *ithm*, *fawāḥish*, *baghy*, *ghībah*, *ḥasad*, *kibr*, each of the thirteen has its Arabic counterpart in a corpus list, and the corpus's own summary sentence is Q 7:33, *say: my Lord has forbidden only the shameful deeds, what is apparent of them and what is concealed, and sin, and unjust aggression*, with the concealed named first because that is where the trouble starts.

Two things need saying honestly about the passage, and the commentary says them in the open.

The first is that the relocation of defilement to the heart is not, in either scripture, the abolition of exterior law. The corpus keeps a short list of forbidden foods, *innamā ḥarrama ʿalaykumu al-maytata wa-l-dama wa-laḥma al-khinzīr* (Q 2:173, Q 5:3), while insisting that what reaches Allah ﷻ is never the substance but the state, which is the same two-level structure the Torah keeps and the same one Mark's spoken teaching assumes: he is arguing against *unwashed hands*, a tradition of the elders, not against Leviticus, and the word he uses of the food is that it *goes into the stomach and is expelled*, an observation about digestion, not a repeal.

The second is the clause that is not spoken. After the digestion sentence Mark adds, in his own voice, three words that no one in the story says: *katharizōn panta ta brōmata*, cleansing all foods. It is a narrator's aside, of the same kind as his explanations of Jewish washing and his glosses of Aramaic, and this commentary reports it as what it is: the evangelist's comment on the implication, written for a mixed community in which the food question was live, and not a sentence placed in the mouth of his subject. The distinction matters and it is checkable in the Greek: the clause is a loose participle, and the two Gospels that copy Mark drop it. Where the corpus keeps a food-law and the aside of Mk 7:19 declares foods cleansed, the two texts stand at different places, and the commentary names the divergence rather than dissolving it, and names its layer precisely: the divergence is between the corpus and the *narrator's aside*, while the spoken teaching, on the heart as the seat of defilement, is corpus doctrine entire. Both books put the weight in the same organ; they differ on what happens to the shorter exterior list, and one of the two places that difference in an editorial parenthesis rather than in the teacher's mouth.

### The Syrophoenician Mother · Mark 7:24-30

> And from there he arose and went away to the region of Tyre. And he entered a house and did not want anyone to know, yet he could not be hidden. But immediately a woman whose little daughter had an unclean spirit heard of him and came and fell down at his feet. Now the woman was a Gentile, a Syrophoenician by birth. And she begged him to cast the demon out of her daughter. And he said to her: Let the children be fed first, for it is not right to take the children's bread and throw it to the dogs. But she answered him: Yes, Lord; yet even the dogs under the table eat the children's crumbs. And he said to her: For this statement you may go your way; the demon has left your daughter. And she went home and found the child lying in bed and the demon gone.

#### Commentary based on Quran

This is the only exchange in the Gospel that its subject loses, and he awards the victory himself.

The saying he offers is a household picture with an order of service in it, *let the children be fed first*, *prōton*, and the diminutive he uses is not the street-dog of insult but *kynaria*, the little dogs under the table, animals of the house. Even so, the sentence is hard, and Mark does not soften it. It states a sequence, Israel first, and the woman does not dispute the sequence. She works inside it: if the children are being fed at the table, then crumbs are falling now, and crumbs are not the children's portion taken away. She has accepted the priority and located a surplus, which is exactly what the twelve baskets in the previous chapter proved exists.

And the reply is the most remarkable clause in the movement: *dia touton ton logon*, because of this word, go your way. Not *because of your faith*, which is what he says to everyone else, but because of what she *said*. A Gentile woman argues, and the argument is granted, and the healing is done at a distance without a touch, a look, or a formula. The corpus makes the granting of the petition a standing rule rather than an exception, and states it in the one verse where the messenger is removed from the grammar: *wa-idhā saʾalaka ʿibādī ʿannī fa-innī qarīb, ujību daʿwata al-dāʿi idhā daʿān*, and when My servants ask you about Me, I am near; I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me (Q 2:186). The caller here is a foreign mother with no standing in the covenant, and the answer arrives. The corpus's word over the class she belongs to is *wa-mā arsalnāka illā raḥmatan li-l-ʿālamīn*, We sent you only as a mercy to the worlds (Q 21:107), and its rule for who may call is the widest in the book: *udʿūnī astajib lakum*, call upon Me and I will answer you (Q 40:60), with no ethnicity attached.

Two further things this scene settles quietly. The mission's sequence is real in both scriptures, a messenger is sent first to his own people, *li-tundhira qawman mā undhira ābāʾuhum* (Q 36:6), and the sequence is not a wall, since the corpus's own Prophet ﷺ is sent to the worlds. And the woman's *yes, Lord* is the only place in Mark where anyone calls him *kyrie* to his face and is answered with a granted request, and she does it while conceding the lower place. The book's whole doctrine of received authority has its mirror here in received mercy: it comes to the one who asks from below, and her lowness, like the forerunner's at the sandal-thong, is the position from which the granting is made.

### Ephphatha · Mark 7:31-37

> Then he returned from the region of Tyre and went through Sidon to the Sea of Galilee, in the region of the Decapolis. And they brought to him a man who was deaf and had a speech impediment, and they begged him to lay his hand on him. And taking him aside from the crowd privately, he put his fingers into his ears, and after spitting touched his tongue. And looking up to heaven, he sighed and said to him: Ephphatha, that is, Be opened. And his ears were opened, his tongue was released, and he spoke plainly. And Jesus charged them to tell no one. But the more he charged them, the more zealously they proclaimed it. And they were astonished beyond measure, saying: He has done all things well. He even makes the deaf hear and the mute speak.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The healing is the most physical in the book and the most carefully staged. The man is taken *aside from the crowd*, away from spectators. Fingers into the ears, spittle on the tongue, and then, before a word is spoken, the gesture that has now become this Gospel's signature: *anablepsas eis ton ouranon*, looking up to heaven. He looked up before he broke the bread, and he looks up before he opens the ears. The direction is fixed and Mark keeps recording it.

Then *estenaxen*, he sighed, or groaned. A groan is effort or grief, and it belongs to a creature; the corpus's picture of the same office keeps that register exactly, the messenger who is *ḥarīṣun ʿalaykum*, anxious over them (Q 9:128), and who is told not to destroy himself with grief over those who refuse (Q 18:6, Q 35:8). The healer sighs on the way to the healing, and neither book finds that beneath the office.

And the charge follows the healing, *tell no one*, silencing, six, and it is disobeyed the more he presses it, exactly as the leper disobeyed it.

*Ephphatha*, and Mark glosses it: Aramaic retention, two. The pattern holds, the source language kept at the moment of address, and the Greek supplied in an aside for readers who need it.

The spittle needs its count begun here, because it will matter at the end of the book. *Ptysas*, having spat: spittle, one. It appears next at Bethsaida, where the same substance opens eyes; it is predicted at the third passion announcement, *they will spit on him*; and it is enacted twice in the passion itself, in the sanhedrin's courtyard and in the praetorium. Five occurrences of one fluid: twice as the medium of healing, once as prophecy, twice as humiliation. The servant who spat to open a deaf man's ears is spat upon by men who will not hear, and Mark planted the vocabulary here so that the last two instances would land on a prepared surface.

The crowd's verdict is the closing line and it is a quotation. *He has done all things well*, *kalōs panta pepoiēken*, is the Greek Old Testament's refrain over creation, *God saw everything that he had made, and behold, it was very good* (Genesis 1:31), and the pairing *the deaf hear and the mute speak* is Isaiah's sign-list for the coming deliverance (Isaiah 35:5-6), where the Greek uses the same rare word for the speech-impediment that Mark uses here. The crowd reaches for creation-language and prophecy-language in one breath. And the corpus, listing the works of this same prophet, keeps every item of that list and hangs the permission on each: *and I heal the blind and the leper, and I give life to the dead, by the permission of Allah ﷻ* (Q 3:49). The crowd says *he has done all things well*; the corpus says by whose leave; and the Gospel itself has just shown the healer looking up before he did it.

### The Four Thousand · Mark 8:1-10

> In those days, when again a great crowd had gathered, and they had nothing to eat, he called his disciples to him and said to them: I have compassion on the crowd, because they have been with me now three days and have nothing to eat. And if I send them away hungry to their homes, they will faint on the way. And some of them have come from far away. And his disciples answered him: How can one feed these people with bread here in this desolate place? And he asked them: How many loaves do you have? They said: Seven. And he directed the crowd to sit down on the ground. And he took the seven loaves, and having given thanks, he broke them and gave them to his disciples to set before the people; and they set them before the crowd. And they had a few small fish. And having blessed them, he said that these also should be set before them. And they ate and were satisfied. And they took up the broken pieces left over, seven baskets full. And there were about four thousand people. And he sent them away.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The second feeding is on the far side of the lake, in Gentile country, and Mark keeps its numbers scrupulously distinct from the first: seven loaves rather than five, four thousand rather than five, and *spyridas*, seven of them, a different word for basket from the *kophinoi* of chapter six. The distinctness is the point, and the two Gospels that copy Mark keep both feedings for the same reason. He is not telling one story twice. He is establishing a record with two entries, because he is about to hold an examination on it.

The opening sentence is the whole doctrine of the passage: *I have compassion on the crowd, because they have been with me now three days and have nothing to eat*. The concern is that they will collapse on the road, and *some of them have come from far away*. Provision here is not spectacle; it is logistics under mercy, and the corpus's word for the same care is the pairing it places in the hearts of this prophet's followers, *raʾfatan wa-raḥmatan* (Q 57:27), and its standing description of the Provider, *wa-mā min dābbatin fī al-arḍi illā ʿalā Allāhi rizquhā*, there is no creature on earth but that its provision is upon Allah ﷻ (Q 11:6). Four thousand people three days from home are creatures on the earth, and the provision arrives through a servant's hands, from seven loaves, after thanks are given.

*Eucharistēsas*, having given thanks, and then *eulogēsas*, having blessed: the two verbs of the upward turn again, one for the bread and one for the fish. The pattern is now so consistent that the reader can predict it, which is precisely what the disciples in the next passage will fail to do.

### The Sign Refused · Mark 8:11-13

> The Pharisees came and began to argue with him, seeking from him a sign from heaven to test him. And he sighed deeply in his spirit and said: Why does this generation seek a sign? Truly, I say to you, no sign will be given to this generation. And he left them, got into the boat again, and went to the other side.

#### Commentary based on Quran

He has just fed four thousand people, and they ask for a sign. The request is *to test him*, *peirazontes*, the verb used of the adversary in the wilderness, and Mark records the reaction before the answer: *anastenaxas tō pneumati autou*, he groaned deeply in his spirit. The same verb as at the deaf man's healing, intensified. The groan is the servant's, and it is grief at a demand he will not meet.

*No sign will be given to this generation*: *ou dothēsetai*, a passive again, and the passive is the whole answer. He does not say *I will not give you a sign*. He says none *will be given*, which puts the withholding upstream of himself, where the giving would have had to come from. And the corpus states the same jurisdiction in a single clause, in answer to the same demand: *qul innamā al-āyātu ʿinda Allāh*, say: the signs are only with Allah ﷻ (Q 6:109, and Q 29:50, where the messenger adds *wa-innamā anā nadhīrun mubīn*, and I am only a clear warner). *ʿInda Allāh*, with Him: the preposition this commentary has been tracking since Part II, and its link-count now stands at four, the mother of the book with Him, the provision found with her, what is with Him remaining while what is with you runs out, and now the signs. The corpus adds, in the verse before, the sentence that explains the refusal rather than merely stating it: *wa-mā manaʿanā an nursila bi-l-āyāti illā an kadhdhaba bihā al-awwalūn*, nothing prevented Us from sending the signs except that the former peoples denied them (Q 17:59). A sign given to a demand made in bad faith becomes a judgment, and the mercy is in the withholding.

And this generation has already been given the signs it is asking for. The two feedings sit immediately behind this request, seven baskets of surplus still being carried.
