Mark's arrangement is the argument: the sign-demand is made by people standing in a landscape littered with signs, which is the corpus's constant diagnosis, *wa-ka-ayyin min āyatin fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi yamurrūna ʿalayhā wa-hum ʿanhā muʿriḍūn*, how many a sign in the heavens and the earth they pass by, turning away from it (Q 12:105).

### The Leaven, and the Examination · Mark 8:14-21

> Now they had forgotten to bring bread, and they had only one loaf with them in the boat. And he cautioned them, saying: Watch out; beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and the leaven of Herod. And they began discussing with one another the fact that they had no bread. And Jesus, aware of this, said to them: Why are you discussing the fact that you have no bread? Do you not yet perceive or understand? Are your hearts hardened? Having eyes do you not see, and having ears do you not hear? And do you not remember? When I broke the five loaves for the five thousand, how many baskets full of broken pieces did you take up? They said to him: Twelve. And the seven for the four thousand, how many baskets full of broken pieces did you take up? And they said to him: Seven. And he said to them: Do you not yet understand?

#### Commentary based on Quran

A warning about corruption is heard as a complaint about catering, and the misunderstanding triggers the sharpest questioning in the Gospel. Mark's aside is quietly devastating: *they had only one loaf with them in the boat*. One loaf, in a boat, with the man who has twice fed thousands from less, and they are worried.

The rebuke is a chain of five questions and its middle link is a quotation: *having eyes do you not see, and having ears do you not hear?* The words are Jeremiah's and Ezekiel's, and the corpus writes the same sentence about the same condition: *lahum qulūbun lā yafqahūna bihā, wa-lahum aʿyunun lā yubṣirūna bihā, wa-lahum ādhānun lā yasmaʿūna bihā*, they have hearts with which they do not understand, and eyes with which they do not see, and ears with which they do not hear (Q 7:179). Three organs, all present, all inoperative, in both books, in the same order.

And then the examination itself, which is unique in the Gospels and worth pausing on. He does not explain. He asks them to *count*. How many baskets, the first time? Twelve. The second? Seven. He makes them recite the inventory of what they have already received, and then asks the closing question, *do you not yet understand?*, and Mark does not record an answer. The method is the corpus's own method, in the surah that repeats one refrain thirty-one times, *fa-bi-ayyi ālāʾi rabbikumā tukadhdhibān*, then which of the favours of your Lord will you deny (Q 55), a catechism conducted entirely by enumerating what has been given. Remembering is treated in both books as the cure for anxiety and the ground of faith, and the corpus makes it an imperative addressed to the community about its own history: *wa-dhkurū niʿmata Allāhi ʿalaykum*, and remember the favour of Allah ﷻ upon you (Q 2:231, Q 3:103, Q 5:7). The disciples in the boat have the numbers. They can recite them on demand. What they cannot yet do is read them, and that is the exact condition the next healing is about.

### Bethsaida, in Two Stages · Mark 8:22-26

> And they came to Bethsaida. And some people brought to him a blind man and begged him to touch him. And he took the blind man by the hand and led him out of the village, and when he had spit on his eyes and laid his hands on him, he asked him: Do you see anything? And he looked up and said: I see men, but they look like trees, walking. Then Jesus laid his hands on his eyes again; and he opened his eyes, his sight was restored, and he saw everything clearly. And he sent him to his home, saying: Do not even enter the village.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Mark alone tells this, and it is the only healing in the four Gospels that takes two attempts. Neither of the evangelists who copy him keeps it, which is itself the usual signal that a passage was too rough to carry forward.

Read as a healing it is strange. Read where Mark placed it, immediately after men with eyes who cannot see and immediately before the confession at Caesarea Philippi, it is the hinge of the entire book. The disciples are the blind man. They are about to see, at the first attempt, something true and badly out of focus, Peter's *you are the Messiah*, followed at once by a rebuke because his idea of what that word means is trees walking. Then the long road to Jerusalem is the second touch. Mark diagnosed his own narrative with a miracle.

The intermediate report is the finest phrase in the chapter: *I see men, but they look like trees, walking*. Vision present, resolution absent, the objects confused with each other. The corpus has the exact condition and gives it a name, in the mouths of the Children of Israel about a cow: *inna al-baqara tashābaha ʿalaynā*, cattle are all alike to us (Q 2:70). *Tashābaha ʿalaynā*, they resemble one another to us, is the root the corpus uses for things that look alike and are confused, and it is the same root it uses of the verses that are *mutashābihāt*, ambiguous, which the sound of heart leave alone and the deviant chase (Q 3:7). Half-sight is not blindness and is more dangerous, because it produces confident reports. The blind man reports honestly, and gets the second touch.

And the closing instruction is the seventh silencing, *do not even enter the village*, which is the only one of the nine that removes the healed man from the crowd rather than only from speech.

Three details finish it. He is *led out of the village* by the hand, *exēnenken auton exō tēs kōmēs*: taken out before he is healed, and told at the end not to go back in. The leading-out is a motion the corpus makes the purpose of revelation itself, *kitābun anzalnāhu ilayka li-tukhrija al-nāsa mina al-ẓulumāti ilā al-nūr*, a book We have sent down to you that you may bring people out of the darknesses into the light (Q 14:1), and the same verb governs Q 2:257, where Allah ﷻ is the guardian who brings the faithful *out* of darkness. Sight is given outside the crowd, and the healed man is not sent back into it.

And the second touch is not a failure of power but a picture of how permission works in process. Q 3:49 hangs every work on *bi-idhni Allāh*, per occasion, and nothing in that grammar requires a single occasion. The corpus's own ladder-verse for the raising is a sequence, *thumma... thumma*, then and then (Q 2:28), and its account of guidance is a stage-process, *wa-yazīdu Allāhu alladhīna ihtadaw hudā*, and Allah ﷻ increases in guidance those who are guided (Q 19:76). Sight given in two stages is grace administered in the shape of a road, which is the shape the next three chapters take.

The movement closes here. Messengers were sent out with nothing and came back to the news that a messenger had been killed for saying *it is not lawful*, and the corpus's law would have released the king's oath for the price of ten meals. Thousands were fed twice by a man who looked up before he broke anything, and the corpus preserved his prayer at that table. The passing-by on the water was declined for a boarding. Tradition was separated from commandment in the words the corpus uses for scholars taken as lords. A Gentile mother argued and was answered, because He is near and answers the caller. A deaf man's ears were opened with a groan and a spitting, and the count of that fluid stands at one. A sign was refused because the signs are with Him. And the men who had carried nineteen baskets between them were asked to count, and could count, and could not read, and then Mark healed a blind man twice.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** The provision. Bread that is not counted into existence, and which the corpus states twice with the same negation: *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*, without reckoning, and *min ḥaythu lā yaḥtasib*, from where he does not calculate.

**What moves.** The breaking and the giving. He breaks the loaves down and *edidou*, kept giving, imperfect against the aorists around it, so that the increase lives in the distributing and not in a stock held before it.

**How they bind.** The upward glance. *Anablepsas eis ton ouranon*, he looked up, before a single loaf comes apart, and he never once addresses the bread. The source is named by a gesture, which is what the corpus states outright when it answers the same petition with *innī munazziluhā ʿalaykum*, I am sending it down.

Collapse the provision into the loaves and the disciples' twelve and seven are the right answer instead of a true answer in the wrong register. Collapse the breaking and the multiplication is a lump that appears and the imperfect tense is unreadable. Collapse the glance and the scene is a conjuring, which is precisely the reading both texts spend a gesture and a clause to prevent. ⟀ GOL on the fertility of the fracture.

## The Way · Mark 8:27-10:52

The centre of the book. Three times the coming suffering is announced, three times it is misheard, and three times the correction is a teaching about rank. The whole movement is set on a road, *hodos*, and it ends with a blind beggar following on that road, which is the second touch the last movement's miracle promised.

:::box What to Watch
**The confession is silenced and the suffering is published,** *parrēsia*, two verses apart.

**Peter is answered** in the words the corpus speaks to its own Prophet ﷺ: *not for you is anything of the matter*.

**On the mountain he says nothing,** two figures are removed, and one imperative is left: *listen to him*.

**The seats are not his to give,** and the preparing is done by another.
:::
### Caesarea Philippi · Mark 8:27-30

> And Jesus went on with his disciples to the villages of Caesarea Philippi. And on the way he asked his disciples: Who do people say that I am? And they told him: John the Baptist; and others say, Elijah; and others, one of the prophets. And he asked them: But who do you say that I am? Peter answered him: You are the Christ. And he strictly charged them to tell no one about him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The three public opinions are the three of chapter six, unchanged, and the reader who holds Part I already knows why the first is impossible and the second a category error. Then the question turns inward and Peter answers with the title: *sy ei ho Christos*, you are the Messiah, *al-Masīḥ*, the word the corpus confers eleven times and never withdraws. The confession is correct. It is the correct confession that gets silenced.

*He strictly charged them to tell no one about him*: *epetimēsen autois*, the rebuke-verb, silencing, eight, and the heaviest yet, because now it falls on truth spoken by the inner circle. And this is the point at which the pattern this commentary has counted since the Capernaum synagogue becomes fully legible. What is silenced is never falsehood. The demons were correct. The healed leper was grateful and correct. Peter is correct. What is managed is the *timing and the authorisation* of the announcement, in a province where the word Messiah meant an armed deliverer and would have recruited a following for the wrong thing. The corpus's parallel is not a secret but a schedule: revelation itself is released in portions, *wa-qurʾānan faraqnāhu li-taqraʾahu ʿalā al-nāsi ʿalā mukthin wa-nazzalnāhu tanzīlā*, a recitation We have divided, that you may recite it to people at intervals, and We have sent it down in successive descents (Q 17:106). Deliverance of a true message on a governed schedule is a mercy toward the receivers, in both books.

### The First Announcement, and the Rebuke · Mark 8:31-33

> And he began to teach them that the Son of Man must suffer many things and be rejected by the elders and the chief priests and the scribes and be killed, and after three days rise again. And he said this plainly. And Peter took him aside and began to rebuke him. But turning and seeing his disciples, he rebuked Peter and said: Get behind me, Satan! For you are not setting your mind on the things of God, but on the things of man.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The silence has an exact boundary and Mark marks it in the next sentence. *And he said this plainly*: *kai parrēsia ton logon elalei*. *Parrēsia*, openness of speech, occurs once in this Gospel, here, two verses after the strictest charge to say nothing. The title is embargoed; the suffering is announced openly. Whatever this book is protecting, it is not the information about the cross, which is published the moment the title is sealed. The corpus's Prophet ﷺ is given the identical instruction about his own limits, to be said in the open: *qul mā kuntu bidʿan mina al-rusuli wa-mā adrī mā yufʿalu bī wa-lā bikum*, say: I am not a novelty among the messengers, and I do not know what will be done with me or with you (Q 46:9). Both books publish the vulnerability and manage the title.

Peter, who had the title right, rebukes the man for the announcement, and receives the hardest words in the Gospel: *hypage opisō mou, Satana*. The reason given is not that Peter is wicked but that his mind is in the wrong register: *ou phroneis ta tou theou alla ta tōn anthrōpōn*, you are minding not the things of God but the things of men. The corpus draws that line with the same instrument, and draws it for its own Prophet ﷺ at the moment he most wanted a different outcome: *laysa laka mina al-amri shayʾun*, not for you is anything of the matter (Q 3:128), spoken on a battlefield when he wished to curse his enemies or see them relent, and the verse continues, *aw yatūba ʿalayhim aw yuʿadhdhibahum*, whether He turns to them or punishes them, the disposal is His. And the corpus states the general form of Peter's error as a law of human judgment: *wa-ʿasā an takrahū shayʾan wa-huwa khayrun lakum, wa-Allāhu yaʿlamu wa-antum lā taʿlamūn*, it may be that you hate a thing and it is good for you; Allah ﷻ knows and you do not (Q 2:216). Peter hates the announcement. The corpus's verse is the exact commentary.

And a word about the title that carries this announcement and every one that follows. *The Son of Man* is Mark's own preferred designation in his subject's mouth, and it is heard best in the language actually spoken: *bar enasha*, the son of the human, the human one. In Daniel's vision one like a son of man approaches the Ancient of Days and *is given* dominion, *yehiv*, a passive of conferral (Daniel 7:14): even at its most exalted the phrase names a recipient. The corpus's self-designation for its own prophets runs on the same word, *innamā anā basharun mithlukum*, I am only a human like you (Q 18:110), and its final reduction of this prophet is *in huwa illā ʿabdun anʿamnā ʿalayhi*, he was only a servant whom We favoured (Q 43:59). The announcement that begins here, that the human one must suffer, be killed, and rise, is spoken in a title whose grammar has always been the grammar of the recipient. This commentary reads it that way and notes that Mark gives no other.

### The Terms of Following · Mark 8:34-9:1

> And he called to him the crowd with his disciples and said to them: If anyone would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me. For whoever would save his life will lose it, but whoever loses his life for my sake and the gospel's will save it. For what does it profit a man to gain the whole world and forfeit his soul? For what can a man give in return for his soul? For whoever is ashamed of me and of my words in this adulterous and sinful generation, of him will the Son of Man also be ashamed when he comes in the glory of his Father with the holy angels. And he said to them: Truly, I say to you, there are some standing here who will not taste death until they see the kingdom of God after it has come with power.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The terms are three: deny yourself, take up the cross, follow. *Aparnēsasthō heauton*, let him say no to himself, and the corpus's word for the same discipline is *wa-nahā al-nafsa ʿani al-hawā*, and restrained the self from its craving, with the garden named as the consequence in the next clause (Q 79:40-41). The self-denial of the Gospel and the self-restraint of the corpus are the same operation, and both books make it the entry condition rather than a counsel for the advanced.

The economics that follow are the corpus's own. *What does it profit a man to gain the whole world and forfeit his soul?* The corpus runs that ledger constantly: *wa-mā al-ḥayātu al-dunyā illā matāʿu al-ghurūr*, the life of this world is nothing but the enjoyment of delusion (Q 3:185), and *qul matāʿu al-dunyā qalīlun wa-l-ākhiratu khayrun li-man ittaqā*, say: the enjoyment of this world is little, and the hereafter is better for the one who guards himself (Q 4:77). And the unanswerable question, *what can a man give in return for his soul?*, is the corpus's own rhetorical dead end at the Day: *fa-lan yuqbala min aḥadihim milʾu al-arḍi dhahaban wa-law iftadā bih*, an earth full of gold would not be accepted from one of them, though he offered it as ransom (Q 3:91). Both books put the same price on a soul, which is that it is not purchasable.

Then the shame-clause, and it carries a phrase this reading takes plainly. *When he comes in the glory of his Father with the holy angels*: *en tē doxē tou patros autou*. The glory is the Father's, and the coming is *in* it, which is the grammar of derived and conferred splendour, not of owned splendour. Daniel's *yehiv* again, in a Greek sentence. The corpus's picture of that Day places the majesty in one place only, *wa-jāʾa rabbuka wa-l-malaku ṣaffan ṣaffā*, and your Lord comes, and the angels rank upon rank (Q 89:22), and *al-mulku yawmaʾidhin al-ḥaqqu li-l-Raḥmān*, the sovereignty that Day, the true one, belongs to the Merciful (Q 25:26). A servant arriving in his Lord's glory with his Lord's angels is a sentence both books can carry, and neither book has to be adjusted to carry it.

The last verse is the movement's first hard saying: *there are some standing here who will not taste death until they see the kingdom of God after it has come with power*. The commentary does not paper over it. What can be said from the fixed position is that the corpus knows a category of the living-who-have-not-died-in-the-ordinary-sense and a category of sight granted before the general resurrection, and that its word for the transition is not annihilation but a taking: *kullu nafsin dhāʾiqatu al-mawt*, every soul shall taste death (Q 3:185, Q 21:35, Q 29:57), the same tasting-metaphor Mark uses, and beside it the sentence about those killed in His way, *lā taqūlū li-man yuqtalu fī sabīli Allāhi amwātun, bal aḥyāʾun wa-lākin lā tashʿurūn*, do not say of those slain in the way of Allah ﷻ that they are dead; rather they are living, but you do not perceive (Q 2:154). Death in both books is a threshold with traffic in both directions and a Doorkeeper, and *tasting* it is an event that can be timed. Whether Mark's sentence refers to the transfiguration six days later, which is where he places it, or to the fall of the temple, or to something else, the commentary does not decide, and it notes that Mark's own arrangement puts the mountain in the very next verse.

### The Mountain · Mark 9:2-8

> And after six days Jesus took with him Peter and James and John, and led them up a high mountain by themselves. And he was transfigured before them, and his clothes became radiant, intensely white, as no one on earth could bleach them. And there appeared to them Elijah with Moses, and they were talking with Jesus. And Peter said to Jesus: Rabbi, it is good that we are here. Let us make three tents, one for you and one for Moses and one for Elijah. For he did not know what to say, for they were terrified. And a cloud overshadowed them, and a voice came out of the cloud: This is my beloved Son; listen to him. And suddenly, looking around, they no longer saw anyone with them but Jesus only.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The scene is the book's summit and it is worth stating first what does not happen in it. He is *transfigured*, *metemorphōthē*, passive: the change is worked upon him. His clothes are made white beyond any earthly bleaching, which is a simile about laundry, and Mark reaches for it because he wants the whiteness understood as conferred rather than emitted. Two figures appear and *they were talking with Jesus*, and Mark reports no word of his. In the one scene where a later theology would most want a declaration, the subject of the Gospel says nothing at all. That is checkable in the text, and it is the scene's most eloquent feature.

Peter speaks instead, and proposes three tents, one apiece, and Mark supplies the note that has kept this passage honest for twenty centuries: *for he did not know what to say, for they were terrified*. The narrator marks the proposal as confusion before the correction arrives. And the correction is the whole point of the scene: a cloud, a voice, and then, *suddenly, looking around, they no longer saw anyone with them but Jesus only*. The three-way arrangement is dismantled in a single verse. The two who appeared are removed, and the redirection is total.

The voice says two things and the second is the operative one. *This is my beloved Son*, the words of the baptism, again stopping short of the Psalm's next clause. And then a command that is not in the baptism at all: *akouete autou*, listen to him. The scene ends in an imperative to the hearers, not a definition for the theologians. The corpus's own summit-scene has this shape exactly. When Mūsā AS asks to see, the Lord manifests to the mountain and the prophet falls senseless, and what he says on waking is *subḥānaka tubtu ilayk*, glory be to You, I turn to You (Q 7:143): the disclosure ends in the servant's own act of turning, not in a formula. And the corpus's arrangement of the prophetic company runs the same dismantling of any three-way tableau: it assembles the prophets in long lists and then redirects, *ulāʾika alladhīna hadā Allāhu fa-bi-hudāhumu iqtadih*, those are the ones Allah ﷻ guided, so follow their guidance (Q 6:90), the roll called and the imperative delivered.

What the corpus never does is stack figure upon figure. Its typing is message upon message: *wa-mā arsalnā min qablika min rasūlin illā nūḥī ilayhi annahu lā ilāha illā anā fa-ʿbudūn*, We sent no messenger before you but that We revealed to him that there is no god but Me, so worship Me (Q 21:25), and *sharaʿa lakum mina al-dīni mā waṣṣā bihi Nūḥan wa-lladhī awḥaynā ilayka wa-mā waṣṣaynā bihi Ibrāhīma wa-Mūsā wa-ʿĪsā, an aqīmū al-dīna wa-lā tatafarraqū fīh*, He has instituted for you of the religion what He enjoined upon Nūḥ, and what We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Ibrāhīm and Mūsā and ʿĪsā: that you establish the religion and do not divide over it (Q 42:13). Five names in one sentence, one message, and the closing warning is against splitting. The mountain in Mark performs that verse in narrative: three figures, one voice, one instruction, and then two of the three withdrawn so that the instruction has a single addressee.

And the corpus's warning about what happens after such moments deserves to sit beside this scene, because it is the reason both books put the redirection first. Division comes *after* the knowledge arrives, never before it: *wa-mā tafarraqū illā min baʿdi mā jāʾahumu al-ʿilmu baghyan baynahum*, they did not divide until after knowledge had come to them, out of rivalry among themselves (Q 42:14, and the same clause at Q 45:17 and Q 98:4, with Q 2:213 and Q 3:105 completing the set). Five times the corpus states that the splitting followed the clarity. On a high mountain, at the moment of maximum clarity, a disciple proposed three tents; the cloud came, and the arrangement was taken down.

### Coming Down · Mark 9:9-13

> And as they were coming down the mountain, he charged them to tell no one what they had seen, until the Son of Man had risen from the dead. So they kept the matter to themselves, questioning what this rising from the dead might mean. And they asked him: Why do the scribes say that first Elijah must come? And he said to them: Elijah does come first to restore all things. And how is it written of the Son of Man that he should suffer many things and be treated with contempt? But I tell you that Elijah has come, and they did to him whatever they pleased, as it is written of him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Silencing, nine, and this one carries an expiry date: *until the Son of Man had risen from the dead*. The embargo is temporary by design, exactly as the lamp of Mk 4:22 was hidden only in order to be set out.

Then Mark records something no evangelist inventing a seamless narrative would write: *they kept the matter to themselves, questioning what this rising from the dead might mean*. Three men who have just seen the mountain do not know what the phrase means. The Greek is *ek nekrōn*, from among the dead, a partitive: not *from death* as an abstract state but *out of the dead ones*, a population. And their puzzlement is honest, because the resurrection they had been taught was general and at the end; a single rising out of the middle of the dead was not a category they held. The corpus supplies both categories and keeps them distinct. The general one is the ladder of Q 2:28, *kuntum amwātan fa-aḥyākum thumma yumītukum thumma yuḥyīkum thumma ilayhi turjaʿūn*, you were dead and He gave you life, then He causes you to die, then He gives you life, then to Him you return; and Q 40:11 states the same arithmetic in the mouths of those who lived it, *rabbanā amattanā ithnatayni wa-aḥyaytanā ithnatayn*, our Lord, You made us die twice and gave us life twice. And beside the general ladder the corpus keeps particular risings: the sleepers raised after their long term, *thumma baʿathnāhum* (Q 18:12); the man who was made to die a hundred years and then raised with his food unspoiled and his donkey rebuilt before his eyes (Q 2:259); the slain man struck with part of the cow and returned to speak (Q 2:72-73). Individual raisings out of the population of the dead are a thing the corpus narrates plainly. The disciples' confusion is a fact about first-century expectation, not a gap in either scripture.

The Elijah exchange then settles the identification the crowds kept fumbling. *Elijah has come, and they did to him whatever they pleased*: the forerunner, already beheaded four chapters back. The corpus's own instrument for this is the office, not the person. It calls Yaḥyā AS *muṣaddiqan bi-kalimatin min Allāh* (Q 3:39), a confirmer standing in the appointed place, and it never transmigrates a soul; what recurs in both books is the *function*, the one sent ahead to prepare, and Mark's sentence says the function was filled and the filler was killed.

One clause in this passage is more difficult than any commentary should pretend, and the honest report is short. *How is it written of the Son of Man that he should suffer many things and be treated with contempt?* No single passage of the Hebrew scriptures says that. Daniel 7 has the exalted human figure who is *given* dominion; Isaiah 53 has the servant despised and crushed; and the welding of the two, an exalted son of man who suffers, happens in this sentence and not in any source it could be quoting. The Qumran library, which preserves the messianic expectations of the period in the community's own hand, keeps the two apart: a priestly and a royal messiah are named together (1QS 9:11), a coming figure's works are listed as healing and raising the dead (4Q521), and no securely readable text presents a *suffering* messiah, the one candidate being a fragment whose damaged line reads either way. So the scriptural warrant for a suffering Son of Man is asserted here rather than cited, and the corpus, six centuries later, is the book that declines to build that fusion at all: it keeps the servant's honour and the raising and states, at Q 4:157-158, that the ending did not run the way the plotters recorded. The commentary marks the seam, states what the manuscripts show, and does not manufacture a source that the evidence does not contain.

### The Boy at the Foot of the Mountain · Mark 9:14-29

> And when they came to the disciples, they saw a great crowd around them, and scribes arguing with them. And immediately all the crowd, when they saw him, were greatly amazed and ran up to him and greeted him. And he asked them: What are you arguing about with them? And someone from the crowd answered him: Teacher, I brought my son to you, for he has a spirit that makes him mute. And whenever it seizes him, it throws him down, and he foams and grinds his teeth and becomes rigid. So I asked your disciples to cast it out, and they were not able. And he answered them: O faithless generation, how long am I to be with you? How long am I to bear with you? Bring him to me.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The mountain descends into a shouting match. The disciples have failed publicly, the scribes are pressing the failure, and the father's sentence carries the movement's key word: *ouk ischysan*, they were not able, literally they had not the strength.

Strength, four, and with this instance the chain is complete and worth reading whole. The forerunner announced one *mightier* than himself (Mk 1:7). The teaching said no one can plunder the strong man's house unless he first binds the strong (Mk 3:27). On the far shore, *no one had the strength* to subdue the man among the tombs (Mk 5:4). Here, nine chapters in, the men who were *given* authority over unclean spirits find they have not the strength to use it. And the chain will close on Peter in a garden, *you had not the strength to watch one hour* (Mk 14:37). Read in sequence it is a single argument: strength in this Gospel is never a possession, it is a grant, and the grant is renewed per occasion or it is not there. The corpus states that as the plain condition of every messenger, *wa-mā kāna li-rasūlin an yaʾtiya bi-āyatin illā bi-idhni Allāh* (Q 40:78), and states it of the strongest creature in its own narrative when the corpus's Sulaymān AS asks for the throne and the one *with knowledge from the Book* delivers it and immediately says *hādhā min faḍli rabbī*, this is from the bounty of my Lord (Q 27:40).

> And they brought the boy to him. And when the spirit saw him, immediately it convulsed the boy, and he fell on the ground and rolled about, foaming at the mouth. And Jesus asked his father: How long has this been happening to him? And he said: From childhood. And it has often cast him into fire and into water, to destroy him. But if you can do anything, have compassion on us and help us. And Jesus said to him: If you can! All things are possible for one who believes. Immediately the father of the child cried out and said: I believe; help my unbelief!

*How long has this been happening?* He asks, and receives information. The question is not a device; the narrative has him learn the case history from the father, exactly as he learned who touched him by turning and looking. The corpus's grammar for its prophets, *wa-lā aʿlamu al-ghayb* (Q 6:50), fits the scene without adjustment.

Then the exchange that turns the whole passage. The father says *if you can do anything*, and the phrase is thrown back at him: *to ei dynē*, this *if you can!* The ability is not the variable under discussion, and the answer relocates it: *panta dynata tō pisteuonti*, all things are possible for the one who trusts. Not *for me*, but *for the one who trusts*, which moves the question from the healer's capacity to the receiver's posture, and moves the capacity itself upstream of both. The corpus performs the identical relocation, and in the mouth of this same prophet. When the disciples ask *hal yastaṭīʿu rabbuka an yunazzila ʿalaynā māʾidatan mina al-samāʾ*, is your Lord able to send down a table from heaven, the answer is not a demonstration but a correction of posture: *ittaqū Allāha in kuntum muʾminīn*, be mindful of Allah ﷻ if you are believers (Q 5:112). The ability question was the wrong question in both scenes, and both texts answer it by turning it around.

And the father's reply is one of the great sentences of the Gospel: *pisteuō; boēthei mou tē apistia*, I believe; help my unbelief. A petition made from inside an acknowledged deficiency, asking the One addressed to supply what the petitioner lacks in order to petition. The corpus's standing prayer has the same shape, *ihdinā al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*, guide us to the straight path, asked by people already on it, and *rabbi zidnī ʿilmā*, my Lord, increase me in knowledge (Q 20:114), the one increase its Prophet ﷺ is commanded to keep asking for. Faith in both books is not a stock that is either held or missing; it is a thing given, and asked for, and increased, *li-yazdādū īmānan maʿa īmānihim*, that they might increase in faith along with their faith (Q 48:4).

> And when Jesus saw that a crowd came running together, he rebuked the unclean spirit, saying to it: You mute and deaf spirit, I command you, come out of him and never enter him again. And after crying out and convulsing him terribly, it came out, and the boy was like a corpse, so that most of them said: He is dead. But Jesus took him by the hand and lifted him up, and he arose. And when he had entered the house, his disciples asked him privately: Why could we not cast it out? And he said to them: This kind cannot be driven out by anything but prayer.

The boy is left *like a corpse*, and the crowd pronounces him dead, and the lifting is the same gesture as the girl's in chapter five: *kratēsas tēs cheiros*, taking the hand, *ēgeiren*, he raised him. Mark keeps rehearsing that verb.

And the private answer is the movement's quietest and largest sentence. *This kind cannot be driven out by anything but prayer.* The earliest manuscripts read exactly that, and later copies add *and fasting*, a scribal supplement that the oldest witnesses do not carry. The unadorned original is stronger and it is the whole doctrine of this Gospel in nine words: authority that was genuinely given can be genuinely unavailable, and what re-opens it is not technique but petition. The one who has been withdrawing to pray since chapter one tells the men who failed that the missing element was the thing they had watched him do alone in the dark.

And here the corpus supplies its most precise grammatical witness, the one this commentary named at its threshold. Through Sūrat al-Baqarah a series of questions comes to the Prophet ﷺ, and every answer is routed through him by command: they ask you about the new moons, *qul*, say; they ask you about fighting in the sacred month, *qul*, say; they ask you about wine and gambling, *qul*, say; they ask you what they should spend, *qul*, say. The pattern is unbroken except once. *Wa-idhā saʾalaka ʿibādī ʿannī fa-innī qarīb, ujību daʿwata al-dāʿi idhā daʿān*, and when My servants ask you about Me, then I am near; I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me (Q 2:186). No *qul*. On the one subject that is Himself, the messenger is deleted from the sentence and the answer is given in the first person, direct. That is the structure of prayer in the corpus, stated by the absence of a single word, and it is the structure Mark's Messiah practises in the desolate place, on the mountain, in Gethsemane, and prescribes here to men who could not do what they had been empowered to do. The line runs from the servant to the Lord with nothing standing in it, and the corpus's grammar proves the vacancy.

### The Second Announcement, and the Argument on the Road · Mark 9:30-37

> They went on from there and passed through Galilee. And he did not want anyone to know, for he was teaching his disciples, saying to them: The Son of Man is going to be delivered into the hands of men, and they will kill him. And when he is killed, after three days he will rise. But they did not understand the saying, and were afraid to ask him. And they came to Capernaum. And when he was in the house he asked them: What were you discussing on the way? But they kept silent, for on the way they had argued with one another about who was the greatest. And he sat down and called the twelve. And he said to them: If anyone would be first, he must be last of all and servant of all. And he took a child and put him in the midst of them, and taking him in his arms, he said to them: Whoever receives one such child in my name receives me, and whoever receives me, receives not me but him who sent me.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The second announcement is briefer and its verb is the one that will govern the passion: *paradidotai*, is being delivered, handed over, a passive in the present tense with the deliverer unnamed. And the response is a silence with fear in it, *ephobounto auton eperōtēsai*, and then, on the road, an argument about rank. Mark places the two side by side without comment. The announcement of the handing-over and the quarrel about precedence are separated by one verse.

The correction is not a scolding but an inversion of the order they were ranking themselves in: *if anyone would be first, he must be last of all and servant of all*. *Diakonos pantōn*, and the serving-verb this Gospel has been storing since the angels in the wilderness and the woman in the kitchen now arrives in its imperative form. The corpus's own picture of the ranks it honours is built on the same inversion, *wa-ʿibādu al-Raḥmāni alladhīna yamshūna ʿalā al-arḍi hawnan*, and the servants of the Merciful are those who walk upon the earth in humility (Q 25:63), a title-list that begins with the word *servants* and describes a gait.

Then the child, and the sentence that is the clearest statement of transmission in the book. *Whoever receives me, receives not me but him who sent me*: *ouk eme dechetai alla ton aposteilanta me*. The correction is inside the sentence, *not me*, and it is the same self-effacement the corpus builds into obedience itself: *man yuṭiʿi al-rasūla fa-qad aṭāʿa Allāh*, whoever obeys the messenger has obeyed Allah ﷻ (Q 4:80). The messenger is transparent to the Sender in both books, and both books say so in one clause that first names the messenger and then moves past him. Mark's version is the more radical of the two, because it uses a negation: it corrects the reception mid-sentence. The receiving stops at nobody until it reaches the One who sent.

### The Outsider, and the Cup of Water · Mark 9:38-41

> John said to him: Teacher, we saw someone casting out demons in your name, and we tried to stop him, because he was not following us. But Jesus said: Do not stop him, for no one who does a mighty work in my name will be able soon afterward to speak evil of me. For the one who is not against us is for us. For truly, I say to you, whoever gives you a cup of water to drink because you belong to Christ will by no means lose his reward.

#### Commentary based on Quran

An unlicensed exorcist, effective, outside the circle, and the circle wants him stopped. The objection is stated with unusual candour: *because he was not following us*. Not *not following you*. The disciples have already begun to think of the mission as a franchise with themselves inside it.

*Do not stop him.* The permission-structure this commentary has tracked from the first chapter cuts both ways: if the works are done by a granted authority and not an owned one, then the grant is the Giver's to place where He wills, and no circle can fence it. The corpus states that as a rule about the Giver rather than about the recipients: *yakhtaṣṣu bi-raḥmatihi man yashāʾ*, He singles out for His mercy whom He wills (Q 2:105, Q 3:74), and *a-hum yaqsimūna raḥmata rabbik*, is it they who apportion the mercy of your Lord (Q 43:32).

And the closing line prices the smallest possible act. *A cup of water* given for the sake of the name will not lose its reward, and the corpus's scale is the same and finer: *fa-man yaʿmal mithqāla dharratin khayran yarah*, whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it (Q 99:7). Both books close their teaching on rank by valuing the least conspicuous deed, and both do it immediately after warning the insiders about gatekeeping.

### The Millstone, the Hand, and the Salt · Mark 9:42-50

> Whoever causes one of these little ones who believe in me to sin, it would be better for him if a great millstone were hung around his neck and he were thrown into the sea. And if your hand causes you to sin, cut it off. It is better for you to enter life crippled than with two hands to go to hell, to the unquenchable fire. And if your foot causes you to sin, cut it off. It is better for you to enter life lame than with two feet to be thrown into hell. And if your eye causes you to sin, tear it out. It is better for you to enter the kingdom of God with one eye than with two eyes to be thrown into hell, where their worm does not die and the fire is not quenched. For everyone will be salted with fire. Salt is good, but if the salt has lost its saltiness, how will you make it salty again? Have salt in yourselves, and be at peace with one another.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The passage opens on the gravest warning in the Gospel about the vulnerable, and this is where the corpus and Mark move in step: harming the small is not a lesser offence in either book. The corpus fences the same class with its own severity, the orphan's property as fire swallowed into the belly (Q 4:10), and the buried infant summoned as a witness on the Day, *bi-ayyi dhanbin qutilat*, for what sin was she killed (Q 81:8-9). Both books make the defenceless the sharpest edge of the judgment.

Then the amputations, and this reading names a divergence rather than dissolving it. Mark's Messiah says cut off the hand, cut off the foot, tear out the eye. The register is the hyperbolic wisdom-speech of that world, and the same speaker who says this heals hands and eyes throughout the book, so nothing in Mark suggests a surgical programme. But the corpus, addressing the identical problem of the organ that leads a man into ruin, legislates rather than exaggerates, and legislates restraint: *qul li-l-muʾminīna yaghuḍḍū min abṣārihim wa-yaḥfaẓū furūjahum*, tell the believing men to lower their gaze and guard their chastity, and the same to the believing women in the next verse (Q 24:30-31). Lower the eye, do not remove it. The two texts share the diagnosis exactly, that the member is the doorway, and they differ in the treatment prescribed, one by shocking figure and one by governed discipline. The commentary states that difference and leaves it standing, and notes only that the corpus's instrument is the one a community can actually be given.

Then the salt, three sentences that have puzzled readers for centuries and that come clear the moment the older law is put back beside them.

Salt in that world is the one kitchen substance that cannot be grown. Grain is sown, oil is pressed, wine is trodden, but salt is dug or evaporated and then carried and bought, and no household produces its own. That single physical fact is why the Torah made it the covenant material. *All the holy offerings that the Israelites present to the LORD I have given to you and your sons and daughters as a perpetual due; it is a covenant of salt forever* (Numbers 18:19), and what that covenant conveys is a *stipend*: the priests receive from the offerings because they hold no land and grow nothing. The idiom persists in the imperial administration of the region, where the officials of the Persian court write to the king *because we eat the salt of the palace* (Ezra 4:14), which means we are on your provision and our loyalty follows our bread. And Leviticus makes it a standing rule of the altar, *with all your offerings you shall offer salt* (Leviticus 2:13), so that nothing is presented to God without the ingredient the presenter could not make.

That is the whole doctrine, and it is the corpus's doctrine of provision rendered in a mineral. A covenant sealed in salt is a covenant with dependence built into its material: the token itself declares that the party bringing it did not produce it. The corpus states the same thing without a token, which is the direction this commentary keeps finding, from applied blood to added salt to the word alone: *wa-mā bikum min niʿmatin fa-mina Allāh*, whatever favour is with you is from Allah ﷻ (Q 16:53), and *wa-in taʿuddū niʿmata Allāhi lā tuḥṣūhā*, if you count the favour of Allah ﷻ you will not number it (Q 14:34).

Then the last clause and its verb. *Have salt in yourselves*: *echete en heautois hala*. Not *become salty*, which would be an instruction to manufacture, but *have it in you*, a possession by nature received. The salt is already given; the instruction is to keep it. And the pairing with the final phrase is the passage's point: *have salt in yourselves, and be at peace with one another*, addressed to men who had spent the last road arguing about who was greatest. The mineral of the covenant is set directly against the quarrel about rank, and the corpus supplies the sentence that closes the loop, spoken about the joining of a community's hearts: *law anfaqta mā fī al-arḍi jamīʿan mā allafta bayna qulūbihim wa-lākinna Allāha allafa baynahum*, had you spent all that is in the earth you could not have joined their hearts, but Allah ﷻ joined them (Q 8:63). Peace among the servants is the second thing in this passage that cannot be produced by the ones who need it, and both are given. Have the salt you were given; be at peace with the brother you were joined to.

A last note on transmission, because it is visible in the manuscripts. Some copies of Mark add to *everyone will be salted with fire* the words *and every sacrifice will be salted with salt*, which is Leviticus 2:13 quoted directly. The addition is not original, and it is a scribe's gloss rather than a corruption of sense: an early reader recognised the altar-law standing behind the sentence and wrote it in the margin, and the margin came into the text. The connection this commentary makes is one the copyists had already made.

### Divorce, and the Beginning · Mark 10:1-12

> And he left there and went to the region of Judea and beyond the Jordan, and crowds gathered to him again. And again, as was his custom, he taught them. And Pharisees came up and in order to test him asked: Is it lawful for a man to divorce his wife? He answered them: What did Moses command you? They said: Moses allowed a man to write a certificate of divorce and to send her away. And Jesus said to them: Because of your hardness of heart he wrote you this commandment. But from the beginning of creation, God made them male and female. Therefore a man shall leave his father and mother and hold fast to his wife, and the two shall become one flesh. So they are no longer two but one flesh. What therefore God has joined together, let not man separate. And in the house the disciples asked him again about this matter. And he said to them: Whoever divorces his wife and marries another commits adultery against her, and if she divorces her husband and marries another, she commits adultery.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The question is a trap between two rabbinic schools, and the answer refuses the terms by going behind the legislation to the design. *Because of your hardness of heart he wrote you this commandment*: the Mosaic permission is classed as an accommodation to a condition, not as the intention. The corpus uses the identical instrument on the identical people, *fa-bimā naqḍihim mīthāqahum laʿannāhum wa-jaʿalnā qulūbahum qāsiyah*, so for their breaking of their covenant We cursed them and made their hearts hard (Q 5:13), and it also classes certain of their restrictions as consequences rather than as the original good, *fa-bi-ẓulmin mina alladhīna hādū ḥarramnā ʿalayhim ṭayyibātin uḥillat lahum*, for the wrongdoing of those who were Jews We forbade them good things that had been lawful to them (Q 4:160). Legislation calibrated to a hardened condition is a category both books hold.

The argument from the beginning is Genesis, and the corpus argues from the same beginning with the same move: *yā ayyuhā al-nāsu ittaqū rabbakumu alladhī khalaqakum min nafsin wāḥidatin wa-khalaqa minhā zawjahā*, O mankind, be mindful of your Lord who created you from a single soul and created from it its mate (Q 4:1). One origin, then the pair, then the derivation of every duty from that origin: the corpus's verse continues straight into the command about kinship ties, exactly as Mark's argument moves from the one flesh to the prohibition on separating.

And here a divergence must be named at its right layer, because the two texts do not legislate the same way. The corpus permits divorce and regulates it in detail: waiting periods, two announcements with the option of return, witnesses, provision, and the standing instruction *fa-imsākun bi-maʿrūfin aw tasrīḥun bi-iḥsān*, retention in kindness or release with excellence (Q 2:229), with the reminder not to forget generosity between them. Mark's sentence, taken as legislation, forbids what the corpus regulates. What the two share, and share exactly, is the weight placed on the bond itself. The corpus calls the marriage contract *mīthāqan ghalīẓan*, a solemn covenant (Q 4:21), and it uses that phrase for only three things in the whole book: the covenant taken from the prophets (Q 33:7), the covenant taken at Sinai (Q 4:154), and this. A marriage ranked with the covenant of the prophets is not a dissoluble convenience, and the corpus's own transmitted teaching calls its permitted dissolution the most detested of permitted things. So the layer report is precise: on the sanctity and origin of the bond the two texts stand together and use the same argument from creation; on whether the bond may be dissolved and under what discipline, they part, one prohibiting and one regulating, and the commentary lets the parting stand where it falls rather than dissolving it upward.

### The Children · Mark 10:13-16

> And they were bringing children to him that he might touch them, and the disciples rebuked them. But when Jesus saw it, he was indignant and said to them: Let the children come to me; do not hinder them, for to such belongs the kingdom of God. Truly, I say to you, whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child shall not enter it. And he took them in his arms and blessed them, laying his hands on them.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The gatekeeping instinct appears for the third time in two chapters, and the reaction is stronger than anywhere else in the book: *ēganaktēsen*, he was indignant. What the child models is not innocence but *receiving*: a child in that world owned nothing, earned nothing, and was given everything, which is the exact posture the whole Gospel has been teaching about authority, provision, strength, and sight. The corpus's word for the original constitution a person is given and then either keeps or overlays is *fiṭra*: *fa-aqim wajhaka li-l-dīni ḥanīfan, fiṭrata Allāhi allatī faṭara al-nāsa ʿalayhā*, set your face to the religion, upright, the original nature of Allah ﷻ upon which He originated humankind (Q 30:30). Entering as a child is returning to what was given before anything was accumulated, and the arms and the blessing that close the scene are Mark's last tenderness before the road turns to Jerusalem.

### The Man Who Ran · Mark 10:17-31

> And as he was setting out on his journey, a man ran up and knelt before him and asked him: Good Teacher, what must I do to inherit eternal life? And Jesus said to him: Why do you call me good? No one is good except God alone. You know the commandments: Do not murder, Do not commit adultery, Do not steal, Do not bear false witness, Do not defraud, Honor your father and mother. And he said to him: Teacher, all these I have kept from my youth. And Jesus, looking at him, loved him, and said to him: You lack one thing: go, sell all that you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me. Disheartened by the saying, he went away sorrowful, for he had great possessions.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The address is unusual and the deflection turns on it. *Didaskale agathe*, good teacher: an adjective attached to a title, and the rabbinic literature of the period offers no parallel for addressing a teacher that way. The man reaches for a compliment, and the compliment is returned to its owner: *why do you call me good? No one is good except God alone*, *ei mē heis ho theos*. Matthew, copying, moves the adjective off the person and onto the deed, asking about *the good thing to do*, and the smoothing is watchable.

From the fixed position this is the plainest sentence in the Gospel and it needs no commentary at all, only its Arabic. But there is more in it than a refusal, because the corpus works this exact root and shows what the deflection is protecting. *Al-Barr* is a divine name, the Good, the Beneficent, sealed at the end of Sūrat al-Ṭūr: *innahu huwa al-Barru al-Raḥīm*, He is the Good, the Merciful (Q 52:28), and the pronoun-plus-definite construction is the same equational form Part II examined. Beneath the Name stands the quality, *al-birr*, and the corpus defines it in one long verse rather than leaving it a mood: *laysa al-birra an tuwallū wujūhakum qibala al-mashriqi wa-l-maghrib, wa-lākinna al-birra man āmana bi-llāh*, goodness is not that you turn your faces east or west, but goodness is one who believes in Allah ﷻ, and the verse continues into the giving of wealth, *out of love for it*, to kin, orphans, the needy, the traveller, the beggar, and for the freeing of necks (Q 2:177). And then the corpus states the condition of attaining it: *lan tanālū al-birra ḥattā tunfiqū mimmā tuḥibbūn*, you will never attain goodness until you spend of what you love (Q 3:92).

Set that beside what happens next in Mark and the two halves lock. The man calls him good; the title is deflected upward to its Owner; and then the man is told the one thing he lacks, which is to give away what he loves. The deflection and the instruction are the same root two forms apart, the Name refused for himself and the quality prescribed for the questioner, and the corpus's verse states the prescription in words the scene is enacting. *Go, sell what you have and give to the poor* is *lan tanālū al-birra ḥattā tunfiqū mimmā tuḥibbūn*, and the sorrow the man leaves with is the measure of how much he loved it.

One item in the commandment list is not from the Decalogue at all: *mē aposterēsēs*, do not defraud, do not withhold what is owed. It belongs to the wage-law, *you shall not oppress a hired worker who is poor and needy; you shall give him his wages on the same day* (Deuteronomy 24:14-15, Leviticus 19:13). Both evangelists who copy Mark drop it, and it is left standing here in a list given to a rich man. The corpus opens an entire surah on the same offence: *waylun li-l-muṭaffifīn*, woe to those who give short measure, who take in full from people and shortchange them when they measure out (Q 83:1-3), and it closes the door with *wa-awfū al-kayla wa-l-mīzāna bi-l-qisṭ*, give full measure and weight in justice (Q 6:152). The commandment inserted for the wealthy man is about what he owes those who work for him, and it is the same substance as the salt: a stipend, unwithheld.

> And Jesus looked around and said to his disciples: How difficult it will be for those who have wealth to enter the kingdom of God! And the disciples were amazed at his words. But Jesus said to them again: Children, how difficult it is to enter the kingdom of God! It is easier for a camel to go through the eye of a needle than for a rich person to enter the kingdom of God. And they were exceedingly astonished, and said to him: Then who can be saved? Jesus looked at them and said: With man it is impossible, but not with God. For all things are possible with God.

The camel and the needle is the one image in this Gospel that crosses into the corpus intact, word for word, with the same two animals of scale and aperture: *lā tufattaḥu lahum abwābu al-samāʾi wa-lā yadkhulūna al-jannata ḥattā yalija al-jamalu fī sammi al-khiyāṭ*, the gates of heaven will not be opened for them, nor will they enter the garden until the camel passes through the eye of the needle (Q 7:40). Transit, full, two, and the ledger notes that the corpus places it in exactly the same argumentative position, on those whose wealth and pride shut the upper door.

And the closing exchange gives the *ʿinda*-chain its Greek link. *Para anthrōpois adynaton, all' ou para theō; panta gar dynata para tō theō*: with men impossible, but not with God, for all things are possible *with God*. *Para tō theō* is the Greek of *ʿinda Allāh*, the preposition this commentary has been counting since the Mother of the Book, and here it carries the same freight: what is impossible in the register of human capacity is not impossible in the register of what is *with Him*. Link five. The corpus's own formula for this exact reassurance is *inna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr*, Allah ﷻ is powerful over all things, repeated across the book, and the whole doctrine of the rich man's case is that entry is not purchased by the entrant, which is why the disciples' question, *then who can be saved*, receives an answer that names nobody's competence but His.

> Peter began to say to him: See, we have left everything and followed you. Jesus said: Truly, I say to you, there is no one who has left house or brothers or sisters or mother or father or children or lands, for my sake and for the gospel, who will not receive a hundredfold now in this time, houses and brothers and sisters and mothers and children and lands, with persecutions, and in the age to come eternal life. But many who are first will be last, and the last first.

The hundredfold is promised, and Mark alone inserts two words into the middle of the promise: *meta diōgmōn*, with persecutions. Neither copyist keeps them. The honesty is the authentication, and the corpus's own promise to those who emigrate for His sake is built the same way, the reward stated and the cost named in the same breath: *wa-man yuhājir fī sabīli Allāhi yajid fī al-arḍi murāghaman kathīran wa-saʿah*, whoever emigrates in the way of Allah ﷻ will find on the earth many a refuge and abundance (Q 4:100), set among verses about those driven from their homes.

And the closing inversion, *many who are first will be last*, is a warning to the men who have just listed their sacrifices, delivered one verse after their spokesman said *see, we have left everything*. The corpus grades the Day by proportion rather than reversal, *fa-man yaʿmal mithqāla dharratin khayran yarah* (Q 99:7), and it warns the ones who count their own credit in its own way: *lā tubṭilū ṣadaqātikum bi-l-manni wa-l-adhā*, do not nullify your charities with reminders of your generosity and with injury (Q 2:264). Peter reminded. The next sentence he hears is that the order can invert.

### The Third Announcement · Mark 10:32-34

> And they were on the road, going up to Jerusalem, and Jesus was walking ahead of them. And they were amazed, and those who followed were afraid. And taking the twelve again, he began to tell them what was to happen to him, saying: See, we are going up to Jerusalem, and the Son of Man will be delivered over to the chief priests and the scribes, and they will condemn him to death and deliver him over to the Gentiles. And they will mock him and spit on him and flog him and kill him. And after three days he will rise.

#### Commentary based on Quran

*Jesus was walking ahead of them*, *ēn proagōn autous*. The verb enters here and it will carry to the end: he goes before them to Jerusalem, he tells them at the supper that he will go before them into Galilee, and the young man at the tomb repeats the word to the women. The one who is delivered is the one walking in front, and the company behind him is amazed and afraid.

The announcement is the fullest of the three, and among its verbs is *emptysousin autō*, they will spit on him. Spittle, three, and this instance is prophecy rather than event: the fluid that opened a deaf man's ears and a blind man's eyes is now foretold as an instrument of contempt. The corpus's general law for the treatment of messengers covers it without needing the detail: *yā ḥasratan ʿalā al-ʿibād, mā yaʾtīhim min rasūlin illā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn*, what regret for the servants; no messenger came to them but they mocked him (Q 36:30).

### The Sons of Zebedee, and the Cup · Mark 10:35-45

> And James and John, the sons of Zebedee, came up to him and said to him: Teacher, we want you to do for us whatever we ask of you. And he said to them: What do you want me to do for you? And they said to him: Grant us to sit, one at your right hand and one at your left, in your glory. Jesus said to them: You do not know what you are asking. Are you able to drink the cup that I drink, or to be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized? And they said to him: We are able. And Jesus said to them: The cup that I drink you will drink, and with the baptism with which I am baptized, you will be baptized, but to sit at my right hand or at my left is not mine to grant, but it is for those for whom it has been prepared.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The third announcement of the killing is followed immediately by a request for cabinet positions. Mark's arrangement is the sharpest in the book, and he does not comment.

And the answer contains the sentence this commentary has been walking toward since chapter one. *To sit at my right hand or at my left is not mine to grant*: *ouk estin emon dounai*. The Greek is unambiguous and no manuscript softens it. Asked for the highest thing in his gift, the answer is that it is not in his gift. The corpus asks the same question as a rebuke to anyone who assumes otherwise: *a-hum yaqsimūna raḥmata rabbik*, is it they who apportion the mercy of your Lord? *Naḥnu qasamnā baynahum maʿīshatahum*, We have apportioned among them their livelihood (Q 43:32). The apportioning is the Lord's, and the Gospel's own Messiah says so in the plainest words available to him.

And the clause that completes it is the third of Mark's formal divine passives: *alla hois hētoimastai*, but for those for whom it has been prepared. Prepared, passive, by the unnamed Preparer, and the Preparer is the one the seats belong to. The count of that construction now stands at three, the cleansing commanded, the hand restored, the seats prepared, and the convention has been running under every major scene in the book. The corpus's own version of the sentence is *aʿadda Allāhu lahum*, Allah ﷻ has prepared it for them (Q 9:89, Q 58:15, and elsewhere), the identical verb with the Agent named rather than reverently omitted. Mark leaves the blank; the corpus fills it; and the two grammars agree that the seats were not the servant's to distribute.

The cup is the other half of the answer, and the transit ledger records it as partial. *The cup that I drink* is the prophets' cup of Isaiah 51:17 and Jeremiah 25:15, the cup of wrath handed to a people to drink; the image carries the sense of a portion assigned and unavoidable, and the Gethsemane prayer will take it up. The corpus's *kaʾs* is never that cup. Every occurrence of the word in the corpus is the drink of the garden, *yusqawna min raḥīqin makhtūm*, given a sealed nectar (Q 83:25), *kaʾsan kāna mizājuhā kāfūrā*, a cup mixed with camphor (Q 76:5), and *lā laghwun fīhā wa-lā taʾthīm*, no idle talk in it and no sin (Q 52:23). The image crossed the earlier scriptures and did not cross into the corpus, which reserved the word for the reward and named the portion of suffering with other vocabulary altogether, *balāʾ* and *fitna* and *ibtilāʾ*. Transit, partial, and the ledger states which way it broke.

> And when the ten heard it, they began to be indignant at James and John. And Jesus called them to him and said to them: You know that those who are considered rulers of the Gentiles lord it over them, and their great ones exercise authority over them. But it shall not be so among you. But whoever would be great among you must be your servant, and whoever would be first among you must be slave of all. For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many.

The political analysis is two words long and exact: *katakyrieuousin*, they lord it down over them, and *katexousiazousin*, they wield authority down upon them. Both compounds are downward. And the inversion follows: not so among you, the great one is the servant, the first is the slave of all. The corpus's picture of the same disease and the same cure is Q 28:83, *tilka al-dāru al-ākhiratu najʿaluhā li-lladhīna lā yurīdūna ʿuluwwan fī al-arḍi wa-lā fasādā*, that final home We assign to those who desire neither exaltation on the earth nor corruption, and its picture of the office of its own Prophet ﷺ is *wa-mā anta ʿalayhim bi-jabbār*, you are not a tyrant over them (Q 50:45), and *lasta ʿalayhim bi-muṣayṭir*, you are not over them a controller (Q 88:22).

Then *the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve*, and the verb this commentary has been storing since chapter one arrives at its inversion. Angels served him in the wilderness; a woman raised from fever served the table; and here the receiving is spent and reversed. The corpus's word for the same figure, in the strongest reduction it offers, is *in huwa illā ʿabdun anʿamnā ʿalayh*, he was only a servant whom We favoured (Q 43:59), and its statement of what he never disdained is *lan yastankifa al-Masīḥu an yakūna ʿabdan lillāh* (Q 4:172). The Gospel's sentence and the corpus's sentence describe one posture, and the Gospel's is the narrative and the corpus's is the doctrine.

The last clause, *to give his life as a ransom for many*, *lytron anti pollōn*, is the one place in Mark where the death is given an interpretation, and the commentary reports what is there rather than what centuries built on it. *Lytron* is the price of release, a commercial and legal word. Mark supplies no theory of how the release operates, no altar, no substitutionary mechanism, no doctrine of imputation; the whole clause is four words at the end of a lesson about not lording it over people, and the lesson is its context. The corpus has one ransom-narrative and one only, and it is stated with the same word-family: *wa-fadaynāhu bi-dhibḥin ʿaẓīm*, and We ransomed him with a mighty sacrifice (Q 37:107), where the Ransomer is Allah ﷻ, the ransomed is the son of Ibrāhīm AS, and the direction runs from above downward as deliverance. So the category of a ransom given is present in both scriptures and its subject in the corpus is always the Giver above. The corpus states elsewhere, in the same breath as its account of that son, the law that no soul carries another's load, *wa-lā taziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā* (Q 6:164, Q 35:18). Both sentences stand in the same book, which means the corpus itself does not read a ransom as a transfer of guilt but as an act of release from above. This commentary reads Mark's clause at the weight Mark gives it, a servant's life laid down under a lesson about service, and it says plainly what is not there: exactly what is exchanged, and to whom, is not stated in this Gospel, and the corpus does not supply that term from its own side either.

#### Structural Commentary · The Servanthood Terminus, Mark 10:45

**What stands.** A conferred height. The title is a citation, and in its source the figure is presented before the throne and *given* dominion, which is why a height is genuinely there to be descended from.

**What moves.** The descent through it. He trades the word down inside his own answer, *diakonos* at one verse and *doulos pantōn* at the next, slave of all, with *of all* removing the last place a hierarchy could hide, and the sentence ends in a table-servant's word.

**How they bind.** The voluntariness. The descent requires the height to be real or it is not a descent, and the height requires the descent to be real or it is a title, and the corpus states both in one verse: *lan yastankifa al-Masīḥu an yakūna ʿabdan lillāh*, the Messiah will never disdain to be a servant of Allah ﷻ (Q 4:172), where *yastankif* presupposes the height in the act of denying the disdain.

Collapse the height and the servanthood is deficiency, which is exactly what the fault-silence of Part I exists to prevent. Collapse the descent and the title stands alone as sovereignty. Collapse the voluntariness and *yastankif* has nothing left to negate. Two moves, the same two, in the same order, in one verse of each scripture, and neither is quoting the other. ⟀ GOL↑ on the servanthood terminus. What the descent terminates in at Mk 10:45, a release-price constituted by the one descending, is read at the weight Mark gives it and is not built into the closure, which stands on the trajectory: a conferred height descended through to table-service.

### Bartimaeus · Mark 10:46-52

> And they came to Jericho. And as he was leaving Jericho with his disciples and a great crowd, Bartimaeus, a blind beggar, the son of Timaeus, was sitting by the roadside. And when he heard that it was Jesus of Nazareth, he began to cry out and say: Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me! And many rebuked him, telling him to be silent. But he cried out all the more: Son of David, have mercy on me! And Jesus stopped and said: Call him. And they called the blind man, saying to him: Take heart. Get up; he is calling you. And throwing off his cloak, he sprang up and came to Jesus. And Jesus said to him: What do you want me to do for you? And the blind man said to him: Rabbi, let me recover my sight. And Jesus said to him: Go your way; your faith has made you well. And immediately he recovered his sight and followed him on the way.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The last healing in the Gospel, and the second touch that Bethsaida's two-stage miracle promised. Between the two blind men lie three announcements of the death and three failures to hear them; the first man saw walking trees, and this one, on the far side of the road, sees and follows.

Two details carry the passage. He *throws off his cloak*, *apobalōn to himation autou*, and a beggar's cloak is his working capital, the thing spread on the ground to catch coins and his only covering at night. He discards the whole of what he owns to come, without being asked, one chapter after a wealthy man was asked and could not. Mark sets the two men opposite each other and lets the arrangement do the work.

And the title he shouts is *Son of David*, its first occurrence in the book, from a beggar in the road, and the crowd tries to silence him, and the silencing does not come from the front this time. He is called instead. The title is neither endorsed nor corrected here; two chapters later, in the temple, it will be put under question by the man himself, and this commentary will meet it there. What is notable now is where it arrives from: the bottom of the social order, shouted, and answered with a question rather than a claim.

The question is the same one the sons of Zebedee were asked, word for word: *what do you want me to do for you?* They asked for thrones and were told the seats are not his to give. The beggar asks to see, and receives it, and the verdict is the one given to the woman in the crowd: *hē pistis sou sesōken se*, your faith has made you well. No touch, no spittle, no formula, no gesture at all. The healing is credited entirely to the receiver's trust and the giving is left unnamed, which is the same grammar the corpus uses for the granting of discernment to those who guard themselves: *in tattaqū Allāha yajʿal lakum furqānan*, if you are mindful of Allah ﷻ He will make for you a criterion (Q 8:29). The corpus's promise of sight to the mindful and Mark's crediting of sight to the trusting are the same transaction read from its two ends.

And the last clause is the movement's closing signature: *and he followed him on the way*, *ēkolouthei autō en tē hodō*. The word for road has been the frame of this whole movement, and the man who was sitting beside it is now walking on it. Mark's arrangement is complete: the blind see in two stages, and the second stage ends in discipleship.

The movement closes here. A correct confession was sealed and the suffering was published in the open. The one who rebuked the announcement was answered in the words the corpus speaks to its own Prophet ﷺ, *not for you is anything of the matter*. On the mountain the subject of the Gospel said nothing, two figures were removed, and one imperative was left. At the foot of it the strength-chain reached the disciples and failed, and the missing element was named as prayer, which is the corpus's one unbrokered channel. The salt was set against the argument about rank. A man was told to spend what he loved and could not. Two men asked for thrones and were told the seats are not his to give but prepared by another. And a beggar threw away everything he had, asked only to see, and got up and walked the road.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** The road, and the *dei* upon it. It is necessary that the Son of Man suffer, stated three times on one journey, with no agent supplied for the necessity and no negotiation admitted.

**What moves.** The following. A confession sealed, a rebuke returned, a man told to spend what he loves, two men asking for thrones, and a beggar throwing off the one thing he owns in order to walk the road.

**How they bind.** The inversion of rank. First and last exchange places, greatness is defined as service, and the two seats are declined in the one sentence where he says a thing is not his to give.

Collapse the road and the announcements are prophecy rather than direction. Collapse the following and the teaching has no cost attached and the rich man simply walks away from an opinion. Collapse the inversion and *ouk estin emon dounai* is modesty rather than structure, and the whole movement's argument about who allocates is lost. ⟀ GOL on the inverted-rank structure.

## The City · Mark 11:1-12:44

The road ends and the city begins. Six days of confrontation in the temple courts, framed by a fig tree and closed by a widow, and the whole movement runs on one psalm that the entry-crowd sings at its opening and the vineyard-parable quotes at its close.

:::box What to Watch
**The crowd blesses the kingdom,** not the king; both copyists move the blessing onto the man.

**The temple clause both copyists lost,** *for all the nations*, is what makes the action a defence of the Gentiles' court.

**Isaiah destroys the vineyard; Mark keeps it** and replaces the tenants. Custodianship transferred, trust untouched.

**Psalm 110's riddle** is two Hebrew words flattened into one Greek word.
:::
### The Entry · Mark 11:1-11

> Now when they drew near to Jerusalem, to Bethphage and Bethany, at the Mount of Olives, he sent two of his disciples and said to them: Go into the village in front of you, and immediately as you enter it you will find a colt tied, on which no one has ever sat. Untie it and bring it. If anyone says to you, Why are you doing this? say, The Lord has need of it and will send it back here immediately. And they went away and found a colt tied at a door outside in the street, and they untied it. And some of those standing there said to them: What are you doing, untying the colt? And they told them what Jesus had said, and they let them go. And they brought the colt to Jesus and threw their cloaks on it, and he sat on it. And many spread their cloaks on the road, and others spread leafy branches that they had cut from the fields. And those who went before and those who followed were shouting: Hosanna! Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord! Blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David! Hosanna in the highest! And he entered Jerusalem and went into the temple. And when he had looked around at everything, as it was already late, he went out to Bethany with the twelve.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Zechariah's oracle of the king coming humble and riding on a colt stands behind this scene, and Mark does not quote it. Matthew, copying, quotes it in full and, following the Hebrew parallelism too literally, produces two animals. The earliest evangelist stages the sign and leaves the reader to recognise it, and the restraint is the pattern this commentary has been counting.

The cloaks on the road are the older scripture's gesture for acclaiming a king, spread under Jehu's feet on the steps when the army proclaimed him (2 Kings 9:13), and they arrive here without a crown or an anointing to follow. Transit, partial: the gesture crossed into the scene, the enthronement it belonged to did not.

*Hosanna* is not praise but petition. *Hōshiʿa-nna*, save now, we beseech you, from Psalm 118:25, and the crowd is shouting a prayer, addressed past the rider to the One who saves. That psalm is about to frame the entire visit: its next verse, *blessed is he who comes in the name of the LORD*, is the crowd's second shout, and its verse about the rejected stone will be the closing line of the vineyard parable two chapters later. One psalm, sung at the gate and quoted in the temple, and both ends of it point at the same Saviour.

And then the sentence that shows how careful this text is. The crowd shouts *blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David*. Not *blessed is the son of David*, and not *blessed is the king*. They bless the *kingdom*. Matthew changes it to *hosanna to the son of David*, and Luke changes it to *blessed is the king who comes*. Both copyists move the blessing from the reign to the man, and the earliest text has it on the reign. This is the same restraint Mark showed at the baptism when the voice stopped short of the Psalm's begetting clause, and it is the reason this Gospel reads cleanly from the fixed position: what arrives is a kingdom, and the one riding is the one announcing it. The corpus's own picture of a granted entry into a sacred city is a promise of security rather than a coronation: *la-tadkhulunna al-masjida al-ḥarāma in shāʾa Allāhu āminīn*, you will surely enter the sacred mosque, if Allah ﷻ wills, in security (Q 48:27), with the conditional *if Allah ﷻ wills* written into the promise itself.

The scene's last verse is an anticlimax that no legend-maker would write. He enters the temple, *looks around at everything*, notices that it is late, and leaves for the night. The palms belong to another Gospel. Mark's rider inspects and withdraws.

### The Fig Tree and the Temple · Mark 11:12-25

> On the following day, when they came from Bethany, he was hungry. And seeing in the distance a fig tree in leaf, he went to see if he could find anything on it. When he came to it, he found nothing but leaves, for it was not the season for figs. And he said to it: May no one ever eat fruit from you again. And his disciples heard it.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Mark's fourth interruption opens, and it is the most artful. A tree is cursed, the temple is cleansed, and the tree is found withered, so that the middle scene is read through the outer ones. Intercalation, four.

And Mark includes the clause every reader stumbles on and no copyist would have invented: *for it was not the season for figs*. He tells us the tree was innocent.
