The detail is not an oversight; it is the signal that the tree is not the subject. Leaves without fruit, appearance without substance, and the temple is one hundred metres away. The corpus's own image for the same condition is the crop that delights the tiller and turns yellow and becomes chaff (Q 57:20), and its judgment on a people whose observance had become display is stated of the earlier community exactly here: *mathalu alladhīna ḥummilū al-tawrāta thumma lam yaḥmilūhā ka-mathali al-ḥimāri yaḥmilu asfārā*, the likeness of those who were charged with the Torah and then did not carry it is the likeness of a donkey carrying volumes (Q 62:5). Full foliage, no load borne.

> And they came to Jerusalem. And he entered the temple and began to drive out those who sold and those who bought in the temple, and he overturned the tables of the money-changers and the seats of those who sold pigeons. And he would not allow anyone to carry anything through the temple. And he was teaching them and saying to them: Is it not written, My house shall be called a house of prayer for all the nations? But you have made it a den of robbers. And the chief priests and the scribes heard it and were seeking a way to destroy him, for they feared him, because all the crowd was astonished at his teaching.

Three things in this scene are Mark's alone, and each one is load-bearing.

The first is the full quotation. *My house shall be called a house of prayer for all the nations*: *pasin tois ethnesin*, from Isaiah 56:7. Matthew and Luke both quote the verse and both drop those three words. The earliest Gospel keeps them, and keeping them is what makes the action intelligible, because the trading stood in the Court of the Gentiles, the one precinct where a non-Israelite could pray, and it had been turned into a market. The complaint is that the nations' place of prayer has been requisitioned. And the corpus legislates precisely that principle for its own sanctuary: *al-masjidi al-ḥarāmi alladhī jaʿalnāhu li-l-nāsi sawāʾan al-ʿākifu fīhi wa-l-bād*, the sacred mosque which We made for mankind, equal are the one who dwells there and the visitor (Q 22:25), and it names the obstruction of worship as among the gravest wrongs: *wa-man aẓlamu mimman manaʿa masājida Allāhi an yudhkara fīhā ismuh*, who is more unjust than one who prevents the mosques of Allah ﷻ from having His name remembered in them (Q 2:114). Mark alone preserves the clause that makes his subject's action a defence of that very rule, and both his copyists lost it.

The second is the merchandise named. *Those who sold pigeons*: the pigeon is the offering the Torah prescribes for the poor who cannot afford a lamb (Leviticus 5:7, 12:8). The overturned tables belong to the trade in the poor's own sacrifice. The corpus's anger falls in the same place, at those who consume people's wealth in falsehood and bar the way of Allah ﷻ, *inna kathīran mina al-aḥbāri wa-l-ruhbāni la-yaʾkulūna amwāla al-nāsi bi-l-bāṭili wa-yaṣuddūna ʿan sabīli Allāh* (Q 9:34), a verse about religious professionals monetising access.

The third is a sentence found in no other Gospel: *he would not allow anyone to carry anything through the temple*. Using the temple courts as a shortcut for porters was already forbidden in the rabbinic ruling of the period, and here the enforcement is stricter than the enforcers'. This is not a man abolishing the sanctuary's law. It is a man applying it where its own custodians had let it lapse, which is the same posture as the tassels on his garment at Gennesaret and the leper sent to the priest in chapter one.

The quotation's second half carries a charge the hearers would have completed for themselves. *A den of robbers* is Jeremiah 7:11, and three verses later that oracle says what happened to Shiloh and promises the same to this house. Mark's subject cites the sermon that ends in a demolition, and two chapters later he will say not one stone will be left on another. The corpus records the demolitions as accomplished fact, twice over: *fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu ūlāhumā baʿathnā ʿalaykum ʿibādan lanā ulī baʾsin shadīdin fa-jāsū khilāla al-diyār*, when the promise of the first of the two came, We sent against you servants of Ours of great might, and they searched through the homes, and again for the second, *wa-li-yutabbirū mā ʿalaw tatbīrā*, and to destroy utterly what they had overcome (Q 17:5-7).

> As they passed by in the morning, they saw the fig tree withered away to its roots. And Peter remembered and said to him: Rabbi, look! The fig tree that you cursed has withered. And Jesus answered them: Have faith in God. Truly, I say to you, whoever says to this mountain, Be taken up and thrown into the sea, and does not doubt in his heart, but believes that what he says will come to pass, it will be done for him. Therefore I tell you, whatever you ask in prayer, believe that you have received it, and it will be yours. And whenever you stand praying, forgive, if you have anything against anyone, so that your Father also may forgive you your trespasses.

The frame closes, and the teaching drawn from it is about prayer rather than about trees. *Have faith in God*, *echete pistin theou*, and then the mountain, and then the general promise, and then a condition.

The mountain-saying has a Quranic counterpart that names the category precisely, and it is worth setting down because it prevents the promise from being read as a licence. *Wa-law anna qurʾānan suyyirat bihi al-jibālu aw quṭṭiʿat bihi al-arḍu aw kullima bihi al-mawtā, bal lillāhi al-amru jamīʿā*, and if there were a recitation by which mountains were moved, or the earth were cleft, or the dead were spoken to, rather, to Allah ﷻ belongs the command entirely (Q 13:31). The corpus raises the exact three miracles, moving a mountain among them, and answers with a correction of ownership rather than a denial of possibility: the command is His, entirely. Mark's promise and the corpus's clause are compatible in the only way that matters, because Mark's own instruction begins *have faith in God* and continues *whatever you ask in prayer*. The moving is asked for, not wielded. The one who says *this kind comes out only by prayer* is not two chapters later handing out mountain-moving as a personal capacity.

And the last verse attaches the condition both books attach to the same request. *Whenever you stand praying, forgive*, and the corpus's own most striking instance is spoken to a man who had sworn to cut off charity to a relative who had slandered his family: *wa-l-yaʿfū wa-l-yaṣfaḥū, a-lā tuḥibbūna an yaghfira Allāhu lakum*, let them pardon and overlook; do you not love that Allah ﷻ should forgive you (Q 24:22). The asker who will not release is asking to receive what he refuses to give, and both scriptures close that door in the same sentence in which they open the other.

### By What Authority · Mark 11:27-33

> And they came again to Jerusalem. And as he was walking in the temple, the chief priests and the scribes and the elders came to him, and they said to him: By what authority are you doing these things, or who gave you this authority to do them? Jesus said to them: I will ask you one question; answer me, and I will tell you by what authority I do these things. Was the baptism of John from heaven or from man? Answer me. And they discussed it with one another, saying: If we say, From heaven, he will say, Why then did you not believe him? But shall we say, From man? They were afraid of the people, for they all held that John really was a prophet. So they answered Jesus: We do not know. And Jesus said to them: Neither will I tell you by what authority I do these things.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The question is the right question, and this commentary has been answering it since chapter one. *By what authority*, *en poia exousia*, and *who gave you this authority*, *tis soi edōken*: the questioners themselves phrase it as a grant. Their grammar is correct even in hostility, exactly as the Nazareth villagers' was.

The counter-question is not evasion; it is a test of whether they can recognise a grant when they have already seen one. *Was the baptism of John from heaven or from man?* If they can locate the source of the forerunner's authority they will have located his, since the two offices are one relay. And their deliberation is the scene's whole judgment: they calculate consequences in both directions and answer neither. Mark records the calculation verbatim, so the reader can see that what looks like agnosticism is arithmetic.

The corpus stages the same self-interrogation and lets it succeed. When Ibrāhīm AS asks the idol-worshippers to consult their gods, *fa-rajaʿū ilā anfusihim fa-qālū innakum antumu al-ẓālimūn*, they returned to themselves and said, indeed it is you who are the wrongdoers (Q 21:64), and then, the corpus adds, *thumma nukisū ʿalā ruʾūsihim*, they were turned upside down on their heads and went back to their position. The turn inward happens, the truth is reached, and the position is resumed anyway. Mark's council performs the first half of that verse and never says the second half aloud. *We do not know* is what the corpus calls being reversed on the head.

And the refusal that follows is just: an answer is owed to a question asked in good faith, and this one was asked by men who had just demonstrated that they would not act on the answer. The corpus's own formula for that condition is *wa-law ʿalima Allāhu fīhim khayran la-asmaʿahum, wa-law asmaʿahum la-tawallaw*, had Allah ﷻ known any good in them He would have made them hear, and had He made them hear they would have turned away (Q 8:23).

### The Vineyard · Mark 12:1-12

> And he began to speak to them in parables. A man planted a vineyard and put a fence around it and dug a pit for the winepress and built a tower, and leased it to tenants and went into another country. When the season came, he sent a servant to the tenants to get from them some of the fruit of the vineyard. And they took him and beat him and sent him away empty-handed. Again he sent to them another servant, and they struck him on the head and treated him shamefully. And he sent another, and him they killed. And so with many others: some they beat, and some they killed. He had still one other, a beloved son. Finally he sent him to them, saying, They will respect my son. But those tenants said to one another, This is the heir. Come, let us kill him, and the inheritance will be ours. And they took him and killed him and threw him out of the vineyard. What will the owner of the vineyard do? He will come and destroy the tenants and give the vineyard to others. Have you not read this Scripture: The stone that the builders rejected has become the cornerstone; this was the Lord's doing, and it is marvelous in our eyes? And they were seeking to arrest him, but feared the people, for they perceived that he had told the parable against them.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The opening sentence is a quotation. The fence, the pit for the press, the tower: four elements in the order Isaiah gives them in the Song of the Vineyard (Isaiah 5:1-2), and Isaiah's own seventh verse supplies the referent, *the vineyard of the LORD of hosts is the house of Israel*. Any hearer in that court knew the song, which is why they perceived immediately that the parable was against them.

But the parable and its source diverge at the decisive point, and the divergence is the reading. In Isaiah the vineyard itself is at fault, producing wild grapes, and the owner destroys it: *I will remove its hedge, and it shall be devoured; I will break down its wall, and it shall be trampled*. In Mark the vineyard is faultless. What has gone wrong is the *tenancy*, and the owner's judgment falls on the custodians and *gives the vineyard to others*. The estate is preserved and the management is replaced. That distinction is the whole shape of what the corpus says happened, and it says it as a general law rather than a single event: *wa-in tatawallaw yastabdil qawman ghayrakum thumma lā yakūnū amthālakum*, and if you turn away He will replace you with another people, and they will not be the likes of you (Q 47:38), and *fa-sawfa yaʾtī Allāhu bi-qawmin yuḥibbuhum wa-yuḥibbūnah*, then Allah ﷻ will bring a people He loves and who love Him (Q 5:54), and *in yashaʾ yudhhibkum wa-yastakhlif min baʿdikum mā yashāʾ*, if He wills He can remove you and bring after you whom He wills (Q 6:133). Custodianship transferred, the trust itself untouched.

The sequence of beaten and killed servants is the corpus's own summary of that history, twice, in nearly identical words: *a-fa-kullamā jāʾakum rasūlun bimā lā tahwā anfusukumu istakbartum fa-farīqan kadhdhabtum wa-farīqan taqtulūn*, is it that whenever a messenger came to you with what your souls did not desire, you grew arrogant, and some you denied and some you killed (Q 2:87, and again at Q 5:70). Two verbs, denied and killed, matching the parable's two treatments exactly.

Then the son, and the parable's most delicate line: *he had still one other, a beloved son*, *huion agapēton*, the word of the voice at the baptism and on the mountain. The tenants' reasoning is quoted, *this is the heir, let us kill him*, and the corpus quotes the same reasoning in the same story: *wa-idh yamkuru bika alladhīna kafarū li-yuthbitūka aw yaqtulūka aw yukhrijūk, wa-yamkurūna wa-yamkuru Allāh, wa-Allāhu khayru al-mākirīn*, and when those who refused plotted against you, to restrain you or kill you or drive you out; and they plotted, and Allah ﷻ plotted, and Allah ﷻ is the best of plotters (Q 8:30). And in the surah that carries this prophet's own mother's family in its name, the two clauses stand consecutively: *wa-makarū wa-makara Allāhu wa-Allāhu khayru al-mākirīn* and then, immediately, *idh qāla Allāhu yā ʿĪsā innī mutawaffīka wa-rāfiʿuka ilayya*, when Allah ﷻ said: O ʿĪsā, I am taking you and raising you to Myself (Q 3:54-55). The plot and the raising in adjacent verses, and the parable in Mark ends the same way: the killing is narrated, and then the rejected stone becomes the cornerstone, and *this was the Lord's doing*.

That closing quotation is Psalm 118:22, and with it the psalm that the crowd sang at the gate closes its own frame. *Hosanna* at the entrance, the rejected stone in the temple, one psalm holding the whole visit, and its own verse naming the doer: *para kyriou egeneto hautē*, this came about from the Lord.

One fence is worth setting, because the parable's owner goes *into another country*. Absence is a feature of the story's mechanics, not a theology: it explains why tenants could imagine seizing an inheritance. Neither book has an absent owner. The corpus states the opposite as its most famous verse, *lā taʾkhudhuhu sinatun wa-lā nawm*, neither slumber seizes Him nor sleep, *wa-huwa al-ʿAliyyu al-ʿAẓīm* (Q 2:255), and Mark's own parable has the owner arriving.

### The Coin · Mark 12:13-17

> And they sent to him some of the Pharisees and some of the Herodians, to trap him in his talk. And they came and said to him: Teacher, we know that you are true and do not care about anyone's opinion. For you are not swayed by appearances, but truly teach the way of God. Is it lawful to pay taxes to Caesar, or not? Should we pay them, or should we not? But knowing their hypocrisy, he said to them: Why put me to the test? Bring me a denarius and let me look at it. And they brought one. And he said to them: Whose likeness and inscription is this? They said to him: Caesar's. Jesus said to them: Render to Caesar the things that are Caesar's, and to God the things that are God's. And they marveled at him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The flattery in the approach is exact and, from the fixed position, accurate: *you are not swayed by appearances*, literally you do not look at the face of men. The corpus's standing description of its own messengers is the same, *wa-lā yakhshawna aḥadan illā Allāh*, they fear none but Allah ﷻ (Q 33:39), and it commands the same posture in judgment, *wa-lā takhshawu al-nāsa wa-khshawn*, do not fear people, fear Me (Q 5:44). His enemies open by stating his character correctly and then try to use it.

The answer runs on one word. *Whose likeness and inscription is this?* *Eikōn*, image. They say Caesar's, and the ruling follows: give back to Caesar what carries Caesar's image. And the second half of the argument is the half he does not say, which every hearer raised on Genesis would complete for himself: the coin carries Caesar's image and is his; the human being carries the image of God and is His. Mark leaves the syllogism open, and the crowd's amazement is the sound of people finishing it in their heads.

Here the two texts genuinely differ in their anthropology and the difference should be stated cleanly rather than smoothed. The corpus has no image-clause. Its word for forming is *ṣawwara* and it means shaping, not resembling: *wa-ṣawwarakum fa-aḥsana ṣuwarakum*, and He formed you and made your forms excellent (Q 40:64), *fī ayyi ṣūratin mā shāʾa rakkabak*, in whatever form He willed He assembled you (Q 82:8), with *al-Muṣawwir*, the Fashioner, as one of His names (Q 59:24). The corpus's fence, *laysa ka-mithlihi shayʾun*, there is nothing like Him (Q 42:11), forecloses the resemblance-reading entirely, and what it puts in the image's place is a different relation, breath and trust: *wa-nafakhtu fīhi min rūḥī*, and I breathed into him of My spirit (Q 15:29), and the *amānah* offered to the heavens and the earth and borne by the human (Q 33:72). So the ownership-argument stands in both books and its ground differs: Mark's is likeness, the corpus's is the breath given and the trust accepted. What both reach is the identical conclusion, that the human belongs to God in a way no coin belongs to Caesar.

And the ruling itself is a ranking, not a partition into equal halves. The corpus states the same order with its own precedence-clause: *aṭīʿū Allāha wa-aṭīʿū al-rasūla wa-ulī al-amri minkum*, obey Allah ﷻ and obey the messenger and those in authority among you, and then, in the same verse, *fa-in tanāzaʿtum fī shayʾin fa-ruddūhu ilā Allāhi wa-l-rasūl*, and if you dispute in anything, refer it to Allah ﷻ and the messenger (Q 4:59). Civil authority is real and it is second, and the appeal runs upward when the two collide. Mark's sentence and the corpus's verse hold the same two obligations in the same order.

### The Resurrection Question · Mark 12:18-27

> And Sadducees came to him, who say that there is no resurrection. And they asked him a question, saying: Teacher, Moses wrote for us that if a man's brother dies and leaves a wife, but leaves no child, the man must take the widow and raise up offspring for his brother. There were seven brothers; the first took a wife, and when he died left no offspring. And the second took her, and died, leaving no offspring. And the third likewise. And the seven left no offspring. Last of all the woman also died. In the resurrection, when they rise again, whose wife will she be? For the seven had her as wife. Jesus said to them: Is this not the reason you are wrong, because you know neither the Scriptures nor the power of God? For when they rise from the dead, they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like angels in heaven. And as for the dead being raised, have you not read in the book of Moses, in the passage about the bush, how God spoke to him, saying, I am the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob? He is not God of the dead, but of the living. You are quite wrong.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The diagnosis comes before the answer and it names two deficits: *you know neither the Scriptures nor the power of God*, *mē eidotes tas graphas mēde tēn dynamin tou theou*. Scripture and power, text and capacity, and the corpus answers both in a single verse, in the same order, about the same question. A man comes with a decayed bone asking who will give life to bones when they are dust, and the reply is: *qul yuḥyīhā alladhī anshaʾahā awwala marratin wa-huwa bi-kulli khalqin ʿalīm*, say: He will give them life who produced them the first time, and He is knowing of all creation (Q 36:79). The power-argument is the first-creation argument, and the scripture-argument is the corpus's own *a-wa-lam yaraw anna Allāha alladhī khalaqa al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa qādirun ʿalā an yakhluqa mithlahum* (Q 17:99). Both deficits, one verse, and the corpus's own preferred proof of the resurrection is exactly the one Mark's Messiah reaches for: go back to how it worked the first time.

Then the marriage-clause, which has been read for centuries as a divergence between the two scriptures and turns out, read closely, to be two halves of one sentence.

Mark's verbs are the verbs of the human transaction. *Gamousin oude gamizontai*: they neither marry nor are given in marriage, the active for the man who takes a wife and the passive for the woman who is given by her family. Both verbs name the contracting, the arrangement, the transfer that a society performs. What is denied is the institution's continued operation, and the reason is given in the same breath: they are *like angels*, that is, no longer in the order where lineage, inheritance, and succession, the whole apparatus the Sadducees' riddle depends on, are the point.

Now the corpus. It speaks of the garden's pairing constantly, and it never once puts the wedding verb in a creature's hands. *Wa-zawwajnāhum bi-ḥūrin ʿīn*, and We paired them (Q 44:54, Q 52:20): the verb is transitive with *We* as its subject, in every occurrence. The corpus's companions are *azwāj muṭahharah*, purified pairs, and they are entered into rather than contracted for, *hum wa-azwājuhum fī ẓilālin ʿalā al-arāʾiki muttakiʾūn*, they and their pairs in shade, reclining on couches (Q 36:56).

Put the two side by side and the sentence completes. The Gospel removes the transaction from the creatures; the corpus assigns the pairing to the Lord. One says the contracting ends; the other says the giving is His. Nothing in Mark denies companionship in the raised state, and nothing in the corpus has anyone marrying there. What both refuse, in their different idioms, is the projection of this world's arrangements, its inheritance law and its seven brothers, onto the next.

This is worth naming honestly rather than filling. What the raised state *is*, from inside, neither text puts into a proposition. The corpus says of it *fa-lā taʿlamu nafsun mā ukhfiya lahum min qurrati aʿyun*, no soul knows what has been hidden for them of comfort for the eyes (Q 32:17), which is a statement that the content is withheld, not a description of it. This commentary locates the deciding input on the far side of that verse and does not cross to it.

The closing argument is grammatical and both books make it. *I am the God of Abraham*: present tense, spoken to Moses long after the patriarchs died, and the inference is that they are alive to Him. *He is not God of the dead, but of the living*, *theos zōntōn*, and the corpus's title for the same argument is a divine name: *wa-tawakkal ʿalā al-Ḥayyi alladhī lā yamūt*, and rely upon the Living who does not die (Q 25:58). And the corpus states the conclusion Mark's argument reaches, as a direct instruction about how to speak of the dead: *wa-lā taqūlū li-man yuqtalu fī sabīli Allāhi amwātun, bal aḥyāʾun wa-lākin lā tashʿurūn*, do not say of those slain in the way of Allah ﷻ that they are dead; rather they are living, but you do not perceive (Q 2:154), and *aḥyāʾun ʿinda rabbihim yurzaqūn*, living, with their Lord, provided for (Q 3:169). *ʿInda rabbihim*, with their Lord: the preposition again, link six, and it is doing the same work as the present tense of the burning bush. The dead are with Him, and what is with Him remains.

The corpus adds one further word that belongs here, because it settles which of the two lives is the real one: *wa-inna al-dāra al-ākhirata la-hiya al-ḥayawān*, and the final home, that is truly the life (Q 29:64), using an intensive form of the word for living rather than the ordinary noun. The Sadducees came to ask which husband a woman would have in a lesser copy of this world. The answer given in both books is that they had the direction of the copying backwards.

### The Greatest Commandment · Mark 12:28-34

> And one of the scribes came up and heard them disputing with one another, and seeing that he answered them well, asked him: Which commandment is the most important of all? Jesus answered: The most important is: Hear, O Israel: The Lord our God, the Lord is one. And you shall love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul and with all your mind and with all your strength. The second is this: You shall love your neighbor as yourself. There is no other commandment greater than these. And the scribe said to him: You are right, Teacher. You have truly said that he is one, and there is no other besides him. And to love him with all the heart and with all the understanding and with all the strength, and to love one's neighbor as oneself, is much more than all whole burnt offerings and sacrifices. And when Jesus saw that he answered wisely, he said to him: You are not far from the kingdom of God. And after that no one dared to ask him any question.

#### Commentary based on Quran

This is the passage in which the two scriptures come closest, and it is worth reading slowly.

The answer begins where the corpus begins. *Hear, O Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is one*, and the Greek is *kyrios heis estin*. The corpus's own opening of the surah of pure devotion is *qul huwa Allāhu aḥad*, say: He is Allah ﷻ, One (Q 112:1), and the corpus keeps two words for oneness which Part II distinguished, *wāḥid* for the numerical unity that excludes partners and *aḥad* for a oneness that admits no composition at all, the form used only here in the whole book. Mark's Messiah quotes the Shema as the first commandment, and the corpus's shortest surah is a single-verse Shema. Both texts place the declaration of the one before every duty that follows from it.

*And you shall love the Lord your God with all your heart*, and the corpus makes love the measuring rod of belief itself: *wa-lladhīna āmanū ashaddu ḥubban lillāh*, and those who believe are stronger in love for Allah ﷻ (Q 2:165), a verse whose whole subject is the misplacement of that love onto rivals. The command and the description are the same content in two moods.

*You shall love your neighbor as yourself*, and the corpus, at Q 4:36, moves straight from *worship Allah ﷻ and associate nothing with Him* into the widest circle of neighbours in either scripture: parents, kin, orphans, the needy, *wa-l-jāri dhī al-qurbā wa-l-jāri al-junub*, the neighbour who is kin and the neighbour who is a stranger, the companion at your side, the traveller, and those your right hands possess. One verse: the first commandment and the second, in that order, with the second spelled out into eight named categories. Mark's pair and Q 4:36 are the same architecture.

Then the scribe answers, and Mark gives him a longer speech than the questioner usually gets. He repeats the oneness, adds *and there is no other besides him*, and then draws a conclusion of his own: that to love Him and the neighbour *is much more than all whole burnt offerings and sacrifices*. That sentence is the corpus's own, stated of the corpus's own rite, at the end of the passage on the sacrificial animals: *lan yanāla Allāha luḥūmuhā wa-lā dimāʾuhā wa-lākin yanāluhu al-taqwā minkum*, neither their flesh nor their blood reaches Allah ﷻ, but the reverence from you reaches Him (Q 22:37). A scribe in the temple courts, standing within sight of the altar, says what the corpus says about the altar, and the answer he receives is *you are not far from the kingdom of God*.

And that reply carries a last precision. *Ou makran ei*, you are not far. The corpus's word for the nearness of the One being discussed is *qarīb*, and it stands in the verse this commentary has returned to throughout: *fa-innī qarīb, ujību daʿwata al-dāʿi idhā daʿān*, I am near, I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me (Q 2:186). The scribe is told he is near; the corpus tells every caller Who is nearer. Nobody dared ask another question after that, and the reason is legible: the exchange had ended in agreement.

### David's Son · Mark 12:35-37

> And as Jesus taught in the temple, he said: How can the scribes say that the Christ is the son of David? David himself, in the Holy Spirit, declared: The Lord said to my Lord, Sit at my right hand, until I put your enemies under your feet. David himself calls him Lord. So how is he his son? And the great throng heard him gladly.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The puzzle as it stands in Greek looks like a paradox: *eipen kyrios tō kyriō mou*, the Lord said to my Lord, the same word twice, and how can a man's descendant be his lord? Centuries of argument for a more-than-human sonship were built on that repetition.

The repetition is not in the original. Psalm 110 opens *neʾum YHWH la-adoni*, and the two words are entirely different. The first is the divine Name. The second is *adoni*, my lord, the ordinary honorific of that language, used by a servant to a master, by a subject to a king, by Sarah of Abraham, and by Joseph's household of Joseph. When the Greek translators rendered the psalm they used *kyrios* for both, because *kyrios* covers the Name and the courtesy alike, and the flattening produced the riddle. The Greek Gospel inherits the flattened text and poses the question that only the flattened text can pose.

The corpus keeps the two tiers the Hebrew kept. *Rabb* is reserved: it is used of Allah ﷻ throughout, and where it appears with a human possessor it is only in the mouths of others describing a master, as when the corpus's Yūsuf AS says *innahu rabbī aḥsana mathwāy*, he is my master who made good my lodging (Q 12:23), and the corpus records that as reported speech in a specific human relation. The human honorific is *sayyid*, and the corpus applies it to a prophet without any risk of confusion: Yaḥyā AS is *sayyidan wa-ḥaṣūran wa-nabiyyan min al-ṣāliḥīn*, a master, restrained, a prophet, of the righteous (Q 3:39). Two words, two levels, never collapsed. And the corpus names the collapse itself as the error it invites everyone to renounce: *wa-lā yattakhidha baʿḍunā baʿḍan arbāban min dūni Allāh*, and that none of us take others as lords besides Allah ﷻ (Q 3:64).

What remains of the passage, once the translation-effect is set aside, is what Mark actually left: a question, unanswered. He does not say *the Christ is more than David's son* and he does not say the scribes are wrong about the lineage. He asks how the two things fit and stops, and the crowd hears him gladly. The corpus's own placement of this figure's future station is likewise a hedge rather than a declaration, and it is spoken to its own Prophet ﷺ: *ʿasā an yabʿathaka rabbuka maqāman maḥmūdā*, it may be that your Lord will raise you to a praised station (Q 17:79). *ʿAsā*, it may be, in the mouth of the Giver about the receiver. Neither text hands the servant a title he did not receive, and Mark's open question is the more careful of the two treatments, since he leaves it open.

### The Scribes and the Widow · Mark 12:38-44

> And in his teaching he said: Beware of the scribes, who like to walk around in long robes and like greetings in the marketplaces and have the best seats in the synagogues and the places of honor at feasts, who devour widows' houses and for a pretense make long prayers. They will receive the greater condemnation.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Four vanities and one crime. The robes, the greetings, the front seats, the head table: the corpus's word for exactly this is *riʾāʾ al-nās*, being seen by people, and it names the whole disease in a short surah that ends on the same two items Mark pairs here, prayer performed for display and the withholding of small kindnesses: *fa-waylun li-l-muṣallīn, alladhīna hum ʿan ṣalātihim sāhūn, alladhīna hum yurāʾūn, wa-yamnaʿūna al-māʿūn*, woe to those who pray, who are heedless of their prayer, who make a display, and withhold small assistance (Q 107:4-7). Long prayers as a pretext and widows' houses devoured is that surah's diagnosis in a single Greek sentence, and the corpus adds the fence about charity ruined by display, *lā tubṭilū ṣadaqātikum bi-l-manni wa-l-adhā ka-lladhī yunfiqu mālahu riʾāʾa al-nās* (Q 2:264).

> And he sat down opposite the treasury and watched the people putting money into the offering box. Many rich people put in large sums. And a poor widow came and put in two small copper coins, which make a penny. And he called his disciples to him and said to them: Truly, I say to you, this poor widow has put in more than all those who are contributing to the offering box. For they all contributed out of their abundance, but she out of her poverty has put in everything she had, all she had to live on.

He sits down opposite the treasury and watches. The scene is observation before it is teaching, and what he sees is arithmetic.

*Lepta duo*, two small coins, and the detail is exact: two is the smallest divisible sum. She could have kept one. The Greek makes the choice visible, because a single *lepton* was a legal offering, and she had a remainder available and did not take it. And what she gives is named twice, once as *everything she had* and once with a word Mark chooses carefully: *holon ton bion autēs*, her whole *bios*. Not *zōē*, the life that animates, but *bios*, the livelihood, the means of living. She put in what she was going to live on.

The corpus states the rule this scene illustrates as arithmetic rather than as sentiment: *li-yunfiq dhū saʿatin min saʿatih, wa-man qudira ʿalayhi rizquhu fa-l-yunfiq mimmā ātāhu Allāh, lā yukallifu Allāhu nafsan illā mā ātāhā*, let the man of abundance spend out of his abundance, and let the one whose provision is restricted spend out of what Allah ﷻ has given him; Allah ﷻ does not burden a soul beyond what He has given it (Q 65:7). Proportion, not amount, and both books measure the gift against what remains rather than against what is deposited. And the corpus knows this widow's exact type, the poor person whose restraint conceals the poverty: *yaḥsabuhumu al-jāhilu aghniyāʾa mina al-taʿaffuf*, the ignorant would think them wealthy because of their restraint (Q 2:273). She was not identified by anyone in the court except the man who was watching.

The corpus also grades the manner, and its grading favours what this scene shows: *in tubdū al-ṣadaqāti fa-niʿimmā hiya, wa-in tukhfūhā wa-tuʾtūhā al-fuqarāʾa fa-huwa khayrun lakum*, if you disclose your charities it is good, and if you conceal them and give them to the poor it is better for you (Q 2:271). Many rich people put in large sums, openly. She put in two coins that nobody would have noticed.

And now the arrangement, because Mark is doing something across these three paragraphs that no single verse of them shows. He puts the scribes who *devour widows' houses* immediately before a widow giving away her last coin, and immediately after her he puts a disciple admiring the temple's masonry and the reply that not one stone will be left on another. Devoured, giving, demolished, in that order, without a connecting word. The institution that consumes the widow receives her last two coins and is then condemned to rubble. Mark's interruptions have been noted four times in this commentary as a device; here he performs the same operation across pericope boundaries, and the argument is entirely in the sequence.

The movement closes with her. A crowd sang a psalm of petition at the gate and the same psalm named the rejected stone in the temple. A tree in full leaf bore nothing and a house of prayer for all the nations had been requisitioned. Tenants were replaced and the estate was kept. A coin bore Caesar's image and the questioner bore another. The Sadducees had the direction of copying backwards. A scribe said what the corpus says about the altar and was told he was not far. Psalm 110's riddle turned out to be two Hebrew words flattened into one Greek word. And a woman with two coins gave both, and the only person who noticed was the one who had sat down to watch.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** The trust. A vineyard leased, a temple held, a treasury administered, a commandment deposited, and not one of them the custodian's property.

**What moves.** The custody. Real administration with real proceeds, tables and doves and long prayers and widows' houses, which is where the whole movement's failure lives.

**How they bind.** The accounting. Fruit returned at the season, the coin returned to the one whose image it carries, the first commandment discharged into the second, and two small coins weighed against large sums by what remained rather than by what was given.

Collapse the trust into the custody and the tenants are correct and the inheritance is theirs. Collapse the custody and there is nothing to be accountable for and the fig tree is arbitrary. Collapse the accounting and the widow's two coins and the scribes' long prayers become indistinguishable, which is exactly the confusion the movement exists to break. ⟀ GOL on the trust-and-accounting structure.

## The Discourse · Mark 13:1-37

One chapter, spoken on a hillside facing the temple, and it is the only sustained discourse in this Gospel. It belongs to a recognisable genre, the apocalypse, and the corpus knows every element of that genre and keeps almost all of them. The one element it refuses is the one this chapter also refuses, at its centre, in the most Quranic sentence Mark ever wrote.

:::box What to Watch
**The genre calculates; this chapter refuses to.** Mk 13:32 and Q 7:187 stand clause for clause.

**The Spirit promised in the dock** is the same strengthening the corpus extends to believers under trial, Q 58:22.

**The corpus exceeds the Gospel** on the cosmic dismantling: seven verbs against two.

**The four watches of the parable** are the four movements of the passion, in order.
:::
### Not One Stone · Mark 13:1-2

> And as he came out of the temple, one of his disciples said to him: Look, Teacher, what wonderful stones and what wonderful buildings! And Jesus said to him: Do you see these great buildings? There will not be left here one stone upon another that will not be thrown down.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The masonry was worth admiring; Herod's platform stones are among the largest ever set by hand. And the reply cashes the Jeremiah quotation of two chapters back, where the den of robbers stood three verses from what happened to Shiloh. The corpus records the two demolitions of this house as accomplished history, *fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu al-ākhirati... wa-li-yudakhkhilū al-masjida kamā dakhalūhu awwala marratin wa-li-yutabbirū mā ʿalaw tatbīrā*, and to enter the sanctuary as they entered it the first time, and to destroy utterly what they had overcome (Q 17:7). The corpus writes it as a completed pattern; Mark writes it as a coming one; and the stones came down within forty years of the sentence.

### The Four on the Mountain · Mark 13:3-13

> And as he sat on the Mount of Olives opposite the temple, Peter and James and John and Andrew asked him privately: Tell us, when will these things be, and what will be the sign when all these things are about to be accomplished? And Jesus began to say to them: See that no one leads you astray. Many will come in my name, saying, I am he! and they will lead many astray. And when you hear of wars and rumors of wars, do not be alarmed. This must take place, but the end is not yet. For nation will rise against nation, and kingdom against kingdom. There will be earthquakes in various places; there will be famines. These are but the beginning of the birth pains.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Four men ask, and they are the four called first, by the sea, in chapter one. Peter and Andrew, James and John, the first names in the book, asking the last question in it. Mark closes his circle quietly and says nothing about having done so.

Their question is the one the whole chapter will decline to answer: *when*, and *what will be the sign*. And the first word of the reply is a warning about being misled, repeated four times through the chapter, *blepete*, watch out. The corpus's counsel to the same question is identical in posture: it treats curiosity about the timing as the wrong appetite and redirects to preparedness.

*Many will come in my name, saying, I am he.* Impersonation is the predicted hazard, and the corpus builds its anti-impersonation locks into its own founding documents: the office sealed by declaration, *khātam al-nabiyyīn* (Q 33:40), and the anti-cult clause revealed on a battlefield when a rumour of the Prophet's death ran through the ranks, *wa-mā Muḥammadun illā rasūlun qad khalat min qablihi al-rusul, a-fa-in māta aw qutila inqalabtum ʿalā aʿqābikum*, Muhammad ﷺ is only a messenger; messengers have passed away before him; if he died or were killed, would you turn back on your heels (Q 3:144).

*The beginning of the birth pains*, *ōdinōn*, and the corpus reaches for the same body for the same event: *yawma tarawnahā tadhhalu kullu murḍiʿatin ʿammā arḍaʿat wa-taḍaʿu kullu dhāti ḥamlin ḥamlahā*, the day you see it, every nursing mother will forget what she nursed, and every pregnant one will deliver her burden (Q 22:2). Labour is the shared figure, and it carries the same double sense in both books: the pains are real and they are the pains of something being born.

> But be on your guard. For they will deliver you over to councils, and you will be beaten in synagogues, and you will stand before governors and kings for my sake, to bear witness before them. And the gospel must first be proclaimed to all nations. And when they bring you to trial and deliver you over, do not be anxious beforehand what you are to say, but say whatever is given you in that hour, for it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit. And brother will deliver brother over to death, and the father his child, and children will rise against parents and have them put to death. And you will be hated by all for my name's sake. But the one who endures to the end will be saved.

*Do not be anxious beforehand what you are to say, but say whatever is given you in that hour, for it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit.* This sentence has a Quranic counterpart that deserves to be stated plainly, because it is the corpus's most generous verse about the Spirit. The threefold strengthening of ʿĪsā AS with the Holy Spirit is well known, *wa-ayyadnāhu bi-rūḥi al-qudus* (Q 2:87, Q 2:253, Q 5:110). What is less noticed is that the corpus extends the same strengthening to ordinary believers under pressure, and in almost the same words: *ulāʾika kataba fī qulūbihimu al-īmāna wa-ayyadahum bi-rūḥin minhu*, those, He has written faith in their hearts and strengthened them with a spirit from Him (Q 58:22). The verb is *ayyada*, the same verb; the recipients are the faithful of a community under trial; and the verse's context is families divided by belief, *lā tajidu qawman yuʾminūna bi-llāhi wa-l-yawmi al-ākhiri yuwāddūna man ḥādda Allāha wa-rasūlah wa-law kānū ābāʾahum aw abnāʾahum*, you will not find a people who believe in Allah ﷻ and the Last Day loving those who oppose Him, though they be their fathers or their sons. Mark's promise of the Spirit speaking in the dock, and his prediction that brother will hand over brother, stand together in one Quranic verse.

And the corpus adds the plain word about family under trial that Mark's harder sentence needs beside it: *inna min azwājikum wa-awlādikum ʿaduwwan lakum fa-ḥdharūhum*, among your spouses and your children are enemies to you, so beware of them, and then, in the same verse, *wa-in taʿfū wa-taṣfaḥū wa-taghfirū fa-inna Allāha ghafūrun raḥīm*, and if you pardon and overlook and forgive, Allah ﷻ is forgiving, merciful (Q 64:14). The betrayal is named, and the instruction after it is pardon.

*The one who endures to the end will be saved*, *ho hypomeinas eis telos*, and the corpus's closing imperative to its own community is built on that root: *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū iṣbirū wa-ṣābirū wa-rābiṭū wa-ttaqū Allāha laʿallakum tufliḥūn*, endure, and outlast others in endurance, and hold your posts, and be mindful of Allah ﷻ, that you may succeed (Q 3:200), the last verse of the surah that carries this prophet's mother's family in its name.

### The Desolating Sacrilege · Mark 13:14-23

> But when you see the abomination of desolation standing where he ought not to be (let the reader understand), then let those who are in Judea flee to the mountains. Let the one who is on the housetop not go down, nor enter his house, to take anything out, and let the one who is in the field not turn back to take his cloak. And alas for women who are pregnant and for those who are nursing infants in those days! Pray that it may not happen in winter. For in those days there will be such tribulation as has not been from the beginning of the creation that God created until now, and never will be. And if the Lord had not cut short the days, no human being would be saved. But for the sake of the elect, whom he chose, he shortened the days. And then if anyone says to you, Look, here is the Christ! or Look, there he is! do not believe it. For false christs and false prophets will arise and perform signs and wonders, to lead astray, if possible, the elect. But be on guard; I have told you all things beforehand.

#### Commentary based on Quran

*The abomination of desolation* is Daniel's phrase (Daniel 9:27, 11:31, 12:11), and the readers of the first century had a dated referent for it: the altar to Zeus erected on the altar of the temple in 167 BCE, which the first book of Maccabees names with the same words. Mark's aside, *let the reader understand*, is the narrator speaking directly to a community that would know what he meant and that he preferred not to name.

The corpus's own word for that category of object works at root level rather than by allusion. *Innamā al-khamru wa-l-maysiru wa-l-anṣābu wa-l-azlāmu rijsun min ʿamali al-shayṭān*, wine and gambling and the *anṣāb* and divining arrows are an abomination of Satan's work (Q 5:90), and *anṣāb* is from the root for *setting up*, things erected and installed. An abomination standing where it ought not to be, in Mark's phrase, is *anṣāb* in the corpus's: the defilement is in the erecting.

The flight instructions are the most concrete lines in the chapter, and their concreteness is the point: do not go down for your goods, do not turn back for your cloak, and the corpus's own evacuation-narratives run identically, *fa-asri bi-ahlika bi-qiṭʿin mina al-layli wa-lā yaltafit minkum aḥad*, so set out with your family in a portion of the night and let none of you look back (Q 11:81). Do not look back is one of the oldest instructions in either scripture.

*If the Lord had not cut short the days, no human being would be saved. But for the sake of the elect, whom he chose, he shortened the days.* Three clauses, and every one of them assigns the action upward: the shortening is His, the election is His, *hous exelexato*, whom He chose. Mercy operating as a limit on the duration of a trial is the corpus's constant: *yurīdu Allāhu bikumu al-yusra wa-lā yurīdu bikumu al-ʿusr* (Q 2:185), *lā yukallifu Allāhu nafsan illā wusʿahā* (Q 2:286). The tribulation is not shortened because the elect are strong. It is shortened because they are not.

And the warning about the false ones ends with a phrase worth marking: they will perform *signs and wonders*, and they will be persuasive enough to endanger even the chosen. Neither book treats the ability to produce marvels as a certificate. The corpus's discipline on this is severe and it is stated of every messenger without exception: *wa-mā arsalnā min qablika min rasūlin wa-lā nabiyyin illā idhā tamannā alqā al-shayṭānu fī umniyyatih*, We sent no messenger or prophet before you but that when he recited, Satan cast something into his recitation, *fa-yansakhu Allāhu mā yulqī al-shayṭānu thumma yuḥkimu Allāhu āyātih*, then Allah ﷻ abolishes what Satan casts and then Allah ﷻ makes His signs firm (Q 22:52). Interference is expected and the correction is the Sender's. Marvels prove capability, never authorisation, which is the same distinction this Gospel drew in its first synagogue when it muzzled a spirit that was telling the truth.

### The Gathering · Mark 13:24-27

> But in those days, after that tribulation, the sun will be darkened, and the moon will not give its light, and the stars will be falling from heaven, and the powers in the heavens will be shaken. And then they will see the Son of Man coming in clouds with great power and glory. And then he will send out the angels and gather his elect from the four winds, from the ends of the earth to the ends of heaven.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The cosmic list is short and Isaiah stands behind it. Sun darkened, moon dimmed, stars falling, powers shaken: four events. And here the corpus does not merely match the Gospel; it exceeds it by a wide margin, and the excess is worth setting down because it settles the question of which book takes this Day more seriously.

The corpus devotes whole surahs to the dismantling. *Idhā al-shamsu kuwwirat*, when the sun is wound up and extinguished; *wa-idhā al-nujūmu inkadarat*, when the stars fall and scatter; *wa-idhā al-jibālu suyyirat*, when the mountains are set moving; *wa-idhā al-biḥāru sujjirat*, when the seas are set boiling (Q 81:1-6). *Idhā al-samāʾu infaṭarat*, when the sky is split; *wa-idhā al-kawākibu intatharat*, when the planets are strewn (Q 82:1-2). *Fa-idhā bariqa al-baṣaru, wa-khasafa al-qamaru, wa-jumiʿa al-shamsu wa-l-qamar*, when the sight is dazzled, and the moon is eclipsed, and the sun and moon are joined (Q 75:7-9). *Fa-idhā al-nujūmu ṭumisat, wa-idhā al-samāʾu furijat, wa-idhā al-jibālu nusifat*, when the stars are obliterated, and the sky is opened, and the mountains are blown away (Q 77:8-10). Seven distinct verbs against the prophets' two, and the corpus's own summary of the scale is *wa-mā qadarū Allāha ḥaqqa qadrih*, they have not measured Allah ﷻ with the measure due to Him (Q 39:67).

One image in that inventory Mark declines. Isaiah's oracle says the heavens will be rolled up like a scroll (Isaiah 34:4), and Mark, quoting the verses around it, leaves that clause out. The corpus takes it up: *yawma naṭwī al-samāʾa ka-ṭayyi al-sijilli li-l-kutub*, the Day We roll up the heaven like the rolling of a scroll for writings (Q 21:104). The image passed from Isaiah into the corpus and around the earliest Gospel, and the ledger records it as a transit Mark declined.

*They will see the Son of Man coming in clouds with great power and glory.* Daniel 7:13 is the source and Daniel's own next verse is the commentary: *to him was given dominion and glory and a kingdom*, *yehiv*, given. The most exalted picture the Hebrew scriptures offer of this figure is a picture of conferral, and Mark's sentence keeps the recipient-grammar throughout, since the power and the glory arrive with him rather than originating in him, exactly as at Mk 8:38 they were *his Father's*. And what he does on arrival is send angels and gather: the corpus's *yawma yajmaʿukum li-yawmi al-jamʿ*, the Day He gathers you for the Day of Gathering (Q 64:9), with the gathering everywhere in the corpus a divine act performed through angelic agency, *wa-jāʾa rabbuka wa-l-malaku ṣaffan ṣaffā* (Q 89:22).

### The Hour · Mark 13:28-32

> From the fig tree learn its lesson: as soon as its branch becomes tender and puts out its leaves, you know that summer is near. So also, when you see these things taking place, you know that he is near, at the very gates. Truly, I say to you, this generation will not pass away until all these things take place. Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away. But concerning that day or that hour, no one knows, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son, but only the Father.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The fig returns, no longer cursed but read: leaves mean the season is close. And then two sayings that have divided readers for centuries, and this commentary reports them rather than resolving them.

*This generation will not pass away until all these things take place.* If *these things* are the stones and the tribulation, the sentence was fulfilled within forty years. If they include the gathering of verse 27, it was not. Mark places the two together and supplies no key, and the honest report is that the referent of *these things* is not fixed by anything in the text. This commentary leaves it standing rather than choosing the reading that would be tidier.

*Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away.* The corpus says the same of what it carries, in the same shape: *kullu man ʿalayhā fān, wa-yabqā wajhu rabbika dhū al-jalāli wa-l-ikrām*, everyone upon it will perish, and there remains the face of your Lord, the Majestic and Honouring (Q 55:26-27), and of the word itself, *lā tabdīla li-kalimāti Allāh* (Q 10:64) and *wa-tammat kalimatu rabbika ṣidqan wa-ʿadlan* (Q 6:115). What endures is the given word, and the corpus is careful to name whose it is; Mark's speaker says *my words*, and the whole of this Gospel has established the direction those words came from, *he does not speak from himself* being the corpus's way of putting it (Q 53:3-4).

And then verse 32, which is the reason this movement exists.

*But concerning that day or that hour, no one knows, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son, but only the Father.* *Oude ho huios, ei mē ho patēr.* An apocalyptic discourse, in a genre whose entire commercial value is the calculation of the timetable, arrives at its climax and disclaims the timetable, and disclaims it in a ladder that names the angels, then the Son, then stops at the Father. This is the most Quranic sentence in the Gospel of Mark, and it can be set beside its counterpart clause for clause.

*Yasʾalūnaka ʿani al-sāʿati ayyāna mursāhā, qul innamā ʿilmuhā ʿinda rabbī, lā yujallīhā li-waqtihā illā huw*, they ask you about the Hour, when will it arrive; say: its knowledge is only with my Lord; none will reveal it at its time but He (Q 7:187). The same question, the same refusal, the same exclusive clause, and the messenger commanded to say it in the first person. And the corpus repeats it: *inna Allāha ʿindahu ʿilmu al-sāʿah* (Q 31:34); *ilayhi yuraddu ʿilmu al-sāʿah*, to Him is referred the knowledge of the Hour (Q 41:47); *wa-ʿindahu ʿilmu al-sāʿah* (Q 43:85). Four times, and each time the preposition is *ʿinda*, with Him. The chain this commentary has tracked from the Mother of the Book now reaches its densest cluster, links seven through ten, and every one of them is about this same Hour.

The corpus does not merely withhold the date; it forecloses the whole calculating enterprise by making suddenness the Hour's defining property. *Lā taʾtīkum illā baghtatan*, it will not come upon you but suddenly (Q 7:187), and the same word again at Q 6:31, Q 12:107, Q 22:55, Q 43:66, and Q 47:18: six occurrences of *baghtatan*, unannounced. And the corpus states why the withholding is itself the mercy: *akādu ukhfīhā li-tujzā kullu nafsin bimā tasʿā*, I almost conceal it, that every soul may be recompensed for what it strives (Q 20:15). A known date would convert striving into scheduling.

So Mk 13:32 is not an embarrassment inside an apocalypse. It is the anti-calculation clause planted at the centre of a calculating genre, and the corpus takes that clause and makes it the whole of its posture. And the verse's ladder is worth reading once more for what it does to the argument of this commentary: the angels do not know, and the Son does not know, and the Father does. Whatever else the title Son carries in this Gospel, it stands here on the creaturely side of a line drawn by knowledge, in the speaker's own mouth, in the one chapter where he is speaking about the end of the world.

### Watch · Mark 13:33-37

> Be on guard, keep awake. For you do not know when the time will come. It is like a man going on a journey, when he leaves home and puts his servants in charge, each with his work, and commands the doorkeeper to stay awake. Therefore stay awake, for you do not know when the master of the house will come, in the evening, or at midnight, or when the rooster crows, or in the morning, lest he come suddenly and find you asleep. And what I say to you I say to all: Stay awake.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The discourse ends in an imperative rather than a schedule, and the parable that carries it is a household with the servants each given a task and a doorkeeper told to stay awake. The corpus's picture of the same arrangement is the deputyship, *huwa alladhī jaʿalakum khalāʾifa fī al-arḍ*, He it is who made you successors on the earth (Q 35:39), and the trust accepted at Q 33:72. Assigned work, an absent hour, and an accounting.

And then Mark does something with the four watches of the night that no reader notices until the passion arrives. *In the evening, or at midnight, or when the rooster crows, or in the morning.* Two chapters later the supper is *when it was evening* (Mk 14:17); the arrest and the trial run through the night; Peter's denial ends when *the rooster crowed* (Mk 14:72); and the delivery to Pilate is *as soon as it was morning* (Mk 15:1). The four watches of the parable are the four movements of the passion, in order. Mark has just told his disciples to stay awake through the night, and in the next chapter they will fall asleep three times in a garden while the hours he named run past them one by one.

The last line universalises it and closes the chapter: *what I say to you I say to all*, *pasin*. The command was never for the four on the mountain.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** The Word, permanent against a perishing cosmos. *Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away*, which is *lā mubaddila li-kalimātih* (Q 6:115) in the same clause-shape.

**What moves.** The dismantling. Sun darkened, moon withholding, stars falling, the powers shaken: a staged unmaking, which the corpus carries in seven verbs where the prophets used two.

**How they bind.** The reserved hour. *Not even the Son*, which keeps the timing on the source side and therefore keeps the whole discourse a summons to watch rather than a schedule to compute.

Collapse the Word into the cosmos and there is nothing that does not pass. Collapse the dismantling and the discourse is metaphor and the warning has no referent. Collapse the reservation and the chapter becomes a calendar, which is the one use both texts foreclose by placing the speaker himself inside the exclusion. ⟀ GOL on permanence against dissolution, with the reservation as the binding term.

## The Cup · Mark 14:1-15:47

Two chapters, one night and one morning, and the density of the writing changes. Mark slows to hours and then to minutes. The movement holds the anointing, the supper, the garden, the arrest, two trials, the denials, and the death, and it closes with a councillor asking for a body.

:::box What to Watch
**The unbidden anointing succeeds,** and the corpus names its category: *ʿafā Allāhu ʿanhā*, a space left unlegislated.

**Mark issues no command to repeat the supper.** *Do this in remembrance* belongs to Luke and Paul.

**In the garden two wills are named** in one sentence, and one is placed beneath the other by the one praying.

**The mockers state the doctrine** when they say he cannot save himself.
:::
### The Woman with the Flask · Mark 14:1-11

> It was now two days before the Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread. And the chief priests and the scribes were seeking how to arrest him by stealth and kill him, for they said: Not during the feast, lest there be an uproar from the people.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The fifth interruption opens: a plot, an anointing, and a betrayal, the middle scene framed by the two that pay for it. Intercalation, five.

> And while he was at Bethany in the house of Simon the leper, as he was reclining at table, a woman came with an alabaster flask of ointment of pure nard, very costly, and she broke the flask and poured it over his head. There were some who said to themselves indignantly: Why was the ointment wasted like that? For this ointment could have been sold for more than three hundred denarii and given to the poor. And they scolded her. But Jesus said: Leave her alone. Why do you trouble her? She has done a beautiful thing to me. For you always have the poor with you, and whenever you want, you can do good for them. But you will not always have me. She has done what she could; she has anointed my body beforehand for burial. And truly, I say to you, wherever the gospel is proclaimed in the whole world, what she has done will be told in memory of her.

She is not named, she is not asked, and she does something no law required and no custom prescribed. She breaks the flask, which is the detail that makes it irreversible: a broken alabaster neck cannot be resealed, so the whole is spent at once. And the objection is a costing, three hundred denarii, a labourer's year, and it is framed as concern for the poor.

The corpus names the category this act belongs to, and names it precisely. *Lā tasʾalū ʿan ashyāʾa in tubda lakum tasuʾkum*, do not ask about things which, if disclosed to you, would trouble you, and the verse continues, *ʿafā Allāhu ʿanhā, wa-Allāhu ghafūrun ḥalīm*, Allah ﷻ has left them alone, and Allah ﷻ is forgiving, forbearing (Q 5:101). *ʿAfā Allāhu ʿanhā*: there is a space the Lawgiver deliberately did not legislate, and the corpus's warning is against the impulse to fill it. The objectors in this room are doing exactly that. They are legislating an unlegislated act, pricing a gift that carried no price, and the answer they receive begins *aphete autēn*, leave her alone.

Two guards keep this from becoming sentimentality. The first is that unbidden does not mean unrejectable: a gift offered freely can be refused, and the corpus records a refusal, when Sulaymān AS sends back the queen's present, *a-tumiddūnani bi-māl*, do you offer me wealth (Q 27:36). So the acceptance here is itself a verdict, and it is stated as one, *kalon ergon ērgasato en emoi*, she has worked a beautiful work on me. The second is that the poor-clause is not a shrug. *You always have the poor with you, and whenever you want, you can do good for them* quotes Deuteronomy 15:11, and that verse is a command, not a resignation: *the poor will never cease from the land, therefore I command you, open wide your hand to your brother*. The clause is a standing imperative with the door held open, and the corpus makes the same provision permanent and obligatory rather than occasional, *wa-fī amwālihim ḥaqqun li-l-sāʾili wa-l-maḥrūm*, in their wealth is a right for the asker and the deprived (Q 51:19), a *ḥaqq*, a due, not a charity.

*She has anointed my body beforehand for burial*: *proelaben myrisai*, she has anticipated. And here Mark sets up a pair he will complete in his last chapter. This anointing is unbidden, unnamed, unplanned, and it succeeds. In chapter sixteen, women will buy spices, plan carefully, name themselves, and arrive to find the body gone; their anointing will fail. The one that worked was the one nobody asked for.

*Wherever the gospel is proclaimed in the whole world, what she has done will be told in memory of her.* A deed bound to the proclamation itself, carried wherever the message goes. The corpus does the same thing four times in one surah, with the same construction, for Nūḥ and Ibrāhīm and Mūsā and Hārūn and Ilyās: *wa-taraknā ʿalayhi fī al-ākhirīn*, and We left for him among later generations (Q 37:78, 108, 119, 129), a remembrance deposited in the future. And it is worth noting what the corpus does with women's names in this register, since Mark leaves this woman unnamed: the corpus names exactly one woman in its whole text, Maryam AS, and refers to every other by her relation or her deed, the wife of Firʿawn, the woman who disputes, the queen who submitted. Both books preserve the acts of women whose names they do not record, and both bind the remembrance to the message rather than to the name.

> Then Judas Iscariot, who was one of the twelve, went to the chief priests in order to betray him to them. And when they heard it, they were glad and promised to give him money. And he sought an opportunity to betray him.

The frame closes on the transaction the woman's act inverts: she spends three hundred denarii on him without being asked, and one of the twelve sells him for a sum Mark does not even bother to record. The two halves of the intercalation are a broken flask and a struck bargain.

### The Supper · Mark 14:12-25

> And on the first day of Unleavened Bread, when they sacrificed the Passover lamb, his disciples said to him: Where will you have us go and prepare for you to eat the Passover? And he sent two of his disciples and said to them: Go into the city, and a man carrying a jar of water will meet you. Follow him, and wherever he enters, say to the master of the house, The Teacher says, Where is my guest room, where I may eat the Passover with my disciples? And he will show you a large upper room furnished and ready; there prepare for us. And the disciples set out and went to the city and found it just as he had told them, and they prepared the Passover.

#### Commentary based on Quran

A man carrying a water jar was a visible anomaly in that city, since the work was women's, and it makes a findable sign in a crowd. The room is *estrōmenon hetoimon*, furnished and ready, prepared in advance by arrangements the disciples did not make, which is the same shape as the colt tied and waiting. The corpus's image for provision found already in place is Maryam AS in the sanctuary: *kullamā dakhala ʿalayhā Zakariyyā al-miḥrāba wajada ʿindahā rizqan*, whenever Zakariyyā entered upon her in the chamber he found provision with her, and when he asked, she said *huwa min ʿindi Allāh*, it is from Allah ﷻ (Q 3:37). *ʿInda*, twice in one verse, the preposition of what is with Him arriving where it is needed. The upper room was furnished before anyone went looking for it.

> And when it was evening, he came with the twelve. And as they were reclining at table and eating, Jesus said: Truly, I say to you, one of you will betray me, one who is eating with me. They began to be sorrowful and to say to him one after another: Is it I? He said to them: It is one of the twelve, one who is dipping bread into the dish with me. For the Son of Man goes as it is written of him, but woe to that man by whom the Son of Man is betrayed! It would have been better for that man if he had not been born.

Evening: the first of the four watches the parable named, and the clock begins to run.

The betrayer is identified by table-fellowship rather than by name, *one who is dipping bread into the dish with me*, which is the Psalter's own bitterness, *even my close friend in whom I trusted, who ate my bread, has lifted his heel against me* (Psalm 41:9). And then a sentence that holds two things most systems try to separate: *the Son of Man goes as it is written of him, but woe to that man by whom he is betrayed*. The event is written and the man is liable, in one breath, with no attempt to soften either half.

The corpus holds the identical pair with the same refusal to resolve it. *Li-man shāʾa minkum an yastaqīm, wa-mā tashāʾūna illā an yashāʾa Allāhu rabbu al-ʿālamīn*, for whoever among you wills to go straight; and you do not will unless Allah ﷻ, Lord of the worlds, wills (Q 81:28-29). Human willing named, divine willing named, in consecutive clauses, and the corpus does not dissolve the first into the second. And *it would have been better for that man if he had not been born* has its counterpart in the wish the corpus records at the reckoning: *wa-yaqūlu al-kāfiru yā laytanī kuntu turābā*, and the one who refused says: would that I were dust (Q 78:40).

> And as they were eating, he took bread, and after blessing it broke it and gave it to them, and said: Take; this is my body. And he took a cup, and when he had given thanks he gave it to them, and they all drank of it. And he said to them: This is my blood of the covenant, which is poured out for many. Truly, I say to you, I will not drink again of the fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new in the kingdom of God.

Read what Mark actually writes, because what is absent from his account is as striking as what is present.

He blesses. He breaks. He gives. He says the words. And then he says nothing else. There is no interpretation supplied, no explanation of how the bread is the body, and, most notably, **no command to repeat it**. *Do this in remembrance of me* is not in Mark and is not in Matthew; it appears in Luke and in Paul. The earliest Gospel records the act and issues no institution. Whatever the later rite became, the first written account of this meal does not establish it, and that restraint belongs on the same list as the dropped begetting clause and the blessed kingdom rather than the blessed king.

*This is my blood of the covenant*: *touto estin to haima mou tēs diathēkēs*. The phrase is Sinai's, and Sinai is where its meaning is fixed. Moses takes the blood of the offerings, throws half against the altar, reads the covenant aloud, and *threw it on the people, and said, Behold the blood of the covenant that the LORD has made with you* (Exodus 24:8). The blood of the covenant is thrown *upon* people. It is never drunk. And the prohibition on drinking it is not a detail but a stated law with a stated reason: *the life of the flesh is in the blood, and I have given it to you upon the altar to make atonement for your souls; therefore no person among you shall eat blood* (Leviticus 17:11-12). The Torah bars the ingestion in the same verse in which it explains the blood's function.

The corpus keeps that bar in force three times, listing blood among the four forbidden things (Q 2:173, Q 5:3, Q 16:115). And then, in the passage on the sacrificial animals of its own pilgrimage, it states the principle that resolves the whole matter: *lan yanāla Allāha luḥūmuhā wa-lā dimāʾuhā, wa-lākin yanāluhu al-taqwā minkum*, neither their flesh nor their blood reaches Allah ﷻ, but the reverence from you reaches Him (Q 22:37).

Set the three scriptures in sequence and an arc appears that neither community usually draws. At Sinai the covenant token is blood, applied externally. In the priestly law the covenant token is salt, added to every offering, and this commentary has already shown what salt declares: dependence, since it cannot be produced by the one who brings it. At the corpus the token is withdrawn entirely and what ascends is *taqwā*, the reverence itself, with the flesh and the blood explicitly stated not to reach Him. Blood applied, then salt added, then nothing material at all: the covenant sign is progressively interiorised across the three revelations until only the disposition rises. Mark's cup stands at the middle of that arc, spoken in Sinai's own words on the night of a Passover, and the corpus stands at its end and says which part of any offering ever arrived.

This is one of the eight places where the reader's own scripture would correct the received practice, and one of the five in which the correction is an operation the corpus performs on its own community first. The eight are gathered at VI.3, in thematic order rather than in order of arrival. Q 22:37 is not addressed to Christians. It is addressed to pilgrims bringing camels, and it tells them what does and does not reach Him.

The last clause of the supper is a vow of abstention with a horizon: *I will not drink again of the fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new in the kingdom of God*. A cup renounced now and a cup promised then, and the corpus's every cup is that second one: *wa-suqū sharāban ṭahūrā*, and they are given a pure drink (Q 76:21), *kaʾsan lā laghwun fīhā wa-lā taʾthīm*, a cup in which is no idle talk and no sin (Q 52:23). The wine of the kingdom in both books is not this wine.

### Gethsemane · Mark 14:26-42

> And when they had sung a hymn, they went out to the Mount of Olives. And Jesus said to them: You will all fall away, for it is written, I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep will be scattered. But after I am raised up, I will go before you to Galilee. Peter said to him: Even though they all fall away, I will not. And Jesus said to him: Truly, I tell you, this very night, before the rooster crows twice, you will deny me three times. But he said emphatically: If I must die with you, I will not deny you. And they all said the same.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The hymn is the Hallel, the second half of which is Psalm 118, the psalm the crowd sang at the gate and the parable quoted in the temple. It frames the visit and it is sung on the way out to the garden.

*I will strike the shepherd* is Zechariah 13:7, and the striking in that oracle is God's own act. The shepherd-image, already logged as a partial transit, appears here in its harshest form. And beside the prediction of scattering stands a promise the disciples do not hear: *after I am raised up, I will go before you*, *proaxō hymas*, the verb from the road to Jerusalem, and the young man at the tomb will repeat it to the women. The failure is announced and the regathering is announced in the same breath.

Peter's protest is the moment the corpus's clause about the community was revealed for: *wa-mā Muḥammadun illā rasūlun qad khalat min qablihi al-rusul, a-fa-in māta aw qutila inqalabtum ʿalā aʿqābikum*, if he died or were killed, would you turn back on your heels (Q 3:144). The turning back is predicted in both books, and in both it is followed by mercy rather than by dismissal.

> And they went to a place called Gethsemane. And he said to his disciples: Sit here while I pray. And he took with him Peter and James and John, and began to be greatly distressed and troubled. And he said to them: My soul is very sorrowful, even to death. Remain here and watch. And going a little farther, he fell on the ground and prayed that, if it were possible, the hour might pass from him. And he said: Abba, Father, all things are possible for you. Remove this cup from me. Yet not what I will, but what you will.

This is the centre of the movement and, from the fixed position, the least difficult passage in the Gospel.

*Abba*: the third of the four preserved Aramaic words, and with the fourth one scene away the pattern can now be stated whole. *Talitha koum* to a dead girl, *ephphatha* to a deaf man, *Abba* in prayer, *Elōi Elōi lema sabachthani* from the cross. All four are moments of *address*, two spoken to the afflicted and two spoken upward, and in every case Mark keeps the language the speaker actually used and glosses it for readers who cannot. A text being remade in Greek categories does not preserve the source language at precisely the moments of address and then translate it in an aside. Aramaic retentions, four, and the count is closed.

*All things are possible for you.* He says it upward, in the second person, about the One he is addressing. Chapter nine had him say *all things are possible for the one who trusts*, and here the same phrase is directed where it belongs, and the request follows: *remove this cup*. He asks. He is refused. And the refusal is accepted in the sentence that is the whole doctrine of this commentary in seven Greek words: *all' ou ti egō thelō alla ti sy*, yet not what I will, but what you will.

Follow *thelō* through this Gospel and the arc is complete here. A leper said *if you are willing, you can make me clean*, and the answer was *thelō, I am willing*. Now the willing one places his own will beneath another's, in the same verb. The corpus's sentence for that placement is the one it addresses to everyone: *wa-mā tashāʾūna illā an yashāʾa Allāh*, and you do not will unless Allah ﷻ wills (Q 76:30, Q 81:29). And its portrait of the servant who prays exactly like this is Ayyūb AS, *innī massaniya al-ḍurru wa-anta arḥamu al-rāḥimīn*, harm has touched me, and You are the most merciful of the merciful (Q 21:83), a petition that states the affliction and names the mercy and issues no demand.

Nothing in this scene requires adjustment from the corpus's position, and a great deal in it would require adjustment from any position that holds the one praying and the One prayed to are the same. Two wills are named in one sentence and one is subordinated to the other, by the speaker, on his face on the ground.

#### Structural Commentary · Gethsemane, Mark 14:32-42

**What stands.** A servant, a cup, and the One addressed as *Abba*.

**What moves.** A petition: *all things are possible for you; remove this cup*.

**How they bind.** *Yet not what I will, but what you will.* The binding clause names two wills and ranks them, and the ranking is performed by the lower of the two.

The reading closes because the three are genuinely distinct. Any reading that identifies the one praying with the One prayed to collapses the first register into the third, and the sentence stops parsing: a will subordinated to itself is not a subordination. The corpus states the same ranking as a universal, *and you do not will unless Allah ﷻ wills* (Q 76:30), and its own portrait of the praying servant, Ayyūb AS at Q 21:83, states the affliction and names the mercy and issues no demand. ⟀ GOL on the two wills in one sentence.

> And he came and found them sleeping, and he said to Peter: Simon, are you asleep? Could you not watch one hour? Watch and pray that you may not enter into temptation. The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak. And again he went away and prayed, saying the same words. And again he came and found them sleeping, for their eyes were very heavy, and they did not know what to answer him. And he came the third time and said to them: Are you still sleeping and taking your rest? It is enough; the hour has come. The Son of Man is betrayed into the hands of sinners. Rise, let us be going; see, my betrayer is at hand.

*Ouk ischysas mian hōran grēgorēsai*: you had not the strength to watch one hour. The chain closes. It opened with the forerunner announcing one *mightier*; it ran through the strong man bound, the man among the tombs whom no one had *strength* to subdue, and the disciples who *were not able* to cast out a spirit; and it ends on Peter, awake for less than an hour. Strength, five, and the argument the sequence makes is finished: in this Gospel strength is never held, it is granted, and where it is not granted a man cannot stay awake.

*The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak.* The corpus's anthropology in one clause: *wa-khuliqa al-insānu ḍaʿīfā*, and the human was created weak (Q 4:28), stated in a verse about mercy and lightening. Neither book treats the weakness as a scandal. Both treat it as the reason the watching has to be prayed for rather than mustered.

And the parable's clock is running exactly as it was set: evening has passed, this is the middle watch, and Peter's rooster is two hours away.

### The Arrest · Mark 14:43-52
