> And immediately, while he was still speaking, Judas came, one of the twelve, and with him a crowd with swords and clubs, from the chief priests and the scribes and the elders. Now the betrayer had given them a sign, saying: The one I will kiss is the man. Seize him and lead him away under guard. And when he came, he went up to him at once and said: Rabbi! And he kissed him. And they laid hands on him and seized him. But one of those who stood by drew his sword and struck the servant of the high priest and cut off his ear. And Jesus said to them: Have you come out as against a robber, with swords and clubs to capture me? Day after day I was with you in the temple teaching, and you did not seize me. But let the Scriptures be fulfilled. And they all left him and fled. And a young man followed him, with nothing but a linen cloth about his body. And they seized him, but he left the linen cloth and ran away naked.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The sign agreed with the arresting party is a kiss, and the word Mark uses for the act itself is intensive, *katephilēsen*, he kissed him warmly. The corpus has no verse about this scene, but it has the general law that covers it: *wa-kadhālika jaʿalnā li-kulli nabiyyin ʿaduwwan shayāṭīna al-insi wa-l-jinni yūḥī baʿḍuhum ilā baʿḍin zukhrufa al-qawli ghurūrā*, thus We appointed for every prophet an enemy, satans of humankind and jinn, inspiring one another with ornamented speech as delusion (Q 6:112). Ornamented speech as the instrument of betrayal, and there is no ornament more complete than affection used as a marker.

The sword is drawn and an ear is cut off, and Mark reports the wound and no healing. Luke, later, adds the healing. The earliest text leaves a man mutilated in the dark and the rebuke that follows is aimed at the arresting party's method rather than at the swordsman: *have you come out as against a robber?* The complaint is that a public teacher available daily in the temple has been taken by night with a mob, which is a complaint about procedure, and the corpus's standing description of its own messenger's conduct is the contrast, *qul hādhihi sabīlī adʿū ilā Allāhi ʿalā baṣīratin*, say: this is my way, I call to Allah ﷻ upon clear evidence (Q 12:108), open, argued, in daylight.

*And they all left him and fled.* The prediction of an hour before is fulfilled in four words, and Mark does not soften it: the flight is total.

Then two verses that no evangelist would invent and none of the others keeps. A young man is following, wearing nothing but a linen sheet; they grab at him; he leaves the sheet and runs away naked. There is no explanation, no name, no lesson drawn, and the detail serves no theological purpose whatever. It is the kind of thing a text contains when it is reporting rather than composing, and it belongs on the same list as the family who thought him mad and the ending that stops on a conjunction. This commentary declines to allegorise it and records it as what it looks like: a memory.

### Before the Council · Mark 14:53-65

> And they led Jesus to the high priest. And all the chief priests and the elders and the scribes came together. And Peter had followed him at a distance, right into the courtyard of the high priest. And he was sitting with the guards and warming himself at the fire.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The sixth and last interruption opens, and Mark builds it as a comparison: inside, a man is asked who he is and answers; outside, a man is asked who he is and denies. Two interrogations run at once in the same building. Intercalation, six.

> Now the chief priests and the whole council were seeking testimony against Jesus to put him to death, but they found none. For many bore false witness against him, but their testimony did not agree. And some stood up and bore false witness against him, saying: We heard him say, I will destroy this temple that is made with hands, and in three days I will build another, not made with hands. Yet even about this their testimony did not agree. And the high priest stood up in the midst and asked Jesus: Have you no answer to make? What is it that these men testify against you? But he remained silent and made no answer.

Mark states twice that the testimony did not agree, *isai hai martyriai ouk ēsan*, and the doubling is a legal observation: under the law of that court two witnesses had to concur, and they did not. The corpus's standard for testimony is stated as a command to its own community and it is the inversion of this scene: *kūnū qawwāmīna bi-l-qisṭi shuhadāʾa lillāhi wa-law ʿalā anfusikum*, be upholders of justice, witnesses for Allah ﷻ, even against yourselves (Q 4:135). What is convened here is witness against a man for the sake of a verdict already chosen.

And he answers nothing. *Ho de esiōpa kai ouk apekrinato ouden.* The silence is the servant of Isaiah 53:7, who *opened not his mouth* before his shearers, and the image crossed this far and no further: transit, partial, since the corpus takes up neither that chapter's silence-figure nor its wounding-figure for this prophet. What the corpus does carry is the disposition, *wa-ṣbir wa-mā ṣabruka illā bi-llāh*, and be patient, and your patience is only through Allah ﷻ (Q 16:127), patience itself named as a thing given rather than summoned.

> Again the high priest asked him: Are you the Christ, the Son of the Blessed? And Jesus said: I am, and you will see the Son of Man seated at the right hand of Power, and coming with the clouds of heaven. And the high priest tore his garments and said: Why do we still need witnesses? You have heard his blasphemy. What is your decision? And they all condemned him as deserving death. And some began to spit on him and to cover his face and to strike him, saying to him: Prophesy! And the guards received him with blows.

Here the long deferral ends, and it ends at the exact point where speaking costs everything.

Trace the count that this commentary has kept since the first synagogue: nine silencings, on demons who spoke correctly, on a cleansed leper, on crowds, on the disciples after Peter's confession, on the three coming down the mountain. The one publication in all that time was of the suffering, spoken *parrēsia*, openly. Now, under direct question, in the one setting where the answer is a capital matter, the embargo is lifted. Nothing was ever being hidden for its own sake; the release comes when the release can no longer be mistaken for a bid for power, because it is a confession made under arrest.

And what he actually says is worth reading closely. *Egō eimi*, I am, answering the question as put. Then the content, and the content is two quotations. *Seated at the right hand of Power* is Psalm 110:1, the psalm whose riddle he had opened in the temple; and *coming with the clouds* is Daniel 7:13, whose next verse says the dominion *was given* to that figure. Note the periphrasis: he says *the right hand of Power*, *tēs dynameōs*, avoiding the Name in the manner of the pious speech of that time, which is the same reverence that produces the divine passives running under this whole Gospel. He answers a question about his office by quoting two texts in which the exalted figure is seated by another and given his dominion by another.

The high priest's response is to tear his robes and name it blasphemy, which is the same charge as in the very first controversy of the book, when scribes said *who can forgive sins but God alone* and used the corpus's own sentence. The commentary said then that the doctrine was right and the application wrong, and the same holds here: what has been claimed is a seat *at the right hand*, which is by definition not the throne, and a coming with clouds in a glory that Daniel and Mark alike call conferred.

Mark then does something with a verb he has used exactly once before. The high priest *tore*, *dierrēxen*, his garments. The heavens were *torn* at the baptism and the sanctuary curtain will be *torn* at the death, both by an unnamed hand from above, and here in the middle a man tears his own clothes as a legal gesture of horror. Three tearings: two from above, opening, and one from below, condemning.

*Some began to spit on him.* Spittle, four, and the prediction of chapter ten is now event. The men who cover his face and demand *prophesy* have devised a test that requires him to see without eyes, and the corpus's word over exactly this class of mockery is *yā ḥasratan ʿalā al-ʿibād, mā yaʾtīhim min rasūlin illā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn* (Q 36:30).

### The Denials · Mark 14:66-72

> And as Peter was below in the courtyard, one of the servant girls of the high priest came, and seeing Peter warming himself, she looked at him and said: You also were with the Nazarene, Jesus. But he denied it, saying: I neither know nor understand what you mean. And he went out into the gateway, and the rooster crowed. And the servant girl saw him and began again to say to the bystanders: This man is one of them. But again he denied it. And after a little while the bystanders again said to Peter: Certainly you are one of them, for you are a Galilean. But he began to invoke a curse on himself and to swear: I do not know this man of whom you speak. And immediately the rooster crowed a second time. And Peter remembered how Jesus had said to him: Before the rooster crows twice, you will deny me three times. And he broke down and wept.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The interruption closes and the comparison stands complete. Inside, under oath, before the court that can kill him, a man says who he is. Outside, at a fire, questioned by a serving girl with no power at all, a man says he does not know him. The one who said *even though they all fall away, I will not* is undone by the least dangerous questioner in the building, and the escalation is exact: a denial, then a denial, then oaths and a self-curse.

Mark reports the whole of it, and it is worth remembering who his first readers understood the source to be. This is the most damaging story in the Gospel about the most prominent figure in the early community, and it is preserved with every detail intact, including the cursing. A text managing its reputation removes this. It is the same authentication as the *could not* at Nazareth and the family who came to seize him.

And the corpus, read on this scene, does something that no reader expects until they see it. It has a clause for exactly this case. In the middle of its severest passage about renouncing faith after having held it, it opens a door and specifies who walks through: *illā man ukriha wa-qalbuhu muṭmaʾinnun bi-l-īmān*, except one who is compelled while his heart is at rest with faith (Q 16:106). The exception is not for the strong. It is for the one whose mouth said what his heart did not, under duress, and the corpus's judgment on him is that he stands outside the condemnation entirely.

Mark supplies precisely the evidence that clause requires. He does not report that Peter shrugged and went home. He reports that Peter *remembered*, and then *epibalōn eklaien*, he broke down and wept. The weeping is the state of the heart made visible in the same hour as the denial, and it is the only thing in the scene that testifies to what was inside the man while his mouth was working. Both texts, in their different ways, refuse to let the words be the whole verdict.

And the last word on Peter in this Gospel is not this scene. Two chapters on, at the tomb, the messenger's instruction will name him separately from the others, *tell his disciples and Peter*, and the singling out is a restoration by name. The corpus's own clause and Mark's own ending agree on the disposition of a man who denied under pressure and wept.

### Before Pilate · Mark 15:1-15

> And as soon as it was morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders and scribes and the whole council. And they bound Jesus and led him away and delivered him over to Pilate. And Pilate asked him: Are you the King of the Jews? And he answered him: You have said so. And the chief priests accused him of many things. And Pilate again asked him: Have you no answer to make? See how many charges they bring against you. But Jesus made no further answer, so that Pilate was amazed.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Morning: the last of the four watches. The parable's clock has run its whole course, evening to midnight to cockcrow to dawn, in exactly the order the chapter named, and the disciples have slept through the middle of it.

Pilate's question is political, not theological, because *King of the Jews* is a charge of sedition rather than a religious title, and the answer is a deflection: *sy legeis*, you say so. It neither affirms in the questioner's terms nor denies, and it is the same manoeuvre as *why do you call me good*: the predicate is handed back to the one who offered it. Then silence again, and Pilate's amazement, *thaumazein*, which Mark records as a fact about the governor's experience of the man.

> Now at the feast he used to release for them one prisoner for whom they asked. And among the rebels in prison, who had committed murder in the insurrection, there was a man called Barabbas. And the crowd came up and began to ask Pilate to do as he usually did for them. And he answered them, saying: Do you want me to release for you the King of the Jews? For he perceived that it was out of envy that the chief priests had delivered him up. But the chief priests stirred up the crowd to have him release for them Barabbas instead. And Pilate again said to them: Then what shall I do with the man you call the King of the Jews? And they cried out again: Crucify him. And Pilate said to them: Why? What evil has he done? But they shouted all the more: Crucify him. So Pilate, wishing to satisfy the crowd, released for them Barabbas, and having scourged Jesus, he delivered him to be crucified.

The governor knows. Mark says it plainly: *he perceived that it was out of envy that the chief priests had delivered him up*, and he asks the crowd twice, and the second time he asks *what evil has he done?* and receives no answer, only volume. Then he releases the murderer and hands over the man he has just described as innocent, and the reason Mark gives is not law and not conviction: *boulomenos tō ochlō to hikanon poiēsai*, wishing to satisfy the crowd.

That is the second official in this Gospel destroyed by an audience. Herod knew John was righteous and killed him because of his guests. Pilate knows this man is innocent and condemns him because of a crowd. Mark has built the same portrait twice, and the corpus's diagnosis, spoken to a prophet about a lesser matter, covers both: *wa-takhshā al-nāsa wa-Allāhu aḥaqqu an takhshāh*, and you feared people, when Allah ﷻ is worthier of being feared (Q 33:37). And the corpus states the general rule for anyone standing where these two men stood: *wa-in tuṭiʿ akthara man fī al-arḍi yuḍillūka ʿan sabīli Allāh*, and if you obey most of those on the earth, they will lead you astray from the way of Allah ﷻ (Q 6:116).

The prisoner's name carries an irony Mark never points at. *Bar-Abba* is Aramaic for *son of the father*. The crowd is offered two men and asks for the other son of the father, and the earliest Gospel sets the two names side by side and says nothing whatever about it.

### The Mocking and the Road · Mark 15:16-21

> And the soldiers led him away inside the palace, that is, the governor's headquarters, and they called together the whole battalion. And they clothed him in a purple cloak, and twisting together a crown of thorns, they put it on him. And they began to salute him: Hail, King of the Jews! And they were striking his head with a reed and spitting on him and kneeling down in homage to him. And when they had mocked him, they stripped him of the purple cloak and put his own clothes on him. And they led him out to crucify him. And they compelled a passerby, Simon of Cyrene, who was coming in from the country, the father of Alexander and Rufus, to carry his cross.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The mockery is a coronation performed as a joke: a robe, a crown, a sceptre-reed, a salute, and genuflection. Every element of an enthronement is present and every one is inverted, and this Gospel, which declined to let the crowd bless the king at the gate, allows a barracks to crown him in mockery.

*Spitting on him*: spittle, five, and the count is closed. The fluid that opened a deaf man's ears at Decapolis and a blind man's eyes at Bethsaida, foretold at the third announcement, enacted in the high priest's courtyard, and enacted here for the last time. Two healings and three humiliations, one substance. The servant who used it to open the senses of others is covered in it by men who will not use theirs.

The corpus has a verse that sits over this scene with unusual exactness, and it is spoken at the reckoning to a man who had been arrogant in the world: *dhuq innaka anta al-ʿazīzu al-karīm*, taste it; indeed you are the mighty, the noble (Q 44:49). The corpus knows the device of the sarcastic honorific, and it reserves it for the Day and for those who claimed height. Here the device runs in the opposite direction, a mock-title conferred by soldiers on a man who never claimed the height, and the corpus's own use of the form shows what the soldiers were doing and to whom such mockery properly belongs.

Then Simon, and Mark gives his sons' names, *Alexander and Rufus*, which is how a first-century writer says *you can ask them*. He is *compelled*, *angareuousin*, a technical loanword for a soldier's right of impressment, and he carries the wood.

And here a distinction should be made carefully, because a reader coming from the corpus will feel it immediately. One man carried another man's cross-beam up a hill; that is a fact of Roman procedure and it is what the verb says. The corpus's law about carrying is about something else entirely and it is stated four times: *wa-lā taziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā*, no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another (Q 6:164, Q 17:15, Q 35:18, Q 53:38). Timber can be shouldered by a passerby; *wizr*, the load of what a soul has earned, cannot be transferred at all. Both sentences are true and they are about different objects, and the distinction between them is itself the teaching: what Simon carried was the instrument, and the corpus's law concerns the account.

### The Crucifixion · Mark 15:22-32

> And they brought him to the place called Golgotha, which means Place of a Skull. And they offered him wine mixed with myrrh, but he did not take it. And they crucified him and divided his garments among them, casting lots for them, to decide what each should take. And it was the third hour when they crucified him. And the inscription of the charge against him read, The King of the Jews. And with him they crucified two robbers, one on his right and one on his left. And those who passed by derided him, wagging their heads and saying: Aha! You who would destroy the temple and rebuild it in three days, save yourself, and come down from the cross! So also the chief priests with the scribes mocked him to one another, saying: He saved others; he cannot save himself. Let the Christ, the King of Israel, come down now from the cross that we may see and believe. Those who were crucified with him also reviled him.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The wine mixed with myrrh was an analgesic, offered by custom to the condemned, and *ouk elaben*, he did not take it. The refusal is recorded without comment and it is the last free act the narrative gives him.

Then Psalm 22 begins to run through the scene, and it runs three times. *They divided his garments among them, casting lots* is Psalm 22:18. *Wagging their heads* is Psalm 22:7. And the cry from the cross will be Psalm 22:1. Mark quotes one psalm at the beginning, the middle, and the end of the crucifixion, and the reader who follows the citations back will find where they lead.

Two robbers, *one on his right and one on his left*. Five chapters earlier two disciples asked for exactly those two places, in his glory, and were told the seats were not his to give but were prepared for those for whom they had been prepared. Mark writes the request and its dark fulfilment into the same book and joins them with nothing.

And the mockery contains the truest sentence anyone speaks in this Gospel. *He saved others; he cannot save himself*: *allous esōsen, heauton ou dynatai sōsai*. They intend it as proof of fraud. It is, precisely, the doctrine. A power that is granted per occasion and never owned is exactly a power one cannot turn on oneself at will, and this commentary has watched that grammar operate since the first divine passive: *be cleansed*, *was restored*, *has been prepared*, *no sign will be given*. The corpus puts the same sentence in the mouth of its own Prophet ﷺ as a commanded declaration: *qul lā amliku li-nafsī nafʿan wa-lā ḍarran illā mā shāʾa Allāh*, say: I hold no power to benefit or harm myself, except what Allah ﷻ wills (Q 7:188), and again *qul innī lā amliku lakum ḍarran wa-lā rashadā*, say: I hold no power over harm or right guidance for you (Q 72:21). The mockers state the servant's own creed and think they are refuting him.

#### Structural Commentary · The Mockery, Mark 15:29-32

**What stands.** A man on a cross who has healed others.

**What moves.** Nothing. No descent, no sign, no self-rescue.

**How they bind.** *He saved others; he cannot save himself.* Read as an accusation it alleges fraud. Read structurally it states the relation the whole book has been drawing: a capacity that arrives per occasion cannot be turned on its holder at will, because it was never held.

The taunt closes the reading rather than breaking it, and the corpus puts the identical sentence in its own Prophet's ﷺ mouth as a commanded declaration: *say, I hold no power to benefit or harm myself, except what Allah ﷻ wills* (Q 7:188). ⟀ GOL on the sentence the mockers got right.

*Come down now, that we may see and believe.* It is the sign-demand of chapter eight, made a third time and in its final form, and the answer given then stands: no sign will be given to this generation, because the signs are with Allah ﷻ (Q 6:109), and a sign granted to a demand made in mockery has never once, in either scripture, produced belief.

### The Cry, and the Veil · Mark 15:33-41

> And when the sixth hour had come, there was darkness over the whole land until the ninth hour. And at the ninth hour Jesus cried with a loud voice: Eloi, Eloi, lema sabachthani? which means, My God, my God, why have you forsaken me? And some of the bystanders hearing it said: Behold, he is calling Elijah. And someone ran and filled a sponge with sour wine, put it on a reed and gave it to him to drink, saying: Wait, let us see whether Elijah will come to take him down. And Jesus uttered a loud cry and breathed his last. And the curtain of the temple was torn in two, from top to bottom. And when the centurion, who stood facing him, saw that in this way he breathed his last, he said: Truly this man was a son of God.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Darkness at noon for three hours, which is Amos's oracle, *I will make the sun go down at noon and darken the earth in broad daylight* (Amos 8:9), and the corpus's own register for the Day includes the same failure of the lights, *wa-khasafa al-qamar* (Q 75:8).

Then the cry, and this commentary owes it a careful reading rather than a fast one.

*Elōi, Elōi, lema sabachthani.* The fourth Aramaic retention, kept and glossed, and Matthew, copying, alters the opening word toward Hebrew and produces a smoother text; Mark's version is the harder and the earlier. And Mark keeps something harder still: the bystanders *mishear it*. They think he is calling Elijah, which is a mistake possible only in the Aramaic he actually used. An evangelist composing a theological set-piece does not build in a mishearing; a witness reports what was said and what people thought they heard.

The words are the first line of Psalm 22, and everything turns on whether one reads the line or the psalm. In that culture a psalm was cited by its opening, and the opening carries the whole with it. So read the whole. The psalm that begins *my God, my God, why have you forsaken me* contains, at its own centre, the flat denial of the abandonment it opened with: *for he has not despised or scorned the suffering of the afflicted one; he has not hidden his face from him, but has listened to his cry for help* (Psalm 22:24). *Lo histir panav*, he has not hidden his face. The psalm answers its own first line, and it ends in a congregation praising and *all the families of the nations* turning to the Lord. Mark has been quoting this psalm since the garments were divided. The reader who follows the third quotation back finds a text whose whole trajectory is from apparent abandonment to declared faithfulness and a gathering of the nations, and the centurion, a Gentile, speaks eight verses later.

The corpus states the psalm's own denial in its own words, and it states it to a prophet in a moment of apparent silence from heaven: *mā waddaʿaka rabbuka wa-mā qalā*, your Lord has not forsaken you, nor is He displeased (Q 93:3). The occasion of that verse, in the tradition of its own community, is a period in which revelation paused and the Prophet ﷺ was mocked for having been abandoned. Psalm 22:24 and Q 93:3 are the same sentence in two languages, and each one arrives at the moment its speaker most looked forsaken.

And the corpus has an entire register for the complaint uttered upward, which it neither rebukes nor conceals. *Innamā ashkū baththī wa-ḥuznī ilā Allāh*, I complain of my anguish and grief only to Allah ﷻ, says Yaʿqūb AS (Q 12:86), and the word *ashkū* is a complaint, addressed to the only address that takes it. Ayyūb AS calls out *massaniya al-ḍurr*, harm has touched me (Q 21:83). Yūnus AS cries *from the darknesses*, and the corpus records the answer: *fa-stajabnā lahu wa-najjaynāhu min al-ghamm, wa-kadhālika nunjī al-muʾminīn*, so We responded to him and delivered him from the distress, and thus do We deliver the believers (Q 21:88). And the corpus puts the sharpest form of the question in the mouths of the faithful and answers it in the same verse: *ḥattā yaqūla al-rasūlu wa-lladhīna āmanū maʿahu matā naṣru Allāh, alā inna naṣra Allāhi qarīb*, until the messenger and those with him said, when will the help of Allah ﷻ come? Truly, the help of Allah ﷻ is near (Q 2:214). The cry and the answer occupy one verse.

So the cry from the cross is not, in either book's register, a rupture in the relation. It is an address, made across the appearance of absence, in the words of a psalm that denies the absence in its own middle, by a man whose scripture-tradition and whose successor-tradition both hold that this is precisely what the afflicted servant does. What lies inside that moment for the one who spoke it is not written in either text, and this commentary does not write it. The interior of that cry is disclosed in no scripture, and the only testimony that could disclose it would have to come from the One addressed.

*And the curtain of the temple was torn in two, from top to bottom.* *Eschisthē*, the verb of the first chapter, where the heavens were *torn* at the baptism. The Gospel's frame closes: the barrier above was opened as the work began and the barrier within is opened as it ends, both *from the top*, both by no named hand. Between them stood a high priest tearing his own robes from below. Two openings and one condemnation, and the two openings are not human acts.

Then the centurion. He has watched executions before, and Mark says exactly what convinced him: *seeing that in this way he breathed his last*, the manner of the dying. And he says *alēthōs houtos ho anthrōpos huios theou ēn*. The Greek has no article, which in that position is characteristically qualitative, and the phrase in a Roman soldier's mouth is what it is: a man's judgment, at a cross, in the vocabulary his own world used for figures of divine favour. Mark's placement is the argument. The confession this Gospel has been silencing for fifteen chapters is finally spoken, and it is spoken by a Gentile, about a corpse, with no power in view.

> There were also women looking on from a distance, among whom were Mary Magdalene, and Mary the mother of James the younger and of Joses, and Salome. When he was in Galilee, they followed him and ministered to him, and there were also many other women who had come up with him to Jerusalem.

The disciples fled at the arrest and are not mentioned again. The women are at the cross, and Mark names three and says there were many more. And the verb he uses of them is *diēkonoun autō*, they served him, the same verb as the angels in the wilderness and the woman raised from fever. The serving that has run under this whole Gospel is, at the end, what is still standing at the foot of the cross.

#### Structural Commentary · The Cry, Mark 15:34

**What stands.** The addressee. *Elōi, elōi*: the vocative comes first and it comes twice, with the possessive attached, before any complaint is made.

**What moves.** The complaint, real and unedited, spoken at the ninth hour out of a darkness the narrator has already put over the whole land, and preserved by Mark in the language it was spoken in.

**How they bind.** The order of the clauses. An address issued into total absence is what the sentence describes and what it simultaneously refutes, because a vocative cannot be issued to nothing. The paradox is not resolved; it is held, and the holding is in the grammar.

The corpus builds the same call the same way. Yūnus AS calls *fī al-ẓulumāt*, from inside the darknesses, and the content of the call is the affirmation of the One called, *lā ilāha illā anta*, with the fault placed at the caller and not at the One called (Q 21:87). And *innamā ashkū baththī wa-ḥuznī ilā Allāh*, I complain of my anguish only to Allah ﷻ (Q 12:86), licenses the register outright: the complaint is real and its direction is the one thing that keeps it from becoming a verdict. Collapse the addressee and there is nobody to cry to. Collapse the complaint and the scene is stoic and Mark's own Aramaic is inexplicable. Collapse the order and the sentence is either a report of abandonment or a formula, and it is neither. ⟀ GOL↑ on the cry.

### The Burial · Mark 15:42-47

> And when evening had come, since it was the day of Preparation, that is, the day before the Sabbath, Joseph of Arimathea, a respected member of the council, who was also himself looking for the kingdom of God, took courage and went to Pilate and asked for the body of Jesus. Pilate was surprised to hear that he should have already died. And summoning the centurion, he asked him whether he was already dead. And when he learned from the centurion that he was dead, he granted the corpse to Joseph. And Joseph bought a linen shroud, and taking him down, wrapped him in the linen shroud and laid him in a tomb that had been cut out of the rock. And he rolled a stone against the entrance of the tomb. And Mary Magdalene and Mary the mother of Joses saw where he was laid.

#### Commentary based on Quran

Joseph is a member of the council that condemned him, and Mark says two things about him that belong together: he was *looking for the kingdom of God*, and he *took courage*, *tolmēsas*. A man on the inside who held a conviction he had not declared, and who declares it by an action at the moment when declaring it is most costly.

The corpus has this figure, and gives him a chapter. *Wa-qāla rajulun muʾminun min āli Firʿawna yaktumu īmānahu*, and a believing man from the family of Pharaoh, who concealed his faith, said (Q 40:28), and the surah is named after him, *Ghāfir*, and his speech runs for a dozen verses, and its argument is procedural: would you kill a man for saying my Lord is Allah ﷻ, when he has brought you clear proofs? A concealed believer inside the hostile council who speaks up when the killing is at hand is a type the corpus knows well enough to build a surah around, and Mark's Joseph is that man at the other end of the same story, arriving one day late to say it with his hands instead of his mouth.

Pilate's surprise is a detail no one would compose. Crucifixion killed slowly, sometimes over days, and the governor *ethaumasen* that it was already over and sent for the centurion to confirm it. Mark includes an official verification of the death from the officer who ran the execution, and he includes it in passing, as procedure, not as apologetic.

And the last verse of the chapter is chain of custody: *Mary Magdalene and Mary the mother of Joses saw where he was laid*. Mark names the witnesses and states that they observed the location. The women who watched the death watched the burial, and in three days they will go to the place they watched.

The movement ends here, in a rock-cut tomb with a stone rolled against it, at evening, on the day before a Sabbath. A woman broke a flask over him and was told it would be remembered wherever the message went. A cup was poured and the covenant was named in Sinai's words, and the corpus said what part of any offering ever reaches Him. A man prayed with his face on the ground and placed his own will beneath another's. His closest companion could not stay awake, and then denied him, and wept. He was asked directly and answered directly, quoting two texts in which the exalted figure receives what he has. He was mocked with a creed he held, that he could not save himself. He cried out in the words of a psalm that denies the abandonment it opens with. The veil was torn from the top. A soldier said what fifteen chapters had suppressed. And a frightened councillor found his courage after everything was already lost.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** The record, written before the bringing-into-being, which the corpus states as a general law at Q 57:22 and which Mark states of this week in three announcements and one *kathōs gegraptai*.

**What moves.** The enacting. A flask broken over his head, a man seeking a convenient hour, a cup asked to pass and then not asked again, a denial sworn three times, and a body taken down before evening. Three moral values and one register of cost.

**How they bind.** The asymmetry. The going is written and the betraying carries a woe, in one sentence, unreconciled, and the corpus states the mechanism in the adversary's own mouth: *do not blame me, blame yourselves*.

Collapse the record into the deed and the woe at Mk 14:21 is unjust. Collapse the deed into the record and the anointing is not hers and the memorial is empty. Collapse the asymmetry and Gethsemane is theatre, since a subordination that was never a real request subordinates nothing. ⟀ GOL on the written-and-willed asymmetry.

## The Tomb · Mark 16:1-20

Eight verses, and then a problem the manuscripts themselves report. The Gospel that began with a torn heaven ends with a rolled stone and a sentence that stops before it finishes.

:::box What to Watch
**The stone is rolled back by a grammar that names nobody,** answering the women's own question.

**He *was raised*,** passive, where the corpus writes the Name in the active: *bal rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh*.

**Peter is named separately** out of the group he had cursed his way out of.

**The book stops on a conjunction,** at fear, and Q 10:62 lifts it.
:::
### The Women at the Tomb · Mark 16:1-8

> When the Sabbath was past, Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James, and Salome bought spices, so that they might go and anoint him. And very early on the first day of the week, when the sun had risen, they went to the tomb. And they were saying to one another: Who will roll away the stone for us from the entrance of the tomb? And looking up, they saw that the stone had been rolled back, for it was very large.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The three named women are the three who watched the death. They buy spices, they plan, they set out at first light, and on the way they realise they have no answer to the one practical problem: the stone.

And here the pair Mark set up two chapters ago completes itself. In Bethany a woman nobody named, who asked nobody's permission and made no arrangements, broke a flask over his head and was told she had anointed his body beforehand for burial, *proelaben*, she anticipated. Here three women who are named, who bought the materials and made the plan, arrive to perform the anointing and find there is no body to anoint. The unbidden anointing succeeded; the organised one failed. Mark writes both and joins them with nothing, and the arrangement is the argument, as it was with the widow and the falling stones.

*Looking up, they saw that the stone had been rolled back*: *anakekylistai*, a perfect passive. Their question was *who will roll it away for us*, and the answer arrives in a grammar that names nobody. The convention that has run under this entire Gospel, the cleansing commanded, the hand restored, the seats prepared, the sign not given, is operating at the door of the tomb: the obstacle is removed and the Remover is unstated.

> And entering the tomb, they saw a young man sitting on the right side, dressed in a white robe, and they were alarmed. And he said to them: Do not be alarmed. You seek Jesus of Nazareth, who was crucified. He has risen; he is not here. See the place where they laid him. But go, tell his disciples and Peter that he is going before you to Galilee. There you will see him, just as he told you.

*Ēgerthē*: he *was raised*. Passive, and it is the passive of the whole book. Not *he rose*, which the Greek could have said and does not; the verb hands the act to the unnamed Agent who has been operating under every scene since a leper was told *be cleansed*. And this is the sentence toward which the corpus's own account has been pointing from the beginning: *bal rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayhi*, rather, Allah ﷻ raised him to Himself (Q 4:158), and *innī mutawaffīka wa-rāfiʿuka ilayya*, I am taking you and raising you to Myself (Q 3:55). Mark's grammar leaves the blank where the corpus writes the Name. The earliest Gospel says *he was raised* and declines to say by whom; the corpus says by whom and uses the active voice. Between them there is no contradiction at all, only a reverent silence and its filling.

And the announcement cashes two threads at once. *He is going before you to Galilee*, *proagei hymas*, the verb from the road to Jerusalem and from the walk to the garden, kept to the last page: the one who goes in front is still in front. And *tell his disciples and Peter*: the denier is named separately, singled out of the group he had cursed his way out of. The corpus's clause for the compelled denier whose heart was at rest (Q 16:106) is answered here in narrative, and the answer is a name on a list.

Also cashed is the prediction Mark placed at the fasting-question in chapter two, where the bridegroom would be *taken away*, *aparthē*, in the passive with no agent named. Fourteen chapters later the taking is reported in the same voice.

> And they went out and fled from the tomb, for trembling and astonishment had seized them, and they said nothing to anyone, for they were afraid.

In the oldest manuscripts, that is the end.

The Greek is *ephobounto gar*, and *gar* is a connective that does not begin sentences and almost never ends them; the book closes on a conjunction, mid-breath, with the reader waiting for the rest of a clause that never comes. It is the strangest ending in ancient literature and it is what the two earliest complete Bibles, Sinaiticus and Vaticanus, both contain.

And the last irony of this Gospel is exact. Fifteen chapters of silencings, nine of them counted here, imposed on demons who spoke correctly, on healed men, on crowds, on disciples. At the end, for the first time, a messenger issues an instruction to *speak*, and the women say nothing to anyone, because they were afraid. The book that suppressed testimony throughout ends with testimony withheld, and stops.

Whether Mark intended that or whether a final leaf was lost cannot be settled, and this commentary does not settle it. What can be said is what the reader is left holding: a stone rolled away by nobody named, a raising in the passive voice, a promise that he goes before them, a denier restored by name, and three frightened women running.

And here the last sentence of the earliest Gospel meets the corpus's own word about fear, and the meeting is the reason this commentary was written. Mark's final clause is *ephobounto gar*, for they were afraid. The corpus's proclamation over those who are His is *alā inna awliyāʾa Allāhi lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa-lā hum yaḥzanūn*, truly, upon the friends of Allah ﷻ there is no fear, nor shall they grieve (Q 10:62). One text breaks off at fear. The other opens by lifting it. The sentence that Mark left unfinished on the page is finished six centuries later in another language, and the reader who holds both books watches it complete across the gap.

The corpus adds, of those who fell in His way, the clause that speaks to what the women thought they had come to anoint: *wa-lā taqūlū li-man yuqtalu fī sabīli Allāhi amwātun, bal aḥyāʾun wa-lākin lā tashʿurūn*, do not say of those slain in the way of Allah ﷻ that they are dead; rather they are living, but you do not perceive (Q 2:154). They went to a tomb with spices and were told to look at an empty shelf. *You do not perceive* is the corpus's own gloss on that morning.

### The Longer Ending · Mark 16:9-20

> Now when he rose early on the first day of the week, he appeared first to Mary Magdalene, from whom he had cast out seven demons. She went and told those who had been with him, as they mourned and wept. But when they heard that he was alive and had been seen by her, they would not believe it. After these things he appeared in another form to two of them, as they were walking into the country. And they went back and told the rest, but they did not believe them. Afterward he appeared to the eleven themselves as they were reclining at table, and he rebuked them for their unbelief and hardness of heart, because they had not believed those who saw him after he had risen. And he said to them: Go into all the world and proclaim the gospel to the whole creation. Whoever believes and is baptized will be saved, but whoever does not believe will be condemned. And these signs will accompany those who believe: in my name they will cast out demons; they will speak in new tongues; they will pick up serpents with their hands; and if they drink any deadly poison, it will not hurt them; they will lay their hands on the sick, and they will recover. So then the Lord Jesus, after he had spoken to them, was taken up into heaven and sat down at the right hand of God. And they went out and preached everywhere, while the Lord worked with them and confirmed the message by accompanying signs.

#### Commentary based on Quran

The manuscript situation is not a matter of opinion and should be stated plainly, because it is the clearest documented instance in the New Testament of a text growing after its author put it down.

These twelve verses are absent from Sinaiticus and Vaticanus, the two oldest complete Greek Bibles. Eusebius and Jerome both report that the accurate copies known to them ended at verse eight. A number of manuscripts carry a different, shorter ending instead, and some carry both, one after the other; one Greek codex inserts an additional paragraph into the middle of these verses. The vocabulary and sentence-rhythm differ measurably from the rest of Mark, and the passage opens by re-introducing Mary Magdalene as though she had not been named three times in the preceding chapter, which is what happens when a paragraph written for one context is attached to another.

The multiplicity is the evidence. The tradition did not merely add an ending; it added *endings*, in the plural, at different times and in different places, because verse eight felt unfinished to everyone who copied it. That is a community completing a book, in the open, in a record we can still read.

And this commentary reads the addition with care rather than with scorn, because two things in it are worth noticing.

The first is that the interpolating hand, whoever he was, still wrote in the received grammar. *Anelēmphthē*: he *was taken up*. Passive, agent unnamed, exactly like *ēgerthē* and *aparthē* and *hētoimastai* before it. Even the man completing the Gospel a century or more later reached instinctively for the construction that hands the act upward, and the corpus's own vocabulary for the same event is the active form of that very verb, *rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayhi*. The convention was so deep in the material that an editor could not write outside it.

The second is what the appended list does that the original never does. *In my name they will cast out demons; they will speak in new tongues; they will pick up serpents; if they drink poison it will not hurt them.* Set that beside how the corpus lists the same category of works for the same figure: *and I heal the blind and the leper, and I give life to the dead, **by the permission of Allah ﷻ***, with the permission-clause attached four separate times in a single verse (Q 3:49), and again in the retelling at Q 5:110. And set it beside how Mark himself narrates: a leper cleansed in the passive, a hand restored in the passive, a man looking up to heaven before he breaks bread, a sign refused because it *will not be given*.

The appended list carries no permission-clause at all. It is an inventory of capacities promised to believers, with the grammar of grant removed. That is precisely the drift this commentary has been tracking since a healed man in the Decapolis was told to say what *the Lord* had done and went out saying what *Jesus* had done: attribution creeping one link down the chain. The first dilution of this Gospel is visible not only in the manuscript record but in the syntax, and the syntax is the more interesting witness, because it shows what was lost rather than merely that something was added.

The serpents and the poison are the clearest markers of legendary accretion, and the reader may weigh them as such. What matters for this commentary is narrower: the earliest recoverable Gospel of Mark ends at *for they were afraid*, and everything after that is the tradition's own hand, documented, dated, and visible.

This last movement closes the Gospel. The women bought their spices too late, the stone had been moved by nobody named, the raising was reported in the voice this whole book speaks in, the denier was called by name, and the last word was fear. What came after the last word came from other hands, and the manuscripts say so themselves.

#### Structural Commentary · The Movement Entire

**What stands.** The completed count. A seventh spent before a first can open, which is the ordinal argument this Gospel has now used three times, at the Sabbath, at the mountain, and here at the door of a tomb.

**What moves.** The going. Women who bought spices when the Sabbath ended and walked out at sunrise to perform an anointing that had already been performed and was no longer possible, asking on the road who would move the stone.

**How they bind.** The unnamed Agent. *It had been rolled back.* *He was raised.* Both perfect passives, the obstacle removed and the Remover unstated, which is the convention the whole book has been writing and which the corpus writes in the active voice with the Name in it: *bal rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh*.

Collapse the count and the first day is a date rather than a beginning. Collapse the going and there is nobody present to be told. Collapse the unnamed Agent and the passives are stylistic, and the one grammar that has run from the leper to the tomb has no meaning at its own conclusion. ⟀ GOL on the unnamed Agent at the door.

# PART V · THE THREADS

Eight things run the length of this Gospel that no single passage displays. They were counted aloud as they arrived; here they are gathered, because a thread is only visible when it is laid out straight. A ninth section stands apart at the end, because the preposition it follows is a Quranic thread rather than a Markan one, and it is the hinge on which the two books turn toward each other.

## V.1 · The Unnamed Agent

The deepest habit of Mark's Greek is the reverent passive: an act reported with the acting party left unstated, in the manner of a writer who will not put the Name in an ordinary sentence. Three instances are formal and unmistakable. *Katharisthēti*, be cleansed, spoken to the leper (Mk 1:41), where the healer states his willing and then commands that a cleansing occur. *Apekatestathē*, the hand was restored (Mk 3:5), with no touch and no formula. *Hētoimastai*, it has been prepared, of the seats at the right and left (Mk 10:40), where the sentence's whole point is that the preparing was done by someone other than the one being asked.

Around those three the convention runs continuously. *Aphientai*, your sins are forgiven (Mk 2:5), in the scene where the scribes quote the corpus's own sentence about who alone forgives. *Aparthē*, the bridegroom will be taken away (Mk 2:20). *Dedotai*, to you it has been given (Mk 4:11). *Metemorphōthē*, he was transfigured (Mk 9:2). *Ou dothēsetai*, no sign will be given (Mk 8:12). And at Mk 13:20 the election clause, *tous eklektous hous exelexato*, where Mark names the Lord for the shortening and then leaves the choosing of the elect standing without a further agent. *Anakekylistai*, the stone has been rolled back (Mk 16:4). *Ēgerthē*, he was raised (Mk 16:6). And, in the appended ending written by a later hand, *anelēmphthē*, he was taken up (Mk 16:19), which shows how deep the habit ran.

Every one of these is a place where the corpus writes the Name in the active voice. *Rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayh*. *Aʿadda Allāhu lahum*. *Man yaghfiru al-dhunūba illā Allāh*. The two texts are performing the same theology with opposite typography: Mark leaves the blank and the corpus fills it. A reader who has been trained by the corpus reads Mark's blanks as blanks, which is what they are.

## V.2 · Strength, Which Is Never Owned

Five occurrences of one root, and read in order they make a single argument.

The forerunner announces *ho ischyroteros*, one mightier than himself, and immediately measures the distance by the sandal-thong (Mk 1:7). The teaching says nobody enters the strong man's house, *tou ischyrou*, without first binding him (Mk 3:27). On the far shore, of the man among the tombs, *no one had the strength*, *oudeis ischyen*, to subdue him, and the arriving authority does it without chains (Mk 5:4). At the foot of the mountain the disciples, who had been given authority over unclean spirits, report *ouk ischysan*, they were not able (Mk 9:18). And in the garden, to Peter: *ouk ischysas mian hōran grēgorēsai*, you had not the strength to watch one hour (Mk 14:37).

The chain descends. It begins with a mightier one announced and ends with a chosen companion unable to stay awake, and in between the only strength that accomplishes anything arrives from outside the one exercising it. That is the corpus's law of every messenger, *wa-mā kāna li-rasūlin an yaʾtiya bi-āyatin illā bi-idhni Allāh* (Q 40:78), and it is why the mockers at the cross, saying *he cannot save himself*, were stating the doctrine and not refuting it.

## V.3 · The Spitting

One substance, five occurrences, and it crosses from healing to humiliation exactly at the midpoint of the book.

He spits and touches the tongue of the deaf man at Decapolis (Mk 7:33). He spits on the eyes of the blind man at Bethsaida (Mk 8:23). At the third announcement he says *they will spit on him* (Mk 10:34). In the high priest's courtyard *some began to spit on him* (Mk 14:65). In the praetorium the soldiers spit while they kneel in mockery (Mk 15:19).

Two healings, one prophecy, two humiliations. The fluid a servant used to open other people's senses is used on him by men who will not open theirs, and Mark laid the vocabulary down four chapters before he needed it.

## V.4 · Four Words He Actually Said

Mark writes in Greek for readers who do not know Aramaic, and four times he keeps the Aramaic and translates it in an aside.

*Talitha koum*, to a dead girl (Mk 5:41). *Ephphatha*, to a deaf man (Mk 7:34). *Abba*, in the garden (Mk 14:36). *Elōi Elōi lema sabachthani*, from the cross (Mk 15:34).

All four are moments of address, two spoken downward to the afflicted and two spoken upward in prayer, and none of them is a formula or a title. A text being recast in Greek philosophical categories does not preserve the original language precisely at the points of address and then gloss it for the reader; that is what a text does when it is carrying a memory it will not smooth. And the fourth is preserved so faithfully that Mark also records the bystanders *mishearing* it, a mistake possible only in the Aramaic actually used.

## V.5 · The Interruptions

Six times Mark opens a story, inserts a second story whole, and returns to the first, so that each reads the other. The device is his signature and no later evangelist uses it as consistently.

The family sets out to seize him and the Beelzebul charge is seated between departure and arrival (Mk 3:20-35): those who should know him best call him mad, those whose office is discernment call him possessed, and the frame asks which misreading the warning falls on. Jairus's errand is suspended for the woman with the flow of blood (Mk 5:21-43), and the two are stitched with a repeated number, twelve years of bleeding and a girl twelve years old. The mission of the Twelve frames the killing of the forerunner (Mk 6:7-30), so that no one hears *they went out and proclaimed* without hearing what proclamation costs. The cursed fig tree frames the temple action (Mk 11:12-21), leaves without fruit on either side of the scene. The plot and the betrayal frame the woman with the alabaster flask (Mk 14:1-11), a struck bargain on each side of a broken jar. And Peter's courtyard frames the council's interrogation (Mk 14:53-72): inside, a man is asked who he is and answers; outside, a man is asked who he is and denies.

A seventh instance is not an interruption but the same instinct working across pericope boundaries: scribes who *devour widows' houses*, then a widow giving her last two coins, then the prediction that not one stone of the building will be left (Mk 12:38-13:2). Devoured, giving, demolished, with no connecting word.

## V.6 · What Crossed, and What Did Not

Images travel between scriptures, and the honest report distinguishes three cases.

**Crossed whole.** The sandal-thong of the servant too low to untie it (Mk 1:7), which the corpus's own tradition keeps in the mouth of its Prophet ﷺ concerning these two figures. And the camel through the needle's eye (Mk 10:25), which the corpus writes with the same two objects and in the same argumentative position, against those whose wealth shuts the upper door: *ḥattā yalija al-jamalu fī sammi al-khiyāṭ* (Q 7:40).

**Crossed in part.** The shepherd and the scattered sheep: the corpus keeps the compassion and the care, *raʾfatan wa-raḥmatan* (Q 57:27), and does not build the shepherd as a title. Esther's half-kingdom oath, borrowed as a frame for Herod's banquet and inverted, since in the older book the machinery saves a people and here it kills a prophet. The cloaks spread on the road (Mk 11:8), which is Jehu's acclamation without the enthronement that followed it. The silent servant before the shearers (Mk 14:61), whose silence-figure the corpus does not take up for this prophet. And the cup, which is the sharpest case: the wrath-cup of Isaiah and Jeremiah reaches Gethsemane, and the corpus reserves *kaʾs* entirely for the drink of the garden, so the image crossed the earlier scriptures and stopped.

**Declined by Mark.** The heavens rolled like a scroll: Isaiah has it, the corpus takes it up as *ka-ṭayyi al-sijill* (Q 21:104), and Mark, quoting the verses around it, leaves it out. Zechariah's oracle of the king on a colt: staged and unquoted, while Matthew quotes it in full. And the second clause of Psalm 2:7, *today I have begotten you*, which the voice at the baptism stops one phrase short of, and which the Western text of Luke's parallel shows was available to be written.

## V.7 · The Roughness That Authenticates

Part III argued that Mark's unpolished surface is the receipt of a text preserved rather than composed. The walk through the Gospel produced the instances, and here they are, twelve of them, in order.

The composite citation filed under Isaiah alone (Mk 1:2), which both copyists correct by dropping the Malachi half. The wrong high priest in the showbread precedent (Mk 2:26), which both copyists silently omit. *He could do no mighty work there* at Nazareth (Mk 6:5), which Matthew converts to *did not*. The permission of a staff and sandals in the mission kit (Mk 6:8-9), which both copyists forbid. *Prayer* alone as the answer at the foot of the mountain (Mk 9:29), to which later hands add *and fasting*. The crowd blessing *the kingdom* at the entry (Mk 11:10), which Matthew moves to the son and Luke to the king. Zechariah's oracle left unquoted (Mk 11:7). No command to repeat the supper (Mk 14:22-24), *do this in remembrance of me* belonging to Luke and Paul. The severed ear left unhealed (Mk 14:47), which Luke heals. The Aramaic cry left in its harder form and its mishearing preserved (Mk 15:34), which Matthew smooths toward Hebrew. Pilate's surprise that the death came so quickly (Mk 15:44), a detail no apologist composes. And the ending on a conjunction (Mk 16:8), which the tradition could not bear and completed in at least three different ways.

Twelve places where the earliest text is harder than the texts derived from it, and in every one the direction of change runs from rough toward smooth. That direction is the argument. It is also the property this reader recognises from his own book, where a messenger's file contains *he frowned and turned away* addressed to himself in the third person.



## V.8 · The Silencings

Nine times in this Gospel someone is told not to speak, and the count was opened in the first synagogue and carried to the council. It is the most explicitly tracked thread in the book and the one its last page turns on.

The demon in the Capernaum synagogue, muzzled mid-confession (Mk 1:25). The demons at the door, silenced *because they knew him* (Mk 1:34). The cleansed leper, told to say nothing and to go to the priest (Mk 1:44). The unclean spirits by the sea, forbidden to make him known (Mk 3:12). The household at Jairus's house, charged strictly that no one should know (Mk 5:43). The Decapolis crowd after the deaf man is opened, charged and proclaiming the more (Mk 7:36). The blind man at Bethsaida, sent home and told not to enter the village (Mk 8:26). The disciples after Peter's confession, charged to tell no one about him (Mk 8:30). And the three coming down from the mountain, told to say nothing *until the Son of Man should have risen from the dead* (Mk 9:9), the only one of the nine with a stated terminus.

Two things follow. The embargo is temporary by design, which the ninth instance says outright, and it is the corpus's own deferral written into narrative: *thumma inna ʿalaynā bayānah*, then upon Us is its explication (Q 75:19), the explication placed after the recitation and assigned upstream. And the silenced parties are of two kinds and only one is a person who understood: the spirits are silenced because their testimony is accurate and inadmissible, which is the Provenance Firewall enacted on a speaker rather than on a text, while the healed and the disciples are silenced because the title in circulation carried a politics the office did not.

And then the inversion, which is the book's closing irony and the reason this thread is worth counting. Every one of the nine is an instruction not to speak, and the last instruction in the Gospel is the opposite: *go, tell his disciples and Peter*. It is the one command to speak, and it is the one that is disobeyed. *They said nothing to anyone, for they were afraid.*

:::box The Eight Threads at a Glance
**The unnamed Agent.** Three formal passives and nine further instances of the convention. Every one is a place the corpus writes the Name in the active.

**Strength.** Five occurrences, descending from *one mightier* to a companion who could not watch an hour.

**The spitting.** Five, crossing from healing to humiliation at the midpoint of the book.

**Four Aramaic words.** All four moments of address, two downward and two upward, each glossed for the Greek reader.

**Six interruptions,** plus a seventh working across pericope boundaries.

**Transits.** Two crossed whole, five crossed in part, three declined by Mark himself.

**Twelve roughnesses,** in every case running from rough toward smooth in the texts derived from Mark.

**Nine silencings,** every one an instruction not to speak, against a single instruction to speak that is disobeyed on the last page.
:::
## V.9 · The Preposition

One Arabic word carries a doctrine, and it appeared at eleven points in this reading: ten in the corpus and once in Mark's Greek, which was counted in its place as it arrived. *ʿInda*, with, in the presence of, in the keeping of.

The Mother of the Book is *ʿindahu*, with Him (Q 13:39). What is with you runs out and *mā ʿinda Allāhi bāq*, what is with Allah ﷻ remains (Q 16:96). Maryam AS is found with provision, and asked, she says *huwa min ʿindi Allāh* (Q 3:37). The signs are *ʿinda Allāh* (Q 6:109), which is the answer to the sign-demand Mark's Messiah refuses in the passive. The appointed term is *ʿindah* (Q 6:2). The martyrs are *aḥyāʾun ʿinda rabbihim*, living, with their Lord (Q 3:169), which is the corpus's form of the burning-bush argument Mark's Messiah makes to the Sadducees. And the Hour is *ʿinda* four times over (Q 7:187, Q 31:34, Q 41:47, Q 43:85), the densest cluster in the corpus, all of it about the one thing Mk 13:32 says the Son does not know.

The eleventh is Mark's own, in Greek, at the place where it matters most, and it was numbered link five in the walk because that is where the Gospel reached it: *para tō theō panta dynata*, with God all things are possible (Mk 10:27), spoken when the disciples ask who then can be saved. What is impossible in the register of human capacity is not impossible in the register of what is with Him. That is the whole doctrine of both books in one preposition, and the Gospel reaches for it at the exact point where human capability has been shown to end.

---

:::box Part V Summary

**The threads were counted as they arrived,** not assembled afterwards, which is why the counts are checkable against the passages rather than against this list.

**One verb of strength, one gesture, four Aramaic words, six interruptions, three divine passives, twelve roughnesses, and nine silencings,** with a ledger of images that crossed between the two scriptures whole, in part, or not at all, and one preposition standing apart at the close.

**Every thread runs in one direction.** Strength is announced, then bound, then found unbindable, then fails in the disciples' hands. Attribution is commanded upward and drifts downward within two verses of being commanded.

**And the roughness is the receipt.** What Mark declined to remove is the reason his text can be trusted, and the two Gospels that copied him let a reader watch the removing happen.
:::

# PART VI · THE CLOSING ARCHITECTURE

## VI.1 · What the Reading Found

The commentary set out from a single asymmetry: four of the five figures the corpus says were given a book carry heavy records, and the fifth carries none. It read that silence as a guard rather than an elevation, and then walked the earliest Gospel from inside the position the corpus builds.

What it found, passage by passage, is one direction running the length of the book. The healer says *if you are willing* before he says *be clean*. The cleansing and the restoring and the preparing are written in a grammar that leaves the acting party unstated. He looks up to heaven before he breaks bread and before he opens ears. He says whoever receives me receives not me but the one who sent me. He says the seats at his right and left are not his to give. He says no sign will be given. He says all things are possible for you, remove this cup, yet not what I will but what you will. He says not even the Son knows the hour. And he is raised in the passive voice by an Agent the book declines to name.

That direction is the corpus's own, attached to every work it records of this prophet, four times in a single verse: *bi-idhni Allāh*, by the permission of Allah ﷻ. The two texts describe one circuit from its two ends. Mark, standing below, records the servant looking up. The corpus, speaking from above, records what was said when he looked and Who answered.

## VI.2 · The Counterfeit Law

One structural observation deserves its own heading, because it explains why the two records differ in the way they do.

The corpus runs two devices on the prophetic file and they must not be confused. On ʿĪsā AS and Yaḥyā AS it runs the clean file, which is the voluntariness guard: servanthood declared by a figure who is never faulted is chosen, where servanthood proved by deficiency would be conceded. On the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ it runs the marked file, which is the anti-counterfeit device: seven recorded corrections, two commands to seek forgiveness, and an anti-cult clause revealed on a battlefield when a rumour of his death ran through the ranks. No impostor writes his own rebukes. The corrections are tamper-evidence.

Mark carries the same tamper-evidence in his own register, and V.7 counted twelve instances of it. A family who thought him mad. A *could not* at Nazareth. Disciples who never understand. Peter called Satan. A wrong high priest. An ending on a conjunction. Both books authenticate by what they declined to remove, and the reader posited in Part I recognises the property immediately, because his own scripture taught it to him.

And the same law explains the longer ending. A tradition that completes a book in three different ways, in the open, in a record we can still read, is a tradition whose additions are visible precisely because the original was not managed. The dilution is documented because the source was not.

## VI.3 · The Eight Corrections

Eight places where the reader's own scripture would correct what the received picture made of the Gospel. They are listed with their locations and, where it applies, with the note that the corpus performs the same operation on its own community first. Five of the eight carry that note.

**One. Generation.** The received picture made the sonship literal, *begotten not made, from the substance of the Father*. The corpus denies offspring and names what replaces it in the same breath, *kun fa-yakūn* (Q 2:117, Q 19:35). And the correction is not needed against Mark, who quotes Psalm 2 at the baptism and stops one clause before *today I have begotten you*.

**Two. Number.** The received picture counted. The corpus forbids the numeral in the very verse in which it supplies three designations for one referent, *wa-lā taqūlū thalāthah* (Q 4:171). Mark never counts persons.

**Three. The equational sentence.** The received picture wrote the identity as a creed. The corpus condemns the convertible identity at Q 5:17 and then writes that very construction in its own voice and fills it correctly, *fa-Allāhu huwa al-Walī wa-huwa yuḥyī al-mawtā* (Q 42:9), six times over. The condemnation was never of the grammar; it was of the occupant. The office of protector and life-giver is real and the quarrel is about who is seated in it.

**Four. The blood.** *This is my blood of the covenant* is Sinai's phrase, and at Sinai the blood is thrown *upon* the people and never drunk, with the ingestion barred by a law that states its own reason (Exodus 24:8, Leviticus 17:11). The corpus keeps the bar three times and then states the principle that resolves it: *neither their flesh nor their blood reaches Allah ﷻ, but the reverence from you reaches Him* (Q 22:37). **This correction is addressed to the corpus's own pilgrims, bringing their own animals.** The covenant token is interiorised across three revelations, blood applied, salt added, and finally nothing material at all.

**Five. Interpretive authority.** The received picture ranked tradition beside commandment. Mark's Messiah separates them, *you leave the commandment of God and hold to the tradition of men* (Mk 7:8), and the corpus names the mechanism: *they took their scholars and their monks as lords besides Allah ﷻ* (Q 9:31), and lists four invented dedications, *Allah ﷻ has not instituted the baḥīra, nor the sāʾiba* (Q 5:103). **The corpus's own community is warned in the same terms**, against inventing what was not given.

**Six. The images and the intercessions.** The received picture developed a devotional apparatus around the figures. The corpus's fence is *laysa ka-mithlihi shayʾun* (Q 42:11), and it fences its own community first and hardest: **its own Prophet ﷺ is told to say *I hold no power to benefit or harm myself*** (Q 7:188), and the anti-cult clause of Q 3:144 was revealed to prevent exactly this development around him. The Gospel's own material runs the same way, since the seats are not his to give and the hour is not his to know.

**Seven. Calculation of the end.** The received picture produced periodised timetables. Mk 13:32 disclaims the timetable at the centre of an apocalypse, and the corpus makes the refusal its whole posture, six times *baghtatan*, suddenly, and the reason stated: *I almost conceal it, that every soul may be recompensed for what it strives* (Q 20:15). **The corpus disciplines its own community here**, since the appetite for dates is not confined to any one tradition.

**Eight. Attribution.** The subtlest and the one this commentary watched happen in the text. A healed man in the Decapolis is told to tell what *the Lord* has done for him, and goes out telling what *Jesus* has done for him (Mk 5:19-20), and Mark preserves the slippage without smoothing it. The same drift is visible in the appended ending, where a list of signs is promised to believers with the permission-clause removed, against Q 3:49, which attaches *bi-idhni Allāh* four times in one verse. **This is the correction the corpus applies most often to everyone, including itself**: *wa-mā bikum min niʿmatin fa-mina Allāh* (Q 16:53).

:::box The Eight Corrections at a Glance
**One. Generation,** answered by *kun fa-yakūn*. Mark never carried it.

**Two. Number,** barred in the verse that supplies three designations for one referent.

**Three. The equational sentence,** where the frame is kept and the occupant refused.

**Four. The blood,** where Q 22:37 states what reaches Him. *Addressed to the corpus's own pilgrims.*

**Five. Interpretive authority,** scholars taken as lords and dedications invented. *The corpus warns its own community in the same terms.*

**Six. The devotional apparatus,** fenced by *laysa ka-mithlihi shayʾun*. *Its own Prophet ﷺ is fenced first and hardest.*

**Seven. Calculation of the end,** refused six times as *baghtatan*. *The corpus disciplines its own community here.*

**Eight. Attribution,** the drift Mark preserved in the Decapolis and the appended ending performs. *This one the corpus applies to everyone, including itself.*
:::

## VI.4 · What the Reader Is Left Holding

Not a harmony, and not a polemic. Two books that were written six centuries apart, in two languages, by communities that have spent most of their shared history telling each other they hold incompatible accounts of one man.

Read together, passage by passage, what they hold is one figure who never claimed what was later claimed for him, whose every ability arrived by permission and was acknowledged upward before it was used, who withdrew alone in the dark to petition, who told his closest companions that the highest thing they wanted was not in his gift, who prayed with his face on the ground that a cup might pass and then placed his own will underneath another's, and who was taken up by a hand that one book names and the other reverently leaves blank.

The corpus says he was only a servant on whom favour was placed. The Gospel says he came not to be served but to serve. There is no daylight between those sentences.

And the last word of the earliest Gospel is fear, cut off mid-clause, and the first word the corpus speaks over those who are His is that the fear is lifted. The reader who holds both books watches one sentence finish across six hundred years, in another language, from the other side.

Everything in this commentary rests on constructions anyone can check: a concordance, a lexicon, a standard grammar, and the manuscripts themselves. Nothing in it asks anyone to take a word on authority, which is the only way a reading of two scriptures that both refuse to rest on authority could honestly be offered.

:::box Part VI Summary

**Eight corrections, named and counted,** and five of them are the same operation the corpus performs on itself: relocating an agency, bounding a quantifier, restoring a permission, correcting a name, releasing a token while the covenant stands.

**The counterfeit law governs texts before it governs offices.** What is faked is what is clean; what authenticates is the defect the original declined to remove; and the defence is publication with the roughness intact.

**And the two books end on one sentence.** The earliest Gospel stops at fear, mid-clause, and the corpus's first word over those who are His is that the fear is lifted.
:::

---

# APPENDIX A · THE STRUCTURAL COMMENTARY

Readers who want to see the instrument rather than only its work will find it here, in plain terms. Nothing in Parts I through VI depends on this appendix; it is offered because a method used throughout should be stated where it can be examined.

## A.1 · The Floor

The commentary stands on one posit: to exist is to actuate. Whatever is, expends something in being what it is; nothing that exists is inert. This is a premise, not a theorem, and it is treated as one. Its only load in this reading is that the kinetic content of a claim, what a passage says happens, is treated as real and irreducible rather than dissolved into metaphor.

## A.2 · Three Registers, Not Two or Four

Any complete claim decomposes into three things: what stands, what moves, and how the two bind. Delete any one and the remainder is incoherent rather than merely incomplete; add a fourth and it collapses back into one of the three. What that test actually shows is narrower than it looks, and the narrower statement is the honest one: it shows that a relational channel is often independent of the other two, which is what makes a structural reading worth performing, and it does not by itself demonstrate that the number is three. So the three registers are stated here as this commentary's working decomposition rather than as a result, and every reading in Parts I through VI stands on the texts it cites and not on the count.

The three must also be genuinely distinct. If the vocabulary filling one slot turns out to be a subset of another's, the slots were never separate and the apparent structure is one thing described twice. That check is what this commentary runs, silently, on every passage: are the existence-content, the kinetic content, and the relational content of this passage independent, and do they hold together?

## A.3 · What a Closing Is

Three independent things enclose something the way three edges enclose a face; three collapsed things enclose nothing. So a reading closes when the three registers stand apart and compose, and breaks when they collapse into each other, and stays open when a register is missing and neither text supplies it.

The mark used for a closing is ⟀. It stands at the end of a Structural Commentary when the three registers of that passage held apart and composed. Raised, ⟀ GOL↑, it marks a passage where a single utterance carries the whole architecture rather than one move within it, and that occurs twice in this book. Where a reading did not close, no mark is set and the reason is given in words. Appendix B lists every reading attempted and the term that binds it.

That is the whole of the machinery, and it explains the three verdicts this commentary uses in plain language throughout. Where a passage was said to hold, the three stood apart and composed. Where a divergence was named, the composition failed at a locatable point and the point was named. And where a text withholds something outright, the commentary says in one sentence what is not there rather than filling it, which is a report about the text and not a verdict on it.

## A.4 · What the Geometry Cannot Do

Two limits, stated plainly because they bound every claim in this book.

The first is that a closing reading is not a true reading. Coherence is not truth; a well-built argument can rest on premises that do not hold. What this commentary certifies, when it says a reading closes, is that the reading is coherent, not that the world is as described.

The second is that the instrument is blind to direction. The test measures whether three registers are independent, and independence is unchanged if a claim is negated. So the structure alone can never tell a claim from its opposite. Direction has to be read from elsewhere: from the sentence itself, from what the words denote, from the arrow of the events. This is why the commentary never rests a finding on structure alone, and why every reading in it is anchored in a construction that can be checked in a lexicon or a manuscript.

## A.5 · Where the Method Was Sovereign

One clause governed the harder passages. Where the two texts diverge on the surface, the structural reading is allowed to finish the sentence the words began: if the two occupy the same geometry, the divergence dissolves and the dissolution is shown; if they do not, the divergence is named and kept.

Three places show it working. On the resurrection of the married, the surface texts appear to conflict and the structure resolves them, since one removes the transaction from creatures and the other assigns the pairing to the Lord, and together they are two halves of one sentence. On the amputations, the structure does not resolve them, since one text uses hyperbolic figure and the other legislates restraint, and the divergence is named at the level of the treatment prescribed. And on food, the divergence was located precisely at the layer where it actually falls, in a narrator's aside rather than in the teacher's mouth, which is a finding about layers rather than a dissolution.

Where neither the words nor the structure could close a question, the commentary said in one sentence what was missing and from which side it would have to come, and left the door standing open. That sentence is not a failure of the method. It is the method, working, at the edge of what any reading can do.

---

# APPENDIX B · READINGS ATTEMPTED

Every structural reading in this commentary, with the term that binds its three registers. Fifteen readings, in the order they were reached, so a passage that earned its own commentary stands immediately before the unit that contains it.

Table*: Every structural reading, with its binding term
