| Where | Reading | Binding term | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mark 1:1-13 | The opening frame: two seats, one descent | the ordering, *opisō mou* | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 1:40-45 | The leper: power, willing, and the touch | the passive imperative | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 1:14-3:6 | Galilee: authority remarked and never derived | the permission, built into the verbs | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 3:7-4:34 | One seed, four soils, and the inverted discriminator | the receiving condition | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 4:35-6:6 | The crossings: a creation already under command | the permission, with Nazareth as control | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 6:7-8:26 | The bread: fertility in the fracture | the upward glance | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 10:45 | The servanthood terminus | the voluntariness | ⟀ GOL↑ |
| Mark 8:27-10:52 | The way: rank inverted on a road with a *dei* | the inversion of rank | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 11:1-12:44 | The city: trust, custody, and accounting | the accounting | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 13:1-37 | The discourse: permanence against dissolution | the reserved hour | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 14:32-42 | Gethsemane: two wills named and ranked | the ranking, performed by the lower | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 15:29-32 | The mockery: a capacity that was never held | *he cannot save himself* | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 15:34 | The cry: an address issued into darkness | the order of the clauses | ⟀ GOL↑ |
| Mark 14:1-15:47 | The cup: written and willed, unreconciled | the asymmetry | ⟀ GOL |
| Mark 16:1-20 | The tomb: the obstacle removed, the Remover unstated | the unnamed Agent | ⟀ GOL |

Two readings carry the raised mark, and both for the same reason: a single utterance in each scripture holds the whole architecture rather than one move within it.

**The servanthood.** Q 4:172 and Mk 10:45. A conferred height presupposed by the very verb that denies disdain of the low place, and a descent through it that ends in a table-servant's word. Two moves, the same order, one verse each, and neither text is quoting the other.

**The cry.** Q 21:87 and Mk 15:34. A doubled vocative issued from inside total darkness, with the complaint preserved rather than edited and the fault located at the caller rather than at the One called. The address precedes the complaint and survives it, which is a paradox held rather than resolved.


---

# APPENDIX C · METHOD DISCLOSURE AND AUTHOR'S POSITION

## C.1 · What was used

This commentary was developed under a verification discipline the author has been building and testing since 2013 and publishes under the name Trisduction. Its content is set out from first principles in Appendix A and can be assessed there without reference to anything else. Summarised in one paragraph: a claim is read on three registers, what stands, what moves, and how the two bind; a reading is admitted when the three are genuinely distinct and their composition encloses something, and is refused when any two collapse together; and the magnitude of a closure is constitutively blind to direction, so direction is supplied by language and never by structure.

The discipline organises and verifies. It generates no evidence and adds no warrant to what it organises. Every conclusion here rests on the two scriptures and on published grammatical, lexical, and text-critical scholarship, and each would stand or fall identically if the discipline had never been applied to it. Appendix A states in its own voice that nothing in Parts I through VI depends on it.

## C.2 · How it was applied here

The three registers were instantiated as follows.

The formal register was grammar. The passive imperative at Mk 1:41 and the passive indicative at Mk 3:5 and Mk 10:40. The separative pronoun that marks the equational construction at Q 5:17 and the same construction affirmed six times over from Q 42:9. The gender agreement that breaks at Q 3:45 to follow the person. The ordinal position of the teaching before the creating at Q 55:2-3. The lexical separation of discrimination from vocalisation. The particle *fa-* that admits no interval between command and result.

The evidential register was distribution. Which predicates the corpus attaches to which terms, how many times, and where it withholds them: the eight occurrences of *kun fa-yakūn* and their clustering, the eleven occurrences of the messianic title and the four that stand inside clauses being corrected, the fourfold permission-clause in a single verse, and the fault-record read across the five who were given a book.

The registrational register was reader-invariance: whether a finding survives a change in the confessional position of the person assessing it. The findings pass that test in a specific and uncomfortable sense. The inventory at II.3 is unwelcome to a polemicist of one tradition and the eight corrections at VI.3 are unwelcome to an apologist of the other, and neither section could have been written to please a reader of either commitment.

## C.3 · Sources, and how they were weighted

The two scriptures are the primary evidence and the only register permitted to close a reading. Every post-canonical interpretive corpus of every tradition enters as witness only, is noted where it corroborates and named where it diverges, and carries no conclusion. This applies evenhandedly: classical Quranic exegesis, patristic and conciliar literature, rabbinic material, the Dead Sea material, and modern critical scholarship all stand downstream of the texts they gloss and are treated the same way. Where a grammatical observation is credited to a named scholar, the credit is for noticing the datum; the datum stands on the text.

Consensus carries no evidential weight in either direction. That a reading is standard is a fact about reception; that a reading is unusual is also a fact about reception. Neither is a fact about the text.

Where the manuscripts divide at a point that matters, the division is reported rather than resolved silently, because the copyists' hands are part of the evidence and are used as evidence at Part III and again at V.7.

## C.4 · What is not claimed

No historical claim is made that the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ read the Gospel of Mark. The reading device is stated as a device in the Prologue and is used as an organising position, licensed by the fact that the corpus performs an extensive reading of the same material, and thirteen surahs carry sustained treatment of it, with passing mentions elsewhere: Q 2, Q 3, Q 4, Q 5, Q 9, Q 19, Q 21, Q 33, Q 42, Q 43, Q 57, Q 61, and Q 66. Every one of them is cited in this commentary and the list is checkable on the same terms as everything else in the book.

No determination is made about the identity of ʿĪsā AS at the level the two traditions actually differ. That is not withheld as a courtesy. It is beyond the reach of the instrument used, for the reason set out at A.4, and an instrument returning a verdict there would be reporting its operator rather than its data.

No transmission claim is made anywhere. The convergence recorded at II.8 is convergence of independent strata on one architecture, which is evidence about the architecture and not about descent.

And no new philology, no new mathematics, and no new textual discovery is claimed. The contribution is the arrangement. Every ingredient is available to any reader with a concordance, a lexicon, a standard grammar, and a critical apparatus.

## C.5 · The author's position

This commentary is uncomfortable for standard positions in at least two places, and both are named here rather than left for a reader to find.

The corpus applies to its own Word a set of attributes it applies to nothing created, and the community that noticed this drew the obvious inference and defended it at cost. The same community reads the Word-language about ʿĪsā AS as though it carried no share of those attributes. That is two treatments of one term, and no textual ground for the split has been offered here or elsewhere. Section II.4 states the problem and II.5 offers the resolution, which costs the received position nothing it needs: the attributes belong to the Word of Allah ﷻ in its residence, the casting proceeds from the Word that carries them and is an act rather than a transfer of the predicated item, and the vessel is a servant who ate food and named the day of his own death.

And VI.3 marks eight places where the reader's own scripture corrects what the received picture made of the Gospel, five of which are the same operation the corpus performs on its own community first. The distinction is load-bearing and is kept there throughout: the first correction is not needed against Mark at all, since Mark quotes the second Psalm and stops one clause before the generation clause; the second observes that Mark never counts persons; and the fifth and the eighth both credit Mark with preserving the very material the corpus is correcting. The corpus's own word for what it does to what came before it is *muhaymin*, custodian, at Q 5:48: standing over the earlier deposit while confirming it. Custody is not replacement, and a custodian who corrects eight items in the reception of a text he confirms, while leaving the text itself standing where it stands, is doing custody.

Finally, and in the register the author regards as governing everything above: the commentary issues no certificate, least of all to itself. *Fa-lā tuzakkū anfusakum, huwa aʿlamu bi-mani ttaqā* (Q 53:32). Do not certify your own purity; He knows best who guards himself.

# APPENDIX D · GLOSSARY OF ARABIC AND GREEK TERMS

Every non-English term that carries argument in this commentary, with the sense it carries here. Arabic first, then Greek, with the two Aramaic words that matter set among the Greek where they occur.

## Arabic

**aḥad** · one, in the sense of one-without-a-second; the word used in negative constructions for not-a-single-one. Contrast *wāḥid*.

**amr** · command, matter, affair. At Q 16:40 and Q 36:82 the creative command by which a thing comes to be, and the root by which Q 17:85 locates the Spirit.

**anṣāb** · set-up stones; called an abomination of Satan's work at Q 5:90, and the corpus's nearest term for what stands where it ought not.

**anṣār** · helpers, supporters. Used of the disciples in the corpus's own approving voice at Q 3:52 and Q 61:14.

**āya**, plural **āyāt** · a sign; also a verse of the Quran.

**awwāb** · ever-returning, one who turns back repeatedly. The epithet of Ayyūb AS and of Dāwūd AS, and a participle that presupposes something to return from.

**ayyada** · to brace, to reinforce, from the root for strength in the hand. The verb of the threefold strengthening at Q 2:87, Q 2:253, and Q 5:110: bracing, not replacing.

**baghtatan** · suddenly, without warning. The corpus's standing adverb for the Hour, six times, and its refusal of the calculating genre.

**bayān** · the faculty of between-making, of holding two things apart and registering the distinction. From the root of *bayna*, between. Given to the human as such at Q 55:4 and retained as a divine undertaking at Q 75:19.

**bi-idhnī, bi-idhni Allāh** · by My permission, by the permission of Allah ﷻ. The clause attached to every miracle the corpus records of ʿĪsā AS, four times in a single verse at Q 5:110.

**dhanb** · offence, fault. Used of the Prophet ﷺ at Q 40:55 and Q 47:19 in a command to seek forgiveness for it.

**furqān** · the criterion, the splitter, from the root meaning to separate. The name of the whole revelation at Q 25:1 and the gift promised to anyone who guards himself at Q 8:29.

**ghayb** · what lies beyond the reach of sight or report; the unseen.

**ḥanīf** · upright, inclining away from all else. Paired with *fiṭra*, the original constitution, at Q 30:30.

**ḥawāriyyūn** · the disciples of ʿĪsā AS.

**ḥikma** · wisdom.

**ḥisāb** · reckoning, counting. *Bi-ghayri ḥisāb*, without reckoning.

**ikhtilāf** · discrepancy, disagreement; the thing Q 4:82 challenges the reader to look for and not find.

**ʿinda** · with, in the presence of, in the keeping of. The preposition that locates the Book, the provision, the signs, and the knowledge of the Hour on the source side.

**Injīl** · the Gospel, treated by the corpus as a book given to ʿĪsā AS, with guidance and light in it.

**iṣṭafā** · to choose out, to select. Used of Maryam AS twice in one verse at Q 3:42.

**jabbār** · a tyrant, one who compels. Denied of Yaḥyā AS at Q 19:14.

**kalima**, plural **kalimāt** · word. Used of ʿĪsā AS at Q 3:45 and Q 4:171 and of the divine speech at Q 18:109 and Q 6:115.

**khatama** · to seal, to set a seal upon. Used of hearts at Q 2:7 and of the prophetic office at Q 33:40.

**khaṭīʾa** · a fault, a lapse; the word Ibrāhīm AS applies to himself, unprompted, at Q 26:82.

**kibr** · arrogance, self-magnification. Its absence is the reason the corpus gives at Q 5:82 for naming a community nearest in affection.

**kun fa-yakūn** · Be, and it is. The creative command, eight occurrences, tabled at II.5a.

**māʾida** · a table set with food; the table requested and granted at Q 5:112-115.

**al-Masīḥ** · the Messiah. Used eleven times of ʿĪsā AS and never withdrawn, including in the clauses correcting what was said about it. Morphologically a passive form: the one acted upon.

**mathal** · a likeness, a similitude. The governing word of Q 3:59, which compares two originations and not two natures.

**mīthāq ghalīẓ** · a solemn covenant. Used of the prophets at Q 33:7, of Banū Isrāʾīl at Q 4:154, and of marriage at Q 4:21.

**muhaymin** · custodian, one who watches over and safeguards. What the corpus calls itself in relation to the scriptures before it, at Q 5:48.

**muqarrabūn** · those brought near. Placed beside the Messiah at Q 4:172, which is what sets the height the verse then denies any disdain from.

**muṣaddiq** · one who confirms or attests; **taṣdīq** is the act of confirming, and it requires two parties.

**musakhkhar** · made subject, placed under command. Used of the sea at Q 45:12.

**muttaqīn** · those who guard themselves, the shield-keepers. The corpus's stated condition for receiving its own guidance, at Q 5:46, and the condition on which the criterion is issued at Q 8:29.

**nafs** · the self, the soul; the bounded layer that tastes death at Q 3:185.

**nasab** · lineage, kinship; a relation between positions in a descent, denied of the Source at Q 37:158.

**qurʾān** · recitation, and also gathering, from a root carrying both senses.

**raʾfa** · compassion. Placed with mercy in the hearts of the followers of ʿĪsā AS at Q 57:27.

**rasūl** · a sent one, a messenger. The office, not the person.

**rizq** · provision, sustenance; not restricted to material food, and twice attached to the negation of counting.

**rūḥ** · spirit, breath. *Rūḥ al-Qudus*, the Holy Spirit; *Rūḥ al-Amīn*, the Trustworthy Spirit.

**ṣiddīq**, feminine **ṣiddīqa** · one of truth; used of Maryam AS at Q 5:75.

**shubbiha** · it was made to resemble; from the root of *mutashābihāt*, the resembling verses of Q 3:7.

**ṣuḥuf** · scrolls, sheets; the scripture of Ibrāhīm AS at Q 87:19.

**taqwā** · guardedness; the practised habit of keeping oneself shielded.

**tawaffā** · to take in full, to receive back. Used of the taking of a self at death and at sleep, and of ʿĪsā AS at Q 3:55 with Allah ﷻ as the agent.

**Tawrāt** · the Torah.

**wāḥid** · one, in the sense of first-of-a-count; a countable one. Contrast *aḥad*.

**waḥy** · revelation given from above.

**wajh** · face; what remains at Q 55:27 and Q 28:88, and never placed under the death-law.

**walad** · a child in the generative sense, offspring; the term whose denial at Q 6:101 is explained by the absence of a consort.

**yastankif** · he disdains, he holds himself above. The verb denied of the Messiah at Q 4:172, and it presupposes a height.

**yatadabbar** · to follow a thing through to its end, from the root for the back or outcome of a thing. The verb of the challenge at Q 4:82.

**Zabūr** · the Psalms.

**ẓulumāt** · darknesses, in the plural. Where Yūnus AS calls from at Q 21:87.

## Greek, with two Aramaic words

**abba** · Aramaic, father. Kept untranslated and then glossed, at Mk 14:36.

**anakekylistai** · it has been rolled back; perfect passive, of the stone at Mk 16:4.

**anastenaxas** · groaning up from within; the only occurrence of the compound in the New Testament, at Mk 8:12.

**anelēmphthē** · he was taken up; passive, in the appended ending at Mk 16:19, which shows how deep the convention ran.

**apekatestathē** · it was restored; of the withered hand at Mk 3:5, with no touch and no formula.

**aphientai** · they are forgiven; at Mk 2:5, in the scene where the scribes quote the corpus's own sentence about who alone forgives.

**automatē** · of itself, without an agent's intervention; of the earth's yield at Mk 4:28.

**bar enasha** · Aramaic, son of man; in ordinary usage, a human being, and the phrase behind the Greek title.

**dedotai** · it has been given; at Mk 4:11.

**diakoneō, diakonos** · to wait at table; a table-servant. The word Mk 10:45 ends on, and the word used of the women at Mk 15:41.

**doulos** · a slave. The word Mk 10:44 trades down to from *diakonos*.

**egō eimi** · I am. At Mk 6:50 in the boat and at Mk 14:62 before the council.

**ephobounto gar** · for they were afraid; the last three words of the Gospel.

**ephphatha** · Aramaic, be opened; kept and glossed at Mk 7:34.

**euthys** · immediately; Mark's characteristic adverb, roughly forty times.

**exousia** · authority, and etymologically permission, from *exesti*, it is permitted. The word the crowd uses of him in the first synagogue.

**gegraptai** · it has been written; the citation-formula, and at Mk 14:21 it stands in the same sentence as a woe.

**genea** · generation; the disputed term at Mk 13:30.

**hē pistis sou sesōken se** · your trust has saved you; said to the woman at Mk 5:34 and to Bartimaeus at Mk 10:52.

**hētoimastai** · it has been prepared; of the seats at Mk 10:40, where the whole point is that the preparing was done by another.

**horkizō** · I adjure, I put you on oath; the demon's own verb at Mk 5:7, which swears the healer by the higher Name.

**ischyros, ischyroteros, ouk ischysan** · strong, mightier, they were not able. The thread that descends from Mk 1:7 to Mk 14:37.

**kairos** · appointed time, as against ordinary elapsed time. The word of the first public sentence at Mk 1:15.

**katharisthēti** · be cleansed; aorist passive imperative, the leper's healing at Mk 1:41.

**kathōs** · just as, according as. With *gegraptai*, the fulfilment formula.

**kraspedon** · the tassel or fringe of a garment, the *ṣīṣīt* the Torah commands at Numbers 15:38.

**kynaria** · little dogs, household animals; the diminutive at Mk 7:27, not the word for scavenging dogs.

**logos** · word, account, reasoned utterance. At Mk 7:29 the woman's own argument is what the healing is granted for.

**lytron** · a release-price, the sum paid to free a captive; Mk 10:45.

**metemorphōthē** · he was transfigured; passive, at Mk 9:2.

**parrēsia** · openly, without reserve; once in Mark, at Mk 8:32, two verses after the charge to say nothing.

**pephimōso** · be muzzled; spoken to an unclean spirit at Mk 1:25 and to the sea at Mk 4:39.

**proagō** · to go on ahead; the thread from Mk 10:32 to Mk 14:28 and Mk 16:7.

**prōton** · first, in sequence. The word that lets the Syrophoenician woman grant the order and deny the exclusion at Mk 7:27.

**schizomenous, eschisthē** · being torn, it was torn; the heavens at Mk 1:10 and the temple curtain at Mk 15:38, the same verb and nowhere else in the book.

**sy legeis** · you say it; the answer to Pilate at Mk 15:2.

**thelō** · I will, I am willing. Granted at Mk 1:41 and subordinated at Mk 14:36.

**yehiv** · Aramaic, it was given; the passive of conferral in the Danielic scene the Son of Man title is cited from.

# APPENDIX E · INDEX OF PRINCIPAL QURANIC CITATIONS

Grouped by the function each verse performs in this commentary. Four hundred and forty-five distinct verses are cited across the book, four hundred and forty-four of them in Parts I through VI and one further in the appendices; what follows is the load-bearing set, gathered so that a reader can find the argument a verse belongs to rather than only the page it fell on.

**The fault-record.** Q 2:37 · Q 7:23 · Q 7:143 · Q 7:150-151 · Q 9:114 · Q 11:46 · Q 11:78-80 · Q 12:24 · Q 12:42 · Q 12:67 · Q 12:84-86 · Q 18:73 · Q 20:93 · Q 20:115 · Q 20:121 · Q 21:87 · Q 26:14 · Q 26:82 · Q 27:20-22 · Q 28:15-16 · Q 37:140 · Q 38:24 · Q 38:31-34 · Q 38:44 · Q 68:48. And of the Prophet ﷺ at Q 8:67-68, Q 9:43, Q 17:73-75, Q 33:37, Q 40:55, Q 47:19, Q 48:2, Q 66:1, Q 80:1, and Q 94:2.

**The three constructions addressed.** Q 4:171 · Q 5:17 · Q 5:72 · Q 5:73 · Q 5:116 · Q 6:101 · Q 9:30 · Q 19:35 · Q 43:81 · Q 112:1-4.

**The equational form, affirmed.** Q 42:9 · Q 22:6 · Q 22:62 · Q 31:30 · Q 51:58 · Q 57:24.

**The ordinal, affirmed, and presence against membership.** Q 9:40 · Q 58:7 · Q 53:20 · Q 37:1-4 · Q 37:164-166.

**The taking, the raising, and the layers.** Q 2:156 · Q 3:55 · Q 3:185 · Q 4:157-158 · Q 5:117 · Q 11:123 · Q 25:58 · Q 28:88 · Q 39:42 · Q 55:26-27 · Q 57:3.

**Transmission and comprehension.** Q 6:50 · Q 7:188 · Q 10:15 · Q 18:65-82 · Q 20:114 · Q 46:9 · Q 53:3-4 · Q 69:44-46 · Q 75:16-19.

**The faculty and the criterion.** Q 2:53 · Q 4:82 · Q 8:29 · Q 8:41 · Q 12:1 · Q 25:1 · Q 26:2 · Q 27:1 · Q 27:16 · Q 28:2 · Q 41:21 · Q 43:2 · Q 44:2.

**Signs, permission, and reception.** Q 3:49 · Q 5:110 · Q 17:59 · Q 40:78 · Q 45:12.

**The unbrokered call.** Q 2:186 · Q 12:86 · Q 21:87 · Q 40:60 · Q 93:3.

**Provision, mercy, and release.** Q 3:37 · Q 6:54 · Q 24:22 · Q 39:53 · Q 65:3 · Q 5:89.

**The bearer, the message, and the counterfeit law.** Q 2:87 · Q 3:144 · Q 25:58 · Q 33:40.

**The hour, and the refusal to calculate.** Q 6:31 · Q 7:187 · Q 12:107 · Q 22:55 · Q 31:34 · Q 43:66 · Q 47:18.

**Custody, continuity, and the substitution of a carrier.** Q 5:13 · Q 5:48 · Q 5:54 · Q 6:164 · Q 9:31 · Q 17:15 · Q 35:18 · Q 42:13 · Q 47:38.

**The adversary's forced theology.** Q 38:82, where Iblīs swears by the Lord's own might in the very act of rebelling, because there is no other vocabulary to rebel in.

**The unlegislated space, and the self-certification bar.** Q 5:101 · Q 22:37.

# REFERENCES

Three works are cited throughout. Everything else named in this commentary is identified in place.

**The Gospel According to Mark.** The Greek text as established in the modern critical editions, with the ending at Mk 16:8 in Codex Sinaiticus and Codex Vaticanus and the longer ending of Mk 16:9-20 treated as a later addition on the grounds set out in the last section of Part IV. Passages are given in a continuous rendering rather than in any single English version, because the commentary reads sequences, placements, and repetitions rather than the wording of one translation, and where a word carries the argument the Greek is given and glossed.

**The Qurʾān.** The received text in the ʿUthmānic recension, cited throughout as surah:verse. Renderings are the author's own, made to carry the grammatical feature under discussion rather than to serve as a devotional translation, and the Arabic is given wherever a rendering is doing work.

Also deposited at PhilArchive, record ISLCTA, https://philpapers.org/rec/ISLCTA. The verification discipline whose method is set out from first principles in Appendix A and whose application to this commentary is disclosed in Appendix C.

And the companion volume to this one: *The Clean Gospel of Mark: A Passage by Passage Commentary on the Earliest Gospel Using Quranic Verses*, Structural Reading Edition. Zenodo, 2 August 2026.
